#Proskenion — System Specification

Project: Proskenion — school auditorium AV control appliance Reference platform: Raspberry Pi CM5 Lite Status: Pre-development specification Version: 3.1 Author: Simon / Symple Solutions Supersedes: System Specification v2.1 and Frontend UI Specification v2.1, now merged into this document Changes in 3.1:

  • Hardware settled — Lenkeng LKV422 HDMI matrix over RS-232 via USB-to-serial (§7.5); DMXking eDMX8 MAX in service, OLA removed for an unverified opendmx serial driver (§7.2.4); presentation audio via optical S/PDIF into ST2 (§7.5); optional Behringer X-Touch Extender control surface (§7.6, §21.25, Phase 9)
  • Lighting reworked — external desk handover is automatic from the booth input, the compositor split into independent DMX and KNX passes, and emergency lighting independence recorded (§7.1, §7.2)
  • Full architecture and UI review worked through — five blockers, six contradictions, eleven gaps, six defects and five improvements, all resolved
  • Device abstraction — every category behind a driver interface with declared capabilities, hardware-independent value domains, transport separated from protocol, fixture profiles replacing hard-coded types (§5.5)
  • §8 restructured as Rules and Scenes — rules say when, scenes say what; scene triggers and KNX bindings collapse into one layer
  • Stack currency — Python floor raised to 3.13 tracking the base OS, frontend to Vite 8, TypeScript 7 and Vitest (§5.1, §22.3)
  • Mixer metering recovered — a capture proved MixPad meters and the data is reachable; the driver now holds MIDI for control and a second connection for metering, with capabilities resolved at connect (§7.3, §5.5)
  • Pages as the everyday surface — channel strips and rule-driven button panels in one layout, group trays, and hirer visibility reduced to page assignment (§21.9)
  • A measured identity palette — ten hues and two neutrals for groups, channels and panels (§21.3)
  • Two end-to-end reviews — the data model and the rules layer, each read against itself and reconciled

Appendix B items 17 to 67.

On the name. Proskenion (προσκήνιον) is the raised stage of a Hellenistic Greek theatre — the narrow platform in front of the skene where solo actors performed, and the word English took "proscenium" from. It was chosen over Proscenium itself, which is taken on PyPI and by two unrelated software projects, and over Cavea, which Wenger Corporation sells as a trademarked audience-seating line for K-12 schools — the same customer in the same room.

The system is referred to throughout this document as the system or the appliance; the name carries no technical meaning and nothing depends on it.

#About this document

This is the single specification for Proskenion. It replaces the previous split between a system specification and a companion frontend specification — that split caused several sections to drift out of step with each other, and merging them removes the problem permanently.

One document remains separate: docs/protocols/cq20b.md, the Allen & Heath CQ-20B MIDI protocol reference. That file is the authoritative source for NRPN addresses, the fader law table, pan values, and message formats, verified against the A&H source PDFs. Protocol values are not reproduced here. Section 7.3 covers the integration model and the handful of key values an architect needs; everything else is a pointer. Duplicating protocol data has already produced errors in earlier revisions and will produce more.

Section 21 is the complete user interface specification, absorbed from the former frontend document.

#1. Project Overview

#Purpose

Proskenion is a self-contained AV control appliance for a school auditorium. It replaces an existing Windows/.NET application and significantly expands what the room can do. The system brings KNX lighting, DMX fixtures, an audio console, a projector, and HDMI video switching under one web-based interface.

The centrepiece of the audio integration is the Allen & Heath CQ-20B — a headless rack-mount digital mixer with no screen, no faders, and no physical controls beyond its I/O connections. It is designed to be driven entirely by software. This application is the primary control surface for it, replacing the need for an expensive physical mixing desk. The A&H MixPad app remains the engineering tool for the parameters MIDI cannot reach: gain, phantom power, EQ, compression, and routing.

#Goals

  • One point of control for every AV system in the room
  • Reliable, long-term operation with minimal maintenance
  • Fully configurable without touching a command line after initial setup
  • Role-gated access for staff, operators, and venue hirers
  • Documented and handover-ready
  • Not locked to a single hardware platform

#Non-goals

  • This is not a commercial product
  • No cloud connectivity is required for normal operation
  • No automatic update checking, no telemetry
  • No simultaneous multi-room control

#Current system

A Windows/.NET console application on a stripped-down Windows 11 machine, which:

  • Connects to a KNX IP gateway and listens for group addresses
  • Controls banks of DMX fixtures through an Enttec Open DMX USB adapter
  • Controls a projector over Ethernet using PJLink

#Replacement system

A Linux appliance that does all of the above, and additionally:

  • Controls DMX fixtures individually or in groups with software fade control, through a web lighting surface
  • Controls the CQ-20B over MIDI/TCP, providing the primary control surface for a mixer that has no physical controls of its own
  • Controls output bus master levels (Main LR plus six auxiliary outputs) for PA, monitors, and recording feeds
  • Controls an HDMI matrix switcher over RS-232
  • Provides a full web configuration and control interface
  • Provides a restricted surface for venue hirers
  • Manages KNX group address feedback and status writes
  • Runs configurable scenes triggered by KNX, the UI, the API, or a schedule
  • Yields DMX control to a visiting lighting desk and takes it back cleanly

#Why a general-purpose computer rather than a microcontroller

An ESP32-class microcontroller was considered early on. The decision to use a full Linux system is architectural, not a matter of preference, and every argument below holds for any Linux computer — Raspberry Pi, x86 mini PC, or a Rockchip single-board computer. The specific board is a reference implementation, covered in Section 2.

#Concurrent persistent connections

The system holds simultaneous, long-lived connections to four network devices — the KNX gateway, the CQ-20B, the projector, the DMX node — plus a persistent serial connection to the HDMI matrix, plus an open-ended number of browser clients over WebSocket. None of these are request/response; all must stay up for the duration of an event.

An ESP32's lwIP stack manages roughly five to eight concurrent connections reliably, fewer under memory pressure or WiFi interference, and it drops or refuses connections silently when that ceiling is reached. A Linux networking stack treats this as a solved problem. The upper bound is irrelevant at this scale.

#KNX daemon maturity

knxd handles KNXnet/IP tunnelling, keep-alive, and gateway reconnection, and presents the application with a clean local socket. It is mature, widely deployed in professional KNX installations, and it absorbs a large amount of protocol complexity invisibly.

There is no equivalent for embedded platforms. Implementing KNXnet/IP tunnelling reliably — including the connection-state edge cases knxd handles without comment — would be a substantial engineering effort with no production track record behind it. For a system that must fire an alarm scene dependably, that is not an acceptable risk.

#Memory and storage

The application runs a Python backend, a web server, a SQLite database, a KNX daemon, and serves a React application to several simultaneous clients. The built frontend alone is several megabytes of static files. This is not a microcontroller workload.

#DMX timing

On Linux, DMX output timing is offloaded to a network DMX node over Art-Net or sACN, which regenerates the physical refresh in hardware. On a microcontroller, WiFi activity causes interrupt-driven preemption that introduces jitter into software-timed DMX, producing visible flicker on dimmers at partial levels. Splitting WiFi and DMX across cores mitigates but does not eliminate it.

#Maintainability and handover

Standard Python, standard Linux, standard tooling. A developer who knows Python and can use SSH can maintain this codebase. The equivalent embedded project would require C/C++ or a constrained MicroPython environment, RTOS task management, and a custom OTA mechanism. The school's ability to find a successor is the primary handover concern, and it is far better served by a conventional Linux application.

#Cost

The price gap between a microcontroller board and a small Linux computer is real but minor next to the KNX gateway, the CQ-20B, the projector, and the installation labour. Saving a hundred dollars on the controller by accepting constraints that would take months to engineer around — if they could be engineered around at all — is not a sensible trade.

#Summary

Consideration Linux appliance ESP32-class
Concurrent TCP connections Effectively unlimited 5–8 reliably
KNX daemon (knxd) Native, production-grade Not available
Memory and storage for the full stack 4 GB RAM, 256 GB SSD ~8 MB PSRAM maximum
DMX timing Offloaded to a network node WiFi contention causes jitter
Development language Python, standard Linux C/C++ or MicroPython
Maintainability Any Linux/Python developer Embedded expertise required
Recovery Bootable USB, SSH, HDMI console Serial flashing, no display
Hardware substitution Any board meeting §2 requirements Vendor-specific

The last row is the one that matters most in 2026. Compute Module supply has been intermittent, and a design tied to one module is a design with a single point of procurement failure.

#2. Hardware Platform

#2.1 Minimum platform requirements

The application targets any 64-bit Linux computer meeting the following. Everything else in this specification is written against these requirements, not against a specific board.

Requirement Minimum Notes
Architecture arm64 or x86-64 64-bit only
RAM 4 GB ~400 MB used under load; headroom is for page cache
Storage One NVMe or SATA SSD, 256 GB Single device holds all partitions (§2.3)
Network Gigabit Ethernet WiFi optional and unused in normal operation
USB Two ports minimum, three preferred Backup media, DMX adapter, recovery keyboard
Watchdog Hardware watchdog at /dev/watchdog Required — see §4.7
Real-time clock Battery-backed Required — see §4.9
Video output HDMI or equivalent Recovery console only, not used in normal operation
Operating system Debian 13 or a derivative Raspberry Pi OS, Armbian, or stock Debian

Power is not specified here because it varies by platform: the reference platform is PoE-powered, an x86 mini PC would be mains-powered behind a UPS. Whichever applies, the controller must be on protected power — see §17.

#2.2 Reference platform

This is what has been built, tested, and documented. Anything else is a port (see docs/hardware/porting.md).

Component Specification
Module Raspberry Pi CM5 Lite, 4 GB RAM
Carrier board Waveshare CM5-PoE-BASE-A
Enclosure Waveshare CM5-POE-BOX-A kit — metal case, fan, PSU
Storage M.2 2280 NVMe SSD, 256 GB minimum
RTC battery CR1220 coin cell in the carrier's holder — required
Power PoE 802.3at — switch port must supply 30 W
Backup media 32 GB USB 3.0 flash drive, permanently inserted
Recovery media 8 GB USB 2.0 flash drive, stored in the rack

CM5 Lite is the preferred variant. The onboard eMMC of a non-Lite CM5 is not used by this design, and it disables the carrier's microSD slot. Lite is cheaper and has generally been the more available SKU.

If a CM5 with eMMC is used — because that is what supply allows — the eMMC becomes a free bonus: flash it with the rescue image (§13.7) and set the boot order to try the SSD first and the eMMC second. That gives a second boot device on the reference platform only. Nothing in this design may depend on it. The recovery USB remains the documented, portable recovery path.

#Anticipated alternative platforms

Not built or tested, but the design accommodates them:

  • x86-64 mini PC or thin client — the easiest port. Mainline kernel, iTCO_wdt watchdog, UEFI boot, RTC standard. Widely available secondhand.
  • Rockchip RK3576 / RK3588 SBC (NanoPi, Radxa, Orange Pi) — arm64 Debian via Armbian. The application stack runs unchanged. The caveat is that these boards typically need a vendor BSP kernel rather than mainline, which makes the watchdog device, thermal zone layout, and NVMe-over-PCIe behaviour board-specific rather than SoC-specific. That is precisely what the platform abstraction layer (§5.4) absorbs.

#2.3 Storage architecture

One SSD holds everything. This is a deliberate reversal of an earlier two-device design that split a read-only eMMC boot medium from a writable NVMe.

The trade-off, stated plainly: a single device means an SSD failure takes the operating system, the application, the database, and the local backup partition together. Two devices would have made those independent events. What single-device buys in return is that the failed component is a commodity M.2 stick obtainable locally the same day, rather than a Compute Module on an unpredictable lead time. Given that the recovery path is well-documented and the real backups live elsewhere, that is the better trade for this installation.

SSD (256 GB minimum)
├── p1   /boot/firmware   FAT32   512 MB   read-only    Boot chain; ESP on x86
├── p2   /                ext4     16 GB   read-only    Root filesystem, slot A
├── p3   /                ext4     16 GB   read-only    Root filesystem, slot B
├── p4   /srv/appliance   ext4      1 GB   read-write   Device identity, fallback config
├── p5   /data            ext4     64 GB   read-write   Application, database, logs, certs
└── p6   /srv/local       ext4   ~140 GB   read-write   Local backup copies, system images

#What each partition is for

p1 — boot chain. Bootloader configuration, kernel and initramfs for both root slots, device tree and overlays. FAT32 both because the Raspberry Pi bootloader requires it and because that makes it an EFI System Partition unchanged on x86. Mounted read-only in normal operation; remounted read-write only during a deliberate OS upgrade.

p2 / p3 — root filesystem, A/B pair. The operating system, system daemons, and everything that must be intact for the machine to boot, bring up a network, terminate TLS, and start a Python process. Mounted read-only with an overlay (§4.3). Only one slot is active; the other holds the previous OS version and is the rollback target (§14.4).

p4 — appliance state. Small, writable, and deliberately excluded from the backup archive. This partition exists for one reason above all others: the device encryption secret must not travel in the same .tar.gz as the values it encrypts. If the secret and the encrypted device passwords were both under /data, they would end up together on the school's file server and the encryption would be decorative.

/srv/appliance/
  ├── device-secret          32 random bytes, generated at first boot, mode 0400
  ├── smtp-fallback.toml     Last-known SMTP config, for alerting when /data is unavailable
  ├── boot-state.json        Active root slot, last known-good slot, update health marker
  └── first-boot.marker

Write volume is a few kilobytes per day.

p5 — application and data. Everything that changes during operation.

/data/
  ├── app/
  │     ├── v1.2.0/          Previous application version
  │     ├── v1.3.0/          Current application version
  │     │     ├── app/       Application code
  │     │     ├── venv/      Per-version virtualenv
  │     │     └── web/       Built React bundle
  │     └── current  →  v1.3.0        (symlink, atomically swapped)
  ├── auditorium.db
  ├── config/
  │     └── baselines/       current.sqlite plus dated copies (§13.5)
  ├── certs/
  ├── logs/
  ├── tmp/                   Streamed uploads and image captures — never RAM
  └── backups/
        └── snapshots/       Pre-change database snapshots (retain 10)

/data/backups/ holds only these pre-change snapshots. The nightly archive is not one of them — it is written straight to /srv/local below, which is where "the local copy" means from here on.

/opt/auditorium is a symlink to /data/app/current, baked into the root filesystem image. Every path elsewhere in this specification refers to /opt/auditorium; only the symlink knows where it actually points.

p6 — local backup copies. Nightly archives (retained 14 days, §13.3) and captured system images (§13.6). There is no separate "/data mirror": the nightly archive already is the copy of everything under /data that matters, and a second, undefined copy alongside it would only be one more thing to keep consistent for no benefit.

This is a convenience tier, not a backup. It protects against filesystem corruption confined to /data, against a bad restore, and against accidental deletion. It does not protect against device failure, because it lives on the same device. The real backups are the USB stick and the network destination. Do not let a future reader assume there are three copies when there are two.

#2.4 Removable media

Medium Mount Role Present
Backup USB, 32 GB /mnt/backup Nightly archives (7 days retained) and system images (2 kept). Data only — no boot partition. Permanently inserted
Recovery USB, 8 GB — Bootable recovery OS and web UI Stored in the rack; inserted only for recovery

Splitting these matters. A permanently-inserted bootable device is a boot-loop risk, and recovery requires the boot order to try USB ahead of the SSD — which means it would attempt the backup stick on every power cycle. Because the backup stick has no bootable partition, the bootloader falls straight through to the SSD every time. Insert the recovery stick, power-cycle, and the recovery environment comes up. No flag files, no boot-order juggling.

A 32 GB stick cannot hold every image and archive it will ever be offered, so the nightly job checks free space before writing and, if a new image would not fit, evicts the oldest image first — never an archive. Archives are small and are what the venue would actually lose sleep over; images are large and are also on /srv/local and recoverable by a fresh capture.

Both should be industrial or endurance-rated where the option exists.

#2.5 DMX interface

Scenario Device Notes
In service DMXking eDMX8 MAX 8-port node, acquired 2026. Art-Net or sACN over Ethernet. Each port is individually configurable as input or output, which is what makes external desk handoff work (§7.2.7). This is the production path.
Spare / fallback Enttec Open DMX USB FTDI-based, no onboard MCU, host-timed. Retained as an emergency fallback via the opendmx serial driver — which has never been tested (§7.2.4). Adequate at best for on/off and slow fades, and cannot track an external desk.
Alternative Enttec USB Pro Onboard PIC for hardware-timed DMX. Not purchased.
Long-term (deferred) 2× Weinzierl 5358 Dedicated KNX↔DMX gateway. Eliminates the DMX transport entirely. Evaluate after pricing.

DMX output sits behind a backend abstraction (§7.2.3), so moving between these is a configuration change.

The eDMX8 MAX is the only DMX path in normal operation. The opendmx driver exists for the spare adapter but is unverified — see the honest assessment in §7.2.4 before relying on it.

#2.6 Downstream devices

Device Protocol Port Status
KNX IP gateway KNXnet/IP tunnelling 3671/udp Existing
Allen & Heath CQ-20B MIDI over TCP 51325/tcp New
Projector PJLink 4352/tcp Existing
Lenkeng LKV422 HDMI matrix RS-232 ASCII Serial, 9600 8N1 New, selected April 2026 (§7.5)
DMXking eDMX8 MAX Art-Net / sACN over UDP 6454/udp (Art-Net), 5568/udp (sACN, E1.31) In service
Behringer X-Touch Extender Mackie Control over RTP-MIDI 5004–5005/udp Optional, Phase 9 (§7.6)
Enttec Open DMX USB USB serial, 250000 8N2 /dev/dmx0 Spare, untested fallback

The LKV422 was purchased in April 2026; insert the actual invoiced figure. It is a budget-class device and replaces the $800–$1,800 shortlist carried in earlier revisions, which is the single largest cost reduction in this revision.

Excluded: the CQ-20B, projector, KNX gateway and lighting fixtures, all either existing or separately procured.

A cold spare SSD in the rack drawer alongside the recovery USB is cheap insurance. It turns an SSD failure from "order a part" into "swap and restore" — a difference of days.

#3. Network Architecture

#3.1 VLAN and addressing

All AV devices sit on a dedicated auditorium VLAN — VLAN 30, 10.2.30.0/24 — on a managed switch in the AV cupboard. A WiFi SSID mapped to VLAN 30 broadcasts on auditorium-area access points and provides wireless access for staff and hirers. A wired drop on the same VLAN is available at the operator position.

10.2.30.0/24 is natively compatible with Art-Net's conventional 10.x.x.x addressing, so Art-Net broadcast discovery works without special routing.

All devices use static addressing. The controller's address is set statically on the device and also reserved by MAC at the switch, so both agree.

Device Address
Router (the VLAN's gateway) 10.2.30.254
Controller 10.2.30.251
X-Touch Extender control surface (optional, §7.6) 10.2.30.100
DMXking eDMX8 MAX 10.2.30.245
CQ-20B 10.2.30.248
Projector 10.2.30.249
KNX gateway 10.2.30.252

The HDMI matrix is not on the network. The Lenkeng LKV422 is controlled over RS-232 through a USB-to-serial cable from the controller (§7.5), so it has no address and no firewall rules.

The school's resolvers on this VLAN are 10.2.40.1 and 122.56.237.1. The addresses above 10.2.30.240 are the ones these devices already had on site — the DMX node, the mixer, the projector and the KNX gateway were not re-addressed to fit the rest of the plan (confirmed on site, 21 September 2026). The controller and the control surface keep theirs.

The old DMXking eDMX4 (10.2.30.220) stays off the network. Since the stage swap-over (29 September 2026) the eDMX8's port A feeds the eDMX4's DMX input, which drives the stage. The eDMX4 re-broadcasts its DMX input as Art-Net universe 0 and has no setting to stop it, so on the network it fed the controller's own output back into the eDMX8 and HTP held the stage at full. It runs unplugged from the network; its DMX input still passes to its outputs. To configure it, first unplug the eDMX8 port A cable from its input, then connect the network, configure, disconnect, and re-patch (docs/hardware/network_map.md, §7.2.7).

The CQ-20B's Ethernet port operates as a DHCP client and its address is configured through MixPad rather than a front panel. Set the address in MixPad and add a matching DHCP reservation at the switch. This is on the pre-installation checklist (§17.4).

#3.2 DNS and TLS

The school uses Cloudflare for DNS. A subdomain — auditorium.obhs.school.nz — is an A record pointing at the controller's private address, 10.2.30.251. The Cloudflare proxy must be disabled (grey cloud, DNS only); a proxied record cannot resolve to a private address.

It must be an A record, not a redirect. A Cloudflare redirect rule runs at Cloudflare's edge and answers with a 301 to some other URL, so the browser ends up connecting to whatever that URL names. Sent to https://10.2.30.251, it is connecting to an address, and the certificate — issued for the hostname — cannot match it: every device gets a full-page certificate warning, every time, which is precisely what §6.16 exists to avoid. A redirect also puts Cloudflare's edge on the path between a browser and a controller that are on the same VLAN, so the hostname stops working when the school's internet does. The A record keeps the whole exchange inside the building.

Certificates are issued by Let's Encrypt using the DNS-01 challenge with the Cloudflare plugin. This requires no inbound internet access to the controller — only outbound HTTPS to Cloudflare's API and to Let's Encrypt.

The Cloudflare API token must be scoped to the single zone with DNS edit permission only. It is the most sensitive credential in the system; a broadly-scoped token would allow DNS changes across the school's entire domain.

A self-signed certificate is the fallback during initial setup and if DNS configuration is unavailable. See §6.16 for what this costs on iOS.

#3.3 Firewall — inbound

nftables on the controller:

Allow:  10.2.30.0/24        → tcp 80, 443      Web interface and health endpoint
Allow:  Admin networks      → tcp 80, 443      Web interface, from administrators' own subnets
Allow:  Management address  → tcp 22           SSH, key-only
Allow:  10.2.30.0/24        → udp 6454         Art-Net inbound
Allow:  10.2.30.100         → udp 5004-5005    RTP-MIDI, control surface (§7.6)
Allow:  10.2.30.248         → udp 51327        CQ-20B native metering (§7.3)
Deny:   everything else

Port 80 serves only /health; all other HTTP traffic redirects to HTTPS.

Admin networks are system.json's admin_networks: subnets outside the auditorium VLAN from which the school administers the appliance (at this site, the office 10.46.0.0/23 and desktop 10.2.10.0/23). They reach the web interface and nothing else — SSH remains the management address's alone. This row was added at the school's request on 24 September 2026, after the first install: a web interface reachable only from inside the auditorium could not be administered from the office. Leave the list empty for the VLAN-only posture this section originally described.

The UDP 6454 rule is needed whenever the Art-Net or sACN backend is active, for two purposes: receiving ArtPollReply from the DMX node for health monitoring, and receiving ArtDmx from a visiting lighting desk during external control (§7.2.7). Both arrive on the same port. It is not required when using the USB fallback.

The CQ-20B metering rule admits the mixer to the fixed UDP port this application announces for meters (§7.3, docs/protocols/cq20b-native.md). It is a standing rule rather than reliance on connection tracking, because the mixer's own meter port is negotiated and meters can arrive before any packet of ours has gone to it.

#3.4 Firewall — outbound

Easy to overlook, and the consequences surface months later. School IT will be restricting a VLAN that has no general business reaching the internet, and without this list, certificate renewal and email alerting will fail silently long after anyone connects the two events.

Destination Port Purpose Failure if blocked
School NTP server (or nz.pool.ntp.org) 123/udp Time synchronisation Degraded time mode, certificate validation problems
Let's Encrypt ACME 443/tcp Certificate issue and renewal Certificate expires after 90 days
Cloudflare API 443/tcp DNS-01 challenge Certificate expires after 90 days
School SMTP relay (host and port held in configuration, not pinned here) 25/tcp Email alerts No notification of any fault
School file server 445/tcp (SMB) or 22/tcp (SFTP) Network backup No offsite backup copy

The relay is a hostname (for example relay.school.nz) held in configuration, not an address pinned in this specification. The firewall resolves that hostname and writes one outbound rule per resolved address, re-resolving on every configuration apply and nightly; if resolution fails it keeps the last known addresses rather than dropping the rule. Port 25 is opened only to those resolved addresses, never to every address — a compromised appliance with an open path to any address on port 25 is an open spam relay, and the firewall's whole purpose is to deny that path. The school's relay in turn accepts mail only from senders on the school network, so an alert cannot leave the site by any route other than this one.

This table belongs in the school IT handover pack (§20.2).

#3.5 Network-layer access — an accepted risk

Any device on VLAN 30 — wired or wireless — can reach the CQ-20B, the projector, the KNX gateway, and the DMX node directly, with no authentication.

This is not a defect in the network design; it is what a flat control VLAN is. But it has a specific consequence for this application that must be stated rather than left implicit:

The permission model in Section 6 governs access through this application only. Someone with access to VLAN 30 can install MixPad and take complete control of the CQ-20B — every fader, gain setting, phantom power, and routing — bypassing the hirer ceilings, the permitted-channel list, and the access toggle entirely. The same is true of the projector over PJLink, the DMX node's web interface, and the KNX gateway.

The technical mitigation would be client isolation on the wireless VLAN plus a switch ACL permitting only 10.2.30.251:443. That is not being implemented, because the auditorium also provides a wired drop on the same VLAN and restricting both would compromise legitimate use.

The accepted position: the permission model is a guard against accident and casual overreach, not against a determined user. For a school auditorium with supervised hires, this is proportionate. Mitigation is procedural rather than technical, and rests on two things:

  1. Hires are supervised by a staff member who is present in the building.
  2. The venue can be restored to a known-good state quickly and reliably — see Venue Baseline Restore (§13.5), which exists specifically to answer this risk.

This position should be reviewed if the room is ever hired unsupervised, or if the VLAN is extended beyond the auditorium.

#4. Operating System and Infrastructure

#4.1 Base OS

Debian 13 (Trixie) or a derivative. On the reference platform, Raspberry Pi OS Lite 64-bit. Minimal install, no desktop environment.

#4.2 Golden image

The partition layout in §2.3 cannot be produced by flashing a stock OS image. Commissioning uses a build script that partitions the SSD, installs the base system into slot A, applies the read-only configuration, creates the /opt/auditorium symlink, installs the package signing trust anchor, removes brltty (§4.12), and writes the systemd units.

This is documented in docs/build/image.md and is a normal part of a process that already exists — the read-only rootfs, symlink, and trust anchor all need doing regardless of how the disk is laid out.

#4.3 Read-only root filesystem

The active root slot is mounted read-only with an overlay; writes go to RAM and are discarded at reboot.

Two things this buys, and both still apply now that the application lives on /data:

Power-loss resilience. A journalled ext4 usually survives an abrupt power cut. A read-only filesystem always does, because it is never in a dirty state to begin with. In a school AV cupboard — someone kills the rack, a breaker trips, the UPS runs out on a Friday night — this is the most realistic failure mode and the single largest reliability lever available.

Drift resistance. In year three, somebody will SSH in to "just install one thing." On a read-only root that change evaporates at the next reboot, and the machine commissioned is still the machine running.

Writable data lives on /data (application, database, certificates, logs, backups) and /srv/appliance (device identity, fallback config). Configuration files the OS expects at fixed paths are handled by one of three patterns:

  1. Symlinked to /data/config/ — for files the OS reads from a fixed location.
  2. Applied at boot by a oneshot service — for settings the kernel reads fresh each boot. auditorium-config-apply.service runs before the main service and applies /data/config/system.json: hostname, nftables rules derived from the device address table, timezone.
  3. Baked into the root image — for genuinely static setup: base nginx configuration, self-hosted fonts, the /opt/auditorium symlink, the signing trust anchor.

Admin UI changes that alter system state write to /data/config/system.json, then restart auditorium-config-apply.service and the relevant system service, applying immediately without a reboot.

#4.4 Filesystem table

Mount by PARTUUID, never by device node — enumeration order is not stable across platforms and /dev/nvme0n1p1 on the reference board may be something else entirely on a port.

# /etc/fstab
PARTUUID=xxxx-01  /boot/firmware  vfat  ro,noatime,nofail                        0 2
PARTUUID=xxxx-04  /srv/appliance  ext4  defaults,noatime,errors=remount-ro,nofail 0 2
PARTUUID=xxxx-05  /data           ext4  defaults,noatime,errors=remount-ro,nofail 0 2
PARTUUID=xxxx-06  /srv/local      ext4  defaults,noatime,nofail                  0 2
LABEL=AVC-BACKUP  /mnt/backup     ext4  defaults,noatime,nofail,x-systemd.device-timeout=5  0 2

The root slot is specified in the bootloader configuration, not in fstab. Partition numbers follow §2.3's six GPT partitions: 1 boot, 2 and 3 the root slots, 4 /srv/appliance, 5 /data, 6 /srv/local.

nofail on /data and /srv/appliance is what makes §4.6 reachable. Without it a failed /data mount drops systemd to the emergency target before multi-user.target, so nginx never starts and the emergency page that exists for exactly this failure can never be served. With it, boot completes far enough to serve that page and to accept SSH, while RequiresMountsFor=/data /srv/appliance (§4.11) still stops the application itself from starting on a broken mount.

noatime avoids pointless metadata writes. errors=remount-ro makes a filesystem error remount read-only rather than panic the kernel, which the application detects and reports. nofail with a short device timeout on the backup USB means a missing or failed stick never delays or blocks boot.

#4.5 Backup media absence

The backup USB is expected to be present but its absence must never affect control.

  • Probed at startup and on a 60-second timer; surfaces as a backup_media status.
  • Amber, not red. Losing it degrades backup only. It does not appear in the operator status bar; it surfaces in Admin → System → Health and Admin → System → Backup.
  • The nightly job skips the local destination, logs skipped: media absent, and completes normally against the network destination. This does not count toward the consecutive-failure escalation in §13.4.
  • After 48 hours with no local backup media, a persistent amber banner appears in the admin UI — that is the point at which the offline copy has been lost rather than merely missed.
  • Hot-insert is supported. A udev rule triggers a remount and the application clears the banner without a restart.
  • If the network destination is also unconfigured or unreachable, the status escalates to red and an email alert fires, because at that point nothing is being backed up at all.

#4.6 /data unavailable — emergency mode

If /data cannot be mounted or is not writable, auditorium-core does not start normally. Instead:

  • nginx serves a static page on 80 and 443 (self-signed certificate) showing the hostname, current time, SSD detection result from smartctl, the last known backup date read from /mnt/backup, and step-by-step recovery instructions mirroring docs/hardware/recovery.md.
  • The health endpoint returns 503 with a fixed, machine-readable payload (§16.7) carrying the failure reason, SMART state, and the last backup date read from /mnt/backup.
  • No device connections are attempted — there is no configuration to load.
  • SSH remains available on the management address.
  • Email alerting works. The SMTP configuration is read from /srv/appliance/smtp-fallback.toml, which is on a different partition. This is the one alert most worth receiving remotely, and it is the reason that file exists.
  • Exit is automatic: repair or replace the storage, reboot, and the system starts normally. There is no "clear emergency mode" step.
  • One exception: not_installed (a fresh image before its first package) ends when an install runs healthily. The helper stops the responder, puts nginx back on its normal site and clears the reason, so the first install does not look like a failure. Every other reason still needs a reboot, so an install can never clear a real fault.

On a single-SSD system, /data being unavailable while the machine still boots is a narrow scenario — filesystem corruption confined to p4, a full partition, or a failed migration. A device failure takes the root filesystem with it and the machine boots to the recovery USB instead (§13.7).

#4.7 Watchdogs

Two layers, running in parallel.

Hardware watchdog. systemd pets /dev/watchdog — provided by the SoC on the reference platform, by iTCO_wdt or equivalent on x86, and by a board-specific driver on Rockchip. If systemd itself hangs, the machine reboots within about 15 seconds. The device path is resolved through the platform layer (§5.4), not hard-coded.

Application watchdog. auditorium-core.service declares WatchdogSec=30s. A dedicated asyncio task calls sd_notify(WATCHDOG=1) every 10 seconds. If the event loop stalls — a deadlock, blocking I/O on the main loop, a runaway task — the notifications stop and systemd restarts the service after 30 seconds. The hardware watchdog catches the case where systemd itself is wedged.

The notify task performs no I/O beyond the notification itself. A blocked event loop is exactly what this is designed to detect; the notify is the canary, not merely a liveness ping.

The notify task also measures event loop lag. It knows when it intended to wake and when it actually did, so the difference is free. It keeps a rolling p50 and p99 over the last five minutes and exposes both in the health screen (§11.2).

This costs nothing and turns "the watchdog fired last night" into "lag climbed steadily for six minutes beforehand, correlated with the backup job". Event loop lag is the best early indicator of the failure the watchdog exists to catch, and it was previously measured only during the soak test (§22.7).

WatchdogSec is suspended for the first 60 seconds after an update, so a slow first start is not mistaken for a hang (§14.5).

#4.8 Platform-specific boot configuration

On the reference platform, /boot/firmware/config.txt:

dtparam=watchdog=on
usb_max_current_enable=1    # if a power-limit warning appears with bus-powered USB devices

No UART overlay is required. The HDMI matrix is reached through a USB-to-serial cable (§7.5), not the GPIO header, which leaves the primary UART's serial console intact as a recovery path and removes the getty and Bluetooth entanglements that a GPIO UART would have introduced.

The path is /boot/firmware/config.txt on Bookworm and later. /boot/config.txt may exist as a compatibility symlink but should not be relied upon.

Boot order is set in the bootloader EEPROM. On a CM5 with eMMC carrying the optional rescue image, order is SSD then eMMC; otherwise SSD only, with USB tried first when the recovery stick is deliberately inserted.

x86 uses UEFI with systemd-boot entries; Rockchip uses U-Boot. Neither has an equivalent of config.txt. Both are covered in docs/hardware/porting.md.

#4.9 Time synchronisation

A battery-backed RTC is a platform requirement (§2.1). On the reference platform this means fitting a CR1220 to the carrier's holder; on x86 and most Rockchip boards it is standard. This is on the pre-installation checklist.

With a working RTC the clock survives power loss, and the boot-time failure mode reduces to "the battery is flat" — a once-in-several-years event with an obvious fix.

# /etc/systemd/timesyncd.conf
[Time]
NTP=ntp.school.nz
FallbackNTP=nz.pool.ntp.org 0.pool.ntp.org 1.pool.ntp.org

The school's NTP server is preferred; the VLAN may have restricted internet access.

Timezone is Pacific/Auckland throughout. Every timestamp in the database, in logs, and in the API uses ISO 8601 with offset: 2026-09-04T14:30:00+12:00.

#Degraded time mode

Retained as a safety net for a flat RTC battery, but deliberately simple. The elaborate monotonic-clock JWT handling of earlier revisions has been removed — it was a large amount of subtle, hard-to-test code defending a case that a working RTC prevents.

  1. On boot, the application waits up to 30 seconds for NTP synchronisation.
  2. If synchronisation succeeds, startup proceeds normally.
  3. If it fails, the application starts in degraded time mode: - HTTPS keeps serving whatever valid Let's Encrypt certificate is already on disk; only renewal is skipped, because a renewal cannot be validated without trusted time. The self-signed fallback certificate is used only if no valid Let's Encrypt certificate exists — falling back needlessly would break iOS's refusal to open a WebSocket over an untrusted certificate (§6.16), and every client validates a certificate against its own clock, not the appliance's - Log entries are prefixed [unverified time] - A persistent amber banner appears in the admin UI: System time not synchronised. Check the RTC battery and the NTP server.
  4. A retry runs every 60 seconds. On the first successful synchronisation the application emits time_sync_recovered, clears the banner, and resumes normal certificate handling.

JWTs are issued normally throughout. A wrong clock may expire sessions early or late; with an RTC fitted, by minutes rather than weeks.

#4.10 Logging

Structured JSON, one object per line, written to /data/logs/ through Python's logging with a JSON formatter. Fields: timestamp (ISO 8601 with offset), level, logger, message, plus context keys such as device, scene_id, user_ident.

  • Rotation — logrotate daily via systemd timer; rotates at 100 MB or daily, whichever comes first; gzip; 90-day retention.
  • Levels — INFO by default per module. DEBUG can be enabled per module from Admin → System → Logs, which writes a flag to /data/config/debug.json and reloads the logging configuration without a restart.
  • Access logs — uvicorn and FastAPI access logs to /data/logs/access.log in combined format, rotating at 50 MB with 30-day retention.
  • Journal — service start and stop events only. Application logs go to files, keeping 90 days of history out of the journal.

#4.11 Service architecture

auditorium-config-apply.service   Oneshot — applies /data/config/system.json before the app
knxd.service                      KNX daemon, manages the KNXnet/IP tunnel
auditorium-core.service           Main application
nginx.service                     TLS termination and static file serving
certbot-renew.timer               Weekly certificate renewal check
auditorium-backup.timer           Nightly data backup
auditorium-verify.timer           Monthly backup integrity verification

auditorium-core declares:

  • RequiresMountsFor=/data /srv/appliance
  • After=knxd.service auditorium-config-apply.service
  • Wants=knxd.service — deliberately not Requires=. Lighting over KNX is one subsystem; the mixer, projector, HDMI matrix and DMX output do not depend on it, and a mistyped knxd.conf must not darken the whole appliance. The thirty-second wait for the socket lives in the application, which marks the KNX domain unavailable and carries on.
  • Restart=on-failure, StartLimitBurst=3, StartLimitIntervalSec=180
  • OnFailure=auditorium-update-rollback.service
  • WatchdogSec=30s
  • TimeoutStopSec=20

If knxd fails to start within 30 seconds, the application starts anyway with the KNX domain marked unavailable.

#4.12 udev — USB serial devices

/dev/ttyUSB* numbering follows enumeration order, which is not stable across boots or across a device being unplugged and returned. Every USB serial device therefore gets a stable symlink, and the application only ever references the symlink.

The HDMI matrix cable is the one that matters in normal operation (§7.5). The DMX rule applies only when the fallback USB adapter is in use, which it is not while the eDMX8 MAX is on Ethernet.

FTDI devices commonly present the default 0403:6001 identifiers, so vendor and product ID cannot distinguish two FTDI cables from each other. Match on serial number.

#Why the chip family matters

Every common USB-serial chip has an in-tree kernel driver. Nothing needs installing, there is no DKMS module and no vendor blob; the device node appears within a second of the cable being plugged in.

Chip family Driver Unique serial number
FTDI FT232R, FT-X ftdi_sio Yes, factory-programmed
Silicon Labs CP210x cp210x Usually
Prolific PL2303 pl2303 Often absent on clones, sometimes duplicated
WCH CH340 / CH341 ch341 Usually none

Driver support is not the differentiator — serial number availability is, because the rules above pin the device path by matching ATTRS{serial}. A cable without one cannot be matched that way, and the fallback is to match a physical USB port:

SUBSYSTEM=="tty", KERNELS=="1-1.2", SYMLINK+="hdmi-matrix"

That works on a fixed appliance, but it binds to whatever is in that socket rather than to the intended device — so a stick plugged into the wrong port during a recovery captures the symlink. Matching on serial is unambiguous in a way a port path is not.

Correction to earlier revisions of this document. It previously stated that Prolific PL2303HXA and XA chips are disabled in current Linux drivers. That is the Windows situation: Prolific phased those chips out in 2012 because of counterfeiting and their Windows driver refuses to bind to them. The Linux pl2303 driver is in the mainline kernel and supports them. The reason to avoid cheap Prolific cables here is the serial number, not the driver.

#brltty

On Debian and Ubuntu, brltty — the braille display daemon — ships udev rules that claim certain CP210x and CH340 devices, because some braille terminals use those chips. It takes the device before the serial driver can present a node, and /dev/ttyUSB0 simply never appears. dmesg shows the driver binding and the node then vanishing, which reads like a hardware fault and is not one.

On a headless appliance it has no purpose:

sudo apt purge brltty

FTDI devices are not affected, which is one more small argument for the specified cable. Removal is on the golden image build (§4.2) and the pre-installation checklist (§17.4).

# /etc/udev/rules.d/99-serial.rules

# HDMI matrix — USB-to-serial cable to the LKV422
SUBSYSTEM=="tty", ATTRS{idVendor}=="0403", ATTRS{serial}=="FTB6SPL2", \
  SYMLINK+="hdmi-matrix", GROUP="dialout", MODE="0660"

# DMX adapter — fallback only; unused while the eDMX8 MAX is in service
SUBSYSTEM=="tty", ATTRS{idVendor}=="0403", ATTRS{idProduct}=="6001", \
  ATTRS{serial}=="AB0C1DEF", SYMLINK+="dmx0", GROUP="dialout", MODE="0660"

Capture each serial at commissioning and record it in docs/hardware/network_map.md:

udevadm info -a -n /dev/ttyUSB0 | grep '{serial}' | head -1

The application references /dev/hdmi-matrix and /dev/dmx0, never a ttyUSB node. Both symlinks are group dialout, and the application user is a member of that group — a permissions failure here is a common and confusingly-reported startup problem, so §7.5 reports it explicitly.

No udev rule is needed for Art-Net or sACN over Ethernet, which is the production DMX path.

#4.13 Nginx

# HTTP — health endpoint only, everything else redirects
server {
    listen 80;
    server_name av.school.nz;

    location /health { proxy_pass http://127.0.0.1:8000/health; }
    location /       { return 301 https://$host$request_uri; }
}

# HTTPS — main application
server {
    listen 443 ssl;
    http2 on;
    server_name av.school.nz;

    ssl_certificate     /data/certs/live/av.school.nz/fullchain.pem;
    ssl_certificate_key /data/certs/live/av.school.nz/privkey.pem;
    ssl_protocols       TLSv1.2 TLSv1.3;

    add_header Strict-Transport-Security "max-age=31536000" always;
    add_header X-Content-Type-Options    "nosniff" always;
    add_header X-Frame-Options           "DENY" always;
    add_header Referrer-Policy           "same-origin" always;

    client_max_body_size 256M;      # KNX imports, backup restores; the update endpoint below overrides this

    root /opt/auditorium/web;

    # Static assets served directly — the shell still loads while the app restarts
    location /assets/ {
        try_files $uri =404;
        expires 1y;
        add_header Cache-Control immutable;
    }

    # The update endpoint carries an OS package's root filesystem image, far
    # bigger than the 256M default. The root filesystem's only buffer space
    # is RAM, and the appliance has 4 GB, so the upload is unbuffered and
    # spooled straight to a temporary file on /data instead.
    location /api/system/update {
        client_max_body_size     2048M;
        proxy_request_buffering  off;
        client_body_temp_path    /data/tmp/nginx-client-body;
        proxy_pass       http://127.0.0.1:8000;
        proxy_set_header Host              $host;
        proxy_set_header X-Real-IP         $remote_addr;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For   $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;
    }

    location /api/ {
        proxy_pass       http://127.0.0.1:8000;
        proxy_set_header Host              $host;
        proxy_set_header X-Real-IP         $remote_addr;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For   $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;
    }

    location /ws {
        proxy_pass         http://127.0.0.1:8000;
        proxy_http_version 1.1;
        proxy_set_header   Upgrade $http_upgrade;
        proxy_set_header   Connection "upgrade";
        proxy_set_header   Host $host;
        proxy_set_header   X-Real-IP $remote_addr;
        proxy_set_header   X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
        proxy_read_timeout 3600s;
        proxy_send_timeout 3600s;
    }

    location /health { proxy_pass http://127.0.0.1:8000/health; }
    location /       { try_files $uri /index.html; }
}

Four details here are load-bearing and easy to lose:

  • X-Forwarded-For and X-Real-IP must be set. Without them FastAPI sees 127.0.0.1 for every request, which silently breaks per-IP rate limiting (one attacker would lock out the whole school), the ip_address column in the audit log, and the failed-attempt email threshold. uvicorn runs with --proxy-headers --forwarded-allow-ips=127.0.0.1.
  • client_max_body_size — the default 1 MB rejects every firmware package and the 5 MB KNX import.
  • proxy_read_timeout on /ws — the default 60 seconds closes idle WebSockets. It would appear to work, because the client reconnects, but the reconnect counter would be meaningless and the resync traffic constant.
  • Static served from disk — the app shell keeps loading during a service restart, which matters during updates.

FastAPI listens on 127.0.0.1:8000 only and is never directly exposed.

#4.14 Bootstrap configuration

/data/config/auditorium.toml, symlinked from /opt/auditorium/config.toml. Contains only what is needed before the database is reachable:

[database]
path = "/data/auditorium.db"

[server]
host = "127.0.0.1"
port = 8000

[logging]
path = "/data/logs"

Everything else — device addresses, KNX group addresses, scenes, mixer configuration, passwords — lives in SQLite and is managed through the admin interface.

The emergency-mode SMTP fallback is not here; it is at /srv/appliance/smtp-fallback.toml, on a partition that survives /data being unavailable.

#5. Application Architecture

#5.1 Technology stack

Layer Technology
Language Python 3.13 — see below
Web framework FastAPI — async, native WebSocket support
Database SQLite via aiosqlite, WAL mode (§15.1)
KNX daemon knxd
DMX — network Custom Art-Net / sACN implementation (§7.2.5)
DMX — USB fallback opendmx serial driver, unverified (§7.2.4)
Serial pyserial-asyncio
Task management asyncio, single event loop throughout
Frontend React 19, TypeScript 7, Vite 8
Frontend tests Vitest
Styling Tailwind CSS 4
Package manager npm, or any compatible alternative

#The Python floor tracks the base OS

requires-python = ">=3.13".

Debian 13 Trixie ships Python 3.13 as its default interpreter, and 3.13 carries upstream security support through October 2029 — comfortably beyond Debian 13's own support window. An earlier revision specified 3.11, which reaches end of life in October 2027, well inside this system's first maintenance cycle.

Targeting a newer interpreter than the base OS ships was considered and rejected. It would mean a source build, a third-party repository, or a bundled standalone runtime — and on a read-only-rootfs appliance examined once a year, that means this project owns CVE tracking for the interpreter instead of Debian doing it. Nothing here needs a post-3.13 feature.

The rule: the floor moves when the OS moves. An A/B upgrade (§14.4) that takes the machine to Debian 14 raises it, which keeps interpreter patching inside a mechanism that already exists rather than creating a second one.

#Frontend versions

Vite 8 replaced the entire bundling pipeline with Rolldown and Oxc, collapsing the long-standing split between one engine for development and another for production into a single Rust pipeline. Straightforward gain, no architectural consequence.

TypeScript 7 is the native Go compiler — roughly an order of magnitude faster on full builds, with strict mode and the 6.0 deprecations as hard defaults, which suits a new codebase.

Lint tooling gates on TypeScript 7.1. TypeScript 7.0 ships without a stable programmatic API, so typescript-eslint and several other tools wait for 7.1. §22.7's pipeline runs lint on every push, so confirm 7.1 has landed before enabling it — or pin a 6.x installation for lint alone in the interim.

npm is the documented default rather than a requirement. §20 promises a successor needs Python and React, and npm is what such a person already has. Any compatible package manager works; nothing in the build depends on which. The JavaScript toolchain never runs on the appliance — §14.1 ships a built bundle, and the controller has no Node runtime — so this is a developer-machine choice, reversible in an afternoon.

#5.2 Directory structure

/opt/auditorium/            →  /data/app/current
  ├── core/
  │   ├── platform.py         Platform abstraction — see §5.4
  │   ├── state.py            Central state store and event bus — see §5.6
  │   ├── knx.py              knxd socket client, DPT codecs, telegram queue
  │   ├── dmx/
  │   │   ├── drivers.py      LightingOutputDriver — Art-Net / sACN / opendmx
  │   │   ├── artnet.py       Art-Net and sACN packet I/O — send and receive
  │   │   ├── universe.py     Universe buffer
  │   │   ├── compositor.py   Level store → group/master scaling → buffer
  │   │   └── fade.py         Fade engine
  │   ├── mixer.py            CQ-20B MIDI/TCP client, NRPN codec, fader law
  │   ├── pjlink.py           PJLink projector client
  │   └── hdmi.py             HDMI matrix client
  ├── rules/
  │   ├── engine.py           Rule loader, trigger dispatch, guard evaluation
  │   └── derived.py          Derived status evaluation and telegram writes
  ├── scene/
  │   └── engine.py           Scene loader, action runner, execution log
  ├── db/
  │   ├── models.py
  │   ├── migrations/forward/
  │   ├── migrations/reverse/
  │   └── crud/
  ├── api/
  │   ├── auth.py   scenes.py   lighting.py   mixer.py
  │   ├── knx.py    hdmi.py     projector.py  hirer.py
  │   ├── system.py setup.py    ws.py
  ├── web/                    Built React output, served directly by nginx
  ├── venv/                   Per-version virtualenv
  ├── config.toml             →  /data/config/auditorium.toml
  └── main.py

#5.3 Concurrency model

Every protocol client is a persistent asyncio task in a single event loop. Each has a run() loop that never exits: it establishes its transport, confirms the device is alive, maintains it, and recovers on failure with exponential backoff. One device going down does not affect any other.

#Connection and liveness are separate

The clients span four transport classes, and for three of them "connected" proves nothing:

Client Transport What actually proves liveness
CQ-20B, PJLink TCP Connection established
knxd Unix socket Socket open
LKV422 HDMI matrix Serial A valid reply to PAXXR — opening the device node succeeds whether or not anything is attached (§7.5)
Art-Net / sACN UDP ArtPollReply — no connection exists to establish
X-Touch surface RTP-MIDI Clock synchronisation alive — the session is negotiated, not connected

The base class therefore requires two methods rather than one:

class ProtocolClient(Protocol):
    async def connect(self) -> None:
        # Establish the transport. May prove nothing about the device.
        ...

    async def probe(self) -> ProbeResult:
        # Confirm the device is alive and responding. Authoritative.
        ...

    async def maintain(self) -> None:
        # Run until the transport is lost. Returns to trigger recovery.
        ...

async def run(self) -> None:
    while True:
        try:
            await self.connect()
        except ConfigurationError as exc:      # wrong path, permission, no route
            await self._set_status(DeviceStatus.ERROR, kind="config", detail=str(exc))
            await self._backoff()
            continue

        result = await self.probe()
        if not result.alive:
            await self._set_status(DeviceStatus.ERROR, kind="device", detail=result.detail)
            await self.disconnect()
            await self._backoff()
            continue

        self._retry_delay = 5                  # reset only after a successful probe
        await self._set_status(DeviceStatus.CONNECTED)
        await self.maintain()                  # blocks until the transport is lost

Status is derived from probe, never from connect. A transport that opened but cannot reach the device is not connected, whatever the socket says.

The two failure kinds surface differently in the UI, because they need different actions:

Kind Meaning What the operator is told
config connect failed Check the address, path, or permissions
device connect succeeded, probe failed Check the cable, power, or device state

§7.5 already had to make this distinction for the serial client, where a port that opens but never replies means wiring rather than configuration. Lifting it into the base class means the next device inherits it rather than reinventing it — and TCP clients get a trivial probe returning the connection state, so nothing is complicated for the simple cases.

Periodic probing during maintain is what drives the health intervals in §11.1. A client whose probe fails twice consecutively returns from maintain and re-enters the recovery path.

Backoff caps at 300 seconds so an extended outage does not turn into sustained connection attempts. It resets only after a successful probe, so a device that accepts connections and then fails to respond does not reset the backoff on every attempt.

No threading anywhere. Blocking calls that cannot be avoided — smartctl, nvme-cli, image capture — run through asyncio.to_thread.

#5.4 Platform abstraction layer

Everything hardware-specific is confined to one module, selected at startup from /proc/device-tree/model or DMI. Without this, platform-specific paths leak into health monitoring, the update mechanism, and the boot logic, and porting becomes an archaeology exercise.

class Platform(Protocol):
    def name(self) -> str: ...
    async def cpu_temperature(self) -> float | None: ...
    async def storage_health(self) -> StorageHealth: ...        # smartctl / nvme-cli
    async def power_source(self) -> Literal["poe", "mains", "unknown"]: ...
    def watchdog_device(self) -> Path | None: ...
    def boot_config_path(self) -> Path | None: ...
    async def active_root_slot(self) -> Literal["a", "b"]: ...
    async def stage_root_slot(self, slot) -> None: ...          # arm the next boot
    async def confirm_root_slot(self) -> None: ...              # make it permanent

Everything that may block is async, even where a given implementation reads a file cheaply. storage_health() shells out to smartctl or nvme-cli; a synchronous call on the event loop would stall it long enough to trip the 30-second application watchdog (§4.7) — and it would do so precisely when the SSD is failing and the reading matters most.

Implementations wrap blocking work in asyncio.to_thread internally, so callers never have to know which methods are cheap on which platform. Only the three pure-lookup methods stay synchronous.

Every implementation applies a 10-second timeout and returns a partial result on expiry rather than raising. A hung smartctl on a dying drive must degrade the health reading, not stall health polling.

Implementations: RaspberryPiPlatform, GenericLinuxPlatform. A Rockchip implementation would be a third, differing mainly in thermal zone discovery and watchdog path.

Everything else in the codebase asks the platform layer rather than reading /sys directly.

#5.5 Device abstraction

Every piece of controllable hardware sits behind a category interface, so replacing it means writing one driver class and re-entering configuration — not changing the core application, the scene engine, the API, or the interface.

This is the difference between changing a mixer being a driver and being a migration.

#Three concepts

Category — a domain interface defined by what the venue needs to do, not by what any particular device offers.

Driver — a class implementing a category interface, registered by key, shipped with the application.

Capabilities — what a given driver actually supports, which the UI, scene engine and permission model read to adapt. Resolved after connect(), not declared statically — see below.

Category interface    ← the core depends only on this
      ↑
   Driver             ← knows one protocol
      ↓
 Capabilities         ← the core reads this to adapt

#Categories

Category Drivers shipped Plausible alternatives
mixer cq20b, stub X32/M32, Qu, Wing, or none at all
lighting_output artnet, sacn, opendmx KNX–DMX gateway
projector pjlink Vendor IP, RS-232, NEC/Epson/Panasonic
video_matrix lkv422, stub Ethernet matrix, HDBaseT, SDI router
control_surface xtouch Any Mackie Control or HUI surface

KNX is deliberately not a category. See below.

#Capabilities

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class MixerCapabilities:
    input_count: int
    output_count: int
    supports_scene_recall: bool
    supports_pan: bool
    supports_mute: bool
    supports_metering: bool          # CQ-20B: true when the native
                                     # connection is up (§7.3)
    meter_min_db: float | None       # -60.0 on the CQ
    meter_max_db: float | None       # +10.0
    meter_point: Literal["post_comp", "post_fader", "post_limiter"] | None
    supports_gain: bool              # False for the CQ-20B — MixPad only
    supports_dca: bool
    min_db: float
    max_db: float

supports_metering demonstrates why this earns its place. Three separate places once hard-coded the same fact: §7.3's protocol notes, §21.13's mixer view, and §7.6's LED meters on the control surface. Under this model all three read one flag — which turned out to matter, because that fact was wrong and every one of them had to be corrected when the CQ's native protocol was found (§7.3). Had the flag existed first, the correction would have been one line.

meter_point is declared rather than left in prose: CQ inputs are post-compressor and outputs post-limiter, which answers "is this channel live?" and would mislead anyone reading it as gain structure.

#Capabilities are resolved at connect, not declared

A driver's capabilities may depend on what the connection actually achieved.

The CQ-20B is the case that forces it (§7.3): the driver holds MIDI for control and a second, reverse-engineered connection for metering. If the second is refused — the mixer's client limit is reached, or a firmware change closed the port — the mixer still works completely and only metering is lost.

Static capabilities would then be a lie in the worst direction: the interface would render meters that never move and the control surface would light 64 LEDs that stay dark, with nothing to explain why.

class Driver(Protocol):
    async def connect(self) -> None: ...
    def capabilities(self) -> MixerCapabilities: ...    # valid after connect

Before connection the driver reports its declared maximum, which is what the admin interface shows when configuring hardware that is not yet reachable. After connection it reports what it actually has, and the difference is surfaced rather than hidden — "metering unavailable: the native connection was refused" is a diagnosis; dead meters are a mystery.

This generalises beyond the mixer. A projector's supported inputs are not knowable until it is asked either.

#Category interfaces

Deliberately narrow. Every method is something the venue needs, not the union of what some device happens to offer.

class MixerDriver(Protocol):
    def capabilities(self) -> MixerCapabilities: ...       # valid after connect
    async def set_level(self, refs: list[str], db: float | None) -> None: ...  # None = off
    async def set_mute(self, refs: list[str], muted: bool) -> None: ...
    async def set_pan(self, ref: str, pan: float) -> None: ...           # -1.0 .. 1.0
    async def recall_scene(self, scene_ref: str) -> None: ...
    async def read_state(self, refs: list[str]) -> dict[str, ChannelState]: ...
    def available_refs(self) -> list[ChannelRef]: ...      # populates the admin picker
    def fader_law(self) -> list[LawPoint]: ...             # scale, detents — see below

class LightingOutputDriver(Protocol):
    def capabilities(self) -> LightingCapabilities: ...
    async def send_universe(self, universe: int, data: bytes) -> None: ...
    async def blackout(self) -> None: ...

class ProjectorDriver(Protocol):
    def capabilities(self) -> ProjectorCapabilities: ...
    async def set_power(self, on: bool) -> None: ...
    async def set_input(self, input_ref: str) -> None: ...
    async def read_state(self) -> ProjectorState: ...

class VideoMatrixDriver(Protocol):
    def capabilities(self) -> MatrixCapabilities: ...
    async def route(self, outputs: list[str], input: str) -> None: ...  # §7.5
    async def read_routing(self) -> dict[str, str]: ...
    def available_refs(self) -> MatrixRefs: ...

class ControlSurfaceDriver(Protocol):
    def capabilities(self) -> SurfaceCapabilities: ...
    def manifest(self) -> list[SurfaceControl]: ...        # layout, see below
    async def set_fader(self, strip: int, position: float) -> None: ...   # 0.0–1.0
    async def set_display(self, strip: int, upper: str, lower: str,
                          colour: int) -> None: ...    # 2 rows x 7 chars
    async def set_led(self, strip: int, button: int, state: LedState) -> None: ...

Every driver also implements connect / probe / maintain from §5.3.

capabilities() is a method, not an attribute, because it is resolved after connection rather than declared by the class (below). List parameters are deliberate — one call is one intent the driver can optimise, per the single-intent rule.

available_refs() is what keeps the admin interface generic. The core stores an opaque driver_ref; the driver enumerates valid references with human labels, so the channel picker works against any mixer without core changes.

#Channel references and the virtual surface

A driver enumerates what it can address; the venue decides what to expose. These are separate layers and neither derives from the other.

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ChannelRef:
    ref: str        # driver-meaningful — 'ip1', 'out12', 'main'
    label: str      # human — "Input 1", "Out 1/2 (linked)", "Main LR"
    kind: Literal["input", "output", "main", "fx_return", "dca"]
    stereo: bool

kind comes from the driver rather than being declared by the admin. Picking "Out 1/2" tells the system it is a stereo output; nothing has to be entered twice or can be entered wrongly. A manual override exists for desks whose driver cannot tell, but it is an override rather than the primary path.

#The virtual surface is configuration, not derivation

mixer_channels (§15.6) is the virtual surface. Rows are named by the venue and ordered by sort_order. When a mixer is added, one row is created for every channel the desk has, visible to staff; the venue then names, hides, re-orders or deletes them (Simon's decision, 28 September 2026: what hirers see is controlled by their pages, not by which rows exist). The virtual surface is still the venue's configuration, never re-derived from the desk: names and order are never read back. An installation might show only four of them to operators:

name driver_ref sort_order
Mic 1 ip1 0
Mic 2 ip2 1
HDMI st2 2
Master main 3

There is no expected correlation between a virtual channel's position and the desk's numbering. Adding an AUX between Mic 2 and HDMI is a new row at sort_order 2, pointing at whatever input the desk happens to have free. That is the normal case, not a deviation from one.

This is why a swap is cheap: names, order, visibility, ceilings, scene actions and control surface assignments all survive, because none of them ever depended on the driver's numbering. Only driver_ref changes.

Every desk channel is created with the mixer. Hiding a channel from operators is a switch (visible_staff), and hirers see only what their pages carry, so a full set costs nothing to the people using the room. Add missing channels (Admin → Mixer) creates any desk channel no row covers (an existing mixer, or one whose new driver declares more) and never renames, re-orders, re-points or deletes a row. A driver swap never creates rows itself; the re-map offers it afterwards.

driver_ref is shown in configuration views and never in operator views. The audio engineer needs it to talk about the same channel as someone holding MixPad; the operator does not.

#Naming is ours, not the desk's

A channel's name comes from this system's configuration, never from the mixer.

They are different namespaces for different audiences. The desk's labels belong to the audio engineer and describe a physical input, seen in MixPad. Ours belong to the operator and describe what the venue calls the thing. A virtual channel named "HDMI" maps to st2, whose desk label is whatever the engineer happened to type.

Deriving one from the other would mean the operator's interface changing because someone renamed a channel during soundcheck.

The desk's labels do earn a place in one spot: ChannelRef.label populates the admin picker, where ip1 — Wireless 1 is easier to choose from than a bare reference. That is a configuration aid, and it is never rendered to an operator.

On the CQ those names sit at fixed byte offsets in the FullDataResponse with no version guard (docs/protocols/cq20b-native.md). The driver returns static names in v1 — "Input 1", "ST2" — because a picker showing confidently wrong names during configuration is worse than one showing generic ones. Device-supplied names are a later refinement once those offsets are confirmed.

#One name, and a short form only where the hardware forces it
name        The channel's name. Rendered wherever there is room.
short_name  Nullable. Only the control surface's 7-character scribble
            strip needs it (§7.6). Defaults to truncating `name`.
notes       Free text. The description, not the name.

The virtual surface does not need a short form. A 150 px strip at 13.5 px takes about sixteen characters on one line, which covers any reasonable channel name. Names never wrap — a wrapped label is harder to scan than a shorter one, and the admin editor shows a live width preview so a name that will not fit is visible while typing (§21.21).

The rule that follows, and it is worth stating plainly: if a name does not fit a channel strip, it is being used as a description. notes is where a description goes.

#Truncation is checked, not trusted

Plain truncation is usually fine — "Foldback" becomes "Foldbac" and nobody is confused. It fails in one specific way, and that failure is severe:

Two channels named "Wireless Mic 1" and "Wireless Mic 2" both truncate to "Wirele". The number is the only part that discriminates, and truncation removes exactly that. On a surface where the operator cannot check, two strips would carry the same label.

So the rule is a collision check rather than a cleverer algorithm. A heuristic that turned "Wireless Mic 1" into "Mic 1" would be right often enough to be trusted and wrong often enough to mislead.

short_name is REQUIRED when the effective short form
collides with another channel's in the same bank.

The surface configurator blocks the save and asks for an override on exactly the channels that need one (§21.25). Everything else keeps its truncation and no one has to think about it.

#Ganged channels

One virtual fader may map to several driver references — a desk with discrete mono outputs presented as a stereo pair, or a group fader on a desk with no DCAs.

virtual channel  →  [driver_ref, ...]     one or more

Ganging is absolute, not offset-preserving. Every ref is set to the same value. Offset-preserving ganging is a trim concept and it is what DCAs are for; building a second mechanism into the mapping layer would mean two ways to do one thing with different edge cases.

Two consequences:

  • Divergence. If MixPad moves one side, the refs no longer agree. The fader displays the first ref's value with a divergence indicator, and the next write re-converges them. Hiding it would misrepresent the desk.
  • Hirer ceilings are unaffected. A ganged channel has one ceiling because it is one channel — hirer_max_db sits on mixer_channels, not on the references beneath it. Ganging cannot become a route around a ceiling, because there is only ever one to apply.
#Unmapped channels fail closed

After a driver change, a virtual channel whose reference has no equivalent on the new desk is marked unmapped. It is not controllable, not exposed to hirers, and shown as needing attention — rather than quietly retaining its old ceiling against a reference that no longer resolves.

#Value domains

The core must not store any hardware's wire format. Earlier revisions stored mixer levels as 14-bit NRPN — the CQ-20B's MIDI encoding — in hirer_max_fader, in every saved scene action, over the API and over the WebSocket. Swapping the mixer would have left every hirer ceiling and every scene action silently wrong rather than obviously broken: 10000 is a valid number on another desk with an entirely different meaning.

Value Core representation Driver converts to
Mixer level float dB, null for off NRPN 14-bit, or whatever the desk uses
Pan float −1.0 to +1.0 NRPN with 8192 centre, or equivalent
Lighting level float 0–100, one decimal (§9.2) DMX 0–255, KNX 0–100
Surface fader float 0.0–1.0 position MCU 14-bit pitch bend

"Set this channel to −5 dB" survives a mixer swap. "Set this channel to 10000" does not.

#The fader law is published as data

A fader's travel is not linear in dB, and the curve belongs to the desk, not to this application. Three constraints shape the design:

  • The core cannot own the conversion — a generic curve would put the tablet's thumb at a different position from MixPad's for the same level, which reads as a fault rather than a difference.
  • The driver cannot be called per pixel — a 60 fps drag cannot round-trip to Python for each position.
  • Different surfaces have different resolution: the X-Touch is 14-bit, a touchscreen is pixel-limited.

So the driver publishes its law as a table, fetched once alongside capabilities:

{ "fader_law": [
    { "position": 0.000, "db": null,  "label": "-\u221E" },
    { "position": 0.088, "db": -50.0 },
    { "position": 0.176, "db": -40.0, "label": "-40" },
    { "position": 0.288, "db": -30.0, "label": "-30" },
    { "position": 0.424, "db": -20.0, "label": "-20" },
    { "position": 0.572, "db": -10.0, "label": "-10" },
    { "position": 0.660, "db":  -5.0, "label": "-5"  },
    { "position": 0.766, "db":   0.0, "label": "0", "detent": true },
    { "position": 0.884, "db":   5.0, "label": "+5" },
    { "position": 1.000, "db":  10.0, "label": "+10" }
]}

Twenty to fifty points, linearly interpolated between. The driver emits whatever resolution its hardware needs. db: null is the bottom of travel — off, not a number.

#The table also produces the printed scale

Two optional fields do the work.

label marks a point the desk prints on its panel. Points without one render as minor ticks with no text. This has to be a driver property because desks print different scales — an A&H CQ prints +10 +5 0 −5 −10 −20 −30 −40 −∞, another desk prints a different set and puts unity somewhere else.

Hard-coding one manufacturer's legend would leave the scale confidently wrong on any other hardware, which is worse than having no scale: a fader whose printed 0 dB is not where 0 dB actually is misleads at exactly the moment someone is trusting it.

The scale, the thumb position, the dB readout and the control surface's own fader all interpolate from this one table, so they cannot disagree.

#Detents

detent: true marks a value the fader holds at. Unity is the obvious case and the one operators return to most.

  • The zone is ±1.5 dB, which is around 6–7% of travel on the CQ's law
  • Snapping is to the dB value, not to a position, so the value that goes on the wire is exactly 0.0 rather than 0.03
  • Breaking out takes a deliberate further push rather than a nudge — implementation in §21.2
  • The fader shows it is held: the cap picks up a ring and the readout changes colour (§21.13)

This matters far more on a touchscreen than with a mouse, where a pointer can be placed precisely and a fingertip cannot.

A driver may declare several detents. Lighting faders have none — 0–100 linear has no unity to return to, and a detent at 100% would fight the operator on the most common movement.

Drag  →  position 0.0–1.0  →  interpolate to dB  →  send dB
Frame →  dB                →  interpolate back    →  draw thumb

dB travels on the wire, not position. The server does not need to know which surface a write came from, and a scripted caller can send −5 dB without knowing anything about fader travel.

The control surface (§7.6) converts its 14-bit pitch bend to a 0.0–1.0 position and runs the same table, so a physical fader and an on-screen fader sit at identical positions for the same level — which is what makes motorised recall look correct rather than approximately correct.

Lighting keeps its own rule. Lighting is 0–100 linear with no law and no driver involvement, so a control surface strip's scaling depends on its target domain (§7.6).

#Metering

Meters are the only data in the system with two independent consumers and a rate mismatch at every hop — a mixer emitting at its own rate, a browser batching at 10–15 fps, and a control surface capped per strip. That needs a defined path rather than an ad-hoc one.

driver ~30 fps
  → MeterFrame, keyed by driver_ref
  → mapped to channels via mixer_channel_refs (§15.6)
  → state.mixer.meters                       display-only store
       ├→ WebSocket, batched (§16.8)         → virtual surface  (§21.13)
       └→ control surface driver             → MCU              (§7.6)

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class MeterFrame:
    device_id: int
    levels: dict[str, float | None]   # driver_ref → dB; None = below floor
    at: float                         # monotonic, for staleness
#Meters never enter the control path

Not persisted, not read by the scene engine, never able to influence a fader value or an output.

This is the same rule as lighting's observed store (§7.2.7) and it exists for the same reason: a 30 fps feed must stay out of the dirty set that drives persistence and out of anything that could act on it.
