A meter is a display, and the moment something reads it to make a decision, the system has a control loop nobody designed.

#Ganged channels carry a list

A stereo channel maps to several driver_refs (§5.5), and each gets its own meter — a summed meter would hide one side of a stereo source failing, which is the fault this feature exists to find.

state.mixer.meters: dict[int, list[float | None]]   # channel_id → one per ref

Ordered by the ref's sort_order, so index 0 is left. A mono channel is a one-element list, not a scalar — one shape everywhere, no branching in the interface, and ganging a channel later does not change its type.

#Absent, not zero

A channel with no meter data gets no entry at all, rather than an entry at the floor. That is what makes §21.13's absent-not-empty rule implementable: the interface renders no bar, which reads as not offered, instead of a bar at the bottom, which reads as silence.

#Rate handling
Hop Rate Policy
Driver → core Whatever the device sends Keep latest, no queue
Core → WebSocket 10–15 fps Continuous class, drop oldest (§5.6)
Core → control surface 15 fps per strip Capped in the driver (§7.6)

Meters are the textbook continuous event: a dropped frame is invisible, and the newest value is the only one worth having. Never the discrete class — blocking a producer to deliver a meter update would be exactly backwards.

#Scale

meter_min_db and meter_max_db come from capabilities, not from the interface. The CQ's −60 to +10 is that desk's range, not a universal one, and hard-coding it would be the same mistake as hard-coding a fader legend.

Both consumers scale from the declared range: the browser to bar height, the control surface driver to LED segments.

#Control surface manifests

A control surface driver publishes its physical layout as structured data, not as markup. The interface renders from it.

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class SurfaceControl:
    id: str                  # driver-meaningful, e.g. "fader_3"
    type: Literal["fader", "encoder", "button", "display", "led", "meter"]
    region: str = "strips"   # "strips" | "master" | "transport"
    col: int                 # 0-based within the region
    row: int
    span: int = 1
    label: str | None = None # printed legend — "REC", "SOLO"
    assignable: bool = True  # False for hardwired controls
    motorised: bool = False
    functional: bool = True  # see below

Grid coordinates, not pixels. Control surfaces are grid-shaped, and a grid renders consistently at any scale. Pixel positions would encode one visual size and break on a narrow viewport.

region costs one string and means a full X-Touch's transport section does not require reworking the model later.

#functional — present but unusable

A control that physically exists but cannot be driven in the current configuration is declared as such, and the declaration is re-evaluated whenever capabilities change.

The X-Touch Extender's LED meters are the case. They light when the configured mixer reports supports_metering — for the CQ-20B, whenever its native connection is up (§7.3) — and are declared non-functional when it does not. Either way the configurator states which, rather than offering an assignment that would silently do nothing (§21.25).

An earlier revision declared them permanently non-functional on the belief that no metering existed anywhere in the system. That belief was wrong, and the fact that it took one flag to correct is the argument for capability declaration in the first place.

#Why not driver-supplied SVG

A driver rendering its own panel was considered and rejected on four grounds:

  • It is markup injected into the admin interface. Drivers ship with the application, so this is not remote code — but §6.11 exists precisely to stop an update shipping its own rendering, and this would be a hole in that reasoning.
  • It cannot participate in the design system. Driver-authored SVG would not use the tokens in §21.3, the LED treatment in §21.4, or the contrast rules in §24.4. The result is a panel that looks foreign inside its own admin screen.
  • Interaction needs a schema regardless. Which elements are clickable, and how does a click map to a control? Answering that means defining attribute conventions inside the markup — which is a schema, just an implicit one.
  • Accessibility cannot be retrofitted. §21.25's roles, labels and keyboard traversal need structured data. "Strip 5 fader, assigned to Row 1" cannot be generated reliably from arbitrary paths.
#Physical and virtual differ deliberately
Layout comes from Because
Virtual surface Configuration (§5.5) It describes what the venue chose to expose
Physical surface The driver's manifest It describes hardware that physically exists

You cannot curate an eight-fader surface into having nine. Assignment of controls to targets is configuration on both; only the layout differs.

Banks are physical-surface-only. The X-Touch has eight strips and needs banks to reach more than eight targets. The virtual surface has however many rows the venue created and scrolls. What the two share is the assignable-target vocabulary (§7.6) — a strip may point at a mixer channel, a lighting group, a scene — not the strip and bank tables.

#Transports

Transport and protocol are orthogonal. The X-Touch demonstrates this in hardware: one encoder selects Mackie Control or HUI, a different one selects Network, USB or MIDI (§7.6). The same holds elsewhere — MIDI over TCP, USB and DIN carry identical semantics, and most projector command sets run over both RS-232 and TCP.

A driver therefore declares which transports it works over, and the transport owns addressing. A driver never mentions a host or a device path.

class Transport(Protocol):
    SCHEMA: list[Field]
    async def open(self) -> None: ...
    async def close(self) -> None: ...
    async def send(self, data: bytes) -> None: ...
    async def receive(self, timeout: float) -> bytes: ...
    async def enumerate(self) -> list[PortOption] | None: ...   # None = not enumerable
Transport Configuration Used by
tcp host, port CQ-20B, PJLink, Ethernet matrices
serial device path, baud, bits, parity, stop, flow LKV422, RS-232 projectors
udp host, port, bind port, broadcast Art-Net, sACN, RTP-MIDI
unix_socket path knxd
loopback — Stub drivers and tests
#This is what the connect/probe split was finding

§5.3 separates connect from probe because for serial and UDP a successful connection proves nothing. The reason is now explicit:

connect()   the transport's job — open a socket, a port, a device node
probe()     the driver's job    — PAXXR, ArtPoll, clock sync, %1POWR ?

Backoff, reconnection and the config-versus-device error distinction (§5.3) live in the transport base: one implementation rather than five that drift apart.

#Framing stays with the driver

Transports deliver bytes and datagram boundaries. Where a message ends is protocol-specific — the LKV422 makes the case, since its line terminator is unknown and its reader must be terminator-agnostic (§7.5). Pushing framing into transport configuration would mean inventing a protocol-description language for one uncertain case.

Common helpers — line-delimited, delimiter-terminated, fixed-length — are utilities a driver calls, not configuration.

#No exceptions

Every driver goes through a transport. An earlier revision carried a custom transport for OLA — a daemon with its own client library, which would have bypassed the layer entirely. Removing OLA (§7.2.4) removed the exception with it.

If a future driver appears to need custom, that is the signal the transport layer is missing a case, not an invitation to reintroduce the escape hatch. An abstraction with one declared exception invites a second.

#Configuration schemas

Two schemas, rendered as two sections. The transport supplies addressing; the driver supplies protocol settings only.

# core/transport/tcp.py — defined once, shared by every TCP driver
TCP_SCHEMA = [
    Field("host", type="host", label="IP address", required=True,
          help="Static address; must match the switch reservation"),
    Field("port", type="port", label="Port", required=True, min=1, max=65535),
]

class CQ20BDriver:
    key = "cq20b"
    category = "mixer"
    name = "Allen & Heath CQ-20B"

    SUPPORTED_TRANSPORTS = ["tcp"]
    TRANSPORT_DEFAULTS = {"tcp": {"port": 51325}}

    CONFIG_SCHEMA = [                      # protocol only — no addressing
        Field("midi_channel", type="int", label="MIDI channel",
              default=1, min=1, max=16),
    ]

Stored as:

{
  "transport": { "type": "tcp", "host": "10.2.30.248", "port": 51325 },
  "driver":    { "midi_channel": 1 }
}
#Field definition
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Field:
    key: str
    type: Literal["string", "int", "bool", "enum",
                  "port", "password", "device_path",
                  "host"]   # host and device_path belong to transports
    label: str
    required: bool = False
    default: Any = None
    min: int | None = None          # numerics
    max: int | None = None
    pattern: str | None = None      # strings
    options: list[tuple[str, str]] | None = None   # enum: (value, label)
    depends_on: tuple[str, Any] | None = None      # show only when another field matches
    encrypted: bool = False         # stored per §6.10
    help: str | None = None

host is gone from the driver field vocabulary — it belongs to transports now.

depends_on is what makes the transport picker work: serial fields appear only when serial is selected.

Client-side validation is convenience, not the boundary. The API validates against the same schema and returns validation_failed with per-field detail (§16), because a scripted caller bypasses the form.

#Cross-field validation
async def validate_config(self, config: dict) -> list[ConfigError]: ...

For checks a schema cannot express: a device path that does not exist, an address colliding with another configured device, a baud rate the driver knows this hardware rejects. Runs on save, before connecting.

#No dynamic options

A dropdown populated by querying the device is rejected: enumeration requires a connection, and a connection requires configuration. available_refs() (§5.5) exists for post-connection enumeration and is the right place for it.

Host-level enumeration is different and is supported — see below.

#The field vocabulary is closed

If a driver needs something outside those eight types, the list grows deliberately and the Devices screen learns to render it. A driver never ships its own control — the same reasoning that rejected driver-supplied panel markup.

#Serial port enumeration

serial is the only transport that can enumerate, and it enumerates the host, not a device, so there is no chicken-and-egg.

Enumerate from /dev/serial/by-id/, never /dev/ttyUSB*. pyserial's list_ports reports ttyUSB0, and storing that is precisely the defect §4.12 exists to prevent — the numbering follows enumeration order and moves on replug.

The by-id path is udev-populated, stable across reboots, and carries the device's serial number:

/dev/serial/by-id/usb-FTDI_USB-RS232_Cable_FTB6SPL2-if00-port0

The picker enumerates both, displays the descriptive metadata, and stores the by-id path.

Each option carries vendor, product and serial — three FTDI cables all present 0403:6001 and are otherwise indistinguishable — plus two flags:

  • In use, when another configured device or a getty holds the port. This surfaces §7.5's "device busy" failure at configuration time rather than at first connect.
  • No stable path, when the cable has no serial number and no by-id entry exists. The picker falls back to ttyUSB* and says plainly that the path will move — §4.12's chip-family problem appearing at the moment it can still be acted on by buying a different cable.
#This makes udev rules optional

If by-id paths are stable and free, §4.12's custom rules become documented best practice rather than a requirement — nothing to write at commissioning and no serial to capture by hand. The trade is readability: /dev/hdmi-matrix reads better in a log than a sixty-character path.

Either way the recovery card note stands: replacing a cable means updating the path or the rule, because a new cable has a new serial.

#One intent, one call

Interface methods take the full set of targets for a single intent. Drivers may combine, batch or reorder as their protocol allows. The core never issues N calls for what is one operation.

This is not a style preference. A single call is one intent the driver can optimise; two calls are two intents, and by the time the second arrives the driver has already committed the first and cannot retrospectively combine them.

It applies in three places:

async def route(self, outputs: list[str], input: str) -> None      # §7.5
async def set_level(self, refs: list[str], db: float | None) -> None   # §7.3
async def send_universe(self, universe: int, data: bytes) -> None      # §7.2

The LKV422 driver receiving every output it has sends the single PA{n}R; a partial set becomes sequential PS{o}{i}R writes. That choice belongs to the driver, not to the caller.

The scene engine makes this load-bearing: §8.13 fires actions grouped by delay, so two routing actions at t=0 would be two calls with no way to recombine. Modelling destinations (§7.5) rather than individual outputs is what guarantees one intent per operation.

#Capability degradation

A driver change can leave configuration that the new driver cannot support — scenes with mixer_recall actions against a mixer with no scene recall, for instance.

Affected configuration is disabled with a visible explanation, not hidden. The scene remains, its unsupported actions are marked and skipped at execution with ⊘ unsupported, and the scene list shows which scenes are affected.

Hiding would let a scene silently stop doing part of its job. Blocking the driver change until dependent configuration is removed would make swapping hardest at exactly the moment someone is troubleshooting.

Capability checking is enforced in three places: the scene editor hides or disables unsupported action types, the scene engine skips them at execution, and the API rejects them with validation_failed (§16).

#Driver references and swaps

driver_ref is opaque to the core — "ip1" to the CQ-20B driver, "ch01" to another. There is no automatic translation between vendor addressing schemes.

Changing a driver therefore invalidates every driver_ref for that device. The admin is shown a re-mapping screen listing the old reference, the channel's name, and a picker of the new driver's available_refs(). Names, hirer ceilings, visibility flags, group memberships and control surface assignments are all preserved; only the reference changes.

A positional guess was considered and rejected — it would occasionally be silently wrong, which is worse than being asked.

The one pre-selection is identity. Where the new driver declares the same reference string with the same kind — swapping back to a driver the channel was configured against — the picker starts on it, and the Main channel starts on the new driver's Main (every desk has exactly one, and only Main may map to it). Everything else starts blank. A pre-selection is only a starting position: nothing is applied until the admin confirms the screen.

What is re-mapped, and what is not. Only driver_ref columns are re-mapped: mixer_channel_refs, matrix_inputs.driver_ref and matrix_outputs.driver_ref. Everything else reaches a device through one of those rows by id, which is why it survives. mixer_desk_scenes.scene_ref and scene_actions.projector_input are opaque device references too, but they are not re-mapped: a desk scene or projector input the new driver cannot use is capability degradation (listed, marked and skipped, above), and the admin edits it where it lives. A mixer channel left without an equivalent is marked unmapped and fails closed (§15.6); a matrix input or output has no unmapped state (§15.10), so a re-map that leaves one out is refused. The apply is all or nothing, behind a pre-change snapshot (§16.7).

#Multiple instances

The schema permits several drivers per category. The interface assumes one and shows device pickers only when more than one is configured, so nothing is complicated for the normal installation while the schema does not need reworking to add a second projector later.

#KNX is a subsystem, not a category

KNX is deliberately not behind a driver interface.

A generic building-control interface over KNX would be a rename rather than a generalisation. Group addresses already are an abstract addressing model and DPTs already are a datatype system — wrapping an open standard in a second abstraction with one implementation buys nothing.

DALI is differently shaped rather than merely a different transport: addressed devices with query and response, short addresses and groups, against KNX's broadcast group-address bus. Any interface covering both would be too thin to use or would amount to a full translation layer.

knxd is the transport abstraction, which is why the gateway is swappable without anything else changing (§7.1).

If DALI, BACnet or Modbus is ever added, it is added as its own subsystem alongside KNX, not behind a shared abstraction. This is recorded so that a shared interface is not retrofitted later on the assumption that it was an oversight.

#What this excludes

No dynamic plugin loading. Drivers ship with the application and register in code. Runtime discovery exists in general-purpose platforms to support an ecosystem of thousands; here it would mean loadable code on a system whose §6.11 trust anchor exists specifically to prevent that.

No auto-discovery. Devices are configured by address. There are five of them and they do not move.

No generic entity model. A light/switch/sensor abstraction earns its complexity across thousands of integrations. Five categories with one or two drivers each does not.

#Validating the abstraction

The standard failure of a one-implementation abstraction is that the interface quietly becomes "what the CQ-20B does".

A deliberately minimal stub mixer driver is built in Phase 4 — no scene recall, no pan, no metering, eight channels — and the interface and scene engine must work against it. If the UI breaks, the abstraction is wrong, and finding that out costs an afternoon rather than a rewrite. This is the same technique §7.5 already uses to let Phase 3 proceed without matrix hardware.

#5.6 State store and event bus

This is the single most important internal contract in the system — every protocol client, the scene engine, the WebSocket broadcaster, and the API handlers integrate through it. It is specified here because a successor developer needs to understand it before anything else makes sense.

Shape. Per-domain namespaces holding the current authoritative value of everything the application tracks:

state.devices    {knx, dmx, mixer, projector, hdmi, backup_media} → DeviceStatus
state.lighting   levels {channel_id: float 0–100}, colour {channel_id: RGBW},
                 master, binding_states,
                 external_control (manual | detected | off),
                 observed {channel_id: float}   ← display only, never composited
state.timer      running, started_at, accumulated_ms — the shared show timer
                 (§21.7); broadcast like any other state
state.mixer      main, outputs {id: ChannelState}, inputs {id: ChannelState},
                 meters {channel_id: [dB per ref]}   ← display only, never
                                                        persisted, never read
                                                        by the control path
                 last_recalled_scene, last_change_source {channel_id: "app"|"mixpad"}
state.projector  ProjectorState — nothing is queued during warm-up (B52)
state.hdmi       destinations {id: {input_id, diverged}}, routing {output_ref: input_ref}
state.scenes     running {scene_id: RunContext}, last_result
state.hirer      enabled, pages [id], permitted_channels {id: ceiling}
                 — pages decide what is reachable, ceilings how far (§15.4)
state.system     time_synced, cert_expiry, disk_free, update_pending

Event bus. Publish/subscribe, in-process, synchronous dispatch to async callbacks:

bus.subscribe("lighting.level_changed", handler)
bus.emit(LevelChanged(channel_id=7, level=82.5, source="fade"))

Subscribers are isolated. Each callback is wrapped; an exception is logged with the event type and subscriber name and does not reach the emitter or any other subscriber. Without this, a failing WebSocket broadcast could propagate back into the protocol client that emitted the event — a fault travelling in exactly the wrong direction.

A subscriber that raises on 10 consecutive events is unsubscribed and the failure surfaced in health. A permanently broken consumer should not burn CPU silently for the rest of the deployment.

Bounded queues, with domain-specific overflow. Feeding the bus: Art-Net input at up to 40 fps, CQ-20B echoes, KNX telegrams, control surface events, health polls. Each consumer has a bounded queue, and what happens when it fills depends on what the events are:

Class Examples On overflow
Continuous Levels, fader positions, observed values Drop oldest. The newest value is the truth; a stale one is worthless
Discrete Device status, scene results, mute toggles, binding states Never dropped. The producer is blocked and the stall logged

Drop counts per consumer are exposed in the health screen, so shedding is visible rather than silent. This formalises what §16.8's broadcast batching already does informally, and extends it to every consumer.

Events fall into two classes, and the distinction determines how they reach clients:

  • Continuous — level changes, fader movements, group and master changes. Accumulated into a dirty set and broadcast as a batched frame at 10–15 fps (§16.8). Never sent individually.
  • Discrete — device status transitions, scene start and completion, mute toggles, projector state changes, HDMI source changes. Broadcast immediately.

Persistence. A dirty-flag writer persists selected state to the system_state table. Rapidly-changing values — levels, fader positions — are written at most once per 500 ms. Static changes — external control toggle, device status — write immediately. All pending state is flushed on graceful shutdown.

Ownership is enforced, not conventional. Writers acquire a domain-scoped handle once at startup, and the store records which owner may write which domain:

levels = state.lighting.writer("fade_engine")     # issued at startup
levels.set(channel_id, 82.5)                      # checked against the registry

A write from an unregistered owner raises in development and logs in production. The asymmetry is deliberate: a stray write is a bug, but crashing a control system mid-performance over one is worse than letting it through and recording it.

Without this the rule below is a comment, and Python will not enforce a comment. The violation it prevents — a second component writing a domain it does not own — surfaces as an untraceable last-write-wins bug weeks later, which is the most expensive class of defect this architecture can produce.

Ownership rule. The level store has many writers and one reader: the fade engine, direct sets, scene snapshots, binding rules and KNX dimmer status sync all write it; only the compositor reads it. Each output has exactly one writer — the DMX pass owns the universe buffer, the KNX pass owns the dimmer queue (§7.2.3). The mixer client owns mixer state; the scene engine writes through domain owners rather than directly.

state.lighting.observed is written only by the Art-Net input listener and read only by the broadcaster. Nothing in the control path may read it (§7.2.7). This rule is what keeps last-write-wins (§10.6) coherent and keeps external input out of the output pipeline.

#6. Security

#6.1 Threat model

The system sits on a school VLAN and is not public-facing. The realistic threats are curious students, hirers exceeding what they were given, and accidental misconfiguration. The design addresses those without over-engineering for threats that do not fit the setting.

What this application's security model does not cover is set out in §3.5 and is worth repeating in short: anyone on VLAN 30 can reach the downstream devices directly and control them without going through this application at all. The measures below govern access through the application. They are a guard against accident and casual overreach. They are not a boundary against a determined user, and the specification does not claim otherwise.

#6.2 Access tiers

Tier Access Authentication
Admin Everything — configuration, scenes, system, users Password
Operator Control only — scenes, mixer, lighting, HDMI, projector. No configuration. Password
Hirer Scoped surface — permitted channels, desk scenes, lighting groups 6-digit PIN

#6.3 Staff authentication

There is no username field. The login page presents a single password input. The server checks the submitted password against both the admin and operator hashes. Admin wins if both match.

async def login(password: str, request: Request):
    admin    = await db.get_user("admin")
    operator = await db.get_user("operator")

    # Both are always verified — constant work regardless of outcome
    admin_match    = bcrypt.verify(password, admin.password_hash)
    operator_match = bcrypt.verify(password, operator.password_hash)

    if admin_match:
        return issue_token(tier="admin", token_version=admin.token_version)
    if operator_match:
        return issue_token(tier="operator", token_version=operator.token_version)
    raise HTTPException(401)

Exactly two staff accounts exist. There is no user management beyond setting their passwords.

#6.4 Credentials and tokens

  • Staff passwords — bcrypt, 12 characters minimum
  • Hirer PIN — exactly six digits, bcrypt hashed, never stored in plain text
  • Tokens — JWT in httpOnly cookies with SameSite=Strict, never localStorage

SameSite=Strict prevents cross-site request forgery from other browser tabs, so no separate CSRF token mechanism is required for this threat model.

Tier Expiry
Admin 30 minutes hidden · 12 hours absolute
Operator 30 minutes hidden · 12 hours absolute
Hirer 30 minutes hidden · 12 hours absolute, and invalidated by a PIN change or access being disabled
#Foreground presence is what holds a session, not traffic

"Refreshed on activity" was the earlier wording and it is ambiguous in a way that breaks both directions. If a WebSocket frame counts, the session never expires anywhere — metering alone would refresh it fifteen times a second. If only REST calls count, an operator working entirely over the WebSocket is logged out mid-performance.

The Page Visibility API gives the signal that actually matters:

page visible   →  session held, idle timer paused
page hidden    →  idle timer runs

A booth screen displaying the page all evening holds its session. Nobody arrives at seven o'clock to find a login prompt on a machine inside a locked booth. A tablet in a bag with the screen off is hidden, and times out in thirty minutes — long enough that putting it down to deal with something costs nothing, short enough that one left in a green room is not a live session.

The absolute cap applies to every tier, not only hirers. Twelve hours covers the longest plausible event including setup and pack-down, and nothing lives past it.

Pings, pongs, resyncs and broadcasts are never activity. They are connection liveness, which is a different question from whether anyone is there.

The hirer's absolute expiry is deliberate. The procedural control is that the admin changes the PIN between hires; the absolute lifetime means forgetting once does not leave last month's hirer with a working session on their phone. Twelve hours is generous for a single event.

Tokens carry identity, not permissions. A hirer token contains the tier, a hirer session identifier and token_version — nothing about which channels or groups are permitted.

Earlier revisions resolved permitted IDs into the token at issue time. That reintroduced the defect §15.4 was written to remove: ceilings were read live from the entity columns on every write, but the set of permitted channels came from frozen claims, so lowering a ceiling took effect immediately while removing a channel did not take effect until the PIN changed. An admin removing a channel because a hirer was misusing it would have watched them carry on.

Permissions resolve from the entity columns, which §15.4 makes canonical. To keep that off the hot path they are held in state.hirer (§5.6) and rebuilt on the config-change event — so enforcement costs no database read per request, and the event bus does the job it exists for.

A PIN change or access being disabled increments token_version; a JWT carrying an older version is rejected with 401. That is the only thing token_version invalidates. Changing a permission — a page assignment, a ceiling, one of the three switches (§16.6) — no longer logs anyone out, and an admin adjusting them does not have to think about whether it will; it takes effect through state.hirer on the next broadcast instead.

#6.5 Session expiry

When a staff token expires mid-session, a re-authentication overlay appears over the current page rather than a redirect. The page behind is blurred but its state is preserved. On successful re-authentication the overlay dismisses and work continues in place.

If the token has been expired for more than 30 minutes, a full redirect to /login occurs instead.

#6.6 Hirer access

One PIN, one permission profile. No booking-specific PINs, no time windows, no multiple profiles. The admin changes the PIN between hires and toggles access on and off.

Before a hire: enable access, confirm permitted channels, desk scenes and lighting, change the PIN. After: disable access or change the PIN. Both take seconds. The enabled toggle is an instant kill switch — disabling prevents new logins and drops existing hirer WebSocket connections immediately.

The full pre-hire and post-hire run sheet is in §17.5.

#6.7 Server-side enforcement

Hirer limits are enforced in request handlers, never only in the UI:

@router.post("/mixer/channels/{channel_id}/level")
async def set_level(channel_id: int, db: float | None, user=Depends(require_hirer)):
    perms = state.hirer                       # rebuilt on config change, §5.6
    if channel_id not in perms.permitted_channels:
        raise HTTPException(403)               # permission_denied: unreachable channel
    ceiling = perms.permitted_channels[channel_id]
    clamped = db if db is None or ceiling is None else min(db, ceiling)
    await mixer.set_level(channel_id, clamped)
    return {"db": clamped, "clamped": clamped != db}   # 200 always; §16.8

Both the permitted set and the ceiling come from the same resolved state, so they can never disagree. §15.4's entity columns remain the single source of truth; state.hirer is a cache of them with an explicit invalidation event.

#Changes during an active session

Because permissions are live rather than frozen, an admin's edits take effect immediately.

Admin action Effect on an active hirer
Channel removed Disappears from their interface on the next broadcast
Channel added Appears
Ceiling raised Fader gains travel
Ceiling lowered below the current value The fader is pulled down to the new ceiling and written to the mixer
Lighting or colour permission changed Applies immediately
Access disabled Connection dropped (§6.6)

The active pull-down is deliberate. An admin lowering a ceiling is almost always reacting to something happening at that moment — a microphone too loud in the room. A ceiling that applied only to future movements would leave the channel above it until the hirer next touched the fader, which makes the control look broken to the person who just used it.

The hirer sees the fader move, which is honest: something was changed by staff, and the interface shows it rather than silently diverging.

#6.8 Rate limiting

Endpoint Limit Lockout
Staff login 5 attempts per 5 minutes per IP 15 minutes
Hirer PIN 3 attempts per 10 minutes per IP 30 minutes

An email alert is sent if more than 10 failed attempts arrive from one address within an hour.

Counters are held in memory and reset when the application restarts. That is an acceptable trade-off here: an attacker who can restart the application already has SSH access and has bypassed everything else.

This depends entirely on nginx forwarding the real client address (§4.13). Without it, per-IP limiting degrades to global limiting and one attacker locks out every user.

#6.9 Password reset

Recoverable only with physical or SSH access:

sudo avc-reset-password

The script lives on the read-only root filesystem, not under /opt/auditorium. If a bad update lands or the application directory is damaged, the recovery tool must still be there. It offers:

  • Reset the admin or operator password — writes a new bcrypt hash and increments token_version, invalidating active sessions
  • Reset the hirer PIN — same mechanism
  • Clear rate-limit lockouts — useful after a brute-force attempt has locked out the admin's own address

It does not touch certificates, hostname, network configuration, or device configuration. Those have their own admin flows and their own scripts, documented in docs/hardware/recovery.md.

There is deliberately no forgotten-password flow in the web interface. This is explained in the Users screen (§21.23) so it does not read as an omission.

#6.10 Device credential storage

Device passwords — currently just PJLink — are encrypted at rest inside devices.config, in fields the driver's schema marks encrypted (§5.5), using AES-256-GCM.

The key is derived from /srv/appliance/device-secret: 32 random bytes generated at first boot, mode 0400, owned by the application user.

That partition is deliberately excluded from the backup archive. If the secret travelled in the same .tar.gz as the values it protects, the encryption would be decorative — and that archive is copied to the school's file server, where access control is whatever the file server happens to be set to.

The consequence is worth stating: restoring /data to a replacement machine means re-entering device passwords, because the new machine has a different secret. Restoring /data on the same machine preserves them. This is on the recovery card.

#6.11 Package signing trust anchors

Public keys used to verify packages — the public half of an Ed25519 key pair, one file per key — live as trusted-keys/*.pub under /usr/local/share/auditorium/, on the read-only root filesystem. Any key in the directory can verify a package. Both application packages and OS upgrades are signed and share one verification path (§14.1).

Two properties are load-bearing:

  • The directory must never be read from a path under /opt/auditorium, because that resolves into /data/app/current — which would let an update package ship its own key and self-authorise every subsequent update. The trust anchors have to sit outside anything an update can write.
  • A directory rather than a single file is what makes key rotation possible. Replacing the anchor requires an OS upgrade, which requires a valid signature from the key being replaced; holding two keys at once is what breaks that circularity. The rollover procedure and the key-loss recovery path are in §14.1.

#6.12 WebSocket authentication

Authenticated during the HTTP upgrade handshake: the server validates the JWT cookie on the upgrade request and tags the connection with the user's tier, which determines which broadcasts it receives. Hirers receive state only for their permitted channels.

The Origin header is validated against the configured hostname. Upgrades from any other origin are rejected with 403.

Unauthenticated upgrade requests are rejected with 401.

#6.13 URL structure

/           → redirects to /login
/setup      → First-run wizard; forced destination until setup completes
/login      → Staff password and hirer PIN, one page, two sections
/app        → Operator interface
/admin      → Admin configuration (admin tier only)
/hire       → Hirer surface (PIN required)

Hirers only ever see /hire. The existence of /admin is not exposed to them.

#6.14 Audit log

Security events are written to the security_events table:

event_type   login_success | login_failure | lockout | permission_denied |
             config_changed | pin_changed | access_toggled | password_changed |
             forced_logout | unexpected_origin | update_applied |
             update_auto_rollback | baseline_restored | certificate_renewed |
             backup_restored | os_upgrade_applied | os_upgrade_rolled_back
user_ident   "admin" | "operator" | "hirer" | IP address
ip_address   Real client address (requires §4.13 header forwarding)
timestamp    ISO 8601 with offset
detail       JSON — what was attempted, what was denied

Queryable from the admin interface, filtered by date range and event type. Retained 90 days; older entries pruned during the nightly backup job.

Personal information. ip_address records the network address of devices used by staff, students and hirers, retained for 90 days. Under the Privacy Act 2020 this is likely personal information, and the school should be aware it is collected rather than discover it during a privacy review.

The retention is proportionate to its purpose — diagnosing failed sign-ins and unauthorised access attempts — and 90 days is short. The field is not optional: without it, per-IP rate limiting (§6.8) cannot function and a lockout cannot be attributed. This is noted for the school's records rather than as a change to make, and belongs in the handover pack (§20.2).

#6.15 SSH

Key-only; password authentication disabled. Only the developer's key is authorised initially, with additional keys added explicitly. Restricted to a designated management address by nftables.

#6.16 Self-signed certificate limitations

Several paths land on a self-signed certificate: initial setup before DNS is configured, NTP failure, and certificate expiry.

iOS Safari will refuse to install a PWA to the home screen over an untrusted certificate, and WebSocket connections fail outright rather than presenting a warning. This is a limitation of the platform, not a bug to fix.

The first-run wizard and the recovery card both document how to trust the self-signed certificate on a staff iPad: download it from the controller, then Settings → General → VPN & Device Management → trust. Not a blocker, but it will otherwise be discovered at the worst possible moment.

#7. Device Integrations

Each integration runs as an asyncio task (§5.3) and surfaces its state through the central store (§5.6). This section covers protocol specifics, control and feedback semantics, health strategy, and known limitations for each.

#7.1 KNX

#Architecture

Application ←→ knxd socket ←→ knxd ←→ KNXnet/IP ←→ KNX gateway ←→ KNX bus

knxd owns the KNXnet/IP tunnel, keep-alive, and gateway reconnection. The application speaks to a local socket and never handles KNXnet/IP directly.

knxd's options live in /data/config/knxd.conf (reached from /etc/knxd.conf through a symlink, §4.3, so the gateway address changes without a rebuild; the shipped default is appliance/etc/knxd.conf.default):

KNXD_OPTS="-e 0.0.1 -E 0.0.2:8 --arg=delay=40 -B pace -B single -b ipt:10.2.30.252"

-e 0.0.1 / -E 0.0.2:8   knxd's own address and the client range — local only
--arg=delay=40 -B pace  space outgoing telegrams 40 ms apart
-B single               rewrite every outgoing source to the address the gateway
                        assigned the tunnel (1.1.3 at this installation)
-b ipt:HOST             KNXnet/IP tunnelling to the gateway (§3.1)

-B pace was added on site (30 September 2026): the gateway dropped telegrams from a burst — the panel's status lamps, re-sent together after a press — so the last rows missed their updates. Pacing at 40 ms sits inside the 15 telegrams/second budget below. -B single (29 September 2026) means no 0.0.x source and no client range ever reaches the bus, so nothing the controller sends can clash with an undocumented device on the installation's line; the tunnel's address is the one recorded on the §17.4 checklist. Do not add -u, -i or -d: socket units provide the sockets and the unit runs knxd in the foreground.

#Group address library

Every group address the system touches, in either direction, is registered in the database before use. No raw group address strings appear anywhere in scene configuration.

KNXGroupAddress
  group_address     "1/0/1"
  name              "Stage Lights — Command"
  description       "Sent by the south wall touch panel"
  dpt               "1.001"
  direction         incoming | outgoing | both
  device_id         → KNXDevice, optional grouping
  notes

Supported data point types:

DPT Description Use
1.001 Boolean on/off Switches, alarm states
1.008 Up/down Dimmer direction
1.x Any 1-bit Treated as 1.001 semantically
3.007 Dimming control Relative dim with step count
5.001 0–100% unsigned byte Dimmer level
5.010 0–255 unsigned byte Raw value
9.x 2-byte float Sensor values
20.x HVAC and alarm states Alarm zone enumerations

Unsupported types. A telegram on an address whose registered DPT is not in this list has its raw payload logged and is otherwise ignored — no crash, no partial decode. The KNX library screen surfaces these so the integrator can request support or reclassify the address.

Adding a type means implementing an encode/decode pair in core/knx.py. Keep the list conservative; add only what the installation actually uses.

#Bulk import

Integrators deliver finished projects as ETS exports. Hand-entering 100–200 addresses at commissioning is not acceptable.

Accepted formats:

  • ETS CSV or TSV export — columns Main, Middle, Sub, Name, Description, Data Type; others ignored
  • ETS group address XML from ETS 5 or 6 — parsed directly, column mapping populated automatically
  • Generic CSV or TSV — the wizard shows the first row and lets the admin map columns

The older ETS 3/4 .esf format is not supported. ETS 5 and 6 export CSV and XML, and that is what the integrator will be producing.

Behaviour is specified with the import wizard in §21.19. In summary: upload, map and preview with inline warnings, confirm. The import runs in a single transaction — all or nothing — with a pre-change database snapshot captured first. Duplicate group addresses are rejected by the unique constraint; the admin chooses skip or overwrite for the whole import.

#Incoming telegrams

The application subscribes to every address marked incoming or both. On receipt:

  1. Update the state store
  2. Match against registered scene triggers
  3. Execute any matching scene, subject to debounce (below)
  4. Broadcast the state change to connected clients

Debounce. KNX touch panels commonly send repeated telegrams — a double press, a bus retry, or panels that transmit on both press and release. Without debouncing, a scene fires two or three times in quick succession, and a critical scene firing twice cancels its own first execution partway through.

Each trigger has a debounce window, default 500 ms, configurable per trigger. Telegrams arriving inside the window for a trigger that has already fired are suppressed and logged at DEBUG.

#Outgoing writes

Outgoing writes happen as scene actions and as derived status (§8.6). The application never writes to an address marked incoming-only. Every write is logged.

Rate management. The TP1 bus handles roughly 40–50 telegrams per second, but the tunnelling connection and the gateway have lower practical limits. The application enforces a global budget of 15 telegrams per second through a priority queue:

Priority Type Example
1 Alarm-related Alarm status feedback
2 Scene status feedback "Stage lights on" confirmation to a touch panel
3 Dimmer fade steps Intermediate values during a software fade

Telegrams over budget queue and send in priority order, so alarm traffic is never delayed behind dimmer fades.

#The daily control path

The auditorium's stage lighting is four banks of fixtures, each switched on and off from a KNX touch panel, individually or together, with the panel's indicator reflecting state. That is what the outgoing Windows application does and what staff already know.

This is specified as rules, in §8, not here and not as scenes. Modelling it as scenes would fill the execution log with daily traffic, duplicate status feedback into every scene, and bury the mapping between group addresses and fixture groups where nobody can see it. What follows is the KNX-side summary; §8.8 has the full rule set.

On receipt of a bank command telegram:

  1. The rule sets every member channel's level store entry to on_level or off_level, over fade_ms — a group-level write, the same as moving the group's fader (§9.4)
  2. Nothing else needs forcing: groups set levels and no longer multiply (§9.4, B73), so the recall's result is exactly on_level × the master
  3. Once the frame carrying the final value has been sent, every derived status is recomputed and any whose value changed is written

Step 3 is derived, not edge-triggered. Every binding's status is recomputed after any change, not only the one that fired — which is what makes an All button go red when one bank is switched off individually, and what makes the panel follow a fader dragged in the web interface. See §8.6.

Completion means the frame has been sent, not that the level store was written. Art-Net is fire-and-forget so there is no acknowledgement to wait for, but sending is the closest thing to truth available and it means the panel lights after the room does rather than before.

Debounce applies at the rule layer, default 500 ms (§8.4), because these are the same panels that send repeat telegrams on double-press and bus retry.

Rules touching DMX are suppressed while external control is active (§7.2.7), their derived statuses written 0, and a dedicated external control status address written 1 so the panel shows why. Suppression releases automatically when the desk stops.

#KNX dimmers

Controlled by absolute value writes on DPT 5.001. Three fade modes, per channel:

hardware        Application sends the target only; the dimmer runs its own fade   (default)
hardware_timed  Dimmer exposes a writable fade-time group address
software        Application sends stepped values at 5–10 per second

hardware is the recommended default, and the budget arithmetic explains why. Software fading costs 5–10 telegrams per second per dimmer. Two dimmers fading together consume the entire 15/sec global budget; three overflow it, and the priority queue stretches the fades until they visibly step.

The admin UI warns when more than two channels are set to software.

For a 2-second software fade, 10–20 intermediate values is perceptually smooth for architectural dimming — real actuators interpolate internally between received values.

The KNX integrator must confirm whether internal fade times can be set to zero in ETS on each actuator without affecting physical touch panel behaviour. Until confirmed, use hardware.

#Status feedback

Touch panel indicators are derived status (§8.6), not written by the scene engine or by whatever changed the state (B51). A derived status is recomputed from state after every change and written when its value differs, so a lost telegram corrects itself at the next change rather than leaving a panel wrong for hours.

Derived status reflecting stage lighting is written 0 while external control is active (§8.8) — a panel must not indicate that the controller has the lights on when a visiting desk is driving them.

#Alarm integration

The alarm zone's armed state arrives on a group address from the alarm system's KNX I/O module. Two scenes respond:

  • Armed (1) — All Off: every KNX lighting circuit off, every DMX fixture to zero, projector off, mixer muted
  • Disarmed (0) — Morning Reset: strategic access lighting on over KNX only. DMX fixtures stay at zero and are not brought up

These are ordinary KNX-triggered rules running scenes (§8.8). The armed scene must be marked critical priority (§8.14), which also disables external control before its DMX actions (§7.2.7) so the blackout cannot be defeated by a visiting desk.

At this installation (October 2026). The alarm panel sends no KNX itself: it closes a relay that a KNX I/O module reads (high armed, low disarmed), and that module sends the telegrams the house lights already respond to. The module is not in the site's ETS project, so the arm and disarm group addresses are to be captured from the bus (docs/hardware/network_map.md). Since the stage moved to this controller (29 September 2026) nothing turns the stage DMX off on arming until the armed rule above is bound to the captured address.

#Why DMX stays down until someone raises it

The armed scene's DMX action is a snapshot with every channel at zero (§8.12), and snapshots write to the level store, not to the universe buffer (§7.2.3). That distinction is what produces the required behaviour:

  • The levels genuinely become zero, rather than the output merely being suspended. A suspend would be undone by the compositor on the very next tick.
  • Zeroed levels are persisted (§9.4), so a reboot overnight restores them as zero.
  • Nothing raises them again except a deliberate act — a fader, a stage plan tap, or another scene.

A future maintainer looking to simplify this will be tempted to replace the snapshot with a direct blackout call. That would break the requirement, because a blackout clears output while leaving the model intact.

The armed scene's fade duration defaults to zero — instant — because the building is being secured and empty. A short fade can be configured if a gentler exit is preferred.

#Emergency and egress lighting

Confirmed at design time: emergency lighting in the auditorium is a separate, battery-backed system on its own circuits, entirely independent of KNX and of this controller. No luminaire under this system's control serves an egress function.

This is recorded because it is the question that makes the master dimmer (§9.5) safe. That control scales every KNX and DMX channel and can take the room to zero, which would be a compliance problem if any of those circuits were doing egress duty. They are not.

If the lighting is ever re-scoped — a circuit reassigned, house lighting brought into an egress calculation — this assumption must be re-tested before the change goes in, and a minimum floor imposed on the affected channels that the master cannot scale below.

#Health

  • Monitor the application-to-knxd socket directly
  • Optionally monitor the knxd-to-gateway tunnel state from knxd's log output
  • Optional heartbeat — write to a reserved group address and confirm it echoes back, proving a full round trip through the gateway and bus

The heartbeat writes to a live KNX bus on a schedule and consumes the telegram budget, so it requires a group address the integrator has reserved and left unassigned. Interval and address are configurable and the heartbeat can be disabled entirely, in which case health falls back to socket state alone. The reserved address is on the pre-installation checklist (§17.4).

#7.2 DMX

#7.2.1 Transport options

Two paths, selected in configuration:

  • Network via Art-Net or sACN — in service. The DMXking eDMX8 MAX connects over Ethernet. This is the production path: the network is already 10.x.x.x, Ethernet removes the USB dependency, and it is the only path that supports tracking a visiting lighting desk (§7.2.7). The booth's DMX input is wired to one port of the node for visiting desks, configured to broadcast as Art-Net so the controller can detect and observe it (§7.2.7).

  • USB serial — untested fallback. The opendmx driver drives the spare Enttec Open DMX USB directly over the serial transport. It exists so an emergency path is at least specified; it has never been run against hardware (§7.2.4).

#7.2.2 Full universe frames

DMX512 does not address individual channels. Every packet carries the entire universe — up to 512 sequential values as one frame — and a fixture finds its channel by counting bytes from the start. A full frame takes about 23 ms, giving a ceiling of roughly 44 Hz.

The application's job is to keep correct values in a universe buffer. The transport handles packet timing.

#7.2.3 Output pipeline

This resolves how individual levels, group faders and the master dimmer combine, and how DMX fixtures and KNX dimmers are driven from one model. Groups are not an input to the compositor: a group fader sets its members' levels (§9.4, B73), so the composited output is level × master for a stage fixture and the level alone for a KNX house dimmer.

Level store   per channel: level 0–100 (one decimal)
                           colour r/g/b/w 0–255, colour fixtures only
    ↑ written by: fade engine · direct set · group fader ·
                  scene snapshot · binding rule · KNX dimmer status sync
Master multiplier (0.0–1.0)  ──→  Compositor
                               ↓
        ┌──────────────────────┴──────────────────────┐
        ↓                                             ↓
   DMX pass                                      KNX pass
   → universe buffer (512 bytes, 0–255)          → KNX dimmer write queue
        ↓ read on change                              ↓ change-driven only
   Frame renderer                                 Telegram queue (§7.1)
        ↓                                             ↓
   Backend → node → fixtures                     Actuator → luminaire

Two passes, not one. Earlier revisions ran a single loop that branched per channel type, which meant any condition suspending the compositor suspended KNX dimmers too — including external control (§7.2.7), where the suspension has no business touching KNX at all. The passes are gated independently and share only the level store.

One writer per output. The DMX pass is the sole writer of the universe buffer; the KNX pass is the sole producer of dimmer writes. Everything else — fade engine, scene snapshots, direct sets, group faders, group bindings, status sync — writes the level store. The level store therefore has many writers and exactly one reader.

class Compositor:
    def composite_dmx(self) -> None:
        """Sole writer of the universe buffer."""
        if self._dmx_suspended:                  # external control, §7.2.7
            return
        for ch in self._dmx_channels:
            level = self._resolve(ch)            # clamp → master
            self._write_profile(ch, level)   # roles from ch.profile — see below

    def composite_knx(self) -> None:
        """Sole producer of KNX dimmer writes. Never gated by DMX state, and
        never scaled by the master: house dimmers follow their own level."""
        for ch in self._knx_channels:
            level = min(max(self._levels[ch.id], ch.min_value), ch.max_value)
            if abs(level - self._last_sent_knx.get(ch.id, -1)) >= 0.5:
                self._knx.queue_dimmer(ch, level)   # priority 3, §7.1
                self._last_sent_knx[ch.id] = level

    def _resolve(self, ch) -> float:
        """Clamp first, then scale. See below."""
        level = min(max(self._levels[ch.id], ch.min_value), ch.max_value)
        if ch.min_value > 0:                     # a floor the master cannot
            return level                         # scale below: §7.2.3, §9.5
        level *= self._master                    # groups set levels; they do
        return level                             # not scale (§9.4, B73)
#Behaviour matrix

Stated as a table rather than left implicit in the code above. Blockers B1 and B4 both arose from the compositor being a snippet that quietly accumulated cases — a missing row is visible in a way a missing if branch is not.

Condition DMX pass KNX pass
Normal operation Composite and write the universe buffer Composite and queue dimmer writes
External control active (§7.2.7) Suspended — nothing written Runs normally. House lighting is never gated by DMX state
Channel is a dimmer fixture Level → single DMX slot Level → DPT 5.001 write
Channel is RGB / RGBW Level scales each colour component, unless the profile has a dimmer channel: then the dimmer carries the level alone and colour is written unscaled n/a
Colour changed, level unchanged Recomposited — colour lives in the level store n/a
min_value = 0 Clamp, then master Clamp only. The master does not scale house dimmers (§9.5)
min_value > 0 Clamp only; exempt from the master (§7.2.3) Clamp only, as for every house dimmer
Group fader moved, or recalled by a binding rule (§8.8) Members' levels set; the master applies normally. Groups do not scale (§9.4, B73) Members' levels set directly
Channel in several groups Nothing to resolve: the last group written set the level, as on a desk n/a
Level unchanged, nothing moving for 250 ms No frame composited; the last frame is resent at the keepalive (1 s) No telegram sent; 0.5% deadband
Level changed, or output moving (a fade, a fader, an operator glide) First frame at once, then frames on a steady 40 fps grid until nothing has moved for 250 ms Telegram queued at priority 3

Two rows are the ones that were previously wrong or absent. External control gating the KNX pass was B1 — one flag doing two jobs took out house lighting whenever a visiting desk was patched in. Colour having no composited path was B4 — the snippet passed only a level, so nothing said where colour was read or whether the master scaled it.

#Clamp point

min_value and max_value are applied to the stored level, before master scaling. They express "this fixture's own level never leaves this range", not "its output never leaves this range".

With max_value = 80 and the master at 50%, output is 40 — the cap applies to the channel's level, and the master then scales the whole rig as expected. Clamping after scaling would make the master unable to take a capped fixture below its cap, which is wrong for a master dimmer.

min_value is the mechanism §7.1 requires if a lighting circuit is ever brought into an egress calculation: a floor the master cannot scale below. That case needs the opposite behaviour, so a channel with min_value > 0 is exempt from master scaling entirely and its floor is applied to the output. This is deliberate and rare; the admin UI warns when min_value is set above zero.

Every writer into the level store clamps on write as well, so a stored level is always within range regardless of path (§7.2.6).

#Colour

Colour fixtures hold r/g/b/w in the level store, not only on the channel row. A colour change from the UI, a scene snapshot or the X-Touch writes the store, and the DMX pass composites it — the same route as level, so a level and colour change resolve in one frame rather than racing.

def _write_profile(self, ch, level: float) -> None:
    """Drive whatever roles the fixture's profile declares (§15.9)."""
    # A fixture with a dimmer channel dims there alone and its colour channels
    # carry pure colour, as on a lighting desk. Without one, scaling the colour
    # is the only way to dim it. Scaling both would dim twice.
    has_dimmer = any(c.role == "dimmer" for c in ch.profile.channels)
    scale = 1.0 if has_dimmer else level / 100.0
    colour = self._colours.get(ch.id)
    for c in ch.profile.channels:
        slot = ch.address + c.offset
        if c.role == "dimmer":
            self._universe.write(slot, self._to_dmx(level))
        elif c.role in COLOUR_ROLES:             # red, green, blue, white, amber, uv
            self._universe.write(slot, round(colour[c.role] * scale))
        elif c.role == "unused":
            continue
        else:                                    # pan, tilt, strobe, macro
            self._universe.write(slot, self._attributes[ch.id].get(c.role, c.default))

The channel row's colour_* columns are the power-on default, loaded into the store at startup. The store is authoritative thereafter.

Positional attributes — pan, tilt, strobe, macro — are stored per channel and written unscaled. A moving head does not point somewhere different because the master dimmer moved.

Value scale. Levels are 0–100 with one decimal everywhere — level store, API, WebSocket, scene snapshots, UI. Conversion to DMX 0–255 or KNX 0–100 happens only in the passes above. Colour components are the single exception at 0–255, because they are natively DMX values with no percentage interpretation.

#Rendering is change-driven

The renderer does not run at a fixed rate. Art-Net is not DMX512: the node regenerates the physical DMX refresh on its outputs continuously and holds last value, so the controller does not need to send frames to keep fixtures lit.

Level store dirty     →  composite  →  send frame    at once (40 fps cap)
Output moving         →  composite  →  send frame    every 25 ms, on a fixed grid
Still, under 250 ms   →  resend last frame           every 25 ms (the grid's tail)
At rest               →  resend last frame           every 1 s (keepalive)

At rest this is one packet per second and the compositor does not run at all. The previous fixed 25–40 fps loop burned CPU continuously to send identical frames and was a straightforward over-specification, and that still holds at rest, which is where the appliance spends nearly all its time.

While anything moves, frames go on a steady 40 fps grid (since v0.1.16, 30 September 2026). "Moving" is the level store changing — a fade's steps, fader writes — or an operator glide in progress (below). The grid is scheduled from the previous frame's due time, not from when it went, so it does not drift; changes landing between two frames join the next one. 250 ms after nothing has moved, the grid stops and the keepalive takes over. Sending only on change, as first specified, made the frame rate follow the arrival of fader writes over Wi-Fi: on the rig a drag produced a median of one frame every 50 ms with gaps past 100 ms, each a jump that grew with the drag speed, which the room saw as flicker (§23.1).

Operator glide. A direct operator write over the WebSocket — a fixture, group or master fader (§16.8) — moves the channel's output from where it was to its new composited value in a straight line over 75 ms (three frames), counted from one frame period before the frame that first carries it. The stored level is the target at once, so the interface, persistence, snapshots and a level-basis derived status never see the glide; an output-basis status evaluates the value actually sent, glide included (§8.6). A glide follows the live target, so a master move or fade started mid-glide is joined rather than fought, and a group's members glide in step. REST writes, scenes, rules, binding recalls and explicit fades do not glide, so an explicit fade keeps its own timing. External control drops every glide (§7.2.7).

Configure the node to hold last state on data loss rather than fade to black. A controller restart or a brief network interruption must not black out the rig.

Multi-group membership. Groups set levels (§9.4, B73), so a channel in several groups simply takes the level the last group fader or binding wrote, as on a lighting desk. Earlier revisions made the group fader a multiplier with the highest applying, which left a fixture in "Row 2" at 40% and "Stage all" at 100% ignoring the Row 2 fader entirely; that rule, its ghost mark and its dominated-group hint are gone.

Master dimmer scales stage (DMX) channels only. KNX house dimmers are scaled by neither the master nor group faders: they follow their own level one to one, the way a desk's grand master leaves architectural house lighting alone. A wall-panel change shown on screen is therefore never sent back scaled, and moving the master sends nothing to the KNX bus (§9.5). A binding rule's recall sets its members' levels (§8.8) and is not exempt from the master: the master is not persisted and resets to 100% at boot, so if it is low, someone present chose that this session.

#7.2.4 Backend abstraction

class LightingOutputDriver(Protocol):
    async def connect(self) -> None: ...
    async def send_frame(self, data: bytes) -> None: ...    # full 512-byte universe
    async def blackout(self) -> None: ...
    async def health(self) -> BackendHealth: ...
    async def disconnect(self) -> None: ...

Implementations:

  • opendmx — Enttec Open DMX USB over the serial transport. See the assessment below.
  • ArtNetBackend — eDMX8 MAX or any Art-Net node. Uses core/dmx/artnet.py.
  • sACNBackend — eDMX8 MAX over E1.31. Same module, different framing.
  • KNXGatewayBackend — future, for Weinzierl 5358 units. Translates DMX intent into KNX writes.
#The opendmx driver is unverified

This driver has never been tested against hardware and probably never will be. It is specified so that an emergency path exists on paper. Do not assume it works.

The Enttec Open DMX USB is an FTDI chip with no microcontroller — the host does all the timing:

Open the port at 250000 baud, 8N2
Assert a break for ≥ 88 µs, clear it for ≥ 8 µs
Write the start code (0x00) followed by 512 bytes
Repeat

Roughly forty lines on the serial transport, and framing belongs to drivers anyway (§5.5).

The risk is break timing from Python. Sleep granularity is poor, though in practice syscall overhead alone exceeds 88 µs comfortably, which is how the common approach works. Whether it produces flicker-free output on dimmers at partial levels is a bench question — plug it in, drive a fixture, watch. Twenty minutes.

That test is unlikely to happen, because the eDMX8 MAX is in service and this path exists only for the night the node fails. The spec records the driver rather than claiming it works.

If it is ever needed and turns out not to work, the fallback to the fallback is OLA — installed manually, driving the adapter, with the application pointed at it. That would be a driver written in an emergency rather than a system daemon threaded through the boot sequence and the backup set, which is the difference worth preserving.

Backend selection lives in the database and is changed from the admin UI. Changing it captures a pre-change snapshot and sends a blackout before switching transport.

#7.2.5 Art-Net and sACN implementation

Implemented directly in core/dmx/artnet.py rather than through a third-party library.

The reasoning is worth recording, because the obvious move is to take a dependency. The candidate library, pyartnet, is send-only — no receive path and no ArtPoll — and this design needs both — ArtDmx input for external desk detection and observation (§7.2.7), and ArtPoll for health monitoring (§7.2.8). It also provides a node/universe/channel abstraction, a refresh loop, a fade engine, and output correction curves, none of which this system uses: it has its own fade engine writing into its own buffer, and pushes complete frames. Taking the dependency would mean using perhaps 15% of a library while writing the missing 30% separately, then maintaining a fork of an upstream that has not moved recently in order to graft the receive path on.

The alternative is around 300 lines of well-documented packet handling in the repository, matching this architecture exactly, with both directions on one socket.

Component Scope
ArtDmx output Header, sequence counter, universe encoding, 512-byte payload
ArtDmx input Parse, universe filter, own-source rejection, into the observed store (§7.2.7)
ArtPoll / ArtPollReply Discovery and health; parses node name, firmware version, port states
sACN E1.31 output Root, framing, and DMP layers, priority, CID
Socket One asyncio.DatagramProtocol bound to UDP 6454, both directions

The controller does not answer ArtPoll. It listens for ArtPollReply from its own node, but does not announce itself as an Art-Net node. A visiting desk's discovery scan should show the DMX node and not the controller.

The rationale belongs in ARCHITECTURE.md: full-frame output only, receive required; the available library provides neither the receive path nor anything we use from its abstraction layer.

#7.2.6 Fade engine

Fades write into the level store, never to the transport and never to the universe buffer.

Each channel has at most one active fade. Starting a new fade on a fading channel cancels the previous one from its current value — no snap back to the start.

Easing is smoothstep, t² × (3 − 2t), which matches the perceptual expectation of a theatrical fade better than linear interpolation.

Timing is deadline-based, not accumulated sleeps. asyncio.sleep(0.02) sleeps at least 20 ms; over a 500-step fade a consistent 1 ms overshoot runs the fade half a second long, and two fades started together drift visibly apart.

async def _run_fade(self, ch, target: float, duration_ms: int,
                    colour: Colour | None = None) -> None:
    loop = asyncio.get_running_loop()
    start = self._levels[ch.id]
    start_colour = self._colours.get(ch.id)

    def apply(level: float, t: float) -> None:
        # Clamp on write — every path into the level store enforces the
        # channel's own range, so a stored level is never out of bounds.
        self._levels[ch.id] = round(min(max(level, ch.min_value), ch.max_value), 1)
        if colour is not None and start_colour is not None:
            self._colours[ch.id] = start_colour.lerp(colour, t)
        self._dirty.add(ch.id)

    if duration_ms <= 0:
        apply(target, 1.0)
        return

    t0, duration = loop.time(), duration_ms / 1000
    while True:
        t = min((loop.time() - t0) / duration, 1.0)
        eased = t * t * (3 - 2 * t)
        apply(start + (target - start) * eased, eased)
        if t >= 1.0:
            return
        await asyncio.sleep(0.02)

Self-correcting under load, exact end value, exact duration.

Clamping happens on write, not only in the compositor. A fade to 100 on a channel capped at 80 stores 80, so the stored value never misrepresents what the fixture can do — which matters because the level store is what the UI and the control surface display.

Colour fixtures fade level and colour components in one coordinated task, interpolating colour against the same eased parameter, so a simultaneous level and colour change does not produce a hue shift partway through.

The dirty set drives both the renderer (§7.2.3) and the batched WebSocket broadcast (§16.8). A fade marks only the channels it touches.

#7.2.7 External lighting desk

The venue has a dedicated DMX input in the control booth, wired to one port of the eDMX8 MAX, so a visiting production can plug its own desk into the house rig.

Handover is automatic. The controller detects that a desk is running and stands down; when the desk stops, it resumes. There is no button to remember and no procedure to follow.

#Detection

The node is configured to broadcast its DMX-IN port as Art-Net — Broadcast Threshold set to zero on that port in the configuration utility. The controller listens for those frames.

external_active  =  ArtDmx from the node's own address, on an input universe,
                    within the last 5 s
                    OR the manual flag is set (§ below)

Only the node's own frames count. Port 6454 is shared with every other Art-Net device on the VLAN. During the parallel run with the previous system, the old eDMX4 (10.2.30.220) broadcasts its own DMX input as ArtDmx on universe 0, which is also one of the eDMX8's input universes, and the legacy controller (10.2.30.250) polls the node. Without a source filter the room would sit under external control indefinitely. A visiting desk reaches the controller through the node's DMX-IN port, which the node broadcasts from its own address, so the filter loses nothing. The input universes are a setting on the node's device (input_universes); none configured means detection is off, never "all universes".

Presence of frames is the detection, at the desk's own frame rate, and the same packets carry its levels. One mechanism gives detection and observation together.

ArtPollReply is corroboration, not the signal. Its GoodInput byte reports data-received per port, but the DMXking manual is explicit that it is "only updated each ArtPoll period so not real time" — at a 30-second poll that is far too slow to gate control on. It is used in the health panel to confirm the port is configured as an input and receiving, which is a useful commissioning check.

Hysteresis is asymmetric. Enter on the first frame; leave only after 5 seconds of silence. A marginal DMX cable or a desk rebooting must not flap the room between modes.

A desk sending all zeros still counts as active. It is connected and in control, and a blackout is a legitimate desk state. The consequence — a desk left powered overnight leaves the stage unresponsive at the wall panel — is deliberate, visible on the panel, and resolved by switching the desk off.

#Behaviour while active
Domain Behaviour
DMX output Suspended. composite_dmx() is gated; the controller sends nothing
KNX stage bindings Suppressed. Command telegrams are ignored; status addresses written 0
External control status address Written 1, lighting the panel indicator
House lighting Unaffected. composite_knx() is never gated by this (§7.2.3)
Web UI, X-Touch lighting Read-only, displaying observed levels
Scene DMX actions Skipped, logged ⊘ external_control (§8.15)
Mixer, projector, HDMI Unaffected

The KNX lockout is a deliberate behaviour change and the one thing that differs from current operation. While a desk is running, the wall panel does nothing for stage lighting.

This is correct — a staff member changing stage lighting from a wall panel mid-performance would be worse than the panel not responding — but it must be visible, or it is a support call. Hence the dedicated status address: a staff member pressing a dead button sees a lit "stage lighting under external control" indicator and has their answer without asking anyone.

The bank bindings' own status addresses are written 0 while suppressed, so the panel does not claim the banks are on when the controller is not driving them.

#Observed levels

Incoming ArtDmx populates a separate store, never the level store:

state.lighting.levels     what the controller wants   → composited → output
state.lighting.observed   what is on the wire         → display only

observed is never composited, never read by either compositor pass, and never influences an output value. It exists to answer "what is the stage actually doing" for the UI, the stage plan and the X-Touch scribble strips.

This separation is load-bearing in two ways. It keeps the arbitration failures of a merge design out of the control path entirely. And it prevents a 40 fps input marking all 512 channels dirty in the control-path dirty set, which would put the whole universe into every WebSocket frame.

observed has its own throttle into the batched broadcast (§16.8), and carries only channels that are patched to a fixture — an unpatched channel's value is of no interest to any display.

Display rule: show observed when external control is active, levels otherwise, with an indicator saying which. Never both.

#Resuming

Five seconds of silence and the controller composites from its own model and resumes output. Bindings respond again, their status addresses are rewritten, and the external control status address is cleared.

The controller resumes from its own last values, which may differ from what the desk left on stage — so there can be a visible change at the transition. That is unavoidable and correct: the controller's model is what the operator has been building, and snapping to a departing desk's final state would be worse.

The UI shows the observed levels right up to the transition, so the operator can see what is about to change.

#The manual flag

An External control toggle remains, admin and operator only, forcing the active state regardless of detection.

It is for the case where a desk is patched directly to the fixtures, bypassing the node. There the controller cannot see anything, and continuing to send frames would fight it. Nothing can be observed in that case, so the UI shows last-known controller values at reduced opacity.

The toggle cannot force the state off while frames are arriving. Detection wins, because the alternative is a controller sending into a live desk.

#Fallbacks

Two alternatives are recorded because they solve the same problem differently, and either can be adopted if detection proves unreliable in this installation.

Procedural. Stage banks switched off at the KNX panel before the desk takes over; desk faded out and powered off when finished. Requires nothing of the system. This is the current operating practice and remains the correct answer if the universe configuration below cannot be made to work.

sACN priority takeover. The node supports "sACN Priority takeover for multi-tier controller arrangements". Running the DMX backend on sACN with the controller at a priority above the desk's input source would make the controller authoritative outright rather than merged — the wall panel would always work, including OFF. Enabling external control would drop the controller's priority below the desk's.

This is strictly better where it works, because the failure mode inverts: a forgotten toggle means the desk does not work and the visiting operator asks staff, rather than staff cannot black out the stage with no recovery available to them. It requires confirming what priority the node assigns a DMX-IN source and whether that is configurable.

#Why not merge

Highest-takes-precedence merging was designed and rejected. Staff press OFF while the desk holds a bank at 100%, HTP floors at the desk's value, and the stage stays lit with no recovery available to an ordinary staff member.

Note that this arbitration happens in the node regardless of what the controller does — for the desk's input to reach the fixtures it must be on the same universe as the outputs, so the node is merging two sources in firmware, downstream of anything the application decides. Standing down entirely, rather than adding a second merge on top of one we do not control, is the only coherent option.

#Universe configuration — verify on the bench

The input port must be on the same universe as the output ports for the desk to reach the fixtures through the node's merge. The node then also broadcasts that port's input as Art-Net on that universe — which the controller is itself sourcing.

Whether that is benign, or produces a feedback path, depends on how the node handles receiving broadcasts on a universe it also sources. Three outcomes, in order of preference:

  1. Benign — the controller filters its own frames by source address and everything works as specified
  2. Input broadcasts on a different universe than it merges into, if the port supports it — cleaner, and removes the question entirely
  3. Neither works — fall back to the procedural rule, which needs no input handling at all

This is the single assumption the automatic design rests on. Test it before Phase 2 work begins, not after. The bench procedure is in §22.6.

#Persistence and access

The manual flag is stored in system_state and survives a reboot. Detected state is not persisted — it is re-derived within 5 seconds of startup from whether frames are arriving.

Admin and operator can set the manual flag. Hirers cannot. If external control is active and the hirer has lighting permissions, their lighting tab shows a static message instead of controls.

#Critical scene override
