A critical scene — the alarm All Off — clears the manual flag and resumes output before its DMX actions. It cannot clear detected state, because the desk is still transmitting.

The alarm cannot black out a rig a visiting desk is holding up. The node merges HTP and the controller's zeros lose. This is inherent to any auditorium with a visiting desk, was true of every design considered, and is mitigated procedurally: powering the desk down is on the post-hire checklist (§17.5).

#7.2.8 Health

opendmx driver — the adapter has no return path, so liveness is the serial device node's presence. Unplugging it turns the status red within seconds; nothing confirms that fixtures are receiving.

Art-Net backend — Art-Net is fire-and-forget UDP with no delivery acknowledgement, so reachability is established out of band. Send ArtPoll every 30 seconds; a valid ArtPollReply confirms the node is online and Art-Net is working end to end, and carries node name, firmware version, per-port frame rate and sync status, and the GoodInput byte.

GoodInput is used to confirm the booth input port is configured as an input and receiving — a useful commissioning and diagnostic check. It is not used to gate control; the ArtPollReply refresh rate is far too slow (§7.2.7). No reply within 10 seconds turns the status amber; two consecutive misses turn it red.

sACN backend — E1.31 has no discovery protocol. ICMP echo every 30 seconds confirms network reachability only, not that sACN is being received. Two consecutive failures turn the status red.

Inbound UDP 6454 must be permitted for ArtPollReply (§3.3). The poll must be sent from, and the reply received on, port 6454 itself: the eDMX8 MAX broadcasts every ArtPollReply to the subnet's broadcast address on port 6454, whatever port the poll came from (verified on site, 24 September 2026). The application holds one socket on 6454 for all Art-Net traffic. Only a reply from the node's own address counts toward its health; the node also answers other controllers' polls, and other nodes reply to broadcasts.

#7.3 Allen & Heath CQ-20B

Protocol values are not reproduced here. NRPN addresses, the complete fader law table, pan values, and message formats live in docs/protocols/cq20b.md, verified against the A&H source PDFs stored alongside it. This section covers the integration model, connection semantics, and the handful of values needed to reason about the design. Duplicating protocol data has already produced errors — a fabricated unity value, a wrong pan centre, wrong mute bytes, and a spurious bank-select message — and the rule exists because of them.

#Hardware

A headless rack-mount digital mixer: no screen, no faders, no physical controls beyond I/O and a reset button. Designed to be driven entirely by software, either MixPad or a third-party MIDI application. Here, this application is the primary control surface and MixPad is the engineering tool.

16 mono mic/line inputs, 2 stereo line inputs, stereo Main LR, 6 aux outputs linkable as up to 3 stereo pairs, 4 FX engines, 4 DCAs, 4 mute groups, 96 kHz processing, built-in WiFi, RJ45 Ethernet.

#Verified against
CQ-20B firmware 1.2.2.90653
MIDI protocol document V1.2 (CQ_MIDI_Protocol_V1_2_0_iss5.pdf)
Verified September 2026

Re-verify the NRPN tables after any CQ firmware update. A&H have changed parameter addressing between major protocol revisions before. Record the firmware version on the device configuration screen at each update, and confirm docs/protocols/cq20b.md still matches the shipped protocol document before deploying against new firmware.

#Connection

MIDI over TCP, port 51325, persistent connection, MIDI channel 1.

Control uses NRPN rather than standard control change messages: two bytes of parameter address and up to two of value, giving 16,384 addressable parameters with 14-bit resolution. The CQ transmits NRPN back over the same connection whenever a parameter changes — whether changed here, in MixPad, or by an internal scene recall — which is what keeps the UI in step without polling.

#Connection exclusivity

The CQ-20B permits exactly one TCP/IP MIDI connection at a time. This application holds it persistently.

MixPad — used by the audio engineer for gain, EQ, compression and routing — must connect over the CQ's built-in WiFi or USB, not the wired Ethernet port. Those are separate paths that do not compete for the TCP connection, and changes made there are still relayed to this application over TCP. Up to two MixPad instances and six CQ4You instances can be connected that way simultaneously.

If another client seizes the TCP connection, the reconnect attempt receives a connection refusal rather than a timeout, and the UI must distinguish them:

  • Connection refused — amber, "Another MIDI client is connected. Check that MixPad is using the CQ's WiFi, not Ethernet."
  • Connection timed out — red, "Mixer offline."
  • Connection reset — the CQ rebooted; reconnect normally and resynchronise.

This distinction is on the handover list (§20.2). It is the single most likely cause of a mixer outage during an event, and it is a two-second fix once understood.

#What MIDI reaches

Control Scope In v1
Input fader to Main LR Ip1–16, ST1, ST2, USB, BT Yes
Input mute All inputs Yes
Input pan to Main LR All inputs Optional per channel
Main LR master fader and mute Always present Yes
Output master faders and mutes Out 1–6, individually or linked Yes
Scene recall Scenes 1–128 Yes
Relative level, ±1 dB steps All levels Yes — keyboard control
DCA levels and mutes DCA 1–4 Deferred
Mute group mutes MGRP 1–4 Deferred
Input to output send matrix 20 × 6 Deferred
Input to FX send matrix 20 × 4 Deferred
Soft keys Note on/off Send only — presses are never reported back

Fader values are 14-bit, 0–16383. The application stores and transmits the full resolution and displays dB using A&H's fader law. Raw NRPN values never appear in the interface except as muted reference text on configuration screens.

Key values: unity (0 dB) is NRPN 12544, not the midpoint. Pan centre is NRPN 8192. The default hirer ceiling of 10000 is approximately −5 dB. Scene recall uses bank select 0 plus a program change offset by −1, because the CQ counts scenes from 1 and MIDI program change counts from 0.

#What MIDI does not reach

Gain, phantom power, EQ, compression, gate, routing, pre/post fader configuration, output delay, output limiter, FX parameters, stereo linking, the automatic mic mixer, gain and feedback assistants, and channel names and colours. All are set in MixPad.

The hybrid model handles this cleanly: CQ internal scenes carry everything MIDI cannot reach, and this application handles live performance controls on top.

Phantom power must be enabled before condenser microphones are connected and disabled before they are disconnected. It is not MIDI-controllable, so this is managed exclusively through MixPad. It is a hardware safety requirement.

#Integration model

Scene recall sets the baseline. A bank change plus program change recalls a named scene from the CQ's internal memory, restoring routing, EQ, compression, FX and gain without this application modelling any of it.

Parameter control layers on top for live adjustment: Main LR, input faders and mutes, output masters and mutes, pan, and the hirer surface.

#Channels

Category Count Controlled In v1
Main LR 1 stereo pair Master fader, mute Always present, cannot be deleted
Inputs Up to 20 Send to Main LR, mute, pan Created with the mixer, named and hidden by the admin
Mix outputs 6, linkable as pairs Master fader, mute Created with the mixer (Out 1–6 individually; linking a pair is the admin's choice)
DCAs 4 Level, mute Deferred
Mute groups 4 Mute Deferred

A Main channel is always present. It is created by the first-run wizard's device step as soon as a mixer driver is configured — it cannot be seeded at database initialisation, because a channel must belong to a driver instance (§15.5) and none exists until then. Once created it cannot be deleted.

The admin can rename it and optionally expose it to hirers, which is off by default. On the CQ-20B it is a stereo-linked pair, so one command sets both sides, which is correct for a PA output; the driver reports it as a single stereo ChannelRef (§5.5).

Driver references, not channel numbers. Input NRPN addresses are not contiguous: the mono inputs run consecutively, but the stereo, USB and Bluetooth sources sit at separate offsets. Arithmetic on channel number would need four special cases and would eventually be got wrong.

The core stores an opaque driver_ref (§5.5); this driver uses ip1…ip16, st1, st2, usb, bt and resolves them against its own address tables. The core never parses those strings, and available_refs() supplies them to the admin picker with human labels. A different desk's driver uses whatever naming its own protocol needs.

Unconfigured channels are not tracked. NRPN messages for them are discarded silently.

#Linked stereo outputs

Out 1/2, 3/4 and 5/6 can be linked as stereo pairs in MixPad. When linked, the odd-numbered output's address controls the pair and the even one follows.

Link state is not MIDI-reportable. The application cannot query whether outputs are currently linked. Where the desk's own link is used, the driver reports the pair as a single stereo ChannelRef (§5.5); where it is not, two references can be ganged onto one virtual fader instead. Either way the configuration reflects the admin's intent, not the mixer's live state.

Consequences:

  • If the engineer unlinks a pair in MixPad mid-show while the configuration still says linked, the controller sends to the odd address and the even output stops following. The configuration must be corrected manually.
  • On startup the application cannot verify that its configuration matches. Level values still read correctly — they are separate parameters per output — but the UI grouping will be wrong.
  • Changing link state is a soundcheck task. If it changes during a performance, the operator should flag it for realignment afterwards.

The admin UI states this on the linked checkbox: Set this to match the stereo link state configured on the CQ in MixPad. The mixer does not report link state over MIDI.

#Desk scene library

The admin registers which internal CQ scenes the system knows about:

MixerDeskScene
  scene_number     3        CQ's 1-based number; MIDI sends this minus 1
  name             "Lecture Baseline"
  description      "FOH mix for presentations"
  notes            "Mics 1 and 2 to Main LR, laptop on channel 4. Updated March 2026."
  visible_staff    true

Scene actions reference the library ID, not the number. If the engineer renumbers scenes in MixPad, only the library entry needs updating and every scene configuration follows.

The notes field matters more than it looks — it captures what the audio engineer knows about what each scene actually does, which is otherwise undocumented anywhere.

One desk scene is mandatory: the Venue Default, described in §13.5. It must exist before handover.

#State synchronisation

After connecting and after every scene recall, the application queries current state for Main LR, every configured output, and every configured input, using the get command. Requests are spaced 5–10 ms apart to avoid overwhelming the CQ's MIDI parser; with a full configuration that is roughly 300–500 ms in total. Responses arrive asynchronously and update the state store.

A configurable delay after a scene recall — default 300 ms — lets the CQ finish loading before queries begin. Without it, the queries return the outgoing state.

#Change origin tracking

The CQ echoes every parameter change back, including the application's own writes. The client tracks recently sent values and discards matching echoes, which prevents a feedback loop.

An inbound value that does not match a recent send came from somewhere else — MixPad, or a physical action on the desk. That inference drives the MixPad badge in the mixer view (§21.13), telling the operator that a fader moved externally. It is a small feature that would otherwise be one inference away from being lost.

Change origin is tracked as app | mixpad | surface. The control surface (§7.6) is an equally external mover of the same faders, so a write originating there is tagged surface and badged the same way — a tablet showing a value change with no explanation is the thing this exists to prevent, whichever surface caused it.

#Reconnection

Exponential backoff. On reconnect, read state from the CQ and write nothing until a human acts. The CQ's internal engine is authoritative for its own state.

#Critical scenes and phantom power

A critical scene mutes Main LR and every configured channel using absolute mute-on commands. Phantom power is not MIDI-controllable, so condenser microphones remain phantomed afterwards.

This is acceptable: phantom power creates no audible or safety issue on its own, and the mutes stop signal reaching the PA. If a particular installation needs phantom off for equipment-preservation reasons, that is a manual MixPad step and belongs in the site runbook.

#Metering — two connections

MIDI carries no metering. An earlier revision concluded from this that metering was unavailable by any means, and that MixPad did not meter either. Both statements were wrong. MixPad does meter, and the data is on the wire — on the CQ's proprietary control protocol rather than the documented MIDI one.

The driver therefore holds two connections to the same mixer:

Connection Port Carries Status
MIDI 51325 Scene recall, faders, mutes, pan, relative steps Primary. Documented by A&H
Native 51326 Metering, channel names, stereo link state Additive. Reverse-engineered

MIDI stays primary and keeps all control, for three reasons. It is A&H's published contract, so a firmware change should degrade a display rather than the room. Writing to an undocumented protocol on a live console mid-performance is a different risk class from reading from it. And scene recall is not implemented in the reverse-engineered work — it is central to how the venue operates, so MIDI would be needed regardless.

The native connection is read-mostly: the handshake, a client-init, and periodic keep-alives. Nothing that changes mixer state.

Protocol detail, the record map and the attribution are in docs/protocols/cq20b-native.md, deliberately kept out of this document — it is reverse-engineered and will change.

#What is metered

Every input the venue uses. Confirmed against a live CQ-20B capture:

Ip1 … Ip16      the microphones
ST1 L/R         the AUX feed
ST2 L/R         the HDMI audio via the S/PDIF converter (§7.5)
USB, Bluetooth, four stereo FX returns
Out 1 … Out 6, Main L/R

Levels are post-compressor on inputs and post-limiter on outputs. That suits the question an operator actually asks — is this microphone live? — and it is not a gain-structure tool.

#Failure is degradation, not loss

If the native connection cannot be established, the mixer still works completely. Faders, mutes, scene recall and everything else are on MIDI. Only metering is lost, and the interface says so rather than showing dead meters (§5.5).

Bench-tested, 2026-09-11. Port 51326 accepts a connection while this application holds MIDI on 51325, so the feature exists as designed. The MIDI connection is a separate limit of one and does not count against the CQ's two MixPad slots. The native connection does: the native protocol is MixPad's own, so the CQ counts every native client against those two slots. With both probes connected, one MixPad joined and a second was refused. One MixPad therefore works alongside this application, which is accepted: once the venue is set up almost nothing changes, and one engineer is enough for a minor adjustment. If two MixPad clients are already connected when this application starts, its native connection is the one refused and only metering is lost, returning once a slot frees. Detail in docs/protocols/cq20b-native.md.

#7.4 Projector — PJLink

#Protocol

PJLink Class 1, TCP port 4352. Request and response.

#Authentication

PJLink supports optional password authentication by MD5 challenge-response. The projector's greeting begins either PJLINK 0 (no authentication) or PJLINK 1 {nonce} (authentication required — MD5 the nonce concatenated with the password and send it as hex with the first command).

Both modes are handled transparently:

  1. The device configuration screen has an optional password field; empty means no authentication.
  2. The password is encrypted at rest using the device secret (§6.10).
  3. Mismatch handling: - Projector requires authentication, none configured → red, "Authentication required — set the password in device settings" - Projector requires authentication, wrong password → red, "Authentication failed — check the password." After three failures, hold amber for 60 seconds rather than hammering the projector - Projector does not require authentication but a password is configured → ignored, connection proceeds

A test connection button verifies authentication before saving.

#State model

class ProjectorState(Enum):
    UNREACHABLE = "unreachable"
    OFF         = "off"
    WARMING     = "warming"     # commands rejected
    ON          = "on"
    COOLING     = "cooling"     # commands rejected
    ERROR       = "error"

Commands arriving during WARMING or COOLING are rejected, with device_unavailable and the current state in detail (§16).

An earlier revision queued them. Queuing produces delayed surprise — someone presses power-on during cool-down, nothing happens, they walk away, and ninety seconds later the projector turns itself on in an empty room. Rejection is not silent as long as the interface says why, and §21.14 does.

Sequencing is what scenes are for. §8.13's worked example is exactly this case: t=8000 ms projector set input 1 (after the projector has warmed). The one legitimate use for a queue is already served by the primitive built for it.

The state transition itself is a device_state trigger (§8.3), so a rule can write the panel's status indicator the moment the projector settles — without any polling in the rule layer.

#Minimum warm-up

A projector may report on while its lamp is still warming. The installation's PT-EZ570 reports warming for about 13 seconds and then on, so rejection alone let a power-off through twenty seconds after power-on. The PJLink device therefore carries a min_warmup_s setting (0–600, default 60; 0 disables), and from a successful power-on command the controller holds the shown state at warming for that long, measured from its own command rather than the projector's report (B74):

  • During the hold a reported warming or on is shown as warming everywhere — state.projector, the projector_state frame, device_state triggers and derived status — so power-off and input changes are refused exactly as in real warm-up, and the on transition happens once, when the hold ends.
  • Any other report — off, error, cooling, unreachable — is shown at once. The hold never masks a fault.
  • remaining_s on GET /projector/state carries the hold's countdown in whole seconds, and is null otherwise (§16.5, §21.14).
  • The hold is a time window only and is not persisted: a restart during one trusts the projector.

"Set the input once it has warmed" (§8.13) therefore fires at the end of the hold, so a scene's delay before an input change must be at least min_warmup_s.

#Health and boot

Poll %1POWR ? every 30 seconds while on, every 5 minutes while off.

On startup, query state and issue no power command. The projector may be mid-cycle from before the restart; discover, do not overwrite.

#7.5 HDMI Matrix — Lenkeng LKV422

Hardware was selected in April 2026: a Lenkeng LKV422 4×2 HDMI matrix switch, controlled over RS-232 from the controller's GPIO UART. This replaces the Ethernet-controlled shortlist carried in earlier revisions, and takes the HDMI matrix off the network entirely.

The vendor manual is stored at docs/protocols/lkv422-manual.pdf and the integration notes at docs/protocols/lkv422.md.

#Device summary
Inputs / outputs 4 HDMI in, 2 HDMI out
Video HDMI 2.0, 18 Gbps, up to 4096×2160@60 Hz, HDR10, HDCP 2.2 and 1.4
Control paths RS-232, front panel buttons, IR remote
Serial 3-pin terminal block — GND / Rx / Tx
Serial settings 9600 baud (default), 8 data bits, 1 stop bit, no parity
Audio breakout S/PDIF optical and 3.5 mm stereo, following HDMI output 1 or 2
Power DC 5 V 1 A, under 4 W
Dimensions 250 × 80 × 21 mm, rack ears supplied

The step up from the 2×2 assumed in earlier revisions to 4×2 is free headroom. The two configured inputs remain side of stage and back of house; inputs 3 and 4 are available without further hardware.

#Electrical interface

The matrix is connected by a USB-to-serial cable from the controller to the LKV422's terminal block. It is not wired to the GPIO header.

This was a deliberate reversal of an earlier GPIO UART design, and the reasoning is worth recording:

  • Portability. The cable moves with the controller to any platform. A GPIO UART exists only on the reference board, and the migration path to x86 or a different SBC would have meant rewiring rather than unplugging. This is the same argument that produced the platform layer in §5.4.
  • The risk sits at the cheap end. The LKV422's signalling level is not documented (below). If it is guessed wrong with a GPIO connection, the casualty is the Compute Module and the whole appliance. With a USB cable, the casualty is a $200 switcher.
  • It deletes configuration rather than moving it. No UART overlay, no enable_uart, no serial console entanglement, no getty to disable.
  • It is diagnosable. The cable can be unplugged and connected to any laptop with a terminal program. Given that two protocol behaviours remain unverified, and given that a successor may need to be talked through a fault over the phone, this matters more than it appears to.
  • It is replaceable by anyone. A commodity cable from any electronics supplier, rather than a soldered level-shifter circuit that a maintainer has to understand before touching.

The cost is one USB port, which the eDMX8 MAX freed by moving DMX to Ethernet (§17.2), and a udev rule (§4.12).

#Signalling level — measure before ordering

The vendor manual states "RS-232 3 Pin (GND/Rx/Tx)" without specifying voltage levels, and that phrasing is used for both true RS-232 and TTL-level serial. USB-to-serial cables come in both varieties and they are not interchangeable: a true RS-232 cable drives ±5 to ±12 V, which will damage a TTL port.

With the LKV422 powered and nothing connected to its serial port, measure the TX pin against GND:

Idle reading Interface Cable to order
−5 V to −12 V True RS-232 USB-to-RS-232, e.g. FTDI US232R
+3.3 V 3.3 V TTL USB-to-TTL 3.3 V, e.g. FTDI TTL-232R-3V3
+5 V 5 V TTL USB-to-TTL 5 V, e.g. FTDI TTL-232R-5V

Same form factor and price bracket in each case, and identical handling in software. One measurement decides the order.

Measured, 2026-09-11: −9.21 V on TX against GND with nothing connected. True RS-232, so the specified part below is the right one and can be ordered. Recorded in docs/hardware/network_map.md.

The measurement and its result are recorded on the pre-installation checklist (§17.4) and in docs/hardware/network_map.md, because a maintainer replacing the cable needs to know which variety it is.

#Cable selection

Specified part: FTDI USB-RS232-WE-1800-BT_0.0 — 1.8 m, FT232RQ, true RS-232 levels, bare tinned wire ends.

The wire-ended variant is chosen over a DB9 cable because the LKV422 terminates in a screw terminal block. Bare conductors go straight in: no DB9, no screw-terminal breakout, and no cutting a moulded connector off and then working out which conductor went to which pin.

Two secondary properties earn their place:

  • A unique USB serial number is programmed into the FT232R. This is what the udev rule in §4.12 matches on, and it is what makes /dev/hdmi-matrix unambiguous.
  • Tx and Rx LEDs are visible through the transparent moulding. At commissioning, when the question is whether the switcher is ignoring the controller or replying with something unparseable, seeing whether Rx flickers at all answers half of it immediately.

Order the _0.0 variant. The _3.3 and _5.0 variants add a powered conductor at the wire end. The LKV422 has its own supply, so it is not needed, and a live wire loose in a terminal block is a hazard for no benefit.

A 5 m version, USB-RS232-WE-5000-BT_0.0, is available if the controller and the switcher are not adjacent in the rack.

If the measurement says TTL, the equivalent parts are TTL-232R-3V3-WE or TTL-232R-5V-WE — same family, same FT232R, same unique serial, same kernel driver. Order after measuring, not before.

Buy from an authorised distributor — Mouser, DigiKey, element14/Farnell NZ, or RS Components NZ. Counterfeit FT232R chips are common in the grey market, and the failure that matters here is not the chip dying: it is a duplicated or absent serial number, which silently breaks the udev rule and produces a device path that binds to the wrong thing. A few dollars removes the entire class of problem.

Chip choice is about serial numbers, not driver quality — see §4.12, which corrects a claim carried in earlier revisions of this document.

#Termination

The cable breaks out the full RS-232 signal set — TXD, RXD, GND, plus RTS, CTS and the remaining handshake lines. Three conductors are used.

   Cable                  LKV422 terminal block
   ─────                  ─────────────────────
   TXD  ────────────────►  Rx
   RXD  ◄────────────────  Tx
   GND  ─────────────────  GND

Take the conductor colour mapping from Table 5.1 of FTDI's DS_USB_RS232_CABLES datasheet, and confirm with a meter before crimping. Do not work from memory or from a forum post — the colours are not the ones most people assume.

Insulate the unused conductors individually and heatshrink them back. Loose tinned ends beside a screw terminal is how an intermittent fault appears six months later.

The crossover — TXD to Rx, RXD to Tx — is the part that gets wired straight through and then takes an afternoon to find. Label both ends.

Both devices must share a ground reference. The LKV422 has its own 5 V supply and a grounding screw; bond it to the rack earth as the manual requires.

#Device path and permissions

The cable is reached through a stable udev symlink, /dev/hdmi-matrix, matched by FTDI serial number (§4.12). The application never references a ttyUSB node, because that numbering moves.

The symlink is group dialout and the application user is a member. A missing group membership produces a permission error at startup that is easy to misread as a wiring fault, so the client reports it distinctly (below).

On a ported platform this is unchanged — the same cable, the same udev rule, the same device path. That is the point of choosing USB, and porting.md says so rather than describing an alternative.

#Protocol

ASCII commands over the serial line. Two command syntaxes exist; the application uses the second, because it expresses the operations this system actually performs.

Routing — both outputs together. This is the normal operation, and it is a single command:

PA1R    Both outputs follow input 1
PA2R    Both outputs follow input 2
PA3R    Both outputs follow input 3
PA4R    Both outputs follow input 4

Routing — outputs independently. PS{output}{input}R:

PS11R   Output 1 ← input 1        PS21R   Output 2 ← input 1
PS12R   Output 1 ← input 2        PS22R   Output 2 ← input 2
PS13R   Output 1 ← input 3        PS23R   Output 2 ← input 3
PS14R   Output 1 ← input 4        PS24R   Output 2 ← input 4

Status query. PAXXR returns OKP{a}P{b}, where a is the input feeding output 1 and b the input feeding output 2. A malformed command returns ERR.

→  PAXXR
←  OKP3P1            Output 1 shows input 3; output 2 shows input 1

Maintenance commands, available but not used by the application at runtime:

Reset          Restart the device
Recover        Restore factory settings — also returns baud rate to 9600
Baud9600       Set 9600 baud (default)
Baud19200      Set 19200 baud

Leave the baud rate at 9600. The commands are a handful of bytes and 9600 is ample. More importantly, a front-panel reset or a Recover returns the device to 9600 — so a controller configured for 19200 would silently lose communication after any factory reset, at exactly the moment someone is already troubleshooting.

The manual also documents an alternative combined syntax, PAPXPX — for example PAP3P1 sets output 1 to input 3 and output 2 to input 1. It is functionally covered by the commands above and is not used, so there is one command vocabulary to reason about rather than two.

#Undetermined until bench testing

The manual does not specify two things the client needs. Neither blocks development, and both are resolved on first contact with the hardware:

  1. Line termination. Whether responses end with CR, LF, CRLF, or nothing at all. The parser is written terminator-agnostic — it accumulates bytes and matches on the OKP{d}P{d} and ERR patterns, treating a quiet period as end-of-response — so it works whichever it turns out to be. The observed behaviour is recorded in docs/protocols/lkv422.md.
  2. Whether switch commands acknowledge. The manual documents a return value only for PAXXR. The client therefore does not wait for an acknowledgement on a switch; it sends the command, waits a settling period, then issues PAXXR and confirms the routing actually changed. That converts a fire-and-forget switch into a confirmed one and sidesteps the question entirely.

#Backend

The abstraction defined for development against a stub is unchanged; the LKV422 is one concrete implementation of it.

class VideoMatrixDriver(Protocol):
    capabilities: MatrixCapabilities
    async def route(self, outputs: list[str], input: str) -> None: ...
    async def read_routing(self) -> dict[str, str]: ...        # {output_ref: input_ref}
    def available_refs(self) -> MatrixRefs: ...                # inputs and outputs

One method, taking a list — per the single-intent rule (§5.5). The core states which outputs should follow which input; the driver picks the encoding. Given every output it has, the LKV422 driver sends the single PA{n}R; given a subset, it sends sequential PS{o}{i}R writes.

switch_all existed in an earlier revision as a second method. It was wrong twice over: it made the caller choose the encoding, and PA{n}R sets all outputs, which would be incorrect for a partial group on a larger matrix.

class MatrixCapabilities:
    input_count: int
    output_count: int
    supports_atomic_route: bool    # True for the LKV422

supports_atomic_route = False means a grouped route may briefly show mismatched outputs. Unavoidable on such hardware, and worth the interface knowing so it can say so rather than looking like a fault.

class LKV422Driver(VideoMatrixDriver):
    """Lenkeng LKV422 4×2 HDMI matrix over RS-232, 9600 8N1."""

    RESPONSE = re.compile(rb"OKP(\d)P(\d)|ERR")

    async def route(self, outputs: list[str], input: str) -> None:
        if not 1 <= int(input) <= 4:
            raise ValueError(f"LKV422 inputs are 1-4, got {input}")

        if set(outputs) == {"1", "2"}:
            await self._send(f"PA{input}R")          # atomic, both outputs
        else:
            for o in outputs:                        # partial group, sequential
                await self._send(f"PS{o}{input}R")

        await asyncio.sleep(self._settle_ms / 1000)  # default 250 ms
        routing = await self.read_routing()
        for o in outputs:
            if routing.get(o) != input:
                raise MatrixError(f"route of output {o} to {input} not confirmed")

    async def read_routing(self) -> dict[str, str]:
        reply = await self._request("PAXXR", timeout=1.0)
        m = self.RESPONSE.search(reply)
        if not m or m.group(1) is None:
            raise MatrixError(f"unexpected reply to PAXXR: {reply!r}")
        return {"1": m.group(1).decode(), "2": m.group(2).decode()}

The encoding choice lives here, not in the caller (§5.5). Given both outputs the driver sends one command; given a subset it falls back to sequential writes, which is the behaviour supports_atomic_route declares to the interface.

Serial I/O uses pyserial-asyncio against /dev/hdmi-matrix, keeping the client inside the single event loop (§5.3) with no threading. A stub video matrix driver is retained for tests and for development without hardware (§5.5).

#Connection model

A serial port is not a network socket, and the difference matters for how failure is reported.

Opening /dev/hdmi-matrix succeeds whether or not anything is connected at the other end — there is no link state, no handshake, and no equivalent of connection refused. Successfully opening the port proves nothing. Liveness is established only by a valid reply to PAXXR.

The client therefore treats the query, not the open, as the connection event:

  1. Open /dev/hdmi-matrix. Failure here means a configuration or permissions problem — the cable is unplugged so the symlink does not exist, or the application user is not in dialout — and is reported as such, distinctly from a wiring fault.
  2. Send PAXXR and wait up to 1 second.
  3. A valid OKP{a}P{b} marks the device connected and populates initial routing.
  4. No reply, or a malformed one, marks it offline and retries with the standard backoff (§5.3).

A port that opens but never replies is the signature of wiring problems — TX and RX not crossed, no common ground, or a signalling level mismatch. The error message says so, because that is the failure most likely to occur at commissioning, and because the two causes have completely different fixes.

#Destinations

A destination is a named thing the venue routes to, mapping to one or more physical outputs. It is the video equivalent of a ganged mixer channel (§5.5).

VideoDestination        name, device_id, sort_order
VideoDestinationOutput  destination_id, driver_ref

This venue has one destination — "The room" — covering both outputs. Two of the four inputs are in service: side of stage and back of house. The operator picks a source; both outputs follow, because they are one destination. Normal operation is a two-button toggle.

async def set_source(self, destination_id: int, input_ref: str) -> None:
    outputs = await db.destination_outputs(destination_id)
    await self._driver.route(outputs, input_ref)      # one intent, driver picks the command
    await bus.emit(VideoSourceChanged(destination_id, input_ref))

This is what makes the scene action single. A scene sets a destination's source, so there is one action and one call. Modelling individual outputs would mean two actions at the same delay (§8.13), which the engine fires as two calls with no way to recombine them — and the driver's atomic command would be unreachable.

If the back-of-house return is ever wanted independently, that is a second destination rather than a code change. matrix_outputs already lets the venue name its outputs; a destination is what the operator actually picks.

Divergence detection. The routing poll finds outputs within one destination disagreeing — someone used the front panel or the IR remote — and the interface shows the first output's source with a divergence marker. Selecting any source re-converges them. Because PAXXR returns real device state, this detects out-of-band changes as well as its own.

This is the same behaviour as mixer gang divergence (§5.5), deliberately: one concept, two categories.

#Out-of-band control

The LKV422 accepts input from three places: RS-232, the front panel buttons, and the bundled IR remote. This application controls one of them.

The practical consequences:

  • Anyone in the AV cupboard can change routing from the front panel, and anyone in line of sight of the IR window can change it with the remote. The 30-second status poll detects the change and updates the UI, so the interface never shows a stale source — but it will not prevent the change.
  • The IR remote should be removed from circulation and stored with the recovery card rather than left in the rack or the AV cupboard. It is a control surface with no access control that will otherwise be picked up by a hirer.
  • Front-panel routing changes are corrected by pressing a source button in the interface, or by the "Restore Venue Default" scene (§13.5), which sets HDMI routing as one of its actions.

This is the same shape as the network exposure accepted in §3.5 — the application's permission model governs access through the application — and the same mitigation applies: detect the drift, and make restoring the venue default a single action.

#Audio breakout to the mixer

Presentation audio reaches the CQ-20B through the matrix, arriving on stereo input ST2 and coming under fader and mute control on the operator surface. This replaces an equivalent connection from the switcher being superseded.

#Take the S/PDIF output, not the 3.5 mm

The existing installation feeds ST2 from the old switcher's 3.5 mm analogue output and runs ST2's gain at maximum to get a usable level. That analogue tap is an unspecified, weakly-driven output; there is no reason to expect the LKV422's to be better, and no headroom left to compensate if it is not.

The signal path is therefore:

   LKV422  ──optical S/PDIF──►  S/PDIF-to-analogue converter  ──line──►  CQ-20B ST2
                (TOSLINK)              (DAC, line output)

A converter's line output is a specified stage, typically around 2 V RMS, which lands comfortably in ST2's range and allows the gain to be set sensibly rather than pinned.

#Use optical, not coaxial

TOSLINK is galvanically isolated. It breaks any ground loop between the AV rack and the mixer, which removes a hum path entirely rather than treating it later. Coaxial S/PDIF does not, and offers nothing in exchange at this cable length.

#The bitstream constraint

The LKV422 passes through compressed audio formats, including Dolby Digital, DTS and Atmos. A source set to bitstream output will produce silence or noise on a PCM-only converter.

Two mitigations, applied together:

  1. Set source devices to PCM stereo output where the venue controls them. Laptops and PCs — the overwhelming majority of use here — default to PCM already.
  2. Specify a converter that decodes Dolby Digital and DTS, not a PCM-only unit. It costs roughly $20 more and covers the case where someone connects a games console or a Blu-ray player set to bitstream.
#Output selection

The audio breakout follows HDMI output 1 or 2, selected by a front-panel button, and that selection is not controllable over RS-232. Set it to output 1 at commissioning and record it.

This is harmless in normal operation because both outputs carry the same source, so either selection yields the same audio. It matters only if independent routing is ever used, and it is a front-panel control that someone could nudge — so it is on the post-hire checklist alongside the routing check.

#Health

Poll PAXXR every 25 seconds. The interval is counted from the end of one poll to the start of the next, so a front-panel change is seen at worst one interval plus one exchange later; 25 seconds keeps that inside the 30-second bound §18 and Appendix A state.

Condition Status
Valid OKP{a}P{b} Green — connected, routing known
One missed or malformed reply Amber — showing last-known routing
Two consecutive misses Red — offline
Device node cannot be opened Red — with the configuration-error detail

This is a genuine end-to-end check: a reply proves the cable, the level shifter, the UART configuration, and the device itself are all working. It is stronger evidence than the TCP connection state used for the network devices.

The poll is also what surfaces out-of-band routing changes, so it earns its keep twice.

#Access

Admin and operator only. Hirers have no HDMI controls.

#7.6 Control Surface — Behringer X-Touch Extender

An optional physical control surface, connected over Ethernet, giving motorised faders for both mixer and lighting control. It is the first device in this system that is a control surface rather than something controlled: it both reads from and writes to the state store (§5.6), which makes it a category of its own.

It is optional in the strict sense. Nothing in Phases 1–8 depends on it, the web interface remains a complete control surface without it, and the system must operate normally when it is absent, unplugged, or faulty. It is built in Phase 9 (§18).

#Why this device, and why not something custom

The alternative considered was purpose-built hardware — motorised fader modules, motor drivers, position sensing, capacitive touch, a control board, firmware and an enclosure, with the same protocol layer still to write at the end.

It was rejected on handover grounds rather than cost. This specification is built on the promise in §20 that a successor is a Python and React developer who can maintain the system from the repository. Custom hardware breaks that: it requires electronics skills, board files, and a supply of fader modules that may not exist in five years. For a school auditorium with an explicit succession plan, that is the wrong kind of debt.

The surface is also not a replacement for the web interface. The tablet does things faders structurally cannot — the stage plan, scenes, projector, HDMI, all configuration — and §10.3 already provides genuine multi-touch. The single thing a touchscreen cannot do is motorised recall: fire a scene and the physical faders move to match. That, rather than tactility in the abstract, is what this device is for.

#Device summary
Faders 8 × 100 mm motorised, touch-sensitive
Encoders 8 × rotary/push with 13-segment LED collar
Buttons 32 illuminated — four per channel strip
Displays 8 × colour LCD scribble strips
Meters 8 × 8-segment LED — unusable, see below
Protocols Mackie Control, HUI, standard MIDI
Transport Ethernet (RTP-MIDI), USB-B, USB hub
Power IEC mains, 100–240 V, 30 W
Dimensions approx. 290 × 300 × 97 mm

#The meters

The eight 8-segment LED meters are driven from the mixer's metering feed (§7.3), through MCU's channel-pressure messages.

An earlier revision stated they would remain permanently dark, on the belief that no metering existed anywhere in the system. That belief was wrong — the data is on the CQ's native protocol.

They light only when the driver reports supports_metering (§5.5). If the native connection is unavailable, the meters stay dark and the surface configurator says why rather than leaving 64 LEDs unexplained.

Driving them from fader position remains prohibited in every case. That would produce a display that looks like a level reading and is not, which is what §21.13 exists to prevent. Dark meters with a stated reason are correct; invented ones are not.

Rate-capped as in the performance targets below — an unthrottled meter feed would saturate the session link that also carries fader positions.

#Protocol choice

Mackie Control carried over RTP-MIDI. These are two different layers and the distinction matters:

  • RTP-MIDI is the transport — how MIDI bytes cross the network, as opposed to USB or five-pin DIN
  • Mackie Control is the dialect — what those bytes mean

The surface's setup menu selects them separately: one encoder chooses Mackie Control or HUI, another chooses Network, USB or MIDI. The required configuration is Mackie Control + Network.

#Why not Xctl

Behringer's proprietary Xctl protocol, used between an X32 or X-Air and the full X-Touch, was considered and is not applicable: the Extender does not implement Xctl. It was designed to hang off an X-Touch or a DAW, so MCU and HUI are all it has.

Had it been available, MCU would still have been the choice:

  • Xctl is undocumented and community-reverse-engineered, so a firmware update could change it without notice. RTP-MIDI is a published standard and MCU is widely documented.
  • The repository is expected to go public (§20.3), and building on a reverse-engineered vendor protocol raises provenance questions that a documented one does not.
  • MCU is spoken by many manufacturers — Icon, PreSonus, Mackie, Softube among them. If this product line is discontinued in 2032, an MCU implementation accepts a substitute from any of them; an Xctl implementation would be welded to one vendor permanently. This is the same reasoning that produced the platform abstraction in §5.4 and moved the HDMI serial link to USB in §7.5.
  • The one thing Xctl would have added — colour scribble strips — is reachable from MCU mode anyway through a vendor sysex extension (below).

HUI is rejected because its fader encoding is a fiddlier two-message scheme built around Pro Tools, where MCU carries fader position as a single clean 14-bit pitch bend value.

#Network placement and commissioning
Address 10.2.30.100, static, DHCP off
Port UDP 5004 (control), 5005 (data)
Mode SLAVE — the controller initiates the session
Firewall Inbound UDP 5004–5005 from VLAN 30 (§3.3)

SLAVE mode is required so the controller opens the session. That matches the ProtocolClient reconnection pattern (§5.3) and keeps reconnection timing under the controller's control, rather than having the surface call out to a fixed address on its own schedule.

The device sits on VLAN 30 and is therefore covered by the accepted exposure in §3.5.

#Configuration persistence

Mode and network settings are entered by holding the channel 1 Select button while powering on, setting the options with the encoders, then pressing channel 1 Select and power-cycling. That final step writes the settings to non-volatile memory — this is a commissioning procedure, not a per-boot one.

It nonetheless belongs on the pre-installation checklist (§17.4) and the recovery card, because a factory reset or a replacement unit requires it to be redone. A surface silently sitting in the wrong mode presents as a network fault, which is a long way from the actual cause.

The surface's hardware configuration menu also allows remapping button functions. Leave it at defaults. Because this application writes its own MCU interpretation, any button can be given any meaning in software (see banking below) without touching the device. Keeping the hardware at factory settings means a replacement unit needs only the mode and network configuration, not a button map.

#RTP-MIDI transport

Implemented in core/surface/rtpmidi.py rather than through a daemon. This follows the reasoning of §7.2.5: it keeps everything inside the single asyncio event loop, and the MIDI byte-level parsing already exists in the CQ-20B client (§7.3).

A deliberate subset of RFC 6295 is implemented, which is what makes this tractable:

Implemented Omitted
Session invitation and acceptance on the control port Recovery journal
Clock synchronisation (CK0/CK1/CK2 exchange) Multi-peer sessions
MIDI command section, unjournalled Feature negotiation beyond the basics
Sequence numbering and sync loss detection

The recovery journal exists to reconstruct lost messages over unreliable transport. This is a single point-to-point session on a wired local VLAN, and the failure mode without a journal is a dropped fader update that the next state broadcast corrects within ~70 ms. That is an acceptable trade for a large reduction in complexity.

Write the implementation from the protocol documentation — RFC 6295 and Apple's published session notes — not from another implementation's source. Existing Linux implementations are variously commercial or GPL, and §20.3 anticipates this repository being published. Their written explanations are fine; their code is not.

Sync loss is detected when clock synchronisation exchanges stop being answered. The session is torn down and re-invited under the standard backoff, and the surface is marked offline.

#Mackie Control dialect

Only the parts of MCU that map onto real controls are implemented. Address assignments follow the standard MCU allocation.

Control Direction Message
Fader position both Pitch bend, channels 1–8, 14-bit
Fader touch in Note on/off, 0x68–0x6F
Encoder rotation in CC 0x10–0x17, relative
Encoder push in Note 0x20–0x27
Encoder LED collar out CC 0x30–0x37
Channel buttons in Notes 0x00–0x1F, four per strip
Button LEDs out Same notes; velocity 127 on, 0 off, 1 flashing
Scribble strip text out Sysex F0 00 00 66 14 12 <offset> <chars> F7 — 2 rows × 7
Scribble strip colour out Sysex F0 00 00 66 14 72 <c1..c8> F7
Meters out Channel pressure, 0xD0 | (strip << 4) | segment

These addresses follow the standard MCU allocation and are to be confirmed on the bench before implementation proceeds. In particular the sysex device ID byte (14) may differ between the full X-Touch and the Extender. One test message resolves it.

#Meters

Segment values 0–13, sent as channel pressure. The driver converts dB to a segment using the mixer's declared meter_min_db and meter_max_db (§5.5) — it does not assume a range, because that is the desk's property rather than the surface's.

The driver owns peak hold, since the hardware does not: hold the highest segment for about 1.5 seconds, then fall. Without it a meter on 8 discrete LEDs reads as a flicker rather than a level.

Capped at 15 updates per second per strip (below). A strip whose target has no meter data sends nothing, leaving those LEDs dark rather than at segment 0 — the same absent-not-zero rule the interface follows (§21.13).

#Scribble strips carry two rows

Each strip is two rows of seven characters, and both are used:

┌───────┐
│ Mic 1 │  ← short_name, or name where it fits (§5.5)
│ −6.0  │  ← live value, rate-capped
└───────┘

The lower row is what makes the surface self-sufficient: an operator sees what a channel is and where it is set without looking at the tablet, which is most of the reason to have a physical surface at all.

Row contents by target domain:

Target Upper Lower
Mixer channel Channel name dB, or MUTE when muted
Lighting group or channel Group or fixture name Percentage
Scene Scene name Blank, or RUN while executing
Bank control BANK Direction
Unassigned Blank Blank

On a bank change the lower row shows the new bank's name for about two seconds, then reverts to values. Otherwise a bank switch is silent on the hardware and the operator has to look elsewhere to confirm it took.

Both rows share the 4-per-second write cap below — an unthrottled dB readout during a fade would flood the session with LCD sysex.

#Scribble strip colour

Eight colour bytes, one per strip:

00 blank   01 red     02 green   03 yellow
04 blue    05 purple  06 cyan    07 white

Colour is assigned per bank and per strip, which makes bank identity readable without reading text — lighting strips amber, mixer inputs cyan, Main LR white.

Inverted text blocks are not reachable. No sysex message exists for the LCDs' inverse-video mode. Minor, and noted so it is not hunted for.

#Bank model

Standard MCU banking pages through eight consecutive channels, because a DAW owns the mapping. This application owns both ends, so the model is arbitrary per-strip assignment instead.

A bank is eight slots. Each slot points at any target in any domain.

Bank 0  "Venue Default"    Mic 1 · Mic 2 · HDMI · Main LR ·
                           Row 1 · Row 2 · Row 3 · Row 4
Bank 1  "Inputs 1–8"       Ip1 … Ip8
Bank 2  "Inputs 9–16"      Ip9 … Ip16
Bank 3  "Outputs"          Out 1/2 · Out 3/4 · Out 5 · Out 6 · — · — · — · Main LR
Bank 4  "Lighting"         Eight lighting groups · lighting master

Bank 0 is the everyday layout and covers the overwhelming majority of use: two microphones, the HDMI feed, the main output, and the four lighting rows. It is what the surface returns to at startup. The remaining banks exist for the nights that are not ordinary.

Banks are admin-configurable, ordered, and included in the venue baseline (§13.5), so a hire cannot leave the surface remapped.

#Assignable targets
Domain Fader controls Buttons Encoder
mixer_channel Fader level Mute Pan, where the channel allows it
mixer_output Output master Mute —
mixer_main Main LR master Mute —
lighting_channel Fixture level Bump to full while held Colour hue, RGB fixtures only
lighting_group Group level — sets every member's level (§9.4); never an indicator-only group Bump to full while held —
lighting_master Master dimmer Blackout, confirm-on-hold —
rule — Fire a rule (§8.3) —
bank — Bank up / bank down —
unassigned Fader parks at zero, LCD blank — —

Video destinations are deliberately not assignable. The surface is an audio and lighting instrument, and its 32 buttons are committed to mutes, bumps and banking. Adding video_destination as a domain later is one enum value if it is ever wanted — this is a scoping choice, not an omission.

A button fires a rule, not a scene directly. Routing it through the rule layer gives it a guard, debounce and a log entry for free, and means one mechanism decides when things happen regardless of which surface asked (§8.1). A rule with action_type = 'run_scene' covers what a direct scene target would have.

Bank switching is just an assignment. Two buttons in each bank are given the bank target, which is why the device's own configuration menu does not need remapping. Every bank should carry a route back to Bank 0, or an operator can get stranded in a bank with no way out — the configurator warns when a bank has no bank-change button (§21.23).

#Per-strip scaling

Faders are scaled per strip according to what the strip points at. Raw passthrough is not acceptable.

The 14-bit MCU pitch bend range and the CQ's 14-bit NRPN range coincide, which makes passthrough tempting. It would be wrong: the X-Touch's printed scale follows MCU convention, while the CQ's unity sits at NRPN 12544 under A&H's fader law (§7.3). Moving the fader to its printed 0 dB mark would land somewhere else on the mixer.

Target domain Mapping Scribble strip shows
Mixer Physical position → dB → NRPN via A&H's fader law dB
Lighting Linear across the throw, 0–100 (§9.2) Percentage

Because the mapping is per strip rather than per half of the surface, a bank can mix domains freely. It also means the engraved dB markings are only meaningful beside mixer strips; the scribble strip carries the authoritative value in both cases, which is why no physical label overlay is needed.

#Detents on a motorised fader

A law point marked detent (§5.5) applies here too, and the mechanism differs from a touchscreen's.

The X-Touch's faders are motorised, so the surface can drive the cap to the detent value rather than merely reporting a snapped number. While a fader is being moved within the detent zone the controller holds the motor at unity; breaking out releases it. That is closer to a physical detent than anything a touchscreen can offer, and it is the one place this surface is genuinely better than the tablet.

It interacts with touch arbitration below: the motor is not driven while a fader is under a finger, so the hold is applied on release rather than fighting the operator's hand mid-move. The snapped value is what gets sent either way.

#Touch arbitration

The faders are touch-sensitive and report touch as note on/off. Handling this is not optional — without it, a state update arriving while an operator is moving a fader fights their hand.

async def _on_state_change(self, strip: int, value: int) -> None:
    if self._touched[strip]:
        self._pending[strip] = value      # remember, do not drive the motor
        return
    await self._send_fader(strip, value)

async def _on_touch_release(self, strip: int) -> None:
    self._touched[strip] = False
    if (value := self._pending.pop(strip, None)) is not None:
        await self._send_fader(strip, value)   # catch up on release

While touched, the strip is the authoritative source for its target and the motor is not driven. On release, any state change that arrived during the touch is applied, so the fader catches up rather than silently diverging.

This interacts with last-write-wins (§10.6) in the expected way: a fader held on the surface wins against a tablet moving the same control, because the surface is transmitting continuously.

Scene execution is the exception. A running scene is authoritative (§10.6), so a scene writing to a target whose strip is currently touched still applies to the device — the operator's hand simply stops the motor from following until released.

#Access and hirers

The surface is operator-tier by definition. It is a physical object with no PIN, so the permission model in §6 cannot reach it.

The consequence must be handled procedurally, exactly as for the LKV422's IR remote (§7.5): while hirer access is enabled, the surface gives whoever is standing at it unrestricted control of everything in the current bank, with no ceilings.

Two options, chosen per venue and configured in Admin → Control Surface:

  1. Disable while hirer access is enabled (default). The session is torn down, faders park at zero, LCDs read Surface disabled during hire. It re-enables automatically when hirer access is turned off.
  2. Leave enabled. Appropriate only where the surface is physically behind the operator position and hires are staffed throughout.

The choice is recorded in the audit log and shown on the hirer access screen (§21.20), so it is a visible decision rather than a default nobody examined.

#Startup, health and absence

The system must run normally without the surface. It is not in the boot dependency chain, its absence is not an error, and no scene, trigger or control path may depend on it.

  • On startup the client attempts the RTP-MIDI session in the background. Failure is logged and the surface is marked not connected.
  • Once connected, it loads Bank 0, pushes all eight fader positions from current state, writes scribble strip text and colour, and sets button LEDs.
  • Reconnection resynchronises rather than replaying. On session recovery the surface is re-driven from current state; nothing that happened during the outage is re-sent. Same principle as §10.7.
  • Health is the RTP-MIDI session state plus clock-sync liveness, polled on the same 30-second cycle as other devices.
#Performance targets
Path p50 p99
Surface fader movement to CQ-20B NRPN write 40 ms 120 ms
State change to motor update 50 ms 150 ms
RTP-MIDI clock synchronisation jitter — 5 ms

Two rate caps, both necessary rather than cosmetic:

  • Motor updates: 20 Hz per strip. Driving a motor faster than it can physically respond produces audible chatter and no visible benefit.
  • Meter updates: 15 per second per strip. Faster than the LEDs resolve and pure overhead on a link that also carries fader positions.
  • Scribble strip writes: 4 per second per strip. An unthrottled dB readout during a fade would flood the session with LCD sysex — each write is a full sysex message, and eight strips at frame rate would saturate the link that also carries fader positions.

Status appears in the admin health screen and device list. It does not appear in the operator status bar (§21.7) — that bar is reserved for devices whose loss affects the room, and losing the surface only means falling back to the tablet.

#8. Rules and Scenes

#8.1 The distinction

Two mechanisms, deliberately separate.

Rule Scene
Shape A standing relationship — when X, then Y A one-shot sequence — do this list, in this order
Time Instantaneous Delays, ordering, duration
Span One action Many actions across many domains
Outcome Fired or didn't Per-action results
Logged Event rules only Every execution

Rules say when. Scenes say what. A rule that needs steps, delays or ordering runs a scene — which is why the rules engine never grows sequencing, timelines or partial-execution semantics. Scenes already have all of that, tested and logged.

Earlier revisions had only scenes, which left the daily control path — four KNX buttons switching four lighting banks — with nowhere to live. It was specified inside the KNX chapter because there was no layer for it. There is now.

#8.2 Rule shapes

Three, and only three.

A — Event. Something happens; do something.

WHEN  knx 1/2/1 = 1                THEN  projector power on
WHEN  schedule 23:00 daily         THEN  run scene "All Off"
WHEN  mixer offline for 60 s       THEN  notify

B — Binding. A KNX boolean address bound to controllable state, handling both values in one rule.

WHEN  knx 1/0/1                    THEN  lighting group "Row 1" → 100% / 0%

The four stage banks are shape B. Forward is an action; the reverse — the panel's indicator — is shape C.

The panel's all button is four bindings, not one. At this installation 4/0/8 is bound to each of the four row groups ("Stage all → row N"), not to a "Stage all" group: the whole-stage group is indicator-only (§9.4), so it cannot be bound, and the controller behaves as a lighting desk whose rows are the controls (B71). Four bindings on one address fire together, as all matching rules do (§8.7); the operator interface shows them as one button (§21.11).

The telegram's value selects which level applies. A binding requires match_type = 'any' on a 1-bit address: value 1 sets on_level, value 0 sets off_level, both over fade_ms. One rule handles both directions, which is the point of the shape — two rules matching 1 and 0 separately would work but can be edited out of step with each other.

A CHECK enforces the pairing (§8.9), so a binding cannot be configured against a non-boolean address or a narrower match type.

C — Derived status. An outgoing address that continuously reflects state, recomputed whenever that state changes.

STATUS  knx 1/0/11  =  every fixture in "Row 1" at 100%, as the room sees it
STATUS  knx 1/2/2   =  projector state is ON
STATUS  knx 1/0/9   =  external control active

Shape C is not edge-triggered, and that matters. A pair of on/off event rules would leave the panel wrong until the next transition if a telegram were lost on the bus — possibly for hours. A derived rule recomputes on every state change and writes when the value differs from what it last wrote, so it self-corrects at the next opportunity. It is also one rule per address rather than two that can be edited out of step with each other.

#8.3 Trigger sources

A closed set of four, extensible in code rather than by configuration.

An api source was considered and dropped. Direct scene invocation from a button is not a standing rule and should not need one — POST /scenes/{id}/trigger (§16.5) exists for that, and POST /rules/{id}/fire exists for firing a rule explicitly. All four sources below are genuine external events.

Source Fires on
knx A telegram on a registered incoming address, subject to matching (§8.4)
schedule A cron expression, evaluated in Pacific/Auckland
surface A control surface button assigned the rule target (§7.6)
device_state A driver reporting a state transition — offline, online, projector ON, external control cleared. For the projector both vocabularies apply: its connection status and its own operational state (§7.4), so warming or on matches as well as connected

device_state is what makes the projector work without a polling rule. The driver already polls at protocol-appropriate intervals — §7.4 changes rate with power state, which a generic loop could not express — and emits a discrete event on transition (§5.6). Rules subscribe to that event rather than polling themselves.

There are no loop or timer rules. Polling belongs to drivers, where the interval can be protocol-specific and the device is protected from being hammered. A rule that could poll would migrate every driver's state handling into user-editable configuration.

device_state triggers may specify a sustained duration — offline for 60 s — so a brief reconnection does not fire an alert.

#8.4 Matching and debounce

For knx triggers:

match_type Behaviour
any Any value; the telegram value passes to actions declaring trigger_value
equal Value equals match_value
not_equal Value differs
gte / lte Numeric types only
range match_value ≤ value ≤ match_value_max, numeric only

For 1-bit types only any, equal and not_equal are valid; the admin interface disables the rest based on the address's registered DPT.

Debounce applies to knx triggers only, default 500 ms, configurable per rule. It is null for other trigger types — a scheduled rule cannot fire twice in 500 ms, and device_state has trigger_for_ms for its equivalent concern (§8.3).

It is a rule-layer property rather than a scene one. KNX touch panels commonly send repeated telegrams — a double press, a bus retry, or panels that transmit on both press and release. It applies to every rule shape, not only those that run scenes, which is the defect that arose from bindings being specified outside this layer.

#8.5 Guards

A rule may carry one optional guard, from a fixed list:

time_window        between 08:00 and 18:00
external_control   only when external control is / is not active
device_state       only when a named device is in a given state

Not a boolean tree. Allowing and/or composition would require an expression language, an interface for building it, and a debugger for when it misfires. One guard covers the realistic cases; anything more complex is a scene with its own logic.

#8.6 Derived status

Shape C rules are evaluated on state change, not on a timer, and each binds one outgoing address to one predicate.

lighting_group_all_at   every member channel at a given level, by basis
device_state            a named device in a given state
external_control        external control active

basis (on derived_status, §15.8) chooses what lighting_group_all_at compares. level, the default, compares each member's stored level, as first specified. output compares what the room sees: each member's output as last actually sent in a frame — its clamped level × the master (§7.2.3), including where an operator glide has reached — so with the master at 0 every stage row reads off whatever its faders say. A KNX house dimmer is not scaled by the master, so its output is its level. The stage panel's five indicators all use output (B72). A member whose range cannot reach the compared level is compared with the nearest level it can hold, and a group with no members is never "all at" anything.

Every panel indicator is on only when every member is at 100% — rows and all alike. "Any fixture in the row on" was considered and rejected by the owner (B72): the indicator claims what the button does, and the button sets every member to 100%.

device_state on the projector matches the projector's own operational state (§7.4) as well as its connection status; the two vocabularies are separate and either satisfies the status. on_or_warming is an alias for {warming, on}, so a panel's projector button stays lit through warm-up rather than looking dead for a minute; plain on stays exact. The installation's projector indicator is device_state, the projector, on_or_warming.

The level-store subscription is the important part. Status is recomputed when anything changes the underlying state, not only when the rule that set it fired. Someone dragging Row 2's fixtures down in the web interface turns the panel indicator red, because the button is claiming "on" and it is not.

That means bindings hold no state of their own — status is derived, never stored and updated, which removes a class of drift where the panel's belief and the room diverge.

#Re-assert after a panel press

The one exception to writing only on change. A KNX wall panel flips its own icon the moment a button is pressed, before anything has happened. If the press then does not take effect — the projector is warming or cooling (B52), unreachable, a guard blocks the rule, the telegram is debounced — no value changes, nothing would be written, and the panel would go on showing a state the room is not in.

So after a telegram on an enabled rule's trigger address, once the rule layer and whatever the telegram started have finished, every enabled status with an address is re-sent at its current value ("re-assert", B51). It is coalesced 250 ms after the last completion, the wait for completion is capped at 10 s, and a status already written since the press is skipped, since that write reached the panel after the icon flipped. The re-assert rides the ordinary recompute, so every timing rule above still holds — a status waiting for its frame is written when the frame has gone. It changes no value, so it cannot look like a state change to the rule layer (§8.7). On the rig a refused press is corrected within about a second.

#Overlapping groups resolve naturally

The auditorium has four bank buttons and an All button whose status group contains every stage fixture (an indicator-only group, §9.4). Press All On, then turn Bank 2 off: Bank 2's status goes 0, and All's status goes 0 because eleven of fifteen fixtures are at level and not all of them.

One evaluation — is everything this button controls on? — applied to every binding after any change. No mixed state to represent, and a boolean that is honest about what the button claims.

#Coalescing

Statuses are recomputed on the next tick rather than per level change, so a fade emits at most one status telegram rather than one per step. Only the transition matters, not the journey. This also keeps a five-status All press well inside the 15 telegrams/second budget (§7.1).

#8.7 Constraints

Four, and they are what keep this from becoming unexplainable.

No chaining. A rule's actions never trigger another rule. Not bounded depth, not loop detection — a flat prohibition. If "A then B" is genuinely needed, that is a scene.

This matters most for shape C: a status write must never look like a state change to the rule layer, or every binding feeds itself.

One guard, not a tree (§8.5).

All matching rules run, in sort_order, deterministically. No priority resolution and no first-match-wins.

Triggers come from a closed set (§8.3). A new trigger source is a code change, not a configuration one.

#8.8 The auditorium's rules

What this installation actually needs, as an illustration of the whole model:

—— stage lighting, the daily path (addresses as installed) ————
WHEN    knx 4/0/0        THEN   group "Stage row 1" → 100% / 0%
WHEN    knx 4/0/1        THEN   group "Stage row 2" → 100% / 0%
WHEN    knx 4/0/2        THEN   group "Stage row 3" → 100% / 0%
WHEN    knx 4/0/3        THEN   group "Stage row 4" → 100% / 0%
WHEN    knx 4/0/8        THEN   group "Stage row 1" → 100% / 0%   ┐ the all-button:
WHEN    knx 4/0/8        THEN   group "Stage row 2" → 100% / 0%   │ four bindings,
WHEN    knx 4/0/8        THEN   group "Stage row 3" → 100% / 0%   │ one per row
WHEN    knx 4/0/8        THEN   group "Stage row 4" → 100% / 0%   ┘ (§8.2)

STATUS  knx 4/0/4        =      every fixture in "Stage row 1" at 100%  basis output
STATUS  knx 4/0/5        =      every fixture in "Stage row 2" at 100%  basis output
STATUS  knx 4/0/6        =      every fixture in "Stage row 3" at 100%  basis output
STATUS  knx 4/0/7        =      every fixture in "Stage row 4" at 100%  basis output
STATUS  knx 4/0/9        =      every fixture in "Stage all" at 100%    basis output
                                ("Stage all" is indicator-only, §9.4)

—— projector —————————————————————————————————————————————————
WHEN    knx 3/0/0 = 1    THEN   run scene "Projector on"
WHEN    knx 3/0/0 = 0    THEN   run scene "Projector off"
STATUS  knx 3/0/1        =      projector on_or_warming
STATUS  (reserved)       =      projector is WARMING or COOLING

—— alarm ————————————————————————————————————————————————————
WHEN    knx 0/5/0 = 1    THEN   run scene "All Off"    → scene is critical
WHEN    knx 0/5/0 = 0    THEN   run scene "Morning Reset"

—— housekeeping —————————————————————————————————————————————
WHEN    schedule 23:00   THEN   run scene "All Off"
STATUS  knx 1/0/9        =      external control active

A binding's recall is a group-level write — the same as moving the group's fader: every member's level is set to on_level or off_level. Groups set levels and do not multiply (§9.4, B73), so there is nothing left to force: a group fader left low from an evening show set its members' levels, and the morning's recall sets them again. Earlier revisions forced the group's multiplier to 1.0 on recall; with no multiplier, that rule is gone.

The master dimmer applies normally and needs no exemption: it is not persisted and resets to 100% at boot (§12.3), so the only way it is low is that someone present set it this session. Because the stage indicators use the output basis (§8.6), a low master turns them off — the panel says what the room shows. Under the old multiplier model a stored-level status could report 1 while the room sat dimmed, because it compared the level store and the multiplier attenuated after it; that reasoning does not apply to an output-basis status, which compares what was actually sent.

The alarm addresses below are placeholders until the installation's alarm telegrams are captured (§7.1 Alarm integration).

#The projector needs three addresses, not two

DPT 1.001 is boolean; the projector has six states, two of which last 60–90 seconds. Writing 0 until fully ON leaves the button apparently dead for a minute and a half. Writing 1 optimistically lies, and lies persistently if warm-up fails.

A second address carrying "changing state" is the only option that neither lies nor looks broken. Both are DPT 1.001, so no new datatype is needed — it is one more reserved address from the integrator, which is on the §17.4 checklist alongside the heartbeat and external-control addresses.

If the panels can render a multi-state indicator, a single DPT 20.x or 5.010 enum carrying the full state is better. Confirm with the integrator rather than assuming.

#Suppression during external control

lighting_group rules are suppressed while external control is active (§7.2.7), and every derived status reflecting stage lighting is written 0.

run_scene rules are not suppressed. They fire normally, and the scene skips its own DMX actions with ⊘ external_control per §8.15.

The distinction matters and the blanket version would have been a defect: the alarm scene combines DMX, mixer and projector actions, so suppressing the whole rule would silently drop the mutes and the projector-off whenever a visiting desk happened to be connected — a partial failure in the one scene that must work.

This is a rule-layer property, applied once here rather than half-described in §7.1 and §7.2.7 separately.

#8.9 Rule data model

Rule
  name, enabled, sort_order, notes

  trigger_type        knx | schedule | surface | device_state
  knx_address_id      → KNXGroupAddress          (knx)
  match_type          any | equal | not_equal | gte | lte | range
  match_value, match_value_max
  debounce_ms         default 500, knx triggers only
  cron                                            (schedule)
  trigger_device_id   → Device                    (device_state)
  trigger_state       offline | online | on | off | …
  trigger_for_ms      sustained duration, optional

  guard_type          time_window | external_control | device_state | null
  guard_value

  action_type         run_scene | lighting_group | notify
  scene_id            → Scene                     (run_scene)
  lighting_group_id   → LightingGroup             (lighting_group)
  on_level, off_level, fade_ms                    (lighting_group)
  message                                          (notify)

Three inline action types, then scenes for everything else. lighting_group exists because the daily path should not fill the scene list and the execution log with four one-action scenes; notify because an alert is not a scene.

notify writes to the same alert path as §11.4, using the rule's message as the body and the SMTP configuration already held there. It is subject to the same rate limiting, which matters: a flapping device with a device_state rule would otherwise generate mail every few seconds. Anything multi-step is run_scene, which inherits scene execution's ordering, failure semantics and logging.

scene_triggers and knx_group_bindings are gone — both were rules before this layer existed.

DerivedStatus
  name, enabled
  knx_address_id      → KNXGroupAddress, outgoing, DPT 1.001, optional, unique
  source_type         lighting_group_all_at | device_state | external_control
  lighting_group_id   → LightingGroup, compare_level      (lighting_group_all_at)
  device_id           → Device, compare_state             (device_state)
  basis               level | output, default level         (lighting_group_all_at)

compare_state for the projector may name its operational state (§7.4) as well as a connection status, and accepts the alias on_or_warming (§8.6). basis was added by migration 011 (§15.8).

UNIQUE on the address, when one is set: two rules writing one address would be non-deterministic.

The address is optional (migration 006). A derived status with no address is still evaluated and broadcast on every state change, like any other — a page button's lamp reads it exactly as a wall-panel indicator would — it is simply never written to the KNX bus. Requiring an address forced a status that exists only for a page button, such as "House at 100%", to claim one it had no physical indicator to drive.

#8.10 Execution and logging

Event rules are logged — trigger, guard result, action, outcome — to rule_execution_log, retained 90 days like the scene log.

Derived status rules are not logged. They fire on every state change and would flood it. A live monitor view instead, in the same shape as the KNX telegram monitor (§21.19), showing each derived address, its current value, and when it last changed.

#8.11 Scenes — data model

Scene
  name              "Performance Start"
  description
  enabled           bool
  icon              optional icon name
  priority          normal | critical
  protected         bool     Cannot be deleted — used for Restore Venue Default
  visible_operator  bool
  sort_order

SceneAction
  scene_id          → Scene
  sort_order        int      Display order within a delay group
  delay_ms          int      Delay from scene start; equal values fire together
  domain            knx | dmx | mixer_recall | mixer_fader | mixer_mute |
                    projector_power | projector_input | hdmi_source
  [per-domain fields — see below]

#8.12 Scene action domains

Eight domains, reduced from ten. dmx_fade was indistinguishable from dmx with a fade duration, and knx_dimmer was indistinguishable from a KNX write with a level. Collapsing them removes two code paths and two picker tiles with no loss of capability.

Domain Fields Notes
knx knx_address_id, knx_value, knx_source, knx_scale Covers both plain writes and dimmer levels
dmx dmx_snapshot (JSON), dmx_fade_ms A snapshot with a non-zero fade is a fade
mixer_recall mixer_scene_id Recalls a CQ internal scene
mixer_fader mixer_channel_id, mixer_db dB, not a wire value (§5.5). Works for inputs, outputs and Main
mixer_mute mixer_channel_id, mixer_muted Absolute, never toggle
projector_power projector_power (on / off) Renamed from pjlink_power
projector_input projector_input Renamed from pjlink_input
hdmi_source hdmi_destination, hdmi_input_id One destination, one call (§5.5)

Domains name what is being controlled, not how. pjlink_power put a protocol name into the vocabulary every scene is written in — changing to a projector that does not speak PJLink would have meant rewriting every saved scene. Every action also carries an optional device_id; null means the only device in that category (§5.5).
