Actions are gated by driver capability. An action whose driver reports it unsupported is skipped at execution and logged ⊘ unsupported, and the scene editor disables the action type with an explanation rather than hiding it (§5.5).

The scene editor may still label a DMX action with a non-zero fade as "DMX fade" in the timeline where that reads more naturally — it is a presentation choice, not a separate domain.

DMX snapshot format, keyed by lighting channel ID as a string, with levels on the 0–100 scale (§7.2.3) and colour components 0–255:

{
  "domain": "dmx",
  "dmx_fade_ms": 2000,
  "dmx_snapshot": {
    "1": { "level": 100.0 },
    "2": { "level": 78.5, "r": 255, "g": 120, "b": 0 },
    "3": { "level": 70.6, "r": 255, "g": 180, "b": 0, "w": 60 }
  }
}

#8.13 Grouping and sequencing

Actions sharing a delay_ms execute together as a group. Within a group, sort_order controls display order in the editor only; simultaneous actions have no meaningful execution order. The engine groups by delay and fires each group after that delay from scene start.

t=0 ms       DMX fade, channels 1–8 → 80%, over 2000 ms
t=0 ms       PJLink power on
t=0 ms       Mixer recall "Lecture Baseline"
t=1000 ms    Unmute Out 1/2, stage monitors (after the recall has settled)
t=2000 ms    KNX write, house lights off (once the stage fade has finished)
t=60000 ms   PJLink set input 1 (after the projector has warmed)

"After the projector has warmed" means after its minimum warm-up hold (§7.4): until min_warmup_s has passed since the power-on, the projector is shown as warming and an input change is refused. An input step's delay must therefore be at least the projector's min_warmup_s (60 s by default) after the power-on step.

An adjustment that follows a recall goes in a later group. A mixer recall replaces every level and mute the desk scene holds, so an adjustment in the same group may land before the recall and be undone by it. Recall first, then adjust.

#8.14 Priority and mutual exclusion

Normal scenes execute independently and may run concurrently. Per-channel conflicts resolve in the domain handlers — a new fade cancels an in-progress fade on the same channel.

Critical scenes cancel all in-progress scenes before executing. Running fades are interrupted at their current value, pending actions are discarded, and the critical scene takes over immediately. Critical scenes also disable external control (§7.2.7) before their DMX actions, because the alarm must be able to black out the rig regardless of what is patched in.

The alarm-armed scene must be marked critical.

#8.15 Failure policy

Execution does not abort on a failed action. Everything that can execute does. Failures are logged with their reason. A scene that partially executes — the projector was unreachable but the lights came up — is better than one that does nothing.

✓ sent               Dispatched (fire-and-forget domains)
⊘ unsupported        The configured driver does not support this action (§5.5)
✓ confirmed          Confirmed by device response (PJLink)
✗ failed             Unreachable or rejected
⊘ skipped            Domain not configured or disabled
⊘ external_control   DMX action skipped — external control active

#8.16 Scene execution log

SceneExecutionLog
  scene_id
  triggered_by      "knx:1/0/1" | "api:admin" | "api:operator" |
                    "hirer:10.2.30.112" | "schedule"
  started_at
  completed_at
  result            success | partial | failed
  action_results    JSON array of per-action outcomes

result is success when every action is ✓; failed when at least one action is ✗ and none is ✓; and partial otherwise — including a run in which every action was ⊘, skipped by design, such as a lighting-only scene while a visiting desk holds the rig. Red on a scene card therefore always means a device needs looking at.

Queryable from the admin interface. Essential for diagnosing what happened during a hire. Retained 90 days.

#9. Lighting Control

#9.1 Model

DMX fixtures and KNX dimmers are managed through one unified model. The same surface controls both, with the same fade control and scene integration.

LightingChannel
  name                    "House Lights — Centre"
  type                    dmx | knx_dimmer

  # DMX
  profile_id              → FixtureProfile — what each channel does
  device_id               → the lighting output driver instance
  universe                int
  address                 int — start address

  # KNX
  knx_command_address_id  → KNXGroupAddress   DPT 5.001, application writes
  knx_status_address_id   → KNXGroupAddress   DPT 5.001, dimmer writes back
  knx_switch_address_id   → KNXGroupAddress   DPT 1.001, optional
  fade_mode               hardware | hardware_timed | software

  # Stage plan placement
  bar_id                  → LightingBar
  position                real, 0.0 (stage right) to 1.0 (stage left)

  # Colour state, RGB and RGBW only
  colour_r / g / b / w    int 0–255

  visible_staff           bool
  notes

Channel occupancy comes from the fixture's profile (§15.9), not from a hard-coded type. A profile declares its channel count and the role of each channel, so a one-channel dimmer, a four-channel RGBW and a twelve-channel moving head are all rows rather than code paths.

The compositor drives whatever roles a profile declares. dimmer scales; colour roles receive scaled components; pan, tilt, strobe and macro are passed through unscaled from their stored values.

The admin UI validates that no two fixtures overlap and warns inline. The warning does not block saving — during commissioning it is often useful to patch ahead of a rewire.

Colour. A master level scales all colour components proportionally. The picker presents HSV but stores and transmits RGB, matching DMX reality with no conversion layer in the protocol path.

#9.2 Value scale

Stated once, and enforced everywhere:

All lighting levels are 0–100 with one decimal place — in the level store, over the API, over the WebSocket, in scene snapshots, and in the UI. Conversion to DMX 0–255 or KNX 0–100 happens only inside the protocol clients, at the boundary.

Fixture colour components are the single exception at 0–255, because they are natively DMX values with no meaningful percentage interpretation.

max_value on a channel is a 0–100 ceiling regardless of type. One decimal gives 1,000 steps, comfortably finer than DMX's 256.

#9.3 Stage plan model

The stage plan is a first-class feature and needs a data model of its own.

LightingBar
  name         "Bar 3" | "Proscenium"
  sort_order   int — 0 is downstage (proscenium), ascending upstage
  notes

Fixtures are assigned to a bar and positioned along it with position, a value between 0.0 and 1.0.

Orientation convention, from the audience's perspective: the proscenium is at the bottom of the plan, upstage at the top; position 0.0 is stage right (audience left) and 1.0 is stage left (audience right). Stating this prevents the plan being rendered mirrored, which is easy to do and confusing to debug.

position is cosmetic. It affects where the fixture appears on the plan and nothing else — DMX addressing is entirely independent.

#9.4 Groups

LightingGroup
  name            "Row 1 — Front of Stage"
  colour          "#E8A020"     UI colour coding
  sort_order
  indicator_only  false          true: membership only — no fader, never bound

LightingGroupMembership
  group_id  → LightingGroup
  channel_id → LightingChannel
  sort_order

A channel may belong to several groups.

A group fader sets its members' levels (0–100, as every level, §9.2), as on a traditional lighting desk (B73). Moving it writes every member's level to the fader's value, each clamped to its own range; fixture faders then trim members individually, and the next group move sets them again. A channel in several groups takes whatever the last group fader or binding wrote. Output is level × master for a stage fixture, and the level alone for a KNX house dimmer (§7.2.3, §9.5). Members a critical scene holds are left alone and reported (§16.5).

A group fader therefore has no value of its own: it shows its members' highest level, marked "mixed" when they differ (§21.11).

Superseded: earlier revisions made the group fader a multiplier (0.0–1.0) over its members' levels, with the highest multiplier applying to a fixture in several groups, a ghost mark for the group's effect, and a hint when one group dominated another. On the rig that left the row faders dead whenever "Stage all" sat at or above them, and a binding's forced 1.0 did so after every wall-panel use. All of it was removed on 30 September 2026 (B73).

Indicator-only groups (indicator_only, migration 011). An indicator-only group exists for its membership: derived statuses (§8.6), scenes and membership still use it, but it has no fader in any view, cannot be the target of a binding (§8.2) or of a page's group master (§15.12), and a group-level write to it is refused with 422. At this installation "Stage all" is indicator-only — it lights the panel's all indicator — and the Lighting view's Master is the whole-stage fader (B71).

Individual faders display the individual level, not the composited output. This is how lighting desks work and it avoids confusion when the master is below full.

A ghost mark shows where the channel actually lands. A faint secondary mark on a fixture fader's track sits at the composited output, with the effective percentage beside the readout:

   85%  →55%      set to 85%, landing at 55% under a master at 65%

Showing only the set level hides what the rig is doing; showing only the composited result would move every member fader whenever the master moved, which is wrong on a desk. The thumb is what was set, the ghost is what the fixture is doing, and neither number misleads.

Persistence. Individual levels and colour are persisted, so a reboot during a rig session does not lose the work. Groups hold no value to persist (§12.3). The master resets to 100% on restart, which is the safe default.

A binding's recall is a group-level write (§8.8). A bank switched on from the wall panel comes up at exactly its on_level (× the master), whatever the group fader was left at, because the recall sets the levels the fader set. The daily path stays independent of the occasional one with nothing forced.

#9.5 Master dimmer

A single 0–100% multiplier scaling stage (DMX) output without altering stored levels. Applied in the DMX pass only (§7.2.3). KNX house dimmers are outside it: they follow their own fader, the wall panel and scenes one to one, as house lighting sits outside a desk's grand master. The master therefore cannot take the house lights down on its own, and moving it sends nothing to the KNX bus. Not persisted; resets to 100%.

One exemption: channels with min_value > 0 are exempt entirely, because a floor the master can scale below is not a floor (§7.2.3).

A bank recalled by a binding rule is not exempt. The rule sets its members' levels, and the master applies as usual (§8.8). With groups no longer multiplying (§9.4), the master is the only scaling in the DMX pass, and at this installation it is the whole-stage fader.

This control can take the stage to darkness. That is acceptable only because emergency lighting is a separate battery-backed system independent of KNX (§7.1). If any circuit under this system's control is ever brought into an egress calculation, a minimum floor must be imposed on it that the master cannot scale below.

#9.6 KNX dimmer status sync

The application subscribes to every KNX dimmer status address. When a physical touch panel adjusts a dimmer, the dimmer reports its new level, and the application writes that into the level store and broadcasts it — so the on-screen fader follows the wall panel.

The reported level is also recorded as the value the dimmer already holds, so nothing is sent back. Neither group faders nor the master scale a house dimmer (§9.5), so there is no scaled value to send; and re-sending a level while the panel's own fade is running would stop that fade where it was.

#9.7 Snapshots

A Save Look action captures current fixture state as a scene action (§8.3). The fade engine resolves each channel and interpolates to the captured values.

Snapshots reference lighting channel IDs inside JSON, which no foreign key can protect. Deleting a fixture therefore checks every dmx_snapshot for references and, consistent with the delete-protection pattern used elsewhere, blocks the delete and lists the scenes involved. A nightly integrity check reports any orphaned keys that slip through.

#10. Web Interface Behaviour

Visual and screen-by-screen detail is in §21. This section covers behaviour that is not specific to any one screen.

#10.1 Technology

React 19, TypeScript, built with Vite, served as static files by nginx. A persistent WebSocket carries live state.

Browser support: Safari 16+ (iOS and macOS), Chrome 90+, Firefox 100+, Edge 90+. The application uses pointer events, service workers, CSS custom properties, scroll-snap, env(safe-area-inset-*) and WebSocket — all well supported in those versions. iPad Safari is explicitly tested; it is the most likely tablet in a school.

#10.2 Progressive web app

Two manifests at different paths, giving staff and hirers separate installed identities:

  • manifest.staff.json — start URL /app
  • manifest.hirer.json — start URL /hire

Both install to the home screen on iOS and Android. See §6.16 for the self-signed certificate limitation on iOS.

#10.3 Touch faders

Built on the Pointer Events API — not mouse or touch events — and written for this application rather than taken from a library: sub-pixel tracking, genuine multi-touch so two faders can move at once, and the gradient fill, detents and ghost mark of §21.3 and §9.4 drawn from the design tokens. Earlier revisions specified Nexus UI. It was dropped: it has not been released since 2022, ships no TypeScript types or React integration, and renders its own markup, which cannot use the tokens. The hard parts — gesture arbitration, the pending overlay and detents (§21.2) — have to be built either way.

#10.4 First-run wizard

The only bootstrap path. The system must be fully configurable from here with no command line.

Detection. First-run state is the absence of first_run_completed = true in system_state. While in that state, every route redirects to /setup.

Resumability. Each step writes its result and marks itself complete. An abandoned setup resumes at the next incomplete step; completed steps show a summary with an edit option.

Steps:

  1. Welcome — confirms locale and Pacific/Auckland timezone, inherited from the OS and shown for confirmation.
  2. Admin password — minimum 12 characters, entered twice. Also seeds the operator account with a temporary password, changed at step 5.
  3. Network — shows the current address and offers to change it. Changing it hands over to the reconnection screen (§10.8). DNS and reservation guidance is shown but not enforced; the step can be skipped if the network is already correct.
  4. Devices — per device: address, port, any device-specific authentication, and a test connection button. Devices may be skipped and configured later. The wizard does not require anything to be online, though unreachable KNX is warned about.
  5. Operator password — set separately from the admin's.
  6. Certificate — two options side by side: issue via Let's Encrypt using Cloudflare DNS-01, prompting for the API token and subdomain and showing live progress; or generate a self-signed certificate immediately. The self-signed path shows the iOS trust guidance from §6.16. A valid self-signed certificate already installed at first start (naming the hostname and addresses, 30 or more days left) is reused, not reissued: the admin's browser has just accepted it, and replacing it would break the wizard's own session. Whenever a step does replace the served certificate, its response says so (certificate_replaced, certificate_names) and the client asks for a reload before the next action.
  7. Summary and commit — full review, then finish. Commit sets first_run_completed = true and redirects to /app.

Abort safety. Closing the browser mid-setup writes nothing irreversible. Passwords already set are kept, but the system stays in first-run mode until step 7 commits.

Re-running requires a database reset. Individual steps are revisited through their normal admin locations.

#10.5 Status bar

Persistent across every view, one indicator per device:

● KNX   ● DMX   ⚠ Mixer   ● Projector   ○ HDMI

The bar carries devices whose loss affects the room. Two monitored things are deliberately excluded: backup media (§4.5) and the control surface (§7.6). Losing either degrades maintenance or convenience rather than operation, and both appear in the admin health screen instead. §11.1 lists everything monitored; this bar is the operator-facing subset.

Green is connected and healthy; amber is connected but degraded or showing last-known values; red is unreachable or in error; grey is not configured. Colour is never the only signal — see §21.7.

Staff tap an indicator for a detail panel showing last seen, host and port, latency, reconnection count, and last error. Hirers see indicators only, with no interaction.

Backup media status does not appear here (§4.5); it belongs in the admin health and backup screens.

#10.6 Multi-client writes

When several clients are open at once, conflicting writes resolve last-write-wins at the server.

  • Clients apply optimistic updates immediately on local interaction
  • The server processes writes in arrival order; a later write supersedes an earlier one
  • The server broadcasts authoritative state in the next batched frame, and all clients reconcile within roughly 100 ms
  • If two clients hold the same fader, position reflects whichever packet arrived last; the other client sees its optimistic update replaced
  • Scene execution has explicit precedence rules, not merely frequency. Two tiers:
Scene priority An operator write to a channel the scene is driving
normal The operator wins. The scene's fade on that channel is cancelled at its current value; the scene continues on every other channel
critical Rejected. Channels are locked for the duration of execution, with the visible feedback of §21.27

The normal-priority rule is what the fade engine already implements — §7.2.6 cancels an in-progress fade when a new one starts on the same channel, and a direct set is a zero-duration fade. It is also what a lighting operator expects: grabbing a fader takes that channel, on any desk.

Earlier revisions claimed scenes won by last-write-wins. That was both wrong about the implementation and wrong as a design — a fade writing at 50 Hz beats a human by frequency rather than by rule, so the outcome depended on timing rather than intent.

The critical-priority lock exists because the alarm blackout must not be defeatable by someone leaning on a fader.

A channel currently driven by a running scene shows an indicator (§21.12), so taking it over is a deliberate act rather than an accident.

Two operators moving the same fader is an operational coordination problem, not a technical one. This behaviour belongs in the operator training material rather than the interface.

#10.7 WebSocket reconnection

When the connection drops — a network blip, a service restart, a device waking from sleep:

  1. The client reconnects with exponential backoff, starting at 1 second and capping at 30.
  2. During the outage the status bar shows a reconnection indicator rather than a blocking overlay. Existing state stays on screen, and all controls are disabled so no write is issued that would be lost.
  3. On reconnect the client sends {"type": "resync", "domains": [...]}.
  4. The server replies with a full snapshot per domain — the same shape as a normal batched broadcast, carrying every tracked value rather than only changes.
  5. Nothing is replayed. Discrete events that fired during the outage — scene completions, mute toggles — are not re-sent, and no queued local writes are replayed on reconnect. The client sees the resulting state through the snapshot.
  6. Normal batched broadcasts resume.

The no-replay rule is deliberate. Replaying a queued mute-off after a 90-second outage could unmute a live microphone based on an intent formed a minute and a half earlier, in a room whose state has since moved on. Event history remains available in the server-side logs.

If reconnection fails for more than 5 minutes, the client shows a full-screen connection-lost overlay with a manual retry — catching the case where the server has moved address or the tab has been backgrounded for a long time.

#10.8 Network change reconnection

When the controller's address is changed from the admin interface, the flow cannot simply poll the new address from the page the admin is already on: that page is served over HTTPS, and polling an HTTP health endpoint from it is mixed content every browser blocks. Nor can it redirect straight to the new hostname, which needs DNS already updated. Instead:

  1. POST /system/network validates the request, answers 202 with a confirm_token, and asks the helper to apply the change.
  2. The browser is sent to http://{old_address}/reconnect — a static page served from the root image on port 80, not the application. It polls http://{new_address}/health every 3 seconds; CORS is allowed on /health only, so this is the one cross-origin request the appliance permits.
  3. When a Cloudflare token is configured, the appliance updates its own DNS A record before applying, so the new address is already resolvable. Without a token the page instead redirects to https://{new_address} and says DNS needs updating manually.
  4. Confirm or revert. The new address is not final until an authenticated request reaches it: POST /system/network/confirm. If none arrives within 3 minutes, the helper restores the previous network settings. A headless appliance must never be strandable behind a typo'd address.

#11. Health Monitoring

#11.1 Per-device strategy

Device-specific detail is with each integration in §7. In summary:

Device Primary indicator Interval
KNX Application-to-knxd socket state; optional bus heartbeat Continuous / configurable
DMX — USB fallback Serial device node presence 30 s
DMX — Art-Net ArtPoll / ArtPollReply round trip 30 s
DMX — sACN ICMP echo (reachability only) 30 s
CQ-20B TCP connection state; refused and timed-out distinguished Continuous
Projector %1POWR ? 30 s on, 5 min off
HDMI matrix PAXXR status query over serial 25 s
Control surface (optional) RTP-MIDI session and clock-sync liveness 30 s
Backup media Mount presence 60 s

#11.2 System vitals

Read through the platform layer (§5.4), never directly from platform-specific paths.

Metric Amber Red
CPU temperature > 70 °C > 80 °C
Memory used > 80% > 90%
SSD life used > 80% > 95%
SSD temperature > 60 °C > 70 °C
SSD media errors > 0 — (email immediately)
/data free space < 500 MB < 100 MB
/data used > 80% > 90%
Event loop lag, p99 over 5 min > 100 ms > 500 ms
Event bus drop count, continuous > 0 in 5 min sustained

The two /data rows serve different purposes and are not competing thresholds, which an earlier revision left ambiguous:

  • Absolute free space triggers auto-pruning and write suspension. These are the operational thresholds.
  • Percentage used drives the colour of the "used space" row in the health screen. On a 64 GB partition holding a few gigabytes it will effectively never fire, which is correct — it exists to catch runaway growth.

#11.3 Auto-pruning

Below 500 MB free on /data, the system prunes automatically. Pruning is chunked (§15.1) — it runs when the system is already under stress, so it must not hold the write lock long enough to stall control traffic.

  1. Application logs older than 30 days, against the normal 90
  2. Scene execution log entries older than 30 days
  3. Security events older than 30 days
  4. Pre-change snapshots beyond the most recent 5, against the normal 10

Still below 100 MB after pruning, non-essential writes are suspended — execution logging, system logging — to keep the database available. A persistent red banner appears.

Backup archives on /srv/local are pruned by their own retention policy (§13.3) and are not touched by this mechanism; they are on a different partition.

#11.4 Email alerts

Sent when:

  • A device goes from green to red, after 60 seconds — avoids a flurry on a brief hiccup
  • A scene executes with failed actions during a hire session
  • SSD media errors are detected
  • Free space on /data is critically low
  • A backup fails twice consecutively
  • Backup integrity verification fails (a copy was read and found corrupt: backup_untrusted)
  • The archive the monthly check chose is held by no destination at all (backup_missing, email only — no banner, since nothing is known to be corrupt; §13.4)
  • More than 10 failed login attempts arrive from one address within an hour
  • An update fails and is rolled back automatically
  • Both backup destinations are unavailable

Reconnection does not generate email.

SMTP is configured in Admin → System → Email, with a test button. The last-known-good configuration is mirrored to /srv/appliance/smtp-fallback.toml so emergency mode can still send (§4.6).

#12. Startup and Shutdown

#12.1 Boot sequence

1.  Bootloader selects the active root slot (§14.4)
2.  OS ready
3.  /srv/appliance and /data mounted; /srv/local and /mnt/backup mounted with nofail
      └── /data unavailable → emergency mode (§4.6)
4.  auditorium-config-apply applies hostname, firewall, timezone
5.  knxd starts and connects to the gateway
6.  auditorium-core starts
      ├── Verify /data and /srv/appliance are mounted and writable
      ├── Run database migrations (§15.2)
      ├── Detect platform, initialise the platform layer
      ├── Wait for the knxd socket, 30 s timeout
      ├── Restore persisted state: levels, external control
      ├── DMX backend: connect and confirm
      ├── Restore lighting levels and colour
      ├── Evaluate all derived statuses; write those whose value differs
      ├── Start the Art-Net input listener; wait up to 5 s for booth frames
      ├── ── if no external desk detected and no manual flag ──
      │     └── Composite from the restored model, send one frame,
      │         start the change-driven renderer (§7.2.3)
      │   ── if external control is active ──
      │     └── Leave DMX output suspended; suppress bank bindings;
      │         write the external control status address
      ├── Start device connection tasks in parallel:
      │     ├── KNX: connect, read status addresses
      │     ├── PJLink: connect, query state
      │     ├── CQ-20B: connect, read configured channel state
      │     └── HDMI: connect, query routing
      ├── Start health polling
      ├── Start the WebSocket server
      └── Serve HTTP — interface available

The interface becomes available before every device has confirmed connectivity. Last-known state is shown, clearly marked unconfirmed, until each device reports in.

No unconditional blackout. Earlier revisions sent a blackout at every startup. With stage banks driven from KNX (§7.1), a watchdog reboot or power blip during an assembly would black out the stage and leave it dark until someone pressed the panel again.

Instead the controller restores its persisted model and composites from it. If the banks were off, the restored model is zeros and the result is a blackout anyway — the same safe default, but one that survives a restart during use.

Bank state is restored from system_state, not read from KNX. The status addresses are the controller's own output; nothing on the bus is obliged to answer a read request for them. §12.2's "read status addresses" applies to KNX dimmers, which do respond.

The first frame is sent after the lighting output driver confirms it is connected — for Art-Net, after the first successful ArtPollReply. A frame sent to an unconnected backend is silently discarded. If the backend cannot connect within 10 seconds, the failure is logged, DMX goes red, and the frame is retried on each reconnection.

#12.2 Per-domain boot behaviour

Domain On boot Why
DMX Restore persisted levels and composite — unless external control is active Survives a restart mid-assembly; an all-off model still yields a blackout
KNX Read status addresses, write nothing KNX devices hold their own state; discover, do not overwrite
CQ-20B Read state from the mixer The mixer's engine is authoritative for its own state
PJLink Query state, issue no command The projector may be mid warm-up or cool-down
HDMI Query routing with PAXXR Display what is actually set, including front-panel and IR changes

#12.3 State restored at boot

State Restored Notes
Lighting levels and colour Yes Composited and sent on startup
Group multipliers No longer exist Groups set levels (§9.4, B73), so there is nothing to restore. A group_multipliers row persisted by an older version is ignored
Derived statuses Not persisted Recomputed from the level store at startup (§8.6)
Master dimmer No — resets to 100% Safe default
External control — manual flag Yes Changes the boot sequence (§12.1)
External control — detected No Re-derived within 5 s from the booth input
Mixer state No — read from the CQ The mixer is authoritative
Projector, HDMI No — queried The device is authoritative

#12.4 Graceful shutdown

async def shutdown() -> None:
    # 1. Stop accepting new scene triggers
    # 2. Wait for any in-progress scene, 15 s timeout
    # 3. Flush pending state to SQLite (§5.6)
    # 4. Send DMX blackout — SKIPPED if external control is active
    #    Note: the node holds last state, so this is what actually darkens
    #    the rig on a deliberate shutdown
    # 5. Close all device connections cleanly

TimeoutStopSec=20 in the service unit.

Step 4's exception matters for the same reason as at boot: a controller restarting mid-hire must not black out a rig it is not driving.

The flushed state in step 3 is what §12.3 restores. It also serves as a diagnostic record after an unexpected restart.

#13. Backup and Recovery

#13.1 What is protected, and against what

Being precise here matters, because the single-SSD design (§2.3) makes the distinction load-bearing.

Copy Location Protects against Does not protect against
Local /srv/local on the SSD Filesystem corruption in /data, bad restores, accidental deletion Device failure
Backup USB /mnt/backup Device failure, controller failure Fire, theft, someone taking the stick
Network School file server Device failure, controller failure, loss of the rack School server outage

The USB stick and the network destination are the backups. /srv/local is a convenience tier. There are two real copies, not three.

#13.2 Contents

Item Path Included
Application database /data/auditorium.db Yes — via SQLite's online backup API
TLS certificate and key /data/certs/ Yes
Cloudflare API token /data/certs/cloudflare.ini No — deliberately excluded
Network configuration /data/config/system.json Yes
Bootstrap configuration /data/config/auditorium.toml Yes
Application code /data/app/ Yes — a change from earlier revisions
Venue baselines /data/config/baselines/ Yes
Device secret /srv/appliance/device-secret No — deliberately excluded
Root filesystem Slot A or B No — restored from a system image

Two entries deserve comment.

Application code is now included, because it lives on /data. Backups are slightly larger, and in exchange an SSD restore brings back a working application rather than requiring a reimage first.

The device secret is excluded on purpose (§6.10). Restoring /data to a replacement machine means re-entering device passwords, because the new machine has a different secret. Restoring on the same machine preserves them. This is on the recovery card.

#Why the archive is not encrypted

Encrypting the archive was considered and rejected.

This system is examined rarely and a restore may not be needed for years. Under those conditions the realistic failure is not an attacker reading the archive — it is a restore attempt in 2031 stalling because nobody can find the passphrase. An archive that cannot be decrypted is a worse outcome than one that is merely poorly guarded, because the first loses the data outright and the second requires someone to go looking for it on a file server they have no reason to visit.

The exposure is instead reduced by removing the one item whose consequences reach beyond the auditorium.

#Why the Cloudflare token is excluded

The Cloudflare API token can edit DNS for the school's entire zone. It is the only thing in the archive whose blast radius extends outside this installation, and it is also the easiest to replace — a new token is issued at Cloudflare in about two minutes.

Excluding it therefore costs almost nothing and removes the concern without introducing a key that can be lost. Restoring certificates to a machine requires the token to be re-entered or reissued, which is one field in Admin → System → Certificates. That step is on the recovery card and in the restore flow.

The token must still be scoped to a single zone with DNS edit permission only (§3.2).

The TLS private key stays in the archive. It secures a hostname resolving to a private address on a VLAN where §3.5 already concedes that anyone present can control every device directly. Complicating the restore to protect it would buy nothing.

#13.3 Nightly backup

Runs at 03:00 local time (Pacific/Auckland) via systemd timer — not "NZST", which is only correct for half the year.

The job bundles everything above into a timestamped .tar.gz using SQLite's online backup API, so it is safe while the application is running. Log tables are pruned to their retention limits first.

Destination Retention
/srv/local 14 days
Backup USB 7 days
Network — SMB/CIFS or SFTP 30 days

Retention works from the reconciled index (§13.4): each archive's per-destination presence flags are brought into line with what each reachable destination actually lists before anything is pruned, and a row held by no destination at all is dropped, as it is when retention deletes an archive's last copy.

A re-image recreates /srv/local empty (§13.6, §13.7). Only the USB and network copies survive one, which is why the reconcile exists: after the re-image of 28 September 2026 the index still claimed local copies, and the next monthly check reported a merely missing archive as corrupt.

Credentials for the network destination are encrypted at rest in /data/config/backup.json using the device secret. SFTP prefers key authentication, with the private key generated on the device and the public key given to the target server; password authentication is supported as a fallback. SMB uses an encrypted username and password. Stored passwords are never displayed after saving — only a change option.

#13.4 Failure handling and verification

Every attempt writes to system_state under domain='backup'.

  • Success — logged, last_run updated, any prior failure banner cleared
  • Failure — logged with detail, retried once after 10 minutes; if the retry also fails, an email alert and a persistent amber banner
  • Three consecutive nightly failures — banner escalates to red, daily email
  • Backup media absent — the local destination is skipped and logged, and this does not count toward the failure escalation (§4.5)
  • All destinations unavailable — red immediately, because nothing is being backed up

Every backup verifies after writing (B75). Each archive is checksummed with SHA-256 on creation, with a .sha256 sidecar beside every copy. Before it is distributed, the built archive passes the same integrity check the monthly timer runs (below). Each copy is then read back through its own destination and its SHA-256 compared with the archive built; a copy that does not read back identical is removed and that destination's write counts as failed. The result is recorded per archive — checked_at, and checked_destinations, the destinations whose copy read back identical (migration 012) — separately from the monthly check, because the two answer different questions: "was it written correctly?" at 03:00, and "is it still good?" weeks later on media that may have decayed. For local and USB the read-back may come from the page cache; it proves the filesystem holds the right bytes under the right name, and the monthly check is what catches later decay.

The index is reconciled with the destinations at every backup, every verification and the first start after a restore. For each destination that is reachable and can be listed, a row flagged present whose file is not there is cleared, a row not flagged whose file is there is set, and an archive file with no row is adopted only with a matching .sha256 sidecar. A destination that is absent or cannot be listed keeps its flags — unreachable is not missing (§4.5). A row then held nowhere is dropped.

Integrity verification. A monthly timer, or POST /system/backup/verify on demand (optionally for one archive_id, §16.7):

  1. Reconciles the index, then selects a recent backup at random from those a just-listed destination holds
  2. Reads a copy, falling back local → USB → network: a reachable destination without the file has its flag cleared and the next is tried
  3. Verifies its checksum
  4. Opens the archive and confirms the database inside can be opened read-only and passes PRAGMA integrity_check
  5. Records the result under domain='backup', key='verify', with an outcome and the destination read
Outcome Meaning Response
verified A copy was read and passed both checks Clears any untrusted banner
untrusted A copy was read and found corrupt The archive is flagged untrusted; immediate email and banner
missing Every destination the index named was reachable and none holds it Flags cleared, nothing marked; a backup_missing email (§11.4)
unreachable A destination that may hold it could not be reached Nothing — absent media has its own alarm (§4.5)
none No archive is held anywhere yet Nothing to check

Only a copy actually read and found corrupt marks an archive untrusted. This catches silent corruption before it matters — which is the only time anyone would otherwise discover it — without crying corruption over a copy that was never there.

#13.5 Venue Baseline Restore

This exists to answer the accepted network risk in §3.5. If the permission model cannot prevent someone reconfiguring the room, the room must be restorable quickly and reliably.

The problem splits in two, with different mechanisms, and the split matters.

#Controller configuration — the baseline snapshot

A named, deliberately captured snapshot of all configuration, stored as a SQLite copy at /data/config/baselines/current.sqlite — the captured tables below, with the excluded ones emptied — included in the backup set.

Captured:

  • Scenes with their triggers and actions
  • Lighting fixtures, bars, groups and memberships, colour presets, fixture profiles
  • Rules and derived statuses — the daily control path (§8)
  • KNX group addresses and their device groups (§7.1) — bus addresses named by the venue's own configuration, not a driver instance (B42). A rule, a derived status or a KNX dimmer pointing at an address the library no longer holds is exactly the dangling row the fixture-profile reasoning below describes.
  • Mixer channel configuration and its driver references, desk scene library
  • Video destinations and their outputs, matrix input and output naming
  • Control surface banks, strips and buttons (§7.6)
  • Hirer permissions

Deliberately not captured:

  • TLS certificates, network configuration, passwords, the hirer PIN, all logs
  • Driver instances (devices) — including addresses, ports, serial paths and credentials

The devices exclusion is deliberate and worth stating rather than leaving implied. Restoring a baseline must never take the machine off the network or change what hardware it talks to. A baseline restores how the venue is configured, not what it is connected to.

Fixture profiles and driver references are captured because the rows depending on them are meaningless without them — a restored fixture pointing at a deleted profile, or a channel with no reference, is worse than not restoring it at all.

There is one current baseline. Capture replaces it — but does not discard it: the baseline being replaced is kept as a dated copy alongside current.sqlite, and that growing set of dated copies is what is never auto-pruned. "Replaces" and "never auto-pruned" describe two different things, not one contradicting the other.

Three operations:

  • Capture — writes a new current baseline, keeping the one it replaces as a dated copy. Deliberate and admin-only.
  • Compare — a diff of what has changed since the current baseline was captured. The schema may have moved on since then, so compare works against a copy of the baseline migrated forward by the same startup runner that migrates the live database (§15.2), never against the stored file directly. This is the operation that earns its keep: before rolling anything back you can see exactly what drifted and decide whether it was deliberate.
  • Restore — migrates a copy of the baseline forward the same way, takes a pre-change snapshot of the live configuration first so restoring is itself reversible, then applies the migrated copy in a single transaction. Logged as baseline_restored. A baseline never recreates a device (the devices exclusion above), so if a channel the baseline needs points at a device that no longer exists, restore refuses outright and lists which devices are missing, rather than restoring a channel with nothing behind it.

#Physical device state — the Venue Default scene

Most of what a visitor can change on the CQ-20B — gain, EQ, routing, phantom power — is not MIDI-controllable, so this application cannot fix it. The only mechanism that can is a CQ internal scene.

Therefore:

  • A designated "Venue Default" CQ desk scene is a hard requirement, not an optional library entry. It must be saved in MixPad and registered in the desk scene library before handover. It is on the pre-installation checklist under the audio engineer's items.
  • A controller scene named "Restore Venue Default", marked protected so it cannot be deleted and visible_operator, which recalls that desk scene, sets lighting to the house default, sets HDMI to the default source, and sets the projector appropriately. The touch panels follow through derived status (§8.6).
  • One button on the operator Scenes view. After a hire, the operator presses it and the room is back.

Operational dependency. The CQ scene must be re-saved in MixPad whenever the venue's baseline audio setup legitimately changes. If the audio engineer adjusts gain structure and forgets to update the scene, the restore button quietly restores the wrong thing. This is on the post-hire run sheet and in the handover material.

The post-hire run sheet also includes verifying phantom power against the venue default, because that is the one parameter with a hardware-damage consequence and no remote visibility at all.

#13.6 System images

A compressed image of the active root slot, written to /srv/local and the backup USB as .img.gz. Because the root filesystem is read-only, the image is inherently consistent — there is no risk of capturing a mid-write state.

Retain the last 3. Capture is manual and deliberate: after initial setup, after significant OS-level change, and before an OS upgrade.

Application updates no longer require a new image. Since the application lives on /data, the root filesystem stops changing between OS upgrades — which turns image capture from a per-update chore into a rare, deliberate act.

Restoring an image from the admin screen (§21.24) writes it to the standby slot and boots it on trial (§14.4) — the running slot cannot be overwritten while it is running. Only an image this machine captured is accepted. Restoring an image onto a replacement SSD, which has no standby slot to write into until a partition table exists, is done from the recovery environment instead (§13.7).

#13.7 Recovery

#Recovery environment

One image (appliance/recovery/build.sh) serves both boot paths: written to a USB stick with dd from any machine, and to the CM5's onboard eMMC with dd from the running appliance itself, whose own local block device the eMMC is (Q20). The EEPROM boot order is BOOT_ORDER=0xf164 — USB, then the SSD, then the eMMC — so a deliberately inserted recovery stick always wins, the SSD is preferred day to day, and the eMMC is the fallback that needs nobody to have kept a USB stick to hand (§2.2).

The recovery image carries a minimal Debian with:

  • gdisk, parted, e2fsprogs, dosfstools, nvme-cli, smartmontools — partitioning, formatting and diagnosing a replacement SSD. Partitioning itself uses sgdisk (from gdisk), the same tool the golden image build uses, so the layout is reproduced by one implementation rather than two that could drift.
  • python3, python3-flask — the recovery web interface
  • avahi-daemon, networkmanager — network connectivity and .local discovery
  • gzip, zstd, dd, coreutils — image and archive decompression, and image writing

It presents a console on HDMI and a web interface on port 8080 offering:

  • Partition and image a new SSD — recreates §2.3's layout with the PARTUUIDs recorded in the chosen system image's manifest, so cmdline.txt and fstab inside it still resolve without being rewritten, then writes that image to slot A
  • Restore data — restores /data from the most recent archive on the backup USB, an attached drive or a network destination, checking its checksum and every member before writing anything (an archive is unsigned input)
  • Network settings — reconfigure addressing without booting the main system
  • Diagnostics — SMART data, what partitions exist, what the boot partition holds, and whether a chosen image or archive verifies, without writing anything

A failed SSD can be replaced and the system restored entirely from this interface. No SSH, no command line, no laptop required. Every destructive action states plainly what it will erase and asks for confirmation before it runs.

#Scenarios

Failure Recovery Data loss Time
Backup USB fails or is removed Replace the stick; hot-insert is detected None Minutes
/data corrupt, device healthy Restore /data from backup; OS untouched Since last backup ~20 min
SSD fails — CM5 with rescue eMMC Boots to rescue from the eMMC (boot order SSD then eMMC, §2.2); new SSD, partition, image, restore — all through the recovery environment's own web interface, the same as the USB path below Since last backup ~1 hour
SSD fails — CM5 Lite or other platform Boot the recovery USB; new SSD, partition, image, restore Since last backup ~1 hour
Controller module fails Move the SSD to a spare board, or substitute alternative hardware meeting §2.1 None if the SSD survives Hours to days
Total loss New hardware, image, restore from the network backup Since last network backup Same day

The middle rows are the ones the single-SSD design improved. Previously an eMMC failure meant sourcing a Compute Module; the equivalent failure is now a commodity SSD.

Keeping a cold spare SSD in the rack drawer alongside the recovery USB turns rows three and four from "order a part" into "swap and restore."

#Recovery card

A laminated card inside the rack door covering: the controller's address and hostname, the SSH management address, sudo avc-reset-password, how to boot the recovery USB (or, if the SSD itself has failed and no stick is to hand, that the CM5's eMMC already carries the same recovery environment and boots automatically once the SSD is gone — see docs/hardware/recovery.md), whether a cold spare SSD is on the shelf and where, or, if not, that any commodity M.2 NVMe drive works and the recovery environment partitions and images it from scratch (Q21 — this school does not keep one; the wipe-and-image-and-restore drill bench B1 runs is exactly what happens either way, spare or not, it just starts with a trip to buy a drive first), the note that device passwords need re-entering after restoring to replacement hardware, the caution that email will not fire if the SSD has failed entirely — but does fire for the narrower case of /data alone being unavailable with the rest of the SSD healthy (§4.6; bench B1 exercises this one directly, over SSH, without needing the recovery environment at all) — and who to call.

Two items on the card are easy to omit and expensive to rediscover:

  • The HDMI matrix's USB serial cable is pinned by its FTDI serial number (§4.12). Replacing the cable means capturing the new serial and updating the udev rule, or /dev/hdmi-matrix will not appear and HDMI will report offline with no obvious cause. The current serial is on the card and in docs/hardware/network_map.md.
  • The LKV422's IR remote is stored with this card, not in the rack (§7.5).
  • The Cloudflare API token is not in the backup archive (§13.2). A certificate restore needs it re-entered from the password manager, or a fresh one issued at Cloudflare.

#14. Updates

Two independent mechanisms with different lifetimes. Keeping them separate is deliberate — conflating them would mean rebooting for an application change.

Application update OS upgrade
Frequency Regular Rare, deliberate
Mechanism Symlink swap on /data/app A/B root slot switch
Downtime Service restart, a few seconds Full reboot
Rollback Repoint the symlink Boot the other slot

#14.1 Application packages

A .aupkg file — an uncompressed tar with a defined structure, built on the developer's machine and uploaded through the admin interface. The device needs no internet access for updates, and updates are never automatic.

auditorium_v1.3.0.aupkg
  ├── manifest.json       Version, minimum required version, description, changes, checksums
  ├── manifest.json.sig   Ed25519 signature over manifest.json's exact bytes
  └── payload/
      ├── app/            Application code
      ├── wheels/         Vendored Python dependencies as .whl files
      ├── migrations/     Forward and reverse SQL
      └── web/            Built React output

The order is part of the format: manifest.json first, its signature second, everything else under payload/. That is what lets the appliance verify the signature before it reads a single byte it has not yet authenticated, and stream a package larger than its own memory. The container itself is not compressed, because a compressed container must be expanded before it can be checked, which is a decompression bomb by another name; members may be compressed individually, since the manifest states each one's size and digest first.

wheels/ contains every dependency pre-downloaded, so an update applies on a device with no internet access. The build script runs pip download against the current requirements and bundles the result.

One package format, two payload types. The manifest.json carries "type": "application" or "type": "os". Application packages carry app/, wheels/, migrations/ and web/; OS packages carry a root image plus a slot-x/ boot tree for the inactive slot (§14.4) — the kernel, initramfs, cmdline.txt and the rest of what the bootloader needs to start that slot. A single verification path serves both, rather than two formats with two chances to get the security wrong.

Neither cmdline.txt nor /etc/fstab can simply be copied from the package: both name this machine's partition IDs, which differ from the build host's and from the other slot's. The installer writes this machine's root=PARTUUID and the matching /etc/fstab entry itself, from the partition IDs already recorded in boot-state.json, rather than trusting anything the package carries for either. It also adds panic=10 and a bounded rootwait to cmdline.txt, so a slot whose root partition fails to mount — a bad image, a wiped disk, a cable pulled mid-write — panics and reboots instead of sitting at an initramfs prompt nobody in the room can type into; falling back to the other slot is then unattended rather than needing a keyboard.

Both types are signed. An OS image is a considerably larger prize than an application package — it is the whole machine — so signing one and not the other would be the wrong way round. Verification is identical and happens before anything is extracted.

Trust anchors live in /usr/local/share/auditorium/trusted-keys/, on the read-only root filesystem. Any key in that directory can verify a package. They are never read from a path under /opt/auditorium, which resolves into /data/app/current and would let a package ship its own key and self-authorise every subsequent update (§6.11).

A package failing verification is rejected before anything else happens.

#Key rollover

The directory rather than a single file exists to make rotation possible, which a single file does not: the anchor lives on the read-only rootfs, so replacing it requires an OS upgrade, which requires a valid signature from the key being replaced.

Normal rollover is therefore two steps:

  1. Ship an OS package signed with the current key that adds the new public key to trusted-keys/. Both keys are now trusted.
  2. Ship a later OS package signed with the new key that removes the old one.

Between the two steps either key verifies, so there is no window in which the system cannot be updated.

#If the signing key is lost

No further packages of either type can be applied — there is no bypass, by design.

Recovery is to reimage from the golden image build (§4.2) with a fresh key pair, then restore /data from backup. The recovery USB (§13.7) already performs exactly this, so the path exists; it is recorded here so that it is not discovered under pressure.

The consequence is that the signing private key must be backed up in two independent places — the password manager and one offline copy — and that is on the handover list (§20.1). Losing it is not catastrophic, but it converts a ten-minute update into a rebuild.

#14.2 Applying an application update

/data/app/
  ├── v1.2.0/         previous
  ├── v1.3.0/         new
  └── current  →  v1.3.0
  1. Verify the signature; reject if invalid
  2. Check the manifest's minimum required version against the installed version
  3. Display the manifest for review
  4. On confirmation: - Extract to /data/app/v1.3.0/ — the staging directory is the destination, so there is no second copy step - Create /data/app/v1.3.0/venv and install the vendored wheels into it - Test migrations against a copy of the live database; abort cleanly on failure, having changed nothing
  5. The admin chooses to apply now or at the next quiet moment
  6. Apply: - Capture a pre-update database snapshot - Swap atomically: ln -sfn v1.3.0 /data/app/.tmp && mv -T /data/app/.tmp /data/app/current - Restart the service and reload nginx - Migrations are applied by the new version at startup, not here
  7. The reconnection waiting screen takes over (§10.8)
  8. The admin lands on the updated system with a success banner

One application point. Migrations are applied by the migration runner at startup (§15.2) and nowhere else. The update process tests them against a copy of the live database at step 4 and aborts cleanly if they fail, so a broken migration never reaches production — but it does not apply them. Two code paths for one job would mean two chances to get ordering, transaction scope and failure handling wrong, and the seam shows up in rollback.

A migration failure at startup exits with code 2, which is already distinct from other startup failures precisely so the automatic rollback (§14.5) can identify the cause.

ln -sfn on its own is not atomic — it unlinks before it links, leaving a window in which current does not resolve. Creating a temporary symlink and moving it over the target uses rename(2), which is atomic.

The previous two versions are retained; older ones are pruned.

#14.3 Application rollback

Repoint the symlink and restore the pre-update database snapshot — in that order.

Order Intermediate state If the process dies there
Snapshot first, then symlink New code, old schema The new version starts, migrates forward, and silently undoes the rollback
Symlink first, then snapshot Old code, new schema §15.2's schema-ahead guard refuses to start. The rollback service retries and completes

The second fails safe, because a guard that already exists catches the intermediate state. The wrong order is the intuitive one — restore the data, then the code — which is why it is stated explicitly.

This is the real gain over the earlier design, which had to restore a directory tree from a parallel path and hope it was intact. Here, the previous version was never touched — it is a symlink change plus a database restore. That matters most for the automatic path in §14.5, which runs unattended.

Rollback restores the database snapshot, not a reverse migration. Anything written since the update — new scenes, configuration changes, log entries — is lost. Reverse migrations exist for development use; the snapshot approach is safer in production.

#14.4 OS upgrades — A/B root slots

Two root partitions (§2.3). One is active; the other holds the previous OS and is the rollback target.

Applying an OS upgrade writes the new system into the inactive slot, updates the bootloader configuration for that slot, and arms a one-shot boot into it.

Reference platform. The Raspberry Pi bootloader's tryboot mechanism does exactly this. reboot "0 tryboot" boots once using tryboot.txt rather than config.txt. If the new slot fails to come up and the watchdog reboots the machine, the bootloader automatically reverts to config.txt and the previous slot. Confirming a successful upgrade is copying tryboot.txt over config.txt.

Other platforms. x86 uses two systemd-boot entries with a one-shot default; U-Boot uses bootcount with altbootcmd. Same shape, different mechanism — which is why this sits behind stage_root_slot() and confirm_root_slot() in the platform layer (§5.4) and is documented per target in docs/hardware/porting.md.

Confirmation. After booting into the new slot, the application must run healthily for 10 minutes before confirm_root_slot() is called and the slot becomes permanent. Until then, any reboot returns to the previous slot. A trial that never reaches a healthy marker within the 10 minutes is treated the same way: the rollback unit reboots into the previous slot rather than waiting indefinitely for a reboot that might not come on its own. Slot state and the last known-good slot are recorded in /srv/appliance/boot-state.json.

While a slot is on trial, a failed application start means the OS is suspect, not the application. §14.5's automatic recovery normally repoints /data/app/current and restores a database snapshot — but that assumes the OS underneath is sound. During an OS trial it is not yet known to be, so the rollback unit's response changes: instead of rolling back the application, it reboots into the previous slot (§14.5), which restores the OS that was already known good and, with it, the application that was running under it. The two mechanisms are never both attempted for the same failure.

OS upgrades are rare and deliberate, done at a desk with the recovery USB to hand.

#14.5 Automatic recovery from a failed update

If the application fails to start cleanly after an update, the system must recover unattended — the admin may not be present after pressing apply.

Detection. Restart=on-failure with StartLimitBurst=3 and StartLimitIntervalSec=180: three failed starts within three minutes and systemd gives up.

Each startup writes a marker to /srv/appliance/boot-state.json with version and timestamp. After 30 seconds of healthy operation — WebSocket server listening, and either at least one device connected or only known non-blocking device errors — it writes a healthy marker.

The marker lives on /srv/appliance, not in a staging directory that gets reused and cleared.

Automatic rollback. When systemd gives up, OnFailure=auditorium-update-rollback.service fires:

  1. Read boot-state.json. If the last healthy marker is from the previous version, the current one is deemed broken
  2. Repoint /data/app/current to the previous version
  3. Restore the pre-update database snapshot
  4. Start the service normally
  5. Log update_auto_rollback with the failed version and the reason from the service logs
  6. Send a high-priority email
  7. Show a banner: An update failed to start and was rolled back automatically. Review the logs before retrying.

Boot-loop protection. Emergency mode is entered explicitly, not through the hardware watchdog catching a hang — a unit that fails simply exits, it does not wedge systemd, so there is nothing for that watchdog to catch here. Two paths lead there, both already in §16.7's closed reason set:

  • If the rollback unit itself cannot recover — unlikely, but possible if /data is damaged — it logs migration_failed and starts the emergency responder (§4.6) itself, served from the read-only root.
  • If there is no installed version at all — a freshly built appliance before its first package — it starts the emergency responder with not_installed, not migration_failed: nothing failed, and the page says to install a signed package.
  • Separately, the application refuses to start at all when /data has less than 100 MB free, logging disk_full and handing over to the same emergency responder — starting into a near-full disk risks failing mid-write rather than failing cleanly before anything is touched.

Watchdog interaction. WatchdogSec is suspended for the first 60 seconds after an update so a slow first start is not mistaken for a hang.

#15. Data Model

#15.1 Database

SQLite at /data/auditorium.db, accessed through aiosqlite in WAL mode. Backup is a file copy through the online backup API. Schema is versioned by migration.

#Connection model

SQLite permits one writer at a time, WAL included. aiosqlite runs each connection in a dedicated background thread and serialises operations queued to it, so coroutines sharing a connection do not race at the statement level — but they do interleave transactions, and two coroutines interleaving BEGIN/COMMIT on one connection corrupt each other's transaction scope. A rollback in one discards the other's work.

Write connection   Exactly one, guarded by an asyncio.Lock held for the
                   duration of each transactional unit — not each statement
Read connections   Pool of 3–4. Under WAL, readers never block the writer
                   and the writer never blocks readers
Per connection     PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON
                   PRAGMA busy_timeout = 5000
Database-wide      journal_mode = WAL
                   synchronous = NORMAL

synchronous = NORMAL rather than FULL: under WAL this is safe against application crash, and the exposure on sudden power loss is the last transaction. That is already the accepted position — the 500 ms state throttle (§15.11) puts up to half a second of fader movement at risk regardless, so an fsync per commit buys nothing real and costs write throughput on every scene execution.

Everything that writes goes through the lock. The writers are: the state persister, the scene execution log, security events, API handlers, the backup job's pruning, and the migration runner. Nothing may open its own write connection.

#Long-running writes must be chunked

Deleting ninety days of scene_execution_log in a single statement holds the write lock for seconds. During an event that stalls every control write behind it — a scene trigger would visibly hang.

The nightly prune therefore deletes in bounded batches by rowid range, releasing the lock between batches so control traffic interleaves. The same applies to the low-disk auto-prune (§11.3), which by definition runs when the system is already under stress.

#State persister batching

The persister writes one transaction per 500 ms tick, carrying every value that changed in that window, rather than a transaction per changed value.

At two transactions per second the throughput is irrelevant, and the alternative — a write-behind queue that never blocks the caller — was rejected because it reports failures far from where they originated. Keeping errors attributable is worth more here than the latency.

#WAL checkpointing

The WAL grows until checkpointed, and a long-running read can starve automatic checkpoints indefinitely. Automatic checkpointing stays at the 1000-page default, and the nightly backup job runs an explicit wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE) while the system is quiet, before taking the archive.

#Foreign keys and delete behaviour

Foreign keys must be enabled explicitly. SQLite does not enforce them by default; the application executes PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON on every connection. Delete behaviour is stated on every foreign key:

  • ON DELETE CASCADE for ownership — scene to its triggers and actions, group to its memberships
  • ON DELETE RESTRICT for references that must block deletion — a KNX address or desk scene in use by a scene action. This produces the delete-protection behaviour the UI presents (§21.22)
  • ON DELETE SET NULL where a dangling reference is acceptable — execution log to scene, fixture to bar

#15.2 Migrations

Files are NNN_description.sql in db/migrations/forward/, each with a counterpart in reverse/.

At startup, before any other database operation:

  1. Create schema_versions if absent
  2. List forward migrations sorted by numeric prefix
  3. For each not yet recorded: begin a transaction, execute it, insert the row, commit
  4. On any failure: roll back, log, and refuse to start with exit code 2 — distinct from other startup failures, so the automatic rollback in §14.5 can identify the cause
  5. Startup statuses are distinct so §14.5's automatic rollback can tell the causes apart: 1 a configuration error, 2 a migration failure or a schema ahead of the code, 4 /data unmounted or read-only (§4.6).

Schema version guard. If the highest recorded version is newer than the highest shipped migration — the database is ahead of the code, which can happen after a failed rollback or manual intervention — the application refuses to start: The database schema is newer than this application version expects. A rollback or restore is required.

Reverse migrations are for development only. Production rollback uses the pre-update snapshot (§14.3), which is safer for data integrity.

Seed data. 001_schema.sql creates the schema; 002_seed.sql then runs on an empty database and creates:

  • Two users rows, admin and operator, with placeholder hashes flagged for replacement
  • One hirer_config row, disabled, with empty permissions
  • Three fixture_profiles rows: single-channel dimmer, RGB, RGBW
  • No devices and no mixer_channels rows — both are created through the first-run wizard and the Devices screen, because a channel cannot exist without a driver instance to belong to
  • One lighting_bars row, "Proscenium", sort_order=0

#15.3 Retention

Table Retention
scene_execution_log 90 days
rule_execution_log 90 days
security_events 90 days
File logs in /data/logs/ 90 days
Pre-change snapshots 10 most recent
Venue baselines Never auto-pruned
Backup archives Per destination (§13.3), from the reconciled presence flags; a backup_archives row held by no destination is dropped

Pruned during the nightly backup job, or earlier under disk pressure (§11.3).

#15.4 Users and access

-- Always exactly two rows, seeded at first run
CREATE TABLE users (
  id            INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  tier          TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,        -- 'admin' | 'operator'
  password      TEXT NOT NULL,               -- bcrypt
  token_version INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,  -- bumped on change to invalidate JWTs
  updated_at    TEXT NOT NULL
);

-- Exactly one row
CREATE TABLE hirer_config (
  id                  INTEGER PRIMARY KEY CHECK (id = 1),
  pin                 TEXT NOT NULL,               -- bcrypt
  token_version       INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  enabled             INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  lighting_enabled    INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  individual_fixtures INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  colour_enabled      INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  updated_at          TEXT NOT NULL,
  updated_by          INTEGER REFERENCES users(id) ON DELETE SET NULL
);

id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY CHECK (id = 1) rather than DEFAULT 1. In SQLite, INTEGER PRIMARY KEY is a rowid alias and the default is ignored — an insert without an id gets the next rowid, not 1.

Hirer visibility is page assignment; ceilings stay on the channel.

Pages control what. Ceilings control how far.

A hirer is assigned one or more pages (§15.12) and sees exactly what those pages contain — channels, group masters, buttons. Six visible_hirer columns spread across five tables collapse into one join.

Permission Where
What a hirer can reach at all hirer_pages — the pages assigned
How far a fader may travel mixer_channels.hirer_max_db

A ceiling is not a visibility question, so it survives: the same channel may appear on a staff page and a hirer page, and the ceiling applies whenever the session is a hirer regardless of which page it was reached from.

This also makes rule buttons reviewable. A rule bypasses the permission model — it sets a group or fires a scene with no ceiling and no channel filter. Under page assignment, a rule a hirer can fire is a button on a page someone can look at, rather than a flag somewhere.

Earlier revisions duplicated all of this as JSON inside hirer_config, which meant two admin screens edited different copies of the same setting and the server enforcement read only one of them. The columns win: normalised, foreign-key enforced, queryable, and no JSON parsing when clamping a fader on every write.

Admin → Hirer Access is a filtered view over these columns, which is how it is presented to the user anyway. Nothing changes visually. The JWT carries identity only — tier, hirer session identifier and token_version; permissions resolve live from state.hirer (§6.4, Appendix B31).

#15.5 Devices and drivers

-- Driver instances (§5.5). Replaces the earlier device_config table.
CREATE TABLE devices (
  id          INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  category    TEXT NOT NULL,        -- 'mixer'|'lighting_output'|'projector'|
                                    -- 'video_matrix'|'control_surface'
  driver_key  TEXT NOT NULL,        -- 'cq20b'|'pjlink'|'lkv422'|'artnet'|'xtouch'|'stub'
  name        TEXT NOT NULL,
  enabled     INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 1,
  config      TEXT NOT NULL,        -- JSON; shape declared by the driver's CONFIG_SCHEMA
                                    -- fields marked encrypted are stored per §6.10
  created_at  TEXT NOT NULL,
  updated_at  TEXT NOT NULL
);

CREATE INDEX idx_devices_category ON devices(category);

Several instances per category are permitted (§5.5). The interface shows device pickers only when more than one exists.

KNX has no row here. It is a subsystem rather than a driver category (§5.5), configured through its own screens and its own tables below.

#15.6 Mixer

CREATE TABLE mixer_channels (
  id              INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  device_id       INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES devices(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  channel_kind    TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'input',   -- from ChannelRef.kind (§5.5)
  name            TEXT NOT NULL,
  short_name      TEXT,                            -- <=7 chars, scribble strips
                                                   -- only; null = truncate name
  notes           TEXT,
  unmapped        INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,       -- driver change left it unresolved

  visible_staff   INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 1,
  hirer_max_db    REAL,                -- dB, null = no ceiling (§5.5)
  show_pan        INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  tracked         INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 1,
  created_at      TEXT NOT NULL,
  updated_at      TEXT NOT NULL,

  sort_order      INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0
);

-- One virtual channel maps to one or more driver references (§5.5)
CREATE TABLE mixer_channel_refs (
  id         INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  channel_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES mixer_channels(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  driver_ref TEXT NOT NULL,
  sort_order INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,   -- first ref is authoritative for display
  UNIQUE(channel_id, driver_ref)
);

There is no number column. name is the channel's identity and sort_order is its position; a display number tied to the desk's numbering would fight you the moment an AUX is inserted mid-list, and nothing references it — scene actions use mixer_channel_id, surface strips use the channel id, and the interface shows the name.

driver_ref moved to its own table so a virtual fader can gang several references (§5.5). The common case is one row.

unmapped is set when a driver change leaves a reference with no equivalent. An unmapped channel is not controllable and is excluded from hirer access until resolved — failing closed rather than retaining a ceiling against a reference that no longer resolves.

Six NRPN columns and source_key are gone, replaced by one opaque driver_ref. The CQ-20B driver resolves "ip1" to its own NRPN addressing, which is where that knowledge belongs — input addressing is not contiguous on that desk (§7.3), and it will be differently non-contiguous on any other.

hirer_max_db is dB rather than a wire value, so a ceiling survives a mixer swap (§5.5).

CREATE TABLE mixer_desk_scenes (
  id               INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  device_id        INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES devices(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  scene_ref        TEXT NOT NULL,      -- opaque; '3' to the CQ-20B driver
  name             TEXT NOT NULL,
  description      TEXT,
  notes            TEXT,
  is_venue_default INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,   -- exactly one row, §13.5
  visible_staff    INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 1,
  sort_order       INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  created_at       TEXT NOT NULL,
  updated_at       TEXT NOT NULL,

  UNIQUE(device_id, scene_ref)
);

Rows here are meaningless if the configured driver reports supports_scene_recall = false. They are retained and shown disabled rather than deleted (§5.5), because a driver may be changed back.

#15.7 KNX

CREATE TABLE knx_device_groups (
  id          INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name        TEXT NOT NULL,
  description TEXT,
  location    TEXT
);

CREATE TABLE knx_group_addresses (
  id            INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  group_address TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  name          TEXT NOT NULL,
  description   TEXT,
  dpt           TEXT NOT NULL,
  direction     TEXT NOT NULL,             -- 'incoming'|'outgoing'|'both'
  device_id     INTEGER REFERENCES knx_device_groups(id) ON DELETE SET NULL,
  is_heartbeat  INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,   -- reserved address, §7.1
  notes         TEXT,
  created_at    TEXT NOT NULL,
  updated_at    TEXT NOT NULL
);

#15.8 Rules and scenes

CREATE TABLE scenes (
  id               INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name             TEXT NOT NULL,
  description      TEXT,
  enabled          INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 1,
  icon             TEXT,
  priority         TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'normal',   -- 'normal'|'critical'
  protected        INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,       -- cannot be deleted
  visible_operator INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 1,
  sort_order       INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  created_at       TEXT NOT NULL,
  updated_at       TEXT NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE rules (
  id                INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name              TEXT NOT NULL,
  enabled           INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 1,
  sort_order        INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  notes             TEXT,

  -- Trigger (§8.3)
  trigger_type      TEXT NOT NULL,   -- 'knx'|'schedule'|'surface'|'device_state'
  knx_address_id    INTEGER REFERENCES knx_group_addresses(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  match_type        TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'equal',
  match_value       TEXT,
  match_value_max   TEXT,
  debounce_ms       INTEGER,           -- knx triggers only (§8.4)
  cron              TEXT,
  trigger_device_id INTEGER REFERENCES devices(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  trigger_state     TEXT,
  trigger_for_ms    INTEGER,

  -- Guard — one, optional (§8.5)
  guard_type        TEXT,
  guard_value       TEXT,

  -- Action (§8.9)
  action_type       TEXT NOT NULL,   -- 'run_scene'|'lighting_group'|'notify'
  scene_id          INTEGER REFERENCES scenes(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  lighting_group_id INTEGER REFERENCES lighting_groups(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  on_level          REAL,
  off_level         REAL,
  fade_ms           INTEGER,
  message           TEXT,

  created_at        TEXT NOT NULL,
  updated_at        TEXT NOT NULL,

  CHECK (
    (action_type = 'run_scene'      AND scene_id IS NOT NULL)
    OR
    -- A binding needs a boolean address and an 'any' match: the telegram
    -- value selects on_level or off_level (§8.2)
    (action_type = 'lighting_group' AND lighting_group_id IS NOT NULL
                                    AND on_level IS NOT NULL AND off_level IS NOT NULL
                                    AND trigger_type = 'knx' AND match_type = 'any')
    OR
    (action_type = 'notify'         AND message IS NOT NULL)
  )
);

CREATE INDEX idx_rules_knx ON rules(knx_address_id) WHERE knx_address_id IS NOT NULL;

CREATE TABLE derived_status (
  id                INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name              TEXT NOT NULL,
  enabled           INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 1,
  knx_address_id    INTEGER UNIQUE          -- nullable; still unique when present.
                    REFERENCES knx_group_addresses(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
                                             -- No address: evaluated and broadcast
                                             -- like any other, just never written
                                             -- to the bus (e.g. a page button lamp)
  source_type       TEXT NOT NULL,   -- 'lighting_group_all_at'|'device_state'
                                     -- |'external_control'
  lighting_group_id INTEGER REFERENCES lighting_groups(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  compare_level     REAL,
  device_id         INTEGER REFERENCES devices(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  compare_state     TEXT,            -- for the projector, also its operational
                                     -- state, and the alias 'on_or_warming' (§8.6)
  basis             TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'level'   -- migration 011 (§8.6):
                    CHECK (basis IN ('level', 'output'))
);

CREATE TABLE rule_execution_log (
  id           INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  rule_id      INTEGER REFERENCES rules(id) ON DELETE SET NULL,
  triggered_by TEXT NOT NULL,
  fired_at     TEXT NOT NULL,
  guard_result TEXT,               -- 'passed'|'blocked'|null
  result       TEXT,
  detail       TEXT
);

CREATE INDEX idx_rule_log_fired ON rule_execution_log(fired_at DESC);

rules replaces scene_triggers and knx_group_bindings, both of which were rules before that layer existed (§8.1). A rule carries one trigger, one optional guard and one action; anything multi-step uses action_type = 'run_scene' and inherits scene execution's ordering, failure semantics and logging.

derived_status has UNIQUE on its address, when one is set, because two rules writing one address would be non-deterministic. The address is nullable (migration 006, a table rebuild — SQLite cannot drop a NOT NULL constraint in place): a status with no wall-panel indicator, such as a page button's lamp for "House at 100%", is still evaluated and broadcast on every state change, and is simply never written to the KNX bus. Its lighting_group_id is RESTRICT like the rules table's, so deleting a group reports both references rather than blocking on one and silently dropping the other (§21.19).
