These rules are evaluated on state change and are not logged — they fire on every level change and would flood the log (§8.10).

CREATE TABLE scene_actions (
  id               INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  scene_id         INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES scenes(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  sort_order       INTEGER NOT NULL,      -- display order within a delay group
  delay_ms         INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  domain           TEXT NOT NULL,         -- eight domains, §8.12

  knx_address_id   INTEGER REFERENCES knx_group_addresses(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  knx_value        TEXT,
  knx_source       TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'literal',  -- 'literal'|'trigger_value'
  knx_scale        TEXT,

  dmx_snapshot     TEXT,                  -- JSON, keyed by lighting_channels.id
  dmx_fade_ms      INTEGER,

  mixer_scene_id   INTEGER REFERENCES mixer_desk_scenes(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  mixer_channel_id INTEGER REFERENCES mixer_channels(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  mixer_db         REAL,                 -- dB, null = off (§5.5)
  mixer_muted      INTEGER,

  projector_power  TEXT,
  projector_input  TEXT,                 -- opaque input_ref

  hdmi_destination INTEGER REFERENCES video_destinations(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  hdmi_input_id    INTEGER REFERENCES matrix_inputs(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,

  device_id        INTEGER REFERENCES devices(id) ON DELETE CASCADE
                   -- null means "the only device in that category"
);

CREATE TABLE scene_execution_log (
  id             INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  scene_id       INTEGER REFERENCES scenes(id) ON DELETE SET NULL,
  triggered_by   TEXT NOT NULL,
  started_at     TEXT NOT NULL,
  completed_at   TEXT,
  result         TEXT,
  action_results TEXT
);

CREATE INDEX idx_exec_log_started ON scene_execution_log(started_at DESC);
CREATE INDEX idx_exec_log_scene   ON scene_execution_log(scene_id, started_at DESC);

dmx_snapshot references lighting channels inside JSON, which no foreign key can protect. Fixture deletion checks every snapshot for references and blocks if any are found (§9.7).

#15.9 Lighting

CREATE TABLE lighting_bars (
  id         INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name       TEXT NOT NULL,
  sort_order INTEGER NOT NULL,     -- 0 = downstage/proscenium
  notes      TEXT
);

CREATE TABLE lighting_channels (
  id                     INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name                   TEXT NOT NULL,
  type                   TEXT NOT NULL,               -- 'dmx'|'knx_dimmer'
  profile_id             INTEGER REFERENCES fixture_profiles(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  device_id              INTEGER REFERENCES devices(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  universe               INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 1,
  address                INTEGER,                     -- start address; meaning
                                                      -- defined by the output driver

  bar_id                 INTEGER REFERENCES lighting_bars(id) ON DELETE SET NULL,
  position               REAL NOT NULL DEFAULT 0.5,   -- 0.0 SR → 1.0 SL

  colour_r               INTEGER,     -- power-on default; the level store
  colour_g               INTEGER,     -- is authoritative at runtime (§7.2.3)
  colour_b               INTEGER,
  colour_w               INTEGER,

  knx_command_address_id INTEGER REFERENCES knx_group_addresses(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  knx_status_address_id  INTEGER REFERENCES knx_group_addresses(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  knx_switch_address_id  INTEGER REFERENCES knx_group_addresses(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  fade_mode              TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'hardware',

  min_value              REAL NOT NULL DEFAULT 0.0,   -- 0–100 scale, §9.2
  max_value              REAL NOT NULL DEFAULT 100.0,

  visible_staff          INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 1,
  notes                  TEXT,
  created_at             TEXT NOT NULL,
  updated_at             TEXT NOT NULL,

  -- A row is one shape or the other, never half of each
  CHECK (
    (type = 'dmx'        AND profile_id IS NOT NULL
                         AND address    IS NOT NULL
                         AND device_id  IS NOT NULL)
    OR
    (type = 'knx_dimmer' AND knx_command_address_id IS NOT NULL
                         AND profile_id IS NULL
                         AND address    IS NULL)
  )
);

device_id is RESTRICT, not CASCADE. Deleting the lighting output device must never delete the fixtures patched to it; the delete is refused with the list of channels that use it (§16.1), and changing a device's driver is done by re-mapping (§5.5), never by deleting and recreating it.

KNX dimmers legitimately have a null device_id, because KNX is a subsystem rather than a driver category (§5.5) and has no driver instance to belong to. That is correct rather than an oversight, which is why the constraint does not require it.

Without the check, a row with type = 'dmx' and a null profile_id is storable and meaningless. surface_strips carries the same constraint for the same reason (§15.11).

CREATE TABLE fixture_profiles (
  id            INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  manufacturer  TEXT,
  model         TEXT,
  name          TEXT NOT NULL,
  channel_count INTEGER NOT NULL,
  channels      TEXT NOT NULL,     -- JSON, see below
  created_at    TEXT NOT NULL,
  updated_at    TEXT NOT NULL
);

Replaces the fixture_type enum, which hard-coded three fixture shapes into the schema. A profile describes what each of a fixture's channels does:

{ "channels": [
    { "offset": 0, "role": "dimmer", "default": 0 },
    { "offset": 1, "role": "red",    "default": 0 },
    { "offset": 2, "role": "green",  "default": 0 },
    { "offset": 3, "role": "blue",   "default": 0 },
    { "offset": 4, "role": "strobe", "default": 0 }
]}

Roles come from a closed vocabulary: dimmer, red, green, blue, white, amber, uv, pan, tilt, strobe, macro, unused. Closed because the compositor must know what to do with each — an open vocabulary would mean roles the renderer silently ignores.

A twelve-channel moving head becomes a row rather than a code change. The three previous fixture types seed as three profiles, so nothing is lost.

Not GDTF. The full fixture-definition standard is large and this system would use perhaps five percent of it. A hand-entered profile takes two minutes, and §21.19 offers duplicate-and-edit.

CREATE TABLE lighting_groups (
  id             INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name           TEXT NOT NULL,
  colour         TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT '#2E86C1',
  sort_order     INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  indicator_only INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0     -- migration 011 (§9.4): no fader,
                 CHECK (indicator_only IN (0, 1))  -- never bound, not on pages
);

CREATE TABLE lighting_group_memberships (
  id         INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  group_id   INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES lighting_groups(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  channel_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES lighting_channels(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  sort_order INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  UNIQUE(group_id, channel_id)
);

CREATE TABLE colour_presets (
  id            INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name          TEXT NOT NULL,
  r             INTEGER NOT NULL,
  g             INTEGER NOT NULL,
  b             INTEGER NOT NULL,
  w             INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  sort_order    INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0
);

#15.10 Video matrix

CREATE TABLE matrix_inputs (
  id          INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  device_id   INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES devices(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  driver_ref  TEXT NOT NULL,       -- opaque; '1'..'4' to the LKV422 driver
  name        TEXT NOT NULL,
  description TEXT,
  sort_order  INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  UNIQUE(device_id, driver_ref)
);

CREATE TABLE matrix_outputs (
  id          INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  device_id   INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES devices(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  driver_ref  TEXT NOT NULL,
  name        TEXT NOT NULL,
  description TEXT,
  sort_order  INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  UNIQUE(device_id, driver_ref)
);

-- What the operator routes to. One or more physical outputs (§7.5).
CREATE TABLE video_destinations (
  id               INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  device_id        INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES devices(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  name             TEXT NOT NULL,          -- "The room"
  default_input_id INTEGER REFERENCES matrix_inputs(id) ON DELETE SET NULL,
                                           -- restored by Venue Default, §13.5
  sort_order       INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0
);

CREATE TABLE video_destination_outputs (
  id             INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  destination_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES video_destinations(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  output_id      INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES matrix_outputs(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  sort_order     INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,   -- first is authoritative for display
  UNIQUE(destination_id, output_id)
);

device_config is gone — driver instances live in devices (§15.5).

Inputs and outputs carry device_id and an opaque driver_ref like every other addressable thing (§5.5). Earlier revisions used integer input_number / output_number columns, written before the driver model — which left scene_actions.hdmi_input_id resolving to an integer against a driver interface that takes a reference string, and left matrix_outputs orphaned because destinations repeated the reference instead of pointing at the row.

default_input_id moved here from matrix_inputs. A single global default could not express one destination defaulting to the stage feed while another defaults to a foyer source, and the model now permits several destinations.

This venue has one destination covering both outputs. A scene action targets a destination, not an output, which is what keeps it a single action and a single driver call (§5.5).

Matrix rows have no unmapped column, unlike mixer_channels (§15.6). After a driver change every input and output must be given a reference the new driver declares, in the same re-mapping (§5.5); a re-map that leaves one out is refused, rather than leaving a row holding a reference that looks valid and may not exist. Until the re-map is applied, the rows keep their old references.

#15.11 Control surface

Optional; unused when no surface is configured (§7.6).

CREATE TABLE surface_banks (
  id          INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name        TEXT NOT NULL,              -- shown on the LCDs when switching
  sort_order  INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0, -- bank 0 loads at startup
  notes       TEXT,
  created_at  TEXT NOT NULL,
  updated_at  TEXT NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE surface_strips (
  id            INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  bank_id       INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES surface_banks(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  strip_number  INTEGER NOT NULL,         -- 1–8, physical position

  target_domain TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'unassigned',
                -- 'mixer_channel'|'mixer_output'|'mixer_main'|'lighting_channel'
                -- |'lighting_group'|'lighting_master'|'unassigned'
  target_id     INTEGER,                  -- resolved per domain; null for
                                          -- mixer_main, lighting_master, unassigned

  label         TEXT,                     -- LCD text; falls back to the target's name
  colour        INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 7,   -- 0–7, §7.6 scribble strip palette
  encoder_role  TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'none', -- 'none'|'pan'|'colour_hue'

  UNIQUE(bank_id, strip_number),

  CHECK (
    (target_domain IN ('mixer_main', 'lighting_master', 'unassigned')
     AND target_id IS NULL)
    OR
    (target_domain IN ('mixer_channel', 'mixer_output',
                       'lighting_channel', 'lighting_group')
     AND target_id IS NOT NULL)
  )
);

CREATE TABLE surface_buttons (
  id            INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  bank_id       INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES surface_banks(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  strip_number  INTEGER NOT NULL,         -- 1–8
  button_index  INTEGER NOT NULL,         -- 0–3, top to bottom within the strip

  action        TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'none',
                -- 'none'|'mute'|'bump'|'blackout'|'scene'|'bank_up'|'bank_down'
                -- |'bank_goto'
  scene_id      INTEGER REFERENCES scenes(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,   -- action='scene'
  bank_target   INTEGER REFERENCES surface_banks(id) ON DELETE SET NULL,
                                                                    -- action='bank_goto'

  UNIQUE(bank_id, strip_number, button_index)
);

strip_number is a physical position, not a target reference. A bank always has eight strips conceptually; unassigned ones simply carry target_domain = 'unassigned', park the fader at zero and blank the LCD. Storing them explicitly rather than by absence means the configurator can show a complete surface without synthesising rows.

ON DELETE RESTRICT on scene_id follows the delete-protection pattern used elsewhere (§15.1): a scene bound to a surface button reports the binding rather than vanishing from under it.

Target references are not foreign keys, because target_id resolves against a different table per domain. A nightly integrity check reports strips pointing at deleted channels or groups, and the client treats an unresolvable target as unassigned rather than failing.

The CHECK constraint enforces presence, which a foreign key cannot do here: three domains take no target and four require one, so a mixer_channel strip with a null target — storable and meaningless without the constraint — is now rejected at write time rather than discovered at render time.

Surface configuration is included in the venue baseline (§13.5).

#15.12 Pages

The everyday surface (§21.9).

CREATE TABLE pages (
  id          INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  name        TEXT NOT NULL,
  sort_order  INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  is_default  INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,   -- auto-generated, not user-created
  created_at  TEXT NOT NULL,
  updated_at  TEXT NOT NULL
);

-- One horizontal flow of strips, group trays and button panels.
CREATE TABLE page_items (
  id          INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  page_id     INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES pages(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  sort_order  INTEGER NOT NULL,
  kind        TEXT NOT NULL,   -- 'channel'|'group_master'|'panel'

  channel_id  INTEGER REFERENCES mixer_channels(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  lighting_channel_id INTEGER REFERENCES lighting_channels(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  group_id    INTEGER REFERENCES lighting_groups(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  expanded    INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,   -- opening state, kind='group_master'

  panel_title TEXT,                          -- kind='panel'
  panel_width INTEGER,                       -- 1-4; the configured column count.
                                              -- Layout (§21.9) may widen it to 6;
                                              -- rows are computed, not stored.

  CHECK (
    (kind = 'channel'      AND (channel_id IS NOT NULL) != (lighting_channel_id IS NOT NULL))
    OR (kind = 'group_master' AND group_id IS NOT NULL)
    OR (kind = 'panel'     AND panel_title IS NOT NULL
                           AND panel_width BETWEEN 1 AND 4)
  )
);

CREATE TABLE page_buttons (
  id        INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  item_id   INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES page_items(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  col       INTEGER NOT NULL,               -- 0-based, < panel_width
  row       INTEGER NOT NULL,               -- 0-based; (row, col) is a row-major
                                             -- reading order, with no row limit
  label     TEXT NOT NULL,
  rule_id   INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES rules(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
  state_id  INTEGER REFERENCES derived_status(id) ON DELETE SET NULL,
  colour    TEXT,                            -- group palette token (§21.3)
  confirm   INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,
  UNIQUE(item_id, col, row)
);

-- Which pages a hirer may see. Empty = no hirer access.
CREATE TABLE hirer_pages (
  id      INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  page_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES pages(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  UNIQUE(page_id)
);

A page item never defines group membership — that lives on the group (§15.9), because rules and KNX bindings target groups too. A group_master item says only "this group's master goes here" (§21.9).

Pages leave indicator-only groups out (§9.4). Such a group has no fader, so a group_master item naming one is refused with validation_failed, and the generated default page does not place it.

expanded is the state the page opens in. An operator's expand toggle is session-only and does not write here: configuration is the venue's intent, the toggle is the moment's need.

ON DELETE RESTRICT on rule_id follows the delete-protection pattern of §15.1 — a rule bound to a button reports the binding rather than vanishing from under it, and §21.18's reference list includes page buttons.

Rows are computed per viewport, not stored. panel_width (1-4) is the configured column count; the layout may widen it to as many as 6 columns to keep buttons above their floor size (§21.9). (row, col) is a row-major reading order over however many rows the viewport produces — there is no three-row limit — so there is no grid to store and no way to configure a panel of unusably small targets.

#15.13 System and security

The show timer (§21.7) lives in system_state under domain='timer', storing only running and started_at plus any accumulated milliseconds from previous runs. Elapsed is computed, never stored — so the persister writes two fields on start and stop rather than continuously, and a restart mid-performance recovers the timer instead of zeroing it.

CREATE TABLE security_events (
  id         INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  timestamp  TEXT NOT NULL,
  event_type TEXT NOT NULL,
  user_ident TEXT,
  ip_address TEXT,
  detail     TEXT
);

CREATE INDEX idx_security_ts ON security_events(timestamp DESC);

CREATE TABLE system_state (
  id         INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
  domain     TEXT NOT NULL,
  key        TEXT NOT NULL,
  value      TEXT NOT NULL,
  updated_at TEXT NOT NULL,
  source     TEXT,
  UNIQUE(domain, key)
);

CREATE TABLE schema_versions (
  migration  TEXT PRIMARY KEY,       -- full filename, e.g. '003_add_bars.sql'
  applied_at TEXT NOT NULL
);

system_state write throttling. Rapidly-changing values — levels, fader positions — are written at most once per 500 ms through a dirty-flag check, not on every change. This avoids saturating the WAL during active fader use. Static changes — external control, device status, backup results — write immediately. All pending state is flushed on graceful shutdown (§12.4).

#16. API Reference

#16.1 Versioning and conventions

Every endpoint is served under /api/v1/. The prefix is omitted below for readability. A breaking change bumps to /api/v2/ with both served in parallel for one release cycle.

/health is deliberately unversioned and unauthenticated — external monitors and the reconnection screen should not have to track API versions.

#Error responses

Every non-2xx response carries the same envelope. Status codes alone cannot distinguish "not permitted" from "mixer offline" from "value out of range", and those three want different messages and different recovery.

{
  "error": {
    "code": "permission_denied",
    "message": "Channel 3 is not available to this session",
    "detail": { "channel_id": 3 },
    "request_id": "01J8XQ4K2M"
  }
}

message is safe to display. detail is machine-readable and varies per code. request_id appears in the response and in the log line for that request — it is the difference between "it failed" and a searchable incident.

The code vocabulary is closed, and grouped by what the client should do:

Code HTTP Client behaviour
unauthenticated 401 Re-authentication overlay (§6.5)
permission_denied 403 Show and stop; no retry
not_found 404 Show and stop
conflict 409 Reload and offer the diff (below)
in_use 409 Show the reference list (§21.20)
validation_failed 422 Field-level errors from detail
value_out_of_range 422 Clamp to detail.clamped and show the corrected value. Exception: a hirer's mixer level clamped to its ceiling is a success — REST answers 200 with "clamped": true, and the WebSocket nack carries the clamped dB (§16.8, B35)
device_unavailable 503 Show and offer retry
rate_limited 429 Countdown from detail.retry_after
internal_error 500 Show request_id; offer to copy it

WebSocket nack reasons draw from the same vocabulary (§16.8), so one list serves both transports and the frontend has one mapping from code to behaviour.

#Concurrent configuration edits

Control state resolves last-write-wins (§10.6). Configuration does not — two admins editing the same scene would silently lose one of their edits.

Every configuration PUT carries the updated_at value the client read:

PUT /scenes/12
If-Unmodified-Since-Version: 2026-09-08T14:22:03+12:00

A mismatch returns 409 conflict with the current record in detail.current. The UI offers reload or overwrite, showing what differs.

In practice this installation has one admin and the conflict will be rare. It is specified from the start because the column already exists, the check is one comparison, and the failure it prevents is silent — the worst kind to discover months later in a scene that stopped working.

Configuration entities only. Control endpoints and /system actions are unaffected.

#Continuous writes are not REST

Fader and level writes go over the WebSocket, not REST (§21.2). A drag coalesced to 30 Hz would otherwise produce 30 POSTs per second from one person, against a §23.1 budget of 100 requests per second in total — one operator on one fader consuming a third of it.

The division is continuous versus discrete, not important versus unimportant:

Over the WebSocket Over REST
Lighting channel and group levels Scene triggers
Master dimmer Mute toggles
Mixer fader positions Desk scene recall
Pan Projector and HDMI commands
All configuration and /system

The REST endpoints for continuous values are retained for scripting and testing, and are rate-limited accordingly. Interactive clients use the socket. Server-side permission enforcement is identical on both paths (§6.7).

Configuration entities follow standard REST, admin tier, and are not listed individually:

GET    /{entity}            List
POST   /{entity}            Create
GET    /{entity}/{id}       Detail
PUT    /{entity}/{id}       Update
DELETE /{entity}/{id}       Delete — 409 with a reference list if in use
GET    /{entity}/{id}/references   What refers to this

This applies to: scenes, scenes/{id}/actions, rules, derived-status, devices, knx/addresses, knx/device-groups, knx/bindings, lighting/channels, lighting/groups, lighting/bars, lighting/presets, lighting/profiles, mixer/channels, mixer/desk-scenes, hdmi/inputs, hdmi/outputs, hdmi/destinations, pages, pages/{id}/items.

Non-obvious endpoints are listed in full below.

Tier is annotated on every endpoint. No blanks — an unannotated endpoint is a specification defect.

#16.2 Origin policy

The API is same-origin. Access-Control-Allow-Origin is never set; the frontend is served from the same origin behind nginx, so CORS is not needed.

WebSocket upgrades have their Origin validated against the configured hostname and are rejected with 403 otherwise. HTTP requests with unexpected origins are served normally — no browser preflight will succeed and non-browser clients do not care — but are logged as unexpected_origin.

#16.3 Authentication

POST /auth/login              [public]
     { password }
     → JWT in httpOnly SameSite=Strict cookie, { tier, expires_at }
     No username; matched against both accounts, admin wins

POST /auth/hirer              [public]
     { pin }
     → JWT carrying identity only; permissions resolve live from state.hirer (§6.4)

POST /auth/logout             [any]
POST /auth/change-password    [admin, operator]
     { current_password, new_password }
     Updates the current tier's password, increments token_version
GET  /auth/session            [any]   Current tier and expiry

#16.4 First-run

GET  /setup/state             [public, first-run only]   Completed steps, next step
POST /setup/step/{n}          [public, first-run only]   Submit a step
POST /setup/complete          [public, first-run only]   Commit; sets first_run_completed

These return 403 once first_run_completed is true.

#16.6 Hirer configuration

GET  /hirer/config                 [admin]   Assigned pages and channel ceilings
PUT  /hirer/config                 [admin]   Writes hirer_pages and ceilings, then
                                             rebuilds state.hirer and applies
                                             immediately to any active session (§6.7)
POST /hirer/pin                    [admin]   { pin } — increments token_version
POST /hirer/enabled                [admin]   { enabled } — instant kill switch
GET  /hirer/conflicts              [admin]   Desk scenes recalling above a ceiling

/hirer/conflicts powers the warning in §21.20 — it reports where a permitted desk scene would set a channel above that channel's hirer ceiling, so the fader is clamped immediately after recall.

#16.7 System

GET  /health                       [public, unversioned]
     Normal:    200  { status: "ok", version, uptime }
     Emergency: 503  see below
     Liveness only. Served on both 80 and 443, deliberately unversioned
     so external monitors need not track the API version.

Emergency mode payload. When /data is unavailable (§4.6) the endpoint returns 503 with a fixed shape. This is the one response a remote monitor receives during the failure it most needs to characterise, and prose is not parseable:

{
  "status": "emergency",
  "version": "1.3.0",
  "reason": "data_unavailable",
  "detail": {
    "mount_state": "failed",
    "storage": { "detected": true, "smart": "failing", "media_errors": 4 },
    "last_backup": "2026-09-07T03:00:00+12:00",
    "backup_media_present": true
  }
}

reason is a closed set: data_unavailable, data_readonly, migration_failed, disk_full, not_installed (a freshly built appliance on which no application package has ever been installed).

last_backup and backup_media_present are the two facts that decide whether someone drives in tonight or in the morning. Both are read from /mnt/backup, which is a different device — so they are available precisely when /data is not, which is the whole reason the fields are there.

GET  /system/health                [admin]   Full vitals: CPU, memory, SSD SMART,
                                             partitions, backup media, uptime, clients
GET  /system/time                  [admin]   NTP status, RTC presence, degraded flag
GET  /system/version               [admin, operator]

GET  /system/network               [admin]
POST /system/network               [admin]   202 with a confirm_token; applies through
                                             the helper, then the reconnection flow (§10.8)
POST /system/network/confirm       [admin]   { confirm_token } — keeps the new address
GET  /system/network/state         [admin]   applied_at, reverts_at, the previous address

GET  /system/certs/history         [admin]   The served certificate (the current card), its
                                             expiry, and the renewal history
GET  /system/certs/token           [admin]   Whether a Cloudflare token is set — never the token
PUT  /system/certs/token           [admin]   Write-only
POST /system/certs/token/test      [admin]   Validate the Cloudflare token and zone
POST /system/certs/issue           [admin]   DNS-01; progress streamed over WebSocket
GET  /system/certs/download        [public]  The served certificate, application/x-pem-file (§6.16)
POST /system/certs/self-signed     [admin]

#16.8 WebSocket

WS /ws?v=1 JWT cookie validated on the HTTP upgrade; Origin validated. An unknown or missing version is refused: the handshake is completed and the socket immediately closed with code 4001, since a close code cannot be delivered before the upgrade completes. The client presents it as: "Refresh required — the application has been updated." This is the only thing that makes a stale service worker diagnosable rather than mysterious. The connection is tagged with the user's tier, determining what it receives. Unauthenticated upgrades are rejected with 401.

#Client to server

{ "type": "subscribe", "domains": ["lighting", "mixer", "devices"] }
{ "type": "resync",    "domains": ["lighting", "mixer", "devices", "projector", "hdmi"] }
{ "type": "ping" }
{ "type": "pong" }
{ "type": "background" }

{ "type": "set", "domain": "lighting", "id": 7, "value": 82.5, "token": 4413 }
{ "type": "set", "domain": "mixer",    "id": 3, "value": -5.0, "token": 4414 }

Control writes. domain is lighting, lighting_group, master or mixer. A lighting_group write sets every member's level (§9.4). The three lighting domains are operator fader writes, so their output glides over 75 ms while the stored level is the target at once (§7.2.3); REST writes do not glide. token is a client-generated monotonic integer, unique per connection, which the server echoes on acknowledgement — it is what ties a rejection back to the correct pending entry when several gestures are in flight (§21.2).

The server replies per write:

{ "type": "ack",  "token": 4413 }
{ "type": "nack", "token": 4414, "reason": "value_out_of_range", "value": -6.0 }

reason draws from the closed error vocabulary above. nack carries the authoritative value where one exists — a hirer write clamped to a ceiling returns the clamped value in dB, so the client settles there silently rather than reverting to a stale one. A clamp has succeeded (B35): it is not a failure to present, and value_out_of_range is the reason precisely so the client never treats it as one. permission_denied is reserved for a channel the session cannot reach at all. Mixer values on the wire are dB in both directions (§5.5); position is a client-side rendering concern only. On REST the equivalent write answers 200 with the applied db and "clamped": true, never a 4xx (§16.1).

Writes are applied in arrival order per connection. There is no batching of inbound writes: a coalesced drag at 30 Hz is 30 small frames, which is what a WebSocket is for.

#Connection lifecycle

A closed browser is detected immediately. The TCP FIN closes the socket and the connection handler unsubscribes it from the event bus.

A sleeping device is not. A tablet with the screen off or a laptop with the lid closed sends nothing — the socket goes quiet and is indistinguishable from an idle one. So the application owns liveness:

server → { "type": "ping" }        every 20 s
client → { "type": "pong" }
no pong within two intervals (40 s) → close the socket

§10.3 removes nginx's proxy_read_timeout on /ws precisely so an idle socket stays open. That makes detecting a dead one the application's job, not the proxy's.

#Backgrounded clients stop receiving continuous events

A suspended tablet still has meters composed, batched and queued for it. The bounded queues (§5.6) stop that growing without limit, but the work is wasted either way — and metering is the most expensive thing on the wire.

So the client says when it goes away:

{ "type": "background" }     → server sends discrete events only
{ "type": "resync", ... }    → on return; full snapshot per domain

On visibilitychange the browser gets a moment before suspension, which is enough for one frame. If it does not arrive — an abrupt sleep, a dropped network — the pong timeout catches it within 40 seconds and the socket closes.

Nothing new is needed on the way back. resync already exists and already carries a full snapshot per domain, and meters are deliberately not replayed (§16.8) because a stale meter is worse than none. A resync does receive one fresh mixer_meters frame, built from live state at that moment (below).

Backgrounding is not logging out. The session is governed by §6.6's visibility rule: the idle timer starts when the page is hidden, and the socket closing does not shorten it. A tablet picked up after ten minutes reconnects into the same session.

resync is sent automatically on reconnect and on returning to the foreground. The server replies with one full snapshot per domain — the same shape as a normal batched broadcast, carrying every tracked value with "source": "resync".

The client sends ping every 30 seconds to keep intermediaries from closing an idle connection.

#Server to client — batched state

During fades and rapid changes the server batches into frames at 10–15 fps rather than sending per-channel updates. One message carries everything that changed since the last frame.

{
  "type": "lighting_state",
  "channels": {
    "1": { "level": 85.0 },
    "2": { "level": 78.5, "r": 255, "g": 120, "b": 0 }
  },
  "master": 100.0,
  "bindings": { "3": true },
  "external_control": "off",
  "observed": null,
  "source": "fade"
}

Levels are 0–100 with one decimal; colour components are 0–255 (§9.2).

There is no groups section. A group fader sets its members' levels and has no stored value of its own (§9.4, B73), so clients show it from channels. bindings carries each binding rule's bank state, keyed by rule id — whether every member of its group is at its on_level (§21.11).

While external control is active, external_control is "detected" or "manual" and observed carries the levels read from the booth input for patched channels only (§7.2.7). Clients display observed in preference to channels whenever it is present, with an indicator saying so. channels continues to carry the controller's own model throughout, so the transition on resume is visible before it happens.

{
  "type": "mixer_state",
  "main":    { "db": 0.0,  "muted": false },
  "outputs": { "1": { "db": -6.0, "muted": false } },
  "inputs":  { "1": { "db": -5.0, "muted": false, "origin": "mixpad" } },
  "source": "sync"
}

Meters travel in their own message, not inside mixer_state. They change at a different rate, carry no control meaning, and a client that does not render them can ignore the type entirely.

{
  "type": "timer", "running": true, "started_at": "2026-09-10T19:42:11.400+12:00", "accumulated_ms": 0
}

{
  "type": "mixer_meters",
  "channels": { "1": [-12.4], "3": [-18.2, -17.9] },
  "at": 1757462011.482
}

origin is "app", "mixpad" or "surface" and drives the origin badge (§7.3, §21.13).

mixer_meters carries a list per channel, one entry per driver reference in sort order — index 0 is left on a stereo channel, and a mono channel is a one-element list rather than a scalar (§5.5). A channel with no meter data is absent from the object, not present at the floor, which is what lets the interface render no bar rather than an empty one.

Batched as a continuous event at 10–15 fps and never replayed on resync — a stale meter is worse than none. A resync instead gets one fresh mixer_meters frame, built from live state at the moment of sending. That is not a replay, so the reason above still holds; without it a channel whose reading is steady — a silent input — is never dirtied again after its first write (the state store drops unchanged writes, §5.6), and a client that connected later never saw its meter at all.

#Server to client — discrete events

Sent immediately, never batched:

{ "type": "device_status",    "device": "mixer", "status": "connected" }
{ "type": "scene_started",    "scene_id": 3, "triggered_by": "knx:1/0/1" }
{ "type": "scene_completed",  "scene_id": 3, "result": "partial" }
{ "type": "projector_state",  "state": "warming" }
{ "type": "hdmi_source",      "destination_id": 1, "input_id": 1, "diverged": false }
{ "type": "external_control", "state": "detected", "source": "booth_input" }
{ "type": "surface_bank",     "bank_id": 0, "name": "Venue Default" }
{ "type": "surface_touch",    "strip": 3, "touched": true }
{ "type": "banner",           "level": "amber", "key": "backup_media_absent",
                              "text": "Backup media not detected" }
{ "type": "progress",         "operation": "cert_issue", "step": 3, "of": 6,
                              "message": "Waiting for DNS propagation" }

progress carries live output for long operations — certificate issue, image capture, update application — so the UI can show real steps rather than an indeterminate spinner.

#17. Physical Installation

#17.1 Mounting and power

The controller mounts on a 1U rack shelf in the AV cupboard, labelled clearly.

Reference platform: powered by PoE. The switch port must supply 30 W — this is 802.3at PoE+, which delivers 25.5 W at the device. 802.3af (15.4 W at the port, 12.95 W at the device) is insufficient for the module, SSD and fan together.

Other platforms: mains power.

Either way, the controller must be on protected power. A UPS on the AV rack switch is the single largest reliability improvement available to this installation, and it also protects the KNX gateway and the DMX node.

#17.2 USB assignment

The reference carrier has two USB 3.2 Gen1 and two USB 2.0 ports.

Port Assignment
USB 3.0 #1 Backup USB — permanently inserted
USB 3.0 #2 Spare / recovery keyboard
USB 2.0 #1 USB-to-serial cable to the HDMI matrix (§7.5)
USB 2.0 #2 Spare — DMX adapter only if the USB fallback is ever needed

The DMX adapter previously held USB 2.0 #1. The eDMX8 MAX moved DMX to Ethernet, which freed that port for the serial cable — so the matrix costs nothing in ports that was not already spare.

The GPIO header is unused. The serial cable's run to the LKV422 terminal block goes through a DB9 screw-terminal breakout and is labelled at both ends like any other cable.

Record which physical port holds which device in docs/hardware/network_map.md, and do not move them.

If a power-limit warning appears with bus-powered devices attached, add usb_max_current_enable=1 to /boot/firmware/config.txt (§4.8).

#17.3 Labelling

Every cable labelled at both ends. The rack door carries the laminated device map and the recovery card (§13.7).

#17.4 Pre-installation checklist

Grouped by owner, so each list can be sent to the right person.

Symple Solutions

□ SSD partitioned per §2.3, golden image written to slot A
□ Read-only rootfs verified — a reboot discards test writes
□ /opt/auditorium symlink resolves to /data/app/current
□ Package signing public key present at /usr/local/share/auditorium/trusted-keys/*.pub
□ avc-reset-password present on the read-only rootfs
□ RTC coin cell fitted; clock survives a power cycle
□ Watchdog verified — a deliberate hang reboots the machine
□ SSD health baseline captured
□ Backup USB formatted and labelled AVC-BACKUP; auto-mount verified
□ Recovery USB written and boot-tested
□ Cold spare SSD in the rack drawer
□ Application installed; every integration tested on the bench
□ Production network configuration applied
□ Timezone Pacific/Auckland; NTP verified
□ TLS certificate issued for auditorium.obhs.school.nz
□ First-run wizard completed; no default credentials remain
□ Initial system image captured
□ Initial data backup verified against USB and the network destination
□ Venue baseline captured (§13.5)
□ "Restore Venue Default" scene created and marked protected
□ LKV422 serial port voltage measured BEFORE ordering the cable (§7.5)
□ Matching FTDI cable ordered from an authorised distributor —
  USB-RS232-WE-1800-BT_0.0, or the TTL-232R-WE equivalent per the measurement
□ Conductor colours confirmed against datasheet Table 5.1 with a meter
□ TXD→Rx / RXD→Tx crossover and common ground verified at the terminal block
□ Unused conductors individually insulated and heatshrunk
□ brltty removed (§4.12)
□ FTDI serial captured; udev rule written; /dev/hdmi-matrix resolves
□ FTDI serial recorded on the recovery card and in network_map.md
□ Application user confirmed in the dialout group
□ PAXXR returns a valid OKPxPx from the LKV422
□ LKV422 response terminator observed and recorded in docs/protocols/lkv422.md
□ LKV422 baud confirmed at 9600; not changed to 19200
□ LKV422 IR remote removed from the rack and stored with the recovery card
□ Control surface, if fitted: set to Mackie Control + Network, static address,
  SLAVE mode; settings saved and confirmed to survive a power cycle
□ Control surface hardware button map left at factory defaults
□ Control surface hire behaviour chosen and recorded (§7.6)
□ Cables labelled; device map and recovery card laminated and fitted

School IT

□ VLAN 30 configured; auditorium ports and SSID mapped
□ eDMX8 MAX reserved at 10.2.30.90; UDP 6454 permitted within the VLAN
□ eDMX8 booth input port: Broadcast Threshold set to zero (§7.2.7)
□ Node merge scheme and hold-last-state on data loss confirmed (§7.2.3)
□ Universe configuration verified — controller sees desk frames, no loop (§7.2.7)
□ Control surface, if fitted: 10.2.30.100 reserved; UDP 5004–5005 permitted
□ PoE port confirmed at 30 W (802.3at)
□ Switch on a UPS, confirmed operational
□ DHCP reservations set by MAC for every device in §3.1
□ DNS A record created; Cloudflare proxy disabled (grey cloud)
□ Cloudflare API token issued, scoped to the single zone, DNS edit only
□ Outbound firewall rules permitted per §3.4
□ SMB share or SFTP account created for network backup
□ Management address agreed for SSH access
□ §3.5 network exposure reviewed and accepted in writing

KNX integrator

□ Full group address list supplied as ETS CSV or XML export
□ Alarm zone armed/disarmed group address confirmed
□ Heartbeat group address reserved and left unassigned (§7.1)
□ External control status address reserved (§7.2.7)
□ Projector "changing state" status address reserved (§8.8)
□ Confirmed whether panels can render a multi-state indicator — if so, a single
  DPT 20.x or 5.010 enum replaces the projector's two boolean addresses (§8.8)
□ Dimmer internal fade time behaviour confirmed for each actuator
□ Status feedback addresses confirmed for every controlled circuit
□ The controller's KNX individual address recorded (knxd's, or the tunnel's the
  gateway assigns), so its own telegrams never trigger a rule (§8.7)

Audio engineer

□ CQ-20B static address set in MixPad, matching the reservation
□ Baseline CQ scenes configured and named
□ "Venue Default" CQ scene saved — REQUIRED, see §13.5
□ Channel naming agreed and entered in the controller
□ Stereo output link state agreed and matched in the configuration
□ Confirmed: MixPad connects over the CQ's WiFi, never Ethernet (§7.3)
□ Phantom power requirements documented per input
□ ST2 fed from the matrix's optical S/PDIF via the converter, not the 3.5 mm tap
□ ST2 gain set with headroom remaining — not pinned at maximum
□ Source devices set to PCM stereo output where the venue controls them
□ Matrix audio output selection set to HDMI 1 and recorded

#17.5 Hire run sheet

Referenced from §6.6 and §7.2.7, and short enough to laminate.

Before a hire

□ Change the hirer PIN
□ Enable hirer access
□ Confirm permitted channels, desk scenes and lighting groups
□ Recall the appropriate CQ desk scene as the baseline
□ If an external lighting desk is in use:
    □ Confirm it is patched to the booth DMX input — detection is automatic
    □ Confirm the UI shows "under external control" and the panel indicator lights
    □ Enable External Control manually ONLY if the desk bypasses the node
□ Verify the hirer surface loads on the venue tablet

After a hire

□ Disable hirer access
□ Confirm the visiting desk is powered off — the controller resumes 5 s later
□ Disable External Control, if it was set manually
□ Press "Restore Venue Default"
□ Verify phantom power matches the venue default (MixPad — not remotely visible)
□ Confirm the matrix's audio output selection is still HDMI 1 (front panel, §7.5)
□ Run Compare Baseline; investigate anything unexpected

#18. Phased Build Plan

#Phase 1 — Foundation

  • Golden image build script: partitioning, A/B slots, read-only rootfs, symlink, trust anchor
  • Platform abstraction layer with the Raspberry Pi implementation
  • systemd units, watchdog integration, NTP and timezone
  • knxd installed; gateway verified
  • Application skeleton: FastAPI, SQLite with WAL, migration runner, configuration loading
  • Device abstraction (§5.5): category interfaces, driver registry, capability declaration, config schemas, generic device configuration screens
  • State store and event bus (§5.6)
  • /health endpoint
  • Authentication: single password field, JWT cookies, tiers, rate limiting
  • First-run wizard, all seven steps
  • Admin shell: React, routing, authentication, navigation, design tokens

Milestone: the system boots, services start, an admin completes first-run setup and logs in, and device status is displayed.

#Phase 2 — Lighting

  • KNX group address library with CRUD and device grouping
  • KNX bulk import wizard: CSV, TSV and ETS XML with column mapping
  • KNX client: incoming listener, DPT codecs, rate-limited outgoing queue, live monitor
  • Art-Net and sACN implementation, send and receive, with ArtPoll health
  • eDMX8 MAX commissioning: addressing, universe assignment, port direction
  • opendmx driver for the spare adapter — written but unverified (§7.2.4)
  • Universe buffer, compositor, frame renderer
  • Fade engine with deadline-based interpolation
  • Fixture profiles: schema, seeded profiles, profile editor
  • Lighting model: channels, bars, groups, memberships, colour presets
  • Rules and derived status (§8) — the four stage banks, with forced multipliers and panel feedback
  • Stage plan editor: SVG layout, bar management, fixture placement, drag positioning, channel map, conflict detection, multi-select
  • Lighting view: group faders, individual faders, master, fade time
  • KNX status sync and feedback writes
  • Scene engine: triggers with all six match types, debounce, delay groups, priority, failure policy, execution log
  • External control: automatic detection from ArtDmx on the booth input, DMX output suspend, KNX bank lockout with status feedback, observed-level display, manual flag for direct-patched desks

Milestone: a KNX touch panel switches each stage bank on and off with the indicator following; plugging in a booth desk locks the banks out within one frame, lights the panel's external-control indicator, and shows the desk's real levels in the UI; unplugging it restores normal operation within five seconds.

#Phase 3 — Projector and HDMI

  • PJLink client with the full state model and authentication
  • Commands during warm-up and cool-down rejected with the reason shown, never queued (B52)
  • HDMI matrix client: LKV422Backend over pyserial-asyncio, plus the retained stub
  • Confirmed switching — send, settle, verify with PAXXR
  • Routing poll, including detection of front-panel and IR changes
  • Projector and video views
  • Scene actions for both domains

Milestone: a "Performance Start" scene executes end to end across every domain, and a source change made on the matrix's front panel appears in the interface within 30 seconds.

#Phase 4 — Audio

  • MixerDriver interface and the cq20b driver behind it
  • stub mixer driver — no scene recall, no pan, no metering, eight channels — and the interface and scene engine made to work against it (§5.5)
  • CQ-20B MIDI/TCP client with connection exclusivity handling
  • Native metering connection (§7.3), bench-tested and confirmed viable
  • Capabilities resolved at connect, with the interface degrading when metering is absent
  • NRPN codec and fader law conversion
  • Channel configuration with source keys and NRPN population
  • State tracking for inputs, outputs and Main LR
  • Linked stereo output handling
  • Desk scene library, including the Venue Default requirement
  • Mixer view: pinned Main Output, outputs drawer and page, input pagination
  • State sync after recall, with request pacing
  • Change origin tracking and the MixPad badge
  • Reconnection with refused/timed-out distinction
  • Scene actions for recall, fader and mute

Milestone: the mixer view stays in step with the CQ-20B, and scenes recall a baseline then adjust channels and outputs.

#Phase 5 — Hirer surface

  • Pages: schema, editor, panels, button-to-rule binding, group trays with collapse
  • Hirer permission model — page assignment plus channel ceilings
  • PIN authentication, an identity-only JWT with permissions resolved live, absolute expiry
  • Hirer surface: filtered channels with server-side ceilings, permitted desk scenes, permitted lighting
  • Instant kill switch, dropping active connections
  • Rate limiting and session audit logging
  • Hirer PWA manifest and install prompt
  • Ceiling conflict warnings

Milestone: a hirer signs in with a PIN, controls only what they are permitted, cannot exceed configured limits, and can be cut off instantly.

#Phase 6 — System management

  • Network configuration with the reconnection flow
  • Certificate management: DNS-01, self-signed fallback, renewal history, live progress
  • Device connection configuration with test and revert
  • SMTP configuration with test, mirrored to /srv/appliance
  • Backup: destinations, scheduling, history, integrity verification, restore
  • Venue baseline: capture, compare, restore
  • System image capture and restore
  • Recovery USB environment with its web interface
  • Application update: upload, verify, stage, apply, rollback, automatic recovery
  • A/B OS upgrade mechanism
  • System health screen with the platform layer

Milestone: the system is fully operable and maintainable from the admin interface, with no command line required for any routine operation.

#Phase 7 — Polish and hardening

  • Scheduled scene triggers
  • Complete scene execution and security log viewers
  • Pre-change snapshots on every destructive admin action
  • Retention pruning and low-disk auto-prune
  • Inline help throughout the admin interface, and the keyboard shortcut reference
  • Accessibility pass against the §24 checklist, including measured contrast
  • Performance testing under concurrent clients
  • 72-hour soak test (§22.7)
  • Documentation complete: architecture, protocols, hardware, porting, recovery, run sheets

Milestone: ready for production installation.

#Phase 8 — First thirty days

  • Two weeks of shadowed operation with the regular operator
  • Log review at day 30: unexpected errors, connection decay, resource trends
  • Backup and restore verified end to end, on real hardware, from the recovery USB
  • Venue baseline re-captured after any commissioning adjustments
  • Handover session and sign-off

Milestone: operating unattended, with the school able to run and recover it.

#Phase 9 — Control surface (optional)

Deliberately placed after Phase 8, and not on the critical path to a working system.

The reason is not caution about the hardware. The Bank 0 allocation in §7.6 — two microphones, HDMI, Main LR, four lighting rows — is a well-informed hypothesis about how the room is actually used. Phase 8 is what turns it into evidence. Building a static-layout surface before a term of real operation means committing to the layout at the point of least information.

  • RTP-MIDI transport: session negotiation, clock synchronisation, sequence handling (§7.6)
  • Mackie Control encode and decode, verified against the bench device
  • Scribble strip text and colour, including confirming the sysex device ID for the Extender
  • Bank model: schema, resolution, startup bank, bank-change actions
  • Per-strip scaling through A&H's fader law and the lighting scale
  • Touch arbitration with catch-up on release
  • Surface client as a ProtocolClient with backoff and resynchronise-not-replay
  • Graphical configurator (§21.25) with live echo, Identify, and validation
  • Hirer interaction: disable-during-hire toggle, enforced server-side
  • Surface configuration added to the venue baseline (§13.5)
  • Bench verification of MCU addresses, encoder push reporting, and colour sysex

Milestone: a scene recall moves the physical faders to match; an operator mixes two microphones and four lighting rows without touching the tablet; unplugging the surface mid-event changes nothing else.

#19. Documentation

#19.1 Definition of done

A feature is not complete until:

  • Code is written and tested
  • The API endpoint is documented, if applicable
  • Inline help is written for every new admin control
  • CHANGELOG.md is updated
  • ARCHITECTURE.md is updated if a significant decision was made

The tree is as built (October 2026). dmx-node.md sits with the protocol notes rather than the hardware ones, and the run sheets are a directory of separate documents because the controller serves each one, filtered by tier, from the Help sheet. Building, signing and key handover are one document, docs/build/packages.md, because one package format and one verification path serve application updates, OS upgrades and images alike (§14.1).

docs/protocols/cq20b.md is the authoritative protocol reference. Do not duplicate its values anywhere, including in this specification. The A&H PDFs are stored in the repository rather than linked, because vendor URLs do not stay stable.

#19.3 ARCHITECTURE.md

Records why, not what. A successor should be able to understand the reasoning behind:

  • SQLite rather than PostgreSQL
  • Single SSD with A/B root slots, and the failure-independence trade-off accepted in exchange for commodity replaceability
  • Application on /data with an atomic symlink, and why the read-only rootfs still earns its place
  • /srv/appliance existing to keep the device secret out of the backup archive
  • knxd as a daemon rather than KNXnet/IP in the application
  • Hybrid desk-scene recall plus parameter control for the CQ-20B
  • Level store → compositor → universe buffer, rather than writing to the buffer directly
  • The 0–100 lighting scale with conversion only at the protocol boundary
  • A custom Art-Net implementation rather than a library
  • The HDMI matrix on RS-232 rather than Ethernet, and why USB was chosen over the GPIO UART
  • Mackie Control over RTP-MIDI for the control surface rather than Xctl or custom hardware, and why arbitrary per-strip banking replaces MCU's fixed channel paging
  • Hirer permissions on entity columns rather than as JSON
  • asyncio throughout rather than threading
  • The platform abstraction layer and what it is anticipating
  • The accepted network-layer risk and the Venue Baseline response to it
