#20.2 Operational knowledge to transfer

The mechanics are in the documentation. These are the things that are learned rather than read, and each has caused or would cause a support call:

  • MixPad must connect over the CQ's WiFi, never Ethernet. The CQ permits one TCP MIDI connection and this application holds it. MixPad on Ethernet takes it and the mixer appears offline. This is the most likely cause of a mixer outage during an event, and a two-second fix once understood.
  • Metering comes from a second connection to the mixer (§7.3), separate from the MIDI one that carries control. If it is unavailable the mixer still works completely and only the meters are lost — the interface says so. Absent meters are not a mixer fault.
  • Only one MixPad can connect at a time. The CQ allows two MixPad clients, and the controller's metering connection is one of them, because it speaks MixPad's own protocol. Its MIDI connection is separate and does not count. A second tablet is refused, which is expected rather than a fault. The reverse also happens: if two MixPads are already connected when the controller starts, it is the one refused, and the meters go missing while everything else works. Disconnect one tablet and metering returns on its own, within five minutes at most.
  • The Venue Default CQ scene must be re-saved in MixPad whenever the venue's baseline audio setup legitimately changes. Otherwise "Restore Venue Default" quietly restores the wrong thing.
  • Anyone on the auditorium network can control the devices directly (§3.5). The permission model guards against accident, not intent. Supervise hires, and use Compare Baseline afterwards.
  • Stereo output link state cannot be read from the mixer. If the engineer changes it in MixPad, the controller's configuration must be updated by hand.
  • Restoring to replacement hardware requires re-entering device passwords, because the device secret is deliberately excluded from backups.
  • Replacing the HDMI matrix's USB serial cable requires updating the udev rule with the new cable's FTDI serial number, or the device path disappears and HDMI reports offline for no visible reason. The serial is on the recovery card.
  • The control surface, if fitted, has no PIN. Left active during a hire it gives whoever stands at it unrestricted control of the current bank. The default is to disable it while hirer access is on; if that was changed, know why.
  • The control surface's meters follow the same connection. They are dark whenever the mixer's native connection is unavailable, and the surface configurator says why.
  • If the surface is replaced or factory reset, it must be put back into Mackie Control + Network with a static address before it will connect. A surface in the wrong mode looks exactly like a network fault.
  • The HDMI matrix can be changed from its front panel and its IR remote, neither of which this application can prevent. The remote is stored with the recovery card rather than left in the rack, precisely so a hirer does not pick it up. Routing drift is corrected by pressing a source button or running "Restore Venue Default".
  • Backups are not encrypted, and the Cloudflare token is not in them. Restoring certificates needs the token re-entered or reissued. This was a deliberate choice (§13.2) — do not "fix" it by adding encryption without solving passphrase custody first.
  • The security log retains device IP addresses for 90 days (§6.14), which is likely personal information under the Privacy Act 2020. The school should hold this in its records.
  • How to configure hirer access and change the PIN
  • How to add and test a scene
  • How to add a mixer channel, output, or KNX address
  • How to run a manual backup and capture a system image
  • How to apply an update, and what happens if one fails
  • How to recover from an SSD failure using the recovery USB
  • Who to call for KNX, network, and audio issues

#20.3 Public repository

The application code has been public since 26 September 2026 (github.com/SympleNZ/Proskenion, MIT licence), so the school or a successor can continue maintenance whenever the developer moves on. It is published through a filtered public branch, never by pushing the working branch: what the checklist below excludes stays out of the public history. The repository should be self-contained — a developer with reasonable Python and React experience should be able to build, understand and extend the system from it alone.

HANDOVER.md covers credentials and private keys and is kept outside the public repository, referenced but not included. It is in .gitignore from the first commit, not added later — a file that has ever been committed remains in the history whether or not it is deleted.

Before publication (and before each later publish of the public branch):

□ Confirm HANDOVER.md was never committed — check the full history, not the working tree
□ Scan history for tokens, keys, hostnames and addresses
□ Confirm no signing private key is present in any commit
□ Confirm docs/hardware/network_map.md carries no credentials
□ Confirm the A&H PDFs may be redistributed, or replace them with references

The last item is easy to miss. The protocol PDFs are stored in the repository rather than linked because vendor URLs do not stay stable (§19.2), which is the right call for a private repository and needs checking before a public one. The repository is now public, so the check applies to every publish.

#21. User Interface

This section is the complete frontend specification, absorbed from the former companion document. Every screen, component, interaction and state is here, so there is no second document to fall out of step with.

#21.1 Technology

Layer Technology Notes
Framework React 19, TypeScript 7 Strict mode is a hard default in TS 7
Build Vite 8 Rolldown and Oxc pipeline (§5.1)
Unit tests Vitest Shares Vite's transform pipeline (§22.3)
Styling Tailwind CSS 4 CSS-first configuration via @theme
Components shadcn/ui Radix primitives underneath
Touch controls Pointer Events, no library Faders and sliders — see §10.3
Server state TanStack Query Configuration and list data
Live state External store via useSyncExternalStore See below
Routing React Router v6
Toasts Sonner

Theme: dark only. No toggle, no light mode. One stylesheet, no conditional class switching. An auditorium control surface used in low light has no use for a light theme, and it is one less thing to build and maintain.

Live state is not TanStack Query. Batched WebSocket frames (§16.8) carry every changed value at 10–15 fps. Routing those through per-channel query invalidations would trigger dozens of independent rerenders per frame and would not survive a multi-fixture fade on a tablet.

Instead, a single external store receives each frame and components subscribe with useSyncExternalStore — built into React 18 and upwards, exactly the right primitive, and no additional dependency. One frame produces one coordinated render of the affected components.

TanStack Query still handles configuration data: scene lists, the KNX library, device configuration. Anything that changes on a human timescale.

Fonts are self-hosted and served by nginx, never from Google Fonts — the VLAN may have no internet access. WOFF2, Latin subset, under 35 KB total.

src/styles/
  tokens.css       All CSS custom properties — single source of truth
  base.css         Reset, body, global elements
  components.css   shadcn variable overrides mapped to our tokens
  animations.css   Keyframes and transition utilities
  print.css        Print overrides — light background, text labels

tokens.css is imported first and everything references it. No component defines its own colour values.

#21.2 Frontend state architecture

This is the counterpart to §5.6 and carries the same weight. A successor should read it before writing any component, because every live-updating surface in the application depends on it and the failure modes are not obvious from the outside.

#Two kinds of state, two mechanisms

Live state Configuration state
Examples Fader positions, levels, device status, observed levels, surface bank Scene list, KNX library, fixture patch, mixer channel config
Changes 10–15 frames per second On a human timescale
Mechanism External store + useSyncExternalStore TanStack Query

Live state is not TanStack Query. Batched frames (§16.8) carry every changed value at 10–15 fps. Routing them through query invalidation triggers dozens of independent re-renders per frame and will not survive a multi-fixture fade on a tablet.

useSyncExternalStore is built into React and is exactly the right primitive. No additional dependency is required.

#Per-key subscription

The naive implementation keeps one version counter and notifies every subscriber on every change. At 15 fps with fifty subscribed components, a two-channel update re-renders the entire lighting view fifteen times a second.

The store keeps a listener registry keyed by the thing being watched. A frame applies all its changes, collects the set of touched keys, then notifies only those keys' listeners. Work is proportional to what changed, not to how many components exist.

subscribeLevel(channelId, cb)      // one lighting channel
subscribeColour(channelId, cb)     // one colour fixture
subscribeMixer(channelId, cb)      // one mixer strip
subscribeDevice(name, cb)          // one device status
subscribeObserved(channelId, cb)   // one channel, external control (§7.2.7)

Components subscribe at the narrowest level available. FixtureNode subscribes to its own channel; StagePlan subscribes to nothing. FaderStrip subscribes to its own channel; the mixer view subscribes to nothing. StatusBar subscribes to devices only.

#getSnapshot must return primitives

// Correct — a number, referentially stable
getLevel(id: number): number

// Wrong — a new object every call
getChannel(id: number): { level: number; colour: Colour }

A getSnapshot returning a fresh object on each call makes React re-render infinitely, because it compares by reference. This is the single most common way this hook is misused, and it fails loudly rather than subtly, so it is worth stating before someone finds it.

Colour genuinely needs an object. Cache it per channel and replace the reference only when a component actually changes, so equality still holds between frames.

#Optimistic updates are a separate layer

The obvious approach — write the optimistic value into the store, remember the previous one, restore it on failure — breaks on a sequence that occurs constantly:

t=0     User drags fader → optimistic value written, old value saved
t=40ms  Authoritative frame arrives with a different value
t=80ms  Server rejects the write → restores the saved value,
        which is now two updates stale

Two maps instead, with a resolution rule:

authoritative: Map<Key, Value>   // only frames write this
pending:       Map<Key, Value>   // only local gestures write this

getSnapshot(key) => pending.get(key) ?? authoritative.get(key)
  • Frames always write authoritative, whatever is pending. The authoritative model is never polluted by unconfirmed values.
  • On acknowledgement, delete the pending entry. The component falls through to a value that is already correct — no second write, no flash.
  • On rejection, delete the pending entry and trigger the visible rollback of §21.27. The component falls through to the authoritative value, which is current rather than stale.

Reconciliation is one map delete in both directions. That is the whole point of the split.

#Gesture arbitration

While a pointer is down on a fader, authoritative frames for that channel must not move it.

This is the same problem as the X-Touch's motor arbitration (§7.6), and it is resolved the same way deliberately — one concept, two surfaces:

Pointer down    Pending entry holds the gesture value; the component
                renders from pending and ignores authoritative
Frames arrive   Written to authoritative silently; the fader does not move
Pointer up      Pending entry cleared on acknowledgement; the fader
                settles to whatever the server actually holds

If a scene ran during the gesture (§10.6), the fader visibly moves on release. That is correct and honest — the scene is authoritative while running, and hiding the divergence would be worse.

On touch, the gesture starts when it is recognised, not at pointer-down (since v0.1.15). Fader rows scroll sideways, so a finger landing on a fader's track may be the start of a fader move or of a swipe along the row. Until it has moved 8 px it is neither: a release inside that is a tap, which positions the thumb; movement along the fader's own axis first is a fader drag; movement across it first is a scroll, which never changes the level. The fader's hit area carries touch-action: pan-x (pan-y on a horizontal fader), so the browser owns the swipe and cancels the pointer when it takes it. A touch landing on the thumb itself grabs at once without jumping. Mouse, pen and keyboard (§24.2) are unaffected: a mouse press is always a fader press. Horizontally scrolling fader rows also carry scroll arrows (§21.5).

A cancelled pointer ends at its last real position. A pointercancel carries no usable coordinates; the gesture ends where the pointer last actually was (it had been jumping to the top of travel, +10 dB on a mixer fader), and a touch the browser claimed as a scroll leaves the level untouched.

#Continuous writes go over the WebSocket

This is a change to §16, not only to the frontend.

§16.5 places fader writes on REST. A drag coalesced to 30 Hz produces 30 POSTs per second from a single person, against a §23.1 budget of 100 requests per second in total. One operator dragging one fader would consume a third of the budget, and two tablets during a soundcheck would exceed it.

The WebSocket is already open, already authenticated, and already carries state in the other direction.

{ "type": "set", "domain": "lighting", "id": 7, "value": 82.5, "token": 4413 }
{ "type": "ack", "token": 4413 }
{ "type": "nack", "token": 4413, "reason": "permission_denied" }

The token ties an acknowledgement or rejection back to the correct pending entry — without it, the two-map model cannot know which gesture a rejection refers to when several are in flight.

Lighting writes over the WebSocket glide (since v0.1.16). A fixture, group or master fader write moves the DMX output to its new value over 75 ms on the server, while the stored level — and so the pending overlay's reconciliation — is the target at once (§7.2.3). Fader writes over Wi-Fi arrive irregularly; written straight to the output, each was a jump the room saw as flicker. REST writes, scenes, rules and explicit fades do not glide.

REST retains discrete actions: scene triggers, mute toggles, desk scene recall, configuration, and everything under /system. The division is continuous versus discrete, not important versus unimportant.

Server-side permission enforcement is unchanged and applies identically to WebSocket writes (§6.7).

#Fader law is client-side data

Position-to-dB conversion happens in the browser, not on the server. A 60 fps drag cannot round-trip per position, and the law belongs to the driver rather than to the core (§5.5).

The law table is fetched once per mixer device alongside capabilities, cached with configuration state, and interpolated locally:

positionToDb(law, 0.62)   // → -4.2
dbToPosition(law, -5.0)   // → 0.608

dB goes on the wire in both directions. The store holds dB; components convert to position only when drawing. That keeps the pending overlay and every set message in core units, so a rejection carrying an authoritative dB value needs no conversion to reconcile.

The control surface runs the same table server-side for its own faders, so a physical thumb and an on-screen thumb sit at the same place for the same level (§7.6).

#Detents are gesture behaviour, applied before the write

A law point marked detent (§5.5) holds the fader at that value. The logic lives in the gesture handler, not in the store and not on the server:

pointer moves
  → position → dB via the law
  → if within 1.5 dB of a detent and the break-out counter has not run out:
        use the detent's exact dB
        increment the counter only if this move landed beyond the zone,
        and reset it whenever the gesture is back inside
    else:
        use the interpolated dB, reset the counter
  → write to `pending`, send over the WebSocket

Snap to the dB, never to the position. Snapping a position and converting gives 0.03 dB; snapping the value gives 0.0. The second is what a scene will save and what a hirer ceiling compares against.

Breaking out takes a deliberate push. A counter, not a distance threshold: roughly six pointer moves beyond the zone edge before the detent releases. A distance threshold behaves differently on a 4K display and a phone; a movement count behaves the same on both, because it counts intent rather than pixels.

The counter resets whenever the gesture leaves the zone, so approaching unity from either direction feels identical.

While held, the cap shows a ring and the readout changes colour (§21.13), and aria-valuetext announces "0.0 decibels, unity" so the detent is not a visual-only affordance.

Detents apply to mixer faders only. Lighting is 0–100 linear with nothing to return to, and a detent at 100% would fight the most common movement there.

#Store shape

Meters live beside the mixer state, not inside it:

meters: Map<number, (number|null)[]>   // channel → dB per ref, index 0 = left

They arrive in their own message at their own rate (§16.8), carry no control meaning, and are never written to pending — there is no gesture that produces a meter. subscribeMeter(channelId, cb) is a separate subscription so a strip re-renders its bar without re-rendering its fader.

Peak hold and ballistics are client-side. Fast attack, slow release, with the peak held about 1.4 seconds then falling. The server sends instantaneous values; smoothing them there would mean every client got one surface's idea of ballistics, and a meter that has been averaged twice reads sluggishly.

Nothing is replayed on resync. A stale meter is worse than none. A resync instead receives one fresh mixer_meters frame built from live state (§16.8), so a steady — even silent — input shows its meter to a client that connected late.

interface LiveState {
  lighting: {
    levels:    Map<number, number>      // 0–100, one decimal
    colours:   Map<number, Colour>      // 0–255 components
    master:    number                    // no groups map: a group fader sets levels (§9.4)
    bindings:  Map<number, boolean>     // KNX bank states, §7.1
    observed:  Map<number, number>      // external control only, §7.2.7
    externalControl: 'off' | 'detected' | 'manual'
  }
  mixer:    Map<number, { db: number|null; muted: boolean; origin: Origin }>
  meters:   Map<number, (number|null)[]>   // display only, never persisted
  devices:  Map<DeviceName, DeviceStatus>
  scenes:   { running: Set<number>; lastResult: SceneResult | null }
  surface:  { connected: boolean; bank: number; touched: Set<number> }
}

observed and levels are separate maps because the backend keeps them separate for the same reason (§7.2.7). Components read whichever the display rule selects; they never merge them.

#Why not Zustand

Zustand is the obvious candidate, is built on this same hook, and would be adequate at this scale — fifty subscribers is nothing.

It was not chosen for two reasons. It evaluates every selector on every change and compares results, which is O(subscribers) rather than O(changed keys). More decisively, it solves none of the hard part: the pending overlay, the acknowledgement protocol and gesture arbitration all have to be built regardless.

Given that, the store is roughly 150 lines with no dependency — the same reasoning that decided the Art-Net client (§7.2.5) and the fader implementation (§21.5).

Zustand as the subscription mechanics underneath, with the pending layer built on top, is a defensible substitute if a successor would rather not own that code. The pending model above is what must be preserved either way.

#21.3 Design tokens

#Background layers

Four levels of dark. Too few and everything is flat; too many and nothing feels grounded.

--color-bg-base:     #0A0F1A   /* Page background, deepest */
--color-bg-surface:  #111827   /* Cards, panels, sidebar */
--color-bg-elevated: #1C2B3A   /* Modals, sheets, popovers */
--color-bg-overlay:  #243447   /* Hover, selected items */

Each layer is six to eight lightness points above the previous. Not neutral grey — a slight blue-navy cast throughout holds the technical character.

Elevation is expressed through background colour, not shadow. On a dark background a drop shadow darkens what is already dark and reads as nothing.

.elevated {
  background: var(--color-bg-elevated);
  border-top: 1px solid rgb(255 255 255 / 0.06);   /* catches ambient light */
}

.overlay {
  background: var(--color-bg-overlay);
  border: 1px solid rgb(255 255 255 / 0.08);
  box-shadow: 0 24px 64px rgb(0 0 0 / 0.6);        /* positioning context only */
}

#Accent

--color-teal-500: #00B8A0   /* Primary — active states, CTAs, selection */
--color-teal-400: #00D4BC   /* Hover on teal elements */
--color-teal-300: #33DFC9   /* Focus rings, highlights */
--color-teal-900: #00241E   /* Teal-tinted backgrounds */

Used for active navigation, selection, primary buttons, progress, healthy status, and fader fill.

#Semantic colours

Two sets. This split exists because the original single set failed contrast as text on the upper background layers — which is exactly where error messages inside modals and sheets appear.

/* Fills, borders, LEDs, indicators */
--color-success: #22C55E
--color-warning: #F0A500
--color-danger:  #EF4444
--color-info:    #3B82F6

/* Text on any background layer — lighter, contrast-checked */
--color-success-text: #5EE08A
--color-warning-text: #FFC24D
--color-danger-text:  #FF7B7B
--color-info-text:    #7FB0FF

Semantic colours never appear as large fills. They are indicators, badges, dots and border accents. The interface stays dark; these are signals, not decoration.

#Group palette

Identity colours for lighting groups, mixer channel accents, page panels and control surface scribble strips. Ten hues and two neutrals, chosen by measurement rather than by eye.

--group-rose:      #FF4B82   /*   8°  L*60 */
--group-salmon:    #FF7F68   /*  36°  L*67 */
--group-tangerine: #E17B1B   /*  62°  L*62 */
--group-amber:     #E8C26C   /*  86°  L*80 */
--group-lime:      #A3BC00   /* 111°  L*72 */
--group-fern:      #93C896   /* 144°  L*76 */
--group-ocean:     #1CDAF1   /* 214°  L*80 */
--group-azure:     #00A5FE   /* 266°  L*65 */
--group-violet:    #A482C9   /* 310°  L*60 */
--group-orchid:    #F99FD8   /* 340°  L*76 */
--group-silver:    #B4C0CC   /* neutral  L*77 */
--group-white:     #E8EEF4   /* neutral  L*94 */

Every value clears 6:1 on both bg-base and bg-surface, so one colour carries a 3 px accent, a tray tint and an LED without needing variants.

#Selection rules

Measured with CIEDE2000, not CIE76. CIE76 overstates differences in the blue region — an earlier revision of this palette contained two blues measuring ΔE 27 apart that were plainly hard to separate, because under CIEDE2000 they were only 13. Anything added here must be checked the same way.

Hue separation is a constraint in its own right. Two colours can measure far apart and still read as one colour at two brightnesses. Every adjacent pair is at least 24° apart in hue and at least ΔE2000 18.

The blue region needs a wider gap than the reds. Human hue discrimination is poorest between roughly 185° and 290°, so that span requires 44° between members. Ocean and Azure sit 52° apart for exactly this reason.

Distance from the semantic colours is deliberately relaxed. Identity and status appear in different places — a group colour is a strip edge, a tray tint, a panel LED, while green, amber and red live in the status bar, on mute buttons and in banners. The constraint budget is spent on separating the palette from itself, which is what matters when two groups sit side by side. The primary teal is the exception and keeps a wider berth, because it shares contexts.

Colour is a secondary signal (§24.1) — a group tray carries its name and channels carry theirs. That is what makes twelve possible: demanding full separation under colour-blindness collapses the red–green axis and leaves about six usable colours. Instead, confusable pairs separate by lightness: Rose against Fern differ by 16 points of L*, Salmon against Amber by 13, and no confusable pair is closer than 8.

#There is no true red

On a near-black background the only reds bright enough to see are lighter than --color-danger, and a light red is a salmon. Anything darker either disappears — L*45 measures 3.6:1 — or lands on #EF4444 itself, where ΔE2000 falls to 1.4.

Red is also the one status colour that fills a large control: the mute button, three pixels from a channel's accent stripe. Salmon is as close as this theme allows, and the gap is a property of the dark theme rather than an oversight.

#The two neutrals

They sit outside the hue rules, having no hue to separate, and are the only members that stay reliably distinguishable under colour-blindness — everything chromatic partially desaturates while these do not.

White reads as emphasis rather than identity. It is the brightest thing available and dominates whatever sits beside it, so it belongs on a Main or master strip rather than an ordinary group. It is deliberately a shade below --color-text-primary so labels stay dominant over the accent carrying them.

Silver must stay clear of the disabled grey. #4A5B6E means not configured in the status bar and inactive on a control, so a dim grey group would read as broken. Silver sits 39 points of L above it. A mid grey is not usable at all* — #8794A3 lands on --color-text-secondary at ΔE 6.0 and would read as body text.

#Text

--color-text-primary:   #F0F4F8   /* Headings, active values */
--color-text-secondary: #8A9BB0   /* Body text, descriptions */
--color-text-muted:     #7589A0   /* Placeholders, timestamps, meta — base and surface only */
--color-text-disabled:  #4A5B6E   /* Disabled controls only */
--color-text-inverse:   #0A0F1A   /* Text on teal */

--color-text-muted was originally #4A5B6E, which measures 2.75:1 on the base background — below even the 3:1 large-text threshold — while being specified for placeholders and timestamps. Disabled text is exempt from contrast requirements; placeholders and timestamps are not.

It was lightened to #6E8299, which still measured 4.49:1 on bg-surface — failing the 4.5:1 threshold by a hundredth, on the layer where most timestamps actually sit. It is now #7589A0, clearing surface at 4.94:1 with margin.

The constraint is which background, not which size. WCAG treats 11px and 13px identically — both are normal text requiring 4.5:1, and the relaxed 3:1 threshold does not begin until 24px. Muted is permitted on bg-base and bg-surface, and prohibited on bg-elevated and bg-overlay, where it measures 4.01 and 3.53.

A separate --color-text-disabled token carries the original #4A5B6E for genuinely inactive controls, which WCAG exempts.

Measured ratios are in §24.4.

#Device status

--color-status-green: #22C55E   /* Connected and healthy */
--color-status-amber: #F0A500   /* Degraded, or showing last-known values */
--color-status-red:   #EF4444   /* Unreachable or error */
--color-status-grey:  #4A5B6E   /* Not configured */

Never used alone — every status pairs colour with an icon (§21.7, §24.1).

#Faders

--color-fader-track: #060C14
--color-fader-grad:  linear-gradient(to top,
                       #00B8A0 0%, #22C55E 62%, #F0A500 78%,
                       #EF4444 92%, #CC1111 100%);
--color-fader-cover: #060C14   /* Hides the gradient above the thumb */
--color-fader-thumb: #FFFFFF
--color-fader-muted: #4A5B6E
--color-mute-active: #EF4444
--color-mute-off:    #243447

Gradient fill. The track carries the full gradient at all times. A dark cover element sits over it and shrinks downward as the fader rises, revealing colour from the bottom up:

  • Teal and green are visible at normal operating levels, below unity
  • Amber appears as the fader approaches and passes unity (0 dB)
  • Red appears only in the hot zone, +3 dB and above

This gives honest position feedback without implying signal metering. The colour says where in the gain range the fader sits, not how loud anything is (§7.3).

Muted channels extend the cover to full, hiding the gradient entirely. The thumb stays visible and the strip drops to 60% opacity — but opacity is never the only signal (§24.1).

#Typography

--font-body: 'DM Sans', system-ui, sans-serif;   /* All UI text */
--font-mono: 'JetBrains Mono', monospace;        /* Technical values */

One family for text, one for data. Hierarchy comes from weight and size, not from a second typeface. The decorative serif carried in earlier revisions has been removed — the most recent mockup had already dropped it, it added ~15 KB to a self-hosted payload, and a control surface does not need a display face.

JetBrains Mono carries anything technical: DMX addresses, driver references, group addresses, dB readings, log output, IP addresses. A technical value should be recognisable as one by its typeface alone.

--text-xs:      11px / 1.4    /* Status labels, timestamps, channel numbers */
--text-sm:      13px / 1.5    /* Table cells, secondary labels, badges */
--text-base:    15px / 1.65   /* Body text, form labels */
--text-lg:      17px / 1.5    /* Sub-headers, card titles */
--text-xl:      21px / 1.4    /* Panel headers, view titles */
--text-2xl:     26px / 1.3    /* Screen titles, mobile */
--text-3xl:     32px / 1.2    /* Section headings, desktop */

400   Body text, descriptions
500   Labels, navigation, table headers
600   Section headings, buttons, active states
700   Screen titles

Body copy holds to under 80 characters per line.

#Spacing

4px base. No arbitrary values.

--space-1:  4px    --space-2:  8px    --space-3: 12px   --space-4: 16px
--space-5: 20px    --space-6: 24px    --space-8: 32px   --space-10: 40px
--space-12: 48px   --space-16: 64px   --space-20: 80px

Component padding --space-4 standard and --space-3 compact; section gap --space-8; page margin --space-16 desktop and --space-4 mobile; control stack gap --space-3.

#Radius

--radius-sm:   4px      /* Badges, chips, small inputs */
--radius-md:   8px      /* Cards, panels, buttons, modals */
--radius-lg:   12px     /* Large panels, stage plan container */
--radius-xl:   16px     /* Mobile bottom sheets */
--radius-full: 9999px   /* Status dots, toggles, pills */

Everything uses one of these. Never a custom value.

#Touch targets

Requirements, not guidance.

All interactive elements   44 × 44px minimum   (WCAG 2.5.5)
Standard buttons           44px height
Primary operator buttons   64px height          (scene cards, source buttons)
Hirer surface              72px height
Fader thumb                32 × 32px
Mute button                44 × 44px

The mute button minimum is called out because an operator may have one finger on a fader and need to hit mute with another.

#Animation

--duration-fast:      100ms   /* Dot colour, button active */
--duration-base:      200ms   /* Hover, tooltip */
--duration-moderate:  300ms   /* Panel slide, sheet open */
--duration-slow:      400ms   /* Modal appear */
--duration-very-slow: 600ms   /* Scene result reveal */

--ease-out:    cubic-bezier(0, 0, 0.2, 1);       /* Panels, modals */
--ease-in-out: cubic-bezier(0.4, 0, 0.2, 1);     /* State toggles */
--ease-spring: cubic-bezier(0.34, 1.56, 0.64, 1); /* Fader thumb */

Motion is reserved for things that answer an action — opening, confirming, revealing a result. Fixture nodes on the stage plan transition over the actual fade duration, so a 2-second DMX fade takes exactly 2 seconds on screen. That is the one place where non-interactive motion is carrying information.

@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) {
  *, *::before, *::after {
    transition-duration: 50ms !important;
    animation-duration: 50ms !important;
  }
  .led-pulse    { animation: none; }
  .fixture-node { transition: none; }
}

Static LED glow is retained; only the pulse stops.

#Interactive states

Every interactive element defines all five.

Default    Base appearance
Hover      Background moves one layer up, teal border hint
Focus      Teal ring, 2px, 2px offset
Active     Scale 0.97, deeper background
Disabled   40% opacity, not-allowed cursor, no hover

:focus-visible {
  outline: 2px solid var(--color-teal-500);
  outline-offset: 2px;
  border-radius: var(--radius-sm);
}

:focus-visible, not :focus — rings for keyboard navigation, suppressed for mouse. Never removed without a replacement.

#Quick reference

Status dot         10px circle, --radius-full, radial gradient + glow
Nav item active    bg --color-bg-surface, 3px teal left border
Card               bg --color-bg-surface, --radius-md, border rgb(255 255 255/.06)
Button primary     bg teal-500, text inverse, --radius-md, hover teal-400
Button secondary   bg --color-bg-elevated, text primary, --radius-md
Button destructive bg transparent, text danger-text, border danger, hover danger/10
Input              bg --color-bg-base, border rgb(255 255 255/.12), focus teal border
Fader track        bg --color-fader-track, --radius-full, gradient + cover
Scene card         bg --color-bg-surface, --radius-lg, hover --color-bg-elevated
Table header       bg --color-bg-elevated, text muted, --text-xs, weight 600
Table row hover    bg --color-bg-surface

#21.4 Hardware aesthetic

The control surfaces borrow from physical equipment. It is deliberate and it is bounded.

Applied to: status LEDs, mute buttons, scene card execution state, stage plan fixture nodes, active navigation indicators, the external control toggle.

Not applied to: text, tables, form inputs, or admin configuration screens. The metaphor lives in the control surfaces; the admin interface is clean and professional, not theatrical.

#LED indicators

A real LED has three visual characteristics: a bright emitter, a bloom that falls off rapidly, and a specular highlight on the lens.

.led {
  width: 10px;
  height: 10px;
  border-radius: 50%;
}

.led-green {
  background: radial-gradient(circle at 35% 35%,
    #AFFFEE, #22C55E 40%, #15803D 100%);
  box-shadow:
    0 0  4px 2px rgb(34 197 94 / 0.80),
    0 0 10px 4px rgb(34 197 94 / 0.40),
    0 0 20px 6px rgb(34 197 94 / 0.15);
}

.led-amber {
  background: radial-gradient(circle at 35% 35%,
    #FFF3AA, #F0A500 40%, #B45309 100%);
  box-shadow:
    0 0  4px 2px rgb(240 165 0 / 0.90),
    0 0 10px 4px rgb(240 165 0 / 0.45),
    0 0 20px 6px rgb(240 165 0 / 0.18);
}

.led-red {
  background: radial-gradient(circle at 35% 35%,
    #FFAAAA, #EF4444 40%, #991B1B 100%);
  box-shadow:
    0 0  4px 2px rgb(239 68 68 / 0.90),
    0 0 12px 5px rgb(239 68 68 / 0.45),
    0 0 24px 8px rgb(239 68 68 / 0.18);
}

/* Not configured — an unlit lamp behind darkened glass */
.led-grey {
  background: radial-gradient(circle at 35% 35%,
    #6B7E94, #2D3D4F 40%, #1C2B3A 100%);
  box-shadow: inset 0 1px 2px rgb(0 0 0 / 0.5);
}

@keyframes led-pulse {
  0%, 100% { box-shadow: 0 0 4px 2px rgb(240 165 0/.9), 0 0 10px 4px rgb(240 165 0/.45); }
  50%      { box-shadow: 0 0 2px 1px rgb(240 165 0/.4), 0 0  6px 2px rgb(240 165 0/.20); }
}
.led-reconnecting { animation: led-pulse 1.8s var(--ease-in-out) infinite; }

The unlit state matters as much as the lit ones — no outward glow, a slight inset shadow.

#Faders

Real faders sit in machined channels, slightly recessed.

.fader-track {
  background: linear-gradient(to bottom,
    #060C14, #0F1C2A 20%, #0F1C2A 80%, #1A2D40);
  box-shadow:
    inset 0  2px 4px rgb(0 0 0 / 0.8),
    inset 0 -1px 0   rgb(255 255 255 / 0.04);
  border-radius: var(--radius-full);
}

.fader-thumb {
  background: linear-gradient(to bottom, #FFFFFF, #C8D8E8 60%, #A0B4C8);
  box-shadow:
    0 2px 6px rgb(0 0 0 / 0.6),
    0 1px 2px rgb(0 0 0 / 0.8),
    inset 0 1px 0 rgb(255 255 255 / 0.9);
  border-radius: var(--radius-sm);
}

The strips follow the mockups (docs/operator-views.html). The thumb travels inside a box inset from the track by half the thumb's height at each end, so at either end of travel it sits wholly inside the track; what a finger touches and where the thumb is drawn share that one coordinate system, and the printed scale is laid out on the same inset.

#Mute buttons

Console mute buttons are illuminated, so the LED metaphor applies directly.

.mute-active {
  background: #2A0A0A;
  color: #FF7B7B;                    /* danger-text — contrast-checked */
  border: 1px solid #7F1D1D;
  box-shadow:
    0 0  6px 2px rgb(239 68 68 / 0.7),
    0 0 14px 4px rgb(239 68 68 / 0.3),
    inset 0 1px 0 rgb(255 255 255 / 0.08);
}

.mute-inactive {
  background: #111827;
  color: var(--color-text-secondary);   /* was muted — failed contrast at 2.55:1 */
  border: 1px solid #1F2D3D;
  box-shadow: inset 0 2px 4px rgb(0 0 0 / 0.6);   /* pressed-in */
}

#Scene cards

.scene-card-executing {
  border-color: rgb(0 184 160 / 0.6);
  box-shadow:
    0 0  0   1px rgb(0 184 160 / 0.30),
    0 0 16px 4px rgb(0 184 160 / 0.20),
    inset 0 0 24px rgb(0 184 160 / 0.05);
}

On completion the card blooms briefly in the result colour — green, amber or red — then settles.

#Channel strip accent

Three pixels of group colour along the top of each strip. Glanceable grouping without labels; row 1 amber, row 2 blue, visible from across the room.

#Fixture glow

SVG filters give a proper soft glow that box-shadow cannot manage on arbitrary shapes.

<defs>
  <radialGradient id="fixture-grad-{id}">
    <stop offset="0%"   stop-color="#FFFAF0" />   <!-- hot centre -->
    <stop offset="40%"  stop-color="{colour}" />
    <stop offset="100%" stop-color="{colour-dark}" />
  </radialGradient>

  <filter id="fixture-glow-{id}">
    <feGaussianBlur stdDeviation="{level / 100 * 5}" result="blur" />
    <feMerge>
      <feMergeNode in="blur" />
      <feMergeNode in="SourceGraphic" />
    </feMerge>
  </filter>
</defs>

<circle cx="{x}" cy="{y}" r="12"
        fill="url(#fixture-grad-{id})"
        filter="url(#fixture-glow-{id})" />

One formula, one scale. stdDeviation = level / 100 × 5, with level on the 0–100 lighting scale (§9.2). Earlier revisions carried two different formulas in two places, one assuming 0–100 and the other 0–255.

Level 0        Hollow circle, no filter, dark fill
Level 1–25     Faint glow, barely filled
Level 26–75    Visible glow, half filled
Level 76–99    Strong glow
Level 100      Full glow, bright specular, maximum bloom

Filters are defined per fixture, not per level value — 100 filter definitions would be absurd. The stdDeviation attribute updates with the level and transitions over the fade duration.

RGB fixtures use their actual colour in the gradient.

#21.5 Components

Built once, shared across all three shells.

FaderStrip          Vertical fader + label + value + mute
                    Props: channel, value, muted, maxValue, showPan, origin,
                           onValueChange, onMuteToggle
                    Keyboard: §24.2

HorizontalScroller  overflow-x-auto, scroll-snap, overscroll-contain
                    Props: children, snapAlign
                    Scroll arrows: a triangle in the blank margin at each end,
                    shown only while there is more content that way; a tap
                    scrolls one visible width, clamped to the end. At least
                    44 px, with an accessible name ("Scroll right").
                    On every horizontally scrolling fader row: Pages, Lighting
                    groups and fixtures, the phone Mixer row

PageChips           Scrollable range chips with an active indicator
                    Props: pages, activeIndex, onSelect

StatusBar           Device indicators + detail trigger + account popover
                    Props: devices

SceneCard           Operator trigger button
                    Props: scene, onTrigger, isExecuting, lastResult
                    States: idle, executing, success, partial, failed

DeviceDetailSheet   Health detail for one device
                    Props: device, status, lastSeen, lastError, reconnectionCount

ConfirmDialog       Destructive-action wrapper
                    Props: title, description, confirmLabel, onConfirm, isDangerous

EmptyState          Illustration + primary + secondary + optional action
                    Props: icon, title, description, cta

LoadingOverlay      Full-screen reconnection or progress wait
                    Polls a target URL until it responds

ProgressPanel       Streamed step progress with live output
                    Props: operation, steps, currentStep, output

PageHeader          Title + subtitle + actions, consistent across admin

StagePlan           SVG stage layout with interactive fixture nodes
                    Props: mode, bars, fixtures, levels, colours, permitted,
                           onLevelChange, onSelect

FixtureNode         One SVG fixture with LED glow
                    Props: fixture, level, colour, selected, permitted, mode,
                           onTap, onDoubleTap

ReferenceGuard      Delete-protection dialog listing what refers to an entity
                    Props: entity, references, onCancel

src/
  components/
    shared/     Cross-shell components, as above
    operator/
    admin/
    hirer/
    ui/         shadcn base components
  hooks/
    useLiveState.ts     External store + useSyncExternalStore
    useWebSocket.ts     Connection, reconnection, resync
    useToast.ts
    useDeviceStatus.ts
  pages/
    setup/  login/  operator/  admin/  hirer/
  styles/

#21.6 Navigation

Three shells, one codebase, different entry points.

Operator — a tab strip at the top on every supported width; phones are portrait-only (§21.9, B68). Pages is the default landing.

Navigation is never at the bottom: that edge belongs to the status bar (§21.7), and putting tabs there too would place two unrelated things in one strip.

Pages  |  Scenes  |  Mixer  |  Lighting  |  Stage Plan  |  Video  |  Projector

Pages (§21.9) is the everyday surface; the rest is the advanced view. A page carries what the room needs to hand — a few channels, a few buttons. Mixer and Lighting hold the full channel list and every registered fixture, for rigging rather than running.

The stage plan stays its own view because it is spatial rather than a strip flow, and cannot fold into a page.

Admin — sidebar on desktop, drawer behind a hamburger on mobile.

CONTROL          CONFIGURE        SYSTEM           ACCOUNT
  Scenes           KNX Library      Network          Users
  Rules            Lighting         Certificates     Help
  Hirer Access     Mixer            Devices
                   HDMI             Email
                   Control Surface  Backup
                                    Updates
                                    Health
                                    Logs

Focus follows navigation (§24.7). After an in-app navigation each shell moves focus to the new screen's heading (every screen has an h1, visually hidden where the design shows none, made focusable without joining the tab order), so a screen reader announces the new screen and the next Tab continues into it. Not on first load or a redirect, never into an open sheet or dialog (its focus trap owns focus), and never while focus is in a text field. If the heading has not rendered within a second, focus goes to #main. The active navigation item carries a visually hidden ", current page" alongside aria-current, and the skip link shows on :focus, not only :focus-visible.

Admin → System → power. Restart (the application), reboot and shut down (power off) are reachable from Admin → System as well as Admin → Updates (§21.24).

Hirer — the pages assigned to them (§15.12), and nothing else. No other views, and no tabs at all when only one page is assigned.

This replaces the earlier fixed Audio/Lighting split. What a hirer sees is now a layout someone built and can look at, rather than the product of six visibility flags.

┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  [ Hire ]  [ Foyer ]                     │ ← only when >1 page assigned
├──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  (page content fills the remaining height)│
├──────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  [Status indicators only]      [Log out] │
└──────────────────────────────────────────┘

#21.7 Status bar

Persistent along the bottom of every screen.

● KNX  ● DMX  ⚠ Mixer  ● Projector  ○ HDMI        ▸ 00:47:12  ⟲        19:42

The bottom is where status belongs. The top of the screen is for moving around — navigation and page tabs — and mixing the two puts a thing you glance at beside things you tap. On a tablet held in two hands the bottom edge is also where thumbs already are, which matters for the timer controls; and it is where a physical console puts its status strip.

An earlier revision placed it at the top. That put five device indicators between the operator and the navigation they were reaching for.

#At phone width

One summary LED replaces the five indicators (B70). It shows the worst state — red over amber over green. Not configured does not count; if everything is unconfigured it shows neutral. Its accessible name says what is wrong ("Connections: Mixer degraded, HDMI offline", or "all connected"). Tapping it opens a named list of every indicator and its state; for staff each row opens that device's detail sheet, as tapping an indicator does on a wider screen, so nothing the full bar offers is unreachable. The show timer is dropped on phones; the clock and the account chip stay. The five indicators and the timer return above the phone breakpoint.

Sheets at phone width stop above the status bar (--status-bar-height) rather than drawing their footers over it.

Connection changes are spoken once (§24.3). A single visually hidden polite announcer says only what changed — "KNX offline", "KNX and DMX offline. Mixer degraded.", "KNX back online" — coalesced over one second, so a device that drops and returns inside the window is never mentioned and a device retrying while offline stays silent. The device list and the summary LED keep their accessible names but are not live regions.

#Clock and timer

The right end carries two things, both of which earn their place.

#The clock is the appliance's time, not the browser's

Shown to the minute. It is deliberately the server's time, taken from the battery-backed RTC (§2.7), because that is the clock scheduled rules fire against (§8.3). A tablet with a wrong timezone showing its own idea of the time would be worse than showing none — someone would set a 23:00 rule against a clock that is not the one deciding.

Where the two differ by more than a minute the clock carries a small amber marker and the detail sheet says which is which.

#The timer is shared, not per-client

Start, stop, reset. Elapsed time in H:MM:SS, monospaced so the digits do not shift.

It lives on the server and broadcasts like any other state. An operator starting it at the top of an act and a second operator on a tablet in the wings must see the same number — a per-client stopwatch would be two different answers to one question. It also means a page reload or a reconnect does not lose it, which a local timer would.

Only running and started_at are stored, in system_state (§15.13); elapsed is computed. So a restart mid-performance recovers the timer rather than zeroing it, and the persister writes two fields on start and stop rather than continuously.

Operator and admin only. Hirers see the clock and not the timer: it is shared state, and a hirer resetting the venue's running time mid-event is a worse outcome than a hirer not having a stopwatch.

Status is always signalled twice — colour and icon — never colour alone:

State Colour Icon
Healthy Green None (filled dot is the default state)
Degraded, last-known Amber ⚠
Offline, error Red ✕
Not configured Grey, unlit ○

#On very short viewports

Superseded (B68). An earlier revision hid the bar below roughly 420 px of height — a phone in landscape — and moved device state into a navigation rail's overflow. Phones are now portrait-only (§21.9): a phone turned to landscape is covered by a "turn your phone upright" overlay, so the bar is never hidden.

The bar carries devices whose loss affects the room. Backup media (§4.5) and the control surface (§7.6) are monitored but excluded — losing either degrades maintenance or convenience, not operation — and both appear in the admin health screen instead. §11.1 lists everything monitored; this is the operator-facing subset.

Staff tap an indicator for the detail sheet:

┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Allen & Heath CQ-20B                 [×]  │
│  ────────────────────────────────────────  │
│  Status        ● Connected                 │
│  Last seen     Just now                    │
│  Host          10.2.30.71                  │
│  Port          51325                       │
│  Protocol      MIDI / NRPN over TCP        │
│  Latency       3 ms average                │
│  Reconnects    0 since last restart        │
│  Last error    —                           │
│                                            │
│  [Go to device settings]                   │
└────────────────────────────────────────────┘

When the mixer is refused rather than timed out, the sheet says so specifically: Another MIDI client holds the connection. Check that MixPad is using the CQ's WiFi rather than Ethernet.

Hirers see indicators only, with no interaction.

Account popover — an initials chip at the right of the bar, offering change password and sign out.

#The account chip

The only control in the status bar's right end, on every operator and admin view. Tapping opens a short menu rather than adding a second button.

  ┌──────────────────┐
  │  Operator        │   who is signed in, and the tier
  │  ──────────────  │
  │  Admin           │   admin and operator only
  │  Display scale   │   only at or above the design target (§21.9)
  │  ──────────────  │
  │  Log out         │
  └──────────────────┘

Admin is a menu item, not a tab. Configuration is a different activity from running a room, and a tab beside Pages and Scenes would invite tapping into it mid-performance.

#The hirer has one button

A single Log out, no menu, no account chip. There is no admin to reach and no display scale to set (§21.9).

It confirms. Logging out mid-event means finding whoever holds the PIN, so an accidental tap must not do it. The confirmation says what will happen in those terms — "You will need the venue PIN to get back in" — rather than a generic "are you sure".

#Being logged out is not the same as being disconnected

Three states that look similar and mean different things, so they read differently (§21.27):

State What the operator sees
Reconnecting The surface stays, dimmed, with a reconnecting banner. Values are last-known
Session expired Re-authentication overlay over the current page, state preserved (§6.6)
Logged out Return to login. Nothing preserved, because it was deliberate

Backgrounding a tablet produces none of them. The socket may close and the page may suspend, but returning within the idle window reconnects into the same session and resyncs (§16.8). The operator sees a brief reconnecting banner and their own values, not a login screen.

#21.8 Login

One page at /login, staff above and hirer below.

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│              Proskenion                          │
│         av.school.nz                             │
│                                                  │
│   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
│   │  Password                                │   │
│   │  [                              👁 ]     │   │
│   │  [            Sign in              ]     │   │
│   └──────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
│                                                  │
│   ────────────────────────────────────────       │
│                                                  │
│   Hire guest? Enter your PIN                     │
│                                                  │
│   [ ] [ ] [ ] [ ] [ ] [ ]                        │
│                                                  │
│   [           Guest access             ]         │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Staff — one password field, no username. The server checks both accounts; admin wins. On success the button glows briefly and the redirect follows the tier.

Hirer PIN — six individual boxes rather than a single input. Tapping any box opens the numeric keypad on mobile. Digits auto-advance; backspace steps back; paste fills all six. On completion the boxes glow teal left to right and the request fires.

If hirer access is disabled: Hire guest access is not currently available. Please contact venue staff.

#Errors

Wrong password        ⚠ Incorrect password
                      Field value preserved — never cleared on error

Wrong PIN             ⚠ Incorrect PIN
                      Boxes shake briefly, then clear. Staff field unaffected

Rate limited          [ Sign in — try again in 12:34 ]
                      Live countdown replaces the label; re-enables at zero

#Session expiry

Over the current page, not a redirect. The page behind is blurred but its state is preserved.

┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Session expired                           │
│                                            │
│  Your session ended after a period of      │
│  inactivity. Sign in again to continue.    │
│  Your unsaved work is preserved.           │
│                                            │
│  Password  [                       👁 ]    │
│  [           Sign in again           ]     │
└────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Expired under 30 minutes gives the overlay; over 30 minutes redirects to /login.

#Hirer disconnected

When the PIN changes or access is disabled while a hirer is active:

┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ○  Access updated                         │
│                                            │
│  Your access has been updated by venue     │
│  staff. Please speak to staff if you need  │
│  to continue.                              │
└────────────────────────────────────────────┘

No login prompt. The old PIN will not work, so offering one would only mislead.

#Install prompt

After a successful first sign-in, if the app is not installed and the browser supports it.

Android and Chrome get a native prompt with an add and a not-now option; not-now suppresses it for 30 days. iOS Safari gets a one-time instruction — Tap Share, then Add to Home Screen — shown once and never again.

Over a self-signed certificate, iOS will refuse installation. The prompt is suppressed and replaced with a link to the certificate trust instructions (§6.16).

#21.9 Pages — the everyday surface

The surface the room is actually run from. A page is a layout of channel strips and button panels, grouped by activity rather than by domain: audio on one, room control on another, a curated set for a hire.

Mixer (§21.13) and Lighting (§21.11) remain the advanced view — the full channel list, every registered fixture, the stage plan. Those are for rigging; pages are for running. Pages are the default landing.

#Why pages are not banks

The control surface needs banks because it has eight strips and more than eight targets (§7.6). A page is not solving that problem — the virtual surface scrolls.

A page groups by activity, and it is the only place a button panel can sensibly live, because a button has no position in a linear channel scroll. That is what makes it worth having a second organising concept rather than reusing the bank model.

#Layout

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  [ Performance ]  [ Lighting ]  [ Hire · hirer ]              [ + ] │
├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  ┌──────┐┌──────┐┌──────┐┌──────┐  ┌────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐ │
│  │Mic 1 ││Mic 2 ││ HDMI ││ Main │  │ Stage  │  │      Room        │ │
│  │      ││      ││      ││      │  │┌──────┐│  │┌──────┐┌────────┐│ │
│  │  ▓   ││  ▓   ││  ▓   ││  ▓   │  ││Bank 1││  ││Projec││ Screen ││ │
│  │  █   ││  █   ││  █   ││  █   │  │└──────┘│  │└──────┘└────────┘│ │
│  │      ││      ││      ││      │  │┌──────┐│  │┌──────┐┌────────┐│ │
│  │−6 dB ││−8 dB ││−2 dB ││ 0 dB │  ││Bank 2││  ││House ││Speakers││ │
│  │[MUTE]││[MUTE]││[MUTE]││[MUTE]│  │└──────┘│  │└──────┘└────────┘│ │
│  └──────┘└──────┘└──────┘└──────┘  └────────┘  └──────────────────┘ │
│           channel strips            1-wide           2-wide         │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Items sit in one horizontal flow at a shared height, in sort_order. Strips and panels are the same visual family, which is what lets them live together.

#Button panels

A panel declares a column count and a set of buttons. Buttons are square, so the side equals the column width, and how many rows fit follows from the available height rather than being configured.

An earlier revision fixed rows at three. That wasted most of a booth screen, where five fit, and overflowed an 11″ tablet, where three do. Sizing is worked out per viewport instead — see below.

Empty cells render as a dashed outline, so a partly-filled panel reads as deliberate rather than broken.

#Each button fires a rule and takes its lamp from a derived status

Both already exist (§8). A button fires its rule directly (POST /rules/{id}/fire), whatever the rule's trigger type; the lamp is a derived status, evaluated from the same predicate that drives the KNX panel indicator. The wall panel and the tablet therefore cannot disagree — they are reading one source, not two.

PanelButton
  item_id, col, row
  label            short, on the button face — up to three lines
  rule_id          → the rule it fires
  state_id         → derived status for the lamp, optional
  colour           accent when active, from the group palette (§21.3)
  confirm          bool — for anything with consequences
#Two indications, doing different jobs

An LED bar near the top is the precise indicator, trusted standing at the tablet. A border glow is what reads from across the auditorium.

The button face stays dark in both states. Filling it with colour would turn a panel of eight active buttons into eight coloured rectangles competing with the channel strips beside them.

An active button latches down. It drops two pixels, the face darkens to a recessed gradient, and the inset shadow inverts — a lit button physically held in, as on a console. The internal glow is deliberately absent; the ring and the LED carry the state.

A third state exists for anything with a transition: amber and pulsing, used by the projector while warming or cooling (§7.4).

#Sizing and the design target

1920 × 1080 is the design target — the booth touchscreen, the most likely permanent installation and the most constrained of the large displays. Larger screens scale up to it; smaller ones adapt down.

#Faders take the height that is left

A channel strip's furniture — name, reference, dB readout, mute — is a fixed 157 px. Everything above that goes to fader travel, so the target gives 727 px and the tightest landscape tablet still gives 447 px. A 100 mm physical console fader is about 378 px, so every supported target clears a real desk.

Target Viewport Fader travel
Booth touchscreen 1920 × 1080 727 px
iPad Pro 13″ landscape 1366 × 1024 671 px
Laptop 1440 × 900 547 px
iPad Pro 11″ landscape 1194 × 834 481 px
Galaxy Tab S9 11″ landscape 1280 × 800 447 px
iPad Pro 11″ portrait 834 × 1194 841 px
Phone portrait 390 × 844 491 px

The 11″ tablets in landscape are the floor, not the phone. At 800–834 px tall they are tighter than a phone in portrait, and they are the likely roaming device.

#Buttons are square, and never scroll

A panel button's side equals its column width, which is what makes the count predictable rather than emergent. Ideal 126 px, floor 64 px.

Panels never scroll vertically. A scroll indicator was tried twice — a chevron, then an illuminated rail with a count of hidden buttons — and neither read convincingly. The better answer was to remove the case: if the buttons do not fit, the button shrinks and the panel widens until they do.

That also leaves the surface with one scroll direction. Everything scrolls horizontally, nothing scrolls vertically, which is simpler to use than two axes and removes the "did I scroll or tap" ambiguity from a whole class of controls.

#A panel occupies the same footprint as N channel strips

Gaps included. A 2-wide panel is 314 px — two 150 px strips plus the 14 px between them — not 288 px derived from its buttons. The surface then reads as one grid rather than two systems sitting beside each other.

1-wide   150 px      3-wide   478 px
2-wide   314 px      4-wide   642 px

The slack between the buttons and that width becomes the horizontal gap. That is what stops a multi-column panel looking crammed: at full size a 2-wide panel gets 38 px between its columns rather than the 12 px used vertically. Buttons stay square; the extra width goes into the gap, never into the button.

Past 40 px of slack the panel gives up the alignment. Two 96 px buttons held to a 314 px footprint would sit 98 px apart, which is dead space rather than breathing room. Beyond that threshold the panel takes its natural width with a 20 px gap — still generous, and honest that small buttons do not fill a strip-width column.

For a single column the same slack becomes side padding, with the button centred, and the same 40 px threshold applies.

#Size and columns are chosen together
for cols from configured to 6:
    rows = ceil(count / cols)
    side = min(126, floor((available_height - gap*(rows-1)) / rows))
    if side < 64: continue                 # buttons would be too small
    width = cols*side + gap*(cols-1) + padding
    if width <= 0.70 * surface_width: use it
    otherwise remember it if it is the narrowest so far

Searching both together rather than in sequence matters. Expanding columns first commits to a shape before discovering that a slightly smaller button would have kept the configured one — it produced panels twice as wide. Under the joint search a five-button panel configured 1-wide stays one column on every target but a phone in landscape, shrinking from 126 px to 96 px instead.

The venue configured a shape; the layout honours it and adjusts the size. Only where the floor is reached does the arrangement change: at 260 px of panel height a five-row column would need 42 px buttons, so a phone in landscape becomes 2 × 3 at 78 px.

#Phone landscape

Not supported: phones are portrait-only (owner's decision, 29 September 2026; B68). When the viewport is in landscape, under 500 px tall and has a coarse pointer — a phone on its side, never a tablet (the smallest supported tablet landscape is 834 px tall) and never a short desktop window — a full-screen overlay covers every route, login and the hirer surface included: "Turn your phone upright — Proskenion works in portrait on phones." It has nothing to press and nothing to dismiss; the application keeps running underneath, made inert, and rotating back uncovers it at once. The overlay's visibility is a CSS media query, so a rotation is resolved before the next paint.

Superseded: this section previously adapted the shell for phone landscape — the status bar dropped, a 56 px floating navigation rail with two-character labels, compact strip furniture — taking fader travel from 37 px to 226 px. Even so, it was the one target where the shell changed, and 226 px of travel on a 390 px-tall screen was a poor fader. That code remains, covered by the overlay.

#Display scale

Filling the height is right up to a point, and that point arrives sooner than expected. 1080p already gives 201 mm of physical travel on a 24″ panel — twice a console fader. 1440p gives 253 mm. A 4K panel unscaled gives 333 mm, a third of a metre for something moved with one hand, with 126 px buttons stranded beside it.

So a tall display gets a scale factor rather than a longer fader. Everything grows together — strips, type, buttons — and the layout sees a smaller logical viewport, adapting exactly as it does for a smaller device. No second code path.

The scale is app-wide (since v0.1.12): the factor is applied to the root <html> element, so the whole operator and admin interface — not only the Pages surface — sees the smaller logical viewport, and a 4K panel at 2× lays out as 1920 × 1080. The control is in the account-chip menu (§21.7). An earlier build offered it as a slider that scaled the Pages surface alone.

The control appears only at or above 1920 × 1080. Below the design target the layout is already choosing button size and column count, and a manual factor would fight that machinery — which is why tablets and phones need no control and do not get one.

Viewport size is the test, not device type. A booth touchscreen is a coarse pointer and a large display, so it reads as a tablet to every media query that would otherwise decide this. Here "viewport" means the screen's size in CSS pixels, not the browser window's: a 4K monitor with a browser's tab and address bars still has a 3840 × 2160 screen while its window is about 3840 × 2020, and would miss the threshold the window was measured against.

Setting Value
Range 1.0× to 2.0×, in tenths
Default Computed — 2.0× at 4K, 1.3× at 1440p, 1.0× at the target
Storage localStorage, keyed by screen size

No downward travel. Below 1.0× the layout already owns the problem, and scaling down would only push targets under their declared minimums — including §24.6's 72 px on the hirer surface, which a naive scaler would silently defeat.

Keyed by screen size rather than per browser, so docking a laptop to a monitor recalls each screen's own value. It is deliberately not a database setting: a server-side scale would push the booth screen's factor onto someone's phone.

#Two rules that are easy to lose

A scroll is not a tap. A pointer that moves past a small threshold before release is a scroll. Without this, every drag across the surface fires whatever was under the finger — and here that is a lighting bank or a projector.

A panel button's LED is anchored to the top; the label is centred in the space that remains. Stating it as an order — "LED above label" — leads to centring the pair as a group, which is not the same thing and has been got wrong more than once.

#Group trays

A lighting group's master and its members share a tinted tray carrying the group's colour and name. Members keep the colour on their top edge; the master takes a tinted background and a thicker accent.

Which fixtures a master controls becomes a glance rather than a deduction, and it is clearer than ringing each strip: a ring says this one is special, a tray says these belong together, which is the actual relationship.

#The page places a master; it never defines membership

Membership already lives on the group (§15.9), because rules and KNX bindings target groups too (§7.1, §8.8). A page item is therefore only "this group's master goes here", and the tray fills itself.

There is no page-level conflict to resolve, because a page cannot disagree with a definition it does not hold. Place the same master on five pages and all five show the same members; change the group once and all five follow.

Placing a member individually as well as its master is a warning, not an error — it is duplicate control of one channel, which is legal and resolved by last-write-wins (§10.6), but usually a mistake. The editor offers to remove the duplicates.

#Collapsed is the useful default

A group of eight fixtures plus its master is nine strips and most of a page. Collapsed it is one, with a toggle beneath the master showing the member count.

The page stores which state it opens in — expanded for a rigging page, collapsed for a performance one — and an operator's toggle lasts the session without rewriting the layout. Configuration is the venue's intent; the toggle is the moment's need.

The tray slides rather than snapping. Strips are a fixed width, so the target is arithmetic — member count × (strip + gap) — and the transition needs no measurement and causes no layout thrash. Around 300 ms on the width with the members fading slightly faster, collapsing to 50 ms under prefers-reduced-motion (§21.3).

The toggle changes a class on the existing tray rather than re-rendering the page: replacing the node mid-transition would discard the animation, and the surface would appear to snap anyway.

#The ghost mark

A group fader sets its members' levels (§9.4, B73), so a member's thumb moves with its master. The master dimmer is the scaling left: member thumbs stay where they were set while the fixtures sit lower. Showing only the set level hides what the rig is doing; showing the composited result would move every fader whenever the master moved. (Earlier revisions made the group fader a multiplier, with a ghost for its effect too; that is gone, and hirers no longer receive group multipliers, because there are none.)

So each member's track carries a faint secondary mark at its composited output, with the effective percentage beside the readout:

   85%  →55%          set 85%, landing at 55% under a master at 65%

The thumb is what you set, the ghost is what the fixture is doing, and neither number is lying.

Indicator-only groups (§9.4) are left out of pages: they have no master to place, so the page editor does not offer them and a page item naming one is refused (§15.12).

#Different groupings are different rules

A button bringing up banks 1 and 3 together is a rule with that action (§8). Nothing in the page model needs to know about lighting — it places strips and fires rules.

#A default page is generated, never required

A fresh installation shows every visible channel without anyone configuring a layout, per §5.5's rule that the virtual surface cannot require configuration. Pages are a curated layer on top, not a step before audio works.

#Contiguity

A tray renders only where a group's master and members are adjacent in the page's item order. A master placed away from its members — on a Room page, for instance — renders as an ordinary strip with the group's colour and a small member-count marker instead.

Without this rule an unusual layout looks broken the first time someone builds one.

#21.10 Operator — Scenes

The primary operator view. A grid of scene cards, two to three columns on desktop, two on mobile.

Each card shows an icon, the scene name, and when it last ran.

State Appearance
Idle Normal card, subtle hover lift
Executing Pulsing teal border
Success Brief green bloom, settles
Partial Brief amber bloom, settles
Failed Brief red bloom, settles

Critical scenes carry a red outline permanently, so the alarm scene is distinguishable at rest.

Restore Venue Default (§13.5) appears here like any other scene, but is marked protected and cannot be removed from the operator view. It is what the operator presses after a hire.

A result toast appears alongside the card animation (§21.17).

#21.11 Operator — Lighting

┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  [Status bar]                                              │
├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Fade  [══════●═══════════]  2.0s                          │
│  Master [═══════════●═════]  100%    [Save look]           │
│                                      [External control ○]  │
├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Stage banks   ● 1   ○ 2   ○ 3   ● 4    (KNX panel state)  │
│                ⚡ locked out — external control active      │
├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Groups                              ← horizontal scroll   │
│  ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌──────┐              │
│  │Row 1 │ │Row 2 │ │Row 3 │ │Row 4 │ │Wings │              │
│  │ ▓▓▓  │ │ ▓▓▓  │ │ ▓▓   │ │      │ │ ▓▓▓  │              │
│  │  ●   │ │  ●   │ │  ●   │ │  ●   │ │  ●   │              │
│  │ 85%  │ │ 72%  │ │ 60%  │ │  0%  │ │ 72%  │              │
│  │      │ │mixed │ │      │ │      │ │      │              │
│  └──────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘              │
├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Fixtures                            ← horizontal scroll   │
│  (one strip per fixture, group colour accent at top)       │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Both rows scroll horizontally with scroll-snap. RGB and RGBW fader fills take the fixture's current colour; double-tap opens the colour picker.

Channels driven by a running scene show a pulsing teal ring on the fader track (§10.6). Moving one takes it over from the scene, which is intended for normal-priority scenes but should be a deliberate act rather than a surprise. Under a critical scene the ring is red and the fader is disabled for the duration — the write would be rejected, so the control says so rather than accepting a gesture it will discard.

A group fader shows its members' highest level, marked mixed when they differ, because it sets levels and holds none of its own (§9.4, B73). Moving it sets every member to the new value. The Groups row leaves out indicator-only groups (§9.4), which have no fader; at this installation the whole-stage fader is the Master.

Superseded: the dominated-group hint — 3 fixtures held by Full Stage — existed only because a group fader was a multiplier and the highest applied. With groups setting levels nothing can be held by another group, and the hint is gone.

Stage banks show one button per KNX address, not one per binding. The rig's four "Stage all → row N" bindings on 4/0/8 (§8.2) therefore appear as one "Stage all" button, lit when all four banks are.

The mockups show a BUMP button on group strips. There is no bump feature, so it is not built; whether to add one is an open decision.

#External control

The toggle sits in the pinned header and uses the LED treatment: amber and glowing when active, unlit when off.

Detection is automatic (§7.2.7). Plugging a desk into the booth input puts the view into external control within one frame; unplugging it restores normal operation five seconds later. The operator does nothing.

┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ⚡ Under external control — booth desk                     │
│    Showing live fixture levels from the desk.              │
│    Stage banks are locked out at the KNX panel.            │
│    House lighting is unaffected.                           │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Faders and the stage plan show observed levels, live and read-only. The operator sees what the stage is actually doing, not a stale model — which is the main thing this design buys over the alternatives. A small LIVE chip beside the master distinguishes observed values from the controller's own.

The banner has no resume action, because there is nothing to resume: the desk stops and the controller takes over by itself. Offering a button that cannot work would be worse than offering none.

Manual mode — for a desk patched directly to the fixtures, bypassing the node — is a toggle in the header. Nothing can be observed in that case, so faders hold last-known controller values at reduced opacity and the banner says so:

┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  ⚠ DMX output suspended — set manually                     │
│    No booth input detected, so fixture levels are unknown. │
│    Levels shown are the controller's last values.          │
│    [Resume controller output]                              │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Turning that off confirms once, warning that resuming may cause a visible change. The toggle cannot force external control off while frames are arriving — detection wins, and the control is disabled with a tooltip explaining why.

On resume, the view switches from observed to controller values. Because both were being carried throughout (§16.8), the operator can see what is about to change before it does.

Critical scene override. If an alarm scene fires while external control is active, the controller disables it and sends the blackout. The banner updates and a toast reports: External control disabled by alarm scene.

Hirers never see this control. If external control is active and the hirer has lighting permissions, their lighting tab shows: Lighting is currently under external control. Please speak to venue staff if you need it adjusted.

#21.12 Stage plan

An SVG layout from the audience's perspective: proscenium at the bottom, upstage at the top, stage right on the left of the plan (§9.3).

<a href="#after-plan" class="skip-link">Skip stage plan</a>

<svg role="application" aria-label="Stage lighting plan">
  <g role="button"
     aria-label="Stage Wash 3, channel 7, at 72 percent"
     aria-pressed={selected}
     tabIndex={isRovingFocus ? 0 : -1}
     onKeyDown={handleKey} />
</svg>
<div aria-live="polite" class="sr-only">{announcement}</div>

The plan is one tab stop, not sixteen. A roving tabindex gives exactly one fixture tabIndex={0} at a time — the last focused, or the first on entry — and every other fixture -1. Arrow keys move focus within the plan; Tab leaves it.

Without this, a keyboard user tabs through every fixture in the rig to reach anything after the plan. With sixteen fixtures that is sixteen stops to get past a single component.

Arrow Left and Right move along a bar; Up and Down move between bars, preserving horizontal position where one exists. This is the standard composite-widget pattern and it is the same behaviour the surface configurator uses (§21.25), so a keyboard user learns it once.

Unpermitted fixtures are skipped by the roving focus entirely rather than being focusable-but-inert.

Level changes are announced on fade completion, not during: Stage Wash 3 faded to 75 percent.

Capability Admin Operator Hirer
View levels ✓ ✓ ✓
Tap to select ✓ ✓ Permitted only
Double-tap for fader ✓ ✓ Permitted only
Multi-select and group ✓ — —
Long-press context menu ✓ — —
Drag fixture position ✓ — —
Reorder bars ✓ — —
Add fixture ✓ — —
Lock overlay on unpermitted — — ✓

Unpermitted fixtures render at 20% opacity with a lock icon and are not focusable.

Multi-select action bar, animated up from the bottom on first selection:

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  3 selected      [Group]  [Set level]  [✕]   │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Setting a level applies proportionally across the selection.

#21.13 Operator — Mixer

A control surface laid out as a flex row. Input channels fill the main area; the Main Output fader is always pinned to the right. Mix outputs live in a sliding drawer on wide viewports and a dedicated page on narrow ones.

#Wide

┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
