                    including ERR on malformed input and configurable
                    line termination

Areas that specifically need unit coverage because they have already been got wrong:

  • Fader law conversion, both directions, round-tripping every table entry exactly and interpolating between them
  • Pan value conversion, confirming centre is 8192
  • Scene recall offset, confirming CQ scene 1 sends program change 0
  • The lighting value scale, confirming 0–100 in and correct 0–255 or 0–100 out per channel type
  • Compositor arithmetic, including maximum-not-product across overlapping groups, the clamp-before-scale order, and min_value > 0 exemption from master scaling
  • Binding rule recall, confirming the recall sets every member's level, so the result is exactly on_level (× the master) with the group fader left at 40%, and that a binding to an indicator-only group is refused
  • Change-driven rendering, confirming no frame is sent while the level store is clean and that the keepalive fires inside the node's source timeout
  • External control detection, confirming entry on the first frame, exit only after 5 s of silence, no flapping on intermittent input, and that a desk sending all zeros counts as active
  • Observed store isolation, confirming no compositor pass and no output value ever reads state.lighting.observed
  • Fade timing, confirming a 10-second fade completes within 10 ms of 10 seconds under simulated load
  • Trigger matching across all six match types and both DPT classes
  • Scene precedence, confirming an operator write cancels a normal scene's fade on that channel only, and is rejected outright under a critical scene
  • Derived status, confirming an All binding reads 0 when one member bank is off, and that dragging a fixture down in the UI turns the panel indicator red
  • No chaining, confirming a derived status write does not re-enter the rule layer
  • Binding value mapping, confirming telegram value 1 applies on_level and 0 applies off_level through a single rule
  • Selective suppression, confirming a run_scene rule fires during external control and the scene skips only its DMX actions
  • Rule debounce, confirming a repeated panel telegram inside the window does not restart a fade
  • Projector rejection, confirming a power command during COOLING returns device_unavailable rather than queuing
  • Rollback ordering, confirming that dying between symlink swap and snapshot restore leaves the schema-ahead guard to catch it
  • Hirer permission resolution, confirming a channel removed mid-session is refused on the next write and a ceiling lowered below the current value pulls the fader down
  • Write serialisation, confirming two coroutines cannot interleave transactions on the write connection
  • Chunked pruning, confirming a large delete yields the write lock between batches
  • Debounce, confirming repeat telegrams inside the window are suppressed
  • LKV422 response parsing, against CR, LF, CRLF and bare terminations, plus ERR and truncated replies
  • Panel footprint, confirming a 2-wide panel measures exactly two strips plus the gap between them, and that the slack lands in the horizontal gap rather than in the button
  • Panel layout, confirming a five-button panel configured 1-wide keeps one column on every target above a phone in landscape, and that no button falls below 64 px
  • Display scale, confirming the control is absent below 1920 × 1080, that it cannot go under 1.0×, and that the value is recalled per viewport size rather than per browser
  • Show timer, confirming two clients see the same elapsed value, that a reconnect recovers it, and that a restart resumes rather than resets
  • Session visibility, confirming the idle timer runs only while the page is hidden, that pings and broadcasts never extend it, and that the absolute cap applies to every tier
  • Connection liveness, confirming a socket with no pong for two intervals is closed and unsubscribed, and that a closed browser is detected immediately
  • Backgrounding, confirming a backgrounded client receives no continuous events, that returning resyncs without a login, and that meters are not replayed
  • Scroll versus tap, confirming a pointer that moves past the threshold before release does not fire the button under it
  • Page validation, confirming a tray renders only for contiguous group members, a duplicate member is warned, and a button bound to a deleted rule is refused
  • Hirer page scoping, confirming a hirer reaches nothing outside an assigned page and that a ceiling applies whichever page a channel came from
  • Ghost mark, confirming the composited value tracks the master while the thumb does not move; and a group fader showing its members' highest level, "mixed" when they differ
  • Group palette, confirming every adjacent pair clears its hue gap and ΔE2000 18, and that no member falls within ΔE 11 of a semantic colour
  • Scribble collision detection, confirming two channels truncating to the same seven characters is refused and the colliding names are reported
  • Name width validation, confirming a name too wide for a channel strip is flagged while typing rather than at save
  • Meter mapping, confirming a ganged channel yields one value per reference in sort order, and that a channel with no data is absent rather than present at the floor
  • Meter isolation, confirming no control path reads state.mixer.meters and that meters are never persisted
  • Meter scale, confirming both the browser and the control surface driver scale from the driver's declared range rather than a constant
  • Pending overlay reconciliation, confirming a rejection arriving after an authoritative frame settles on the frame's value and not the pre-gesture one
  • Event bus isolation, confirming a raising subscriber does not reach the emitter, and that 10 consecutive failures unsubscribe it
  • Connect versus probe, confirming a transport that opens but does not respond reports device rather than config, and that backoff resets only after a successful probe
  • Ownership enforcement, confirming an unregistered writer raises under the development configuration
  • Fader law round-trip, confirming dbToPosition(positionToDb(p)) returns p within one display pixel across the table, and that every labelled point round-trips exactly
  • Detent snapping, confirming the value sent is the detent's exact dB rather than a converted position, and that the break-out counter resets on leaving the zone from either direction
  • Scale generation, confirming the printed legend comes from the law table and changes with the driver
  • Capability gating, confirming a scene action against a driver reporting it unsupported is skipped and logged, not attempted
  • Driver swap re-mapping, confirming names, order, ceilings, visibility and scene actions survive while references are invalidated. Surface assignments join the test with the control surface in Phase 9 (§18): until then there are none to survive
  • Transport substitution, confirming a driver's probe is unchanged across TCP and serial transports
  • Serial enumeration, confirming by-id paths are preferred, in-use ports are flagged, and a cable with no serial reports an unstable path
  • Single intent, confirming a grouped route produces one driver call and one device command
  • Ganged channels, confirming all references receive the same value and the effective ceiling is the minimum across them
  • Unmapped channels, confirming they are not controllable and are excluded from hirer access
  • Fixture profiles, confirming a twelve-channel profile occupies twelve addresses and that unknown roles are rejected at save
  • Schema shape constraints, confirming a dmx lighting channel with a null profile and a mixer_channel surface strip with a null target are both rejected at write
  • Venue baseline round trip, confirming fixture profiles, driver references, bindings, destinations and surface banks all restore, and that devices is untouched
  • Every row of the compositor behaviour matrix (§7.2.3), which exists to make omissions visible
  • Queue overflow, confirming continuous events drop oldest and discrete events block the producer
  • Optimistic concurrency, confirming a stale updated_at returns 409 with the current record
  • Per-key notification, confirming a single-channel frame re-renders one component and not the view
  • Surface fader scaling, confirming a mixer strip round-trips through A&H's law and a lighting strip stays linear on the 0–100 scale
  • Touch arbitration, confirming no motor write occurs while touched and that a pending value is applied on release
  • Bank validation, confirming a bank with no bank-change button is refused

#22.3 Frontend unit tests

Vitest, in web/src/**/*.test.ts. Run on every commit alongside the backend suite.

Vitest was chosen over the alternatives because it reuses Vite's transform pipeline (§5.1) — the same configuration, aliases and plugins the production build uses. A separate runner needs its own parallel configuration of the same things, and configuration that can drift between test and build is exactly the subtle problem this project does not need.

The frontend carries real logic that browser tests are too slow to cover well:

  • Fader law interpolation (§5.5) — dbToPosition(positionToDb(p)) returns p within one display pixel across the whole table, at both ends and around unity
  • The pending overlay (§21.2) — a rejection arriving after an authoritative frame settles on the frame's value, not the pre-gesture one
  • Per-key notification — a single-channel frame re-renders one component, not the view
  • Gesture arbitration — no authoritative write moves a fader under an active pointer, and the catch-up applies on release
  • Error code mapping (§16) — every code in the closed vocabulary maps to a defined presentation, and value_out_of_range presents as success with a clamp rather than a failure
  • Level and dB formatting — one decimal for lighting, the driver's law for mixer values, -inf rendered rather than printed as a number

Component tests are deliberately thin. The interface is specified screen by screen in §21 and verified end to end by Playwright (§22.6); duplicating that in jsdom would be slow to write and slower to trust. The value here is in the pure logic above, which is where a defect is silent rather than visible.

#22.4 Integration tests

tests/integration/. A real FastAPI test client against an in-memory SQLite database with all migrations applied at setup, and stub protocol backends.

Every endpoint has at least one success test and one covering its primary failure mode, asserting the error envelope and code (§16), not merely the status. Specific scenarios worth naming:

  • Migration runner: fresh database, partial application, a failed migration rolling back, and the schema-ahead guard
  • Hirer ceiling enforcement, confirming the server clamps a value the UI would not have sent
  • Token invalidation on password change and PIN change
  • Baseline capture, compare and restore round trip
  • Scene execution with a failing action, confirming a partial result and that other actions still ran

#22.5 End-to-end tests

Playwright in tests/e2e/, against a local instance with stub backends. Run before each release rather than on every commit.

Journeys: driver swap — configure the stub mixer, verify the interface degrades to its declared capabilities, swap back and re-map references; first-run wizard completion; admin sign-in through scene creation, trigger and log inspection; hirer PIN entry and restricted control; network change with reconnection; update upload, apply and rollback; external control on, track, and off; baseline capture, drift, compare and restore.

#22.6 Hardware-in-the-loop

tests/hil/, skipped by default, run manually against the bench setup before each package build. These cover what stubs cannot:

  • Real KNX bus timing and the telegram budget under load
  • Real CQ-20B scene recall timing, and whether 300 ms is actually enough
  • Real DMX frame output, observed on a scope or a second receiver
  • External control handoff with a real second Art-Net source, confirming no visible discontinuity
  • LKV422 line termination and switch acknowledgement, resolving the two unknowns in §7.5
  • CQ native connection limits (§7.3) — the gating question. Does port 51326 accept a connection while MIDI holds 51325? Does it consume one of the mixer's two MixPad slots? Record both in docs/protocols/cq20b-native.md.
  • Booth input universe configuration — the assumption the automatic handover rests on (§7.2.7). Configure the input port to broadcast, confirm the controller receives desk frames on the output universe, and confirm no feedback path results from the controller sourcing the same universe. If it cannot be made to work, fall back to the procedural rule.
  • Node merge scheme and hold-last-state behaviour, and what priority a DMX-IN source is assigned when merged against sACN (§7.2.7 fallbacks)
  • Surface MCU address map, encoder push reporting, and the scribble strip sysex device ID (§7.6)
  • Fader motor behaviour under rapid scene recall, confirming no oscillation or overshoot
  • Watchdog behaviour under a deliberately hung event loop
  • Power-loss resilience: pull power 20 times during writes and confirm clean boots

#22.7 Soak test

A Phase 7 milestone, before installation.

Setup. Bench deployment with all real devices attached, running 72 hours.

Load. Scheduled scene triggers every 15 minutes; scripted fader movement from a WebSocket client; KNX telegrams at 5/sec for 30 seconds each hour; hourly external control cycle — on, five minutes of simulated Art-Net input, off; daily mixer reconnection cycle — kill the CQ stub, confirm the refused/timed-out distinction, restore, confirm state sync.

The last two are deliberate additions. They exercise the paths most likely to leak resources or accumulate state, and neither was covered by earlier revisions of this plan.

Measured at start and every 12 hours: resident memory, open file descriptors, WebSocket connection count, asyncio task count, SSD write volume, database size, event loop lag.

Pass: memory growth under 5 MB over 72 hours; no unhandled exceptions; no device connection decay; every scheduled scene within 100 ms of its trigger; SSD writes within the §23 budget; task count stable.

Fail: any of the above; any forced reboot; any device status turning red that is not traceable to an injected failure.

#22.8 Continuous integration

The repository is hosted under the symplenz account, private at first and public since 26 September 2026 (github.com/SympleNZ/Proskenion, MIT, published through a filtered public branch; §20.3). CI therefore runs on a public repository.

Pipeline, using the hosting platform's native runner:

Trigger Runs
Every push, any branch Lint, type-check, backend and frontend unit tests
Pull request to main The above, plus integration tests against an in-memory database
Tag matching v* The above, plus end-to-end tests, then build an unsigned package

Hardware-in-the-loop tests (§22.6) are excluded — they require the bench rig and are run manually before a package is released.

Signing runs on the developer's machine, never in CI. Putting the private key into a hosted secret store would place it somewhere that a repository compromise could reach, and the key's loss or theft is more consequential than the convenience is worth (§14.1). The tag build above produces an unsigned artefact only; tools/package.py sign is a separate, deliberate local step that adds the Ed25519 signature, using a private key that never leaves the developer's machine. The matching public keys are the trust anchors read from trusted-keys/ on the appliance (§6.11).

Lint gates on TypeScript 7.1 (§5.1). Until typescript-eslint supports the native compiler, either defer the lint step or pin a 6.x installation for lint alone.

CI is not required before Phase 1, but the suite is structured so that enabling it is a configuration file rather than a refactor.

#23. Performance Targets

Design targets, validated in the soak test. They exist so "is it fast enough?" has a definitive answer.

#23.1 Throughput

Concern Target Measured by
Concurrent WebSocket clients 20 sustained, 50 peak 50 connections holding the lighting view
Concurrent HTTP requests 100/sec sustained wrk against representative endpoints
WebSocket control writes 300/sec sustained 10 clients dragging simultaneously
Database writes 100 inserts/sec sustained Scene execution log under scripted load
KNX outgoing telegrams 15/sec, enforced Priority queue under simultaneous fades
DMX frame rate 25–40 fps, stable — in practice a steady 40 fps while output moves, including a fader drag; the 1 s keepalive at rest (§7.2.3) Second receiver on the universe

A fader drag produces a steady 40 fps (since v0.1.16). On site it had been an irregular ~20 fps: frames were sent only on change, so their timing followed fader writes arriving over Wi-Fi, with gaps past 100 ms. Frames now go on a fixed 25 ms grid while output moves, and an operator write glides over 75 ms between them (§7.2.3).

#23.2 Latency

Operation p50 p99
Scene trigger to first action dispatched 20 ms 100 ms
Critical scene: KNX telegram to DMX blackout on the wire 100 ms 300 ms
Fader movement to DMX output 40 ms 120 ms
WebSocket control write to ack 15 ms 60 ms
Fader movement to CQ-20B NRPN write 30 ms 100 ms
KNX telegram to scene action dispatched 30 ms 150 ms
Page load, cold cache 500 ms 1500 ms
Page load, warm cache with resync 150 ms 400 ms
Sign-in to dashboard available 300 ms 800 ms

The critical-scene row is the safety-relevant path and has an explicit target for that reason.

#23.3 Resources

Resource Idle Under load
CPU < 5% of one core < 30% of one core
Memory, resident < 200 MB < 400 MB
SSD writes per 24h < 200 MB < 1 GB
Open file descriptors < 50 < 150
asyncio tasks < 45 < 80

Counting asyncio tasks. len(asyncio.all_tasks()) inside the process, including the ASGI server's own tasks and the request doing the counting: a leak in the server's per-connection tasks is exactly what the soak (§22.7) must catch. The idle figure is for the reference rig (KNX, one lighting output, a mixer with metering, a projector, a video matrix, two WebSocket clients), measured at about 38. Each further WebSocket adds 3; each device adds its driver's loops (1 for Art-Net or the matrix, 2 for PJLink, 5 for a CQ-20B with metering). A stable count matters more than the number, and tests/integration/test_task_budget.py guards both. The earlier target of fewer than 30 idle predated the device set this rig needs.

Not hard limits, but monitored. Significant deviation from any baseline is treated as a regression.

#24. Accessibility

Target: WCAG 2.2 Level AA for the admin and operator interfaces. Level A for the hirer surface, which is simpler but used by a more varied population.

#24.1 Colour is never the only signal

Status Colour Secondary signal
Healthy Green Filled dot — the default state
Degraded Amber ⚠ warning triangle
Offline Red ✕ cross
Not configured Grey, unlit ○ hollow circle

A muted fader shows a grey fill and a striped pattern, not just reduced opacity. Stage plan fixtures are distinguishable by shape and fill pattern without colour. Where colour is the content — an RGB fixture — operator and admin views offer a text label overlay toggle.

#24.2 Keyboard

The admin interface is the primary keyboard target. Tab order follows the visual layout. shadcn handles its own components; custom components manage tabIndex explicitly.

Escape       Close modal, sheet or popover
Enter        Confirm dialog, submit focused form
Ctrl/Cmd+S   Save the current form
?            Open the keyboard reference

Faders:

Tab            Focus
Arrow Up/Down  Mixer ±1 dB · Lighting ±1%
Page Up/Down   Mixer ±5 dB · Lighting ±10%
Home           Minimum
End            Maximum

Mixer arrow steps move exactly one decibel at any fader position rather than a fixed distance of travel, which the law would turn into an uneven step. How the driver sends it is the driver's business (B41).

Action cards in the scene editor are reorderable by keyboard: focus a card and use Arrow Up and Down. A live region announces Action moved to position 3 of 6.

<FaderStrip
  aria-label="Wireless Mic 1 fader"
  aria-valuemin={0}
  aria-valuemax={1000}
  aria-valuenow={Math.round(position * 1000)}
  aria-valuetext={formatDb(db)}   // "−5.0 decibels", "0.0 decibels, unity", "off"
/>

aria-valuenow carries the fader's position and aria-valuetext the decibel reading from the driver's law, because a level is not linear in position and the bottom of travel is off rather than a number. The interface never sees the desk's wire format (B41): the store holds decibels and the component converts to position only when drawing (§21.2).

#24.3 Screen readers

Semantic HTML with landmark regions, correct heading order, and labels associated with inputs. Live regions announce device status changes, scene results and toasts. Status indicators carry text — Mixer: connected — not colour alone. Faders expose full ARIA value attributes.

Device status changes are announced by one live region only (since v0.1.18, after the Narrator pass of 1 October 2026, where stopping knxd produced "KNX offline" buried in a great deal of other reading). A single visually hidden, polite connection announcer says only what changed — "KNX offline", "KNX and DMX offline. Mixer degraded.", "KNX back online" — coalesced over one second; an unchanged state is never repeated, "connecting" is never spoken, and the text clears after ten seconds so a browse cursor never finds a stale message. Everything that used to announce the same change no longer does: the status-bar device list and the phone summary LED keep their accessible names but are not live regions (§21.7), the device-offline banners are not live (§21.26), and the mixer's notices are not role="status".

Focus follows navigation to the new screen's heading, and the active navigation item says ", current page" (§21.6).

The stage plan uses role="application" on the SVG root with role="button" and a descriptive label on each fixture: Stage Wash 3, channel 7, at 72 percent. Level changes are announced on fade completion rather than during, so a two-second fade produces one announcement rather than a hundred.

Modals and sheets trap focus, close on Escape, and return focus to the triggering element — or, if that element has been deleted, to the nearest logical target.

#24.4 Contrast

Measured ratios for the revised palette. Values below 4.5:1 for normal text are marked.

Foreground bg-base bg-surface bg-elevated bg-overlay
text-primary #F0F4F8 17.33 16.05 13.05 11.47
text-secondary #8A9BB0 6.75 6.25 5.08 4.46 †
text-muted #7589A0 5.33 4.94 4.01 † 3.53 †
teal-500 #00B8A0 7.63 7.07 5.74 5.05
success-text #5EE08A 11.10 10.28 8.36 7.35
warning-text #FFC24D 11.42 10.57 8.60 7.56
danger-text #FF7B7B 7.62 7.06 5.74 5.04
info-text #7FB0FF 8.11 7.51 6.11 5.37
text-inverse #0A0F1A on teal-500 7.63

† Below 4.5:1. Rules that follow from this:

  • text-secondary is not used on bg-overlay for body text. Overlay is a hover and selection layer, and text on it uses text-primary.
  • text-muted is used only on bg-base and bg-surface. It is prohibited on elevated and overlay.
  • text-disabled #4A5B6E is used only for disabled controls, which WCAG exempts. It never carries information.
  • The original single semantic set failed as text on the upper layers, which is why §21.3 defines separate -text variants.

Size is not a contrast variable here. An earlier revision carried a rule prohibiting text-muted below --text-sm. That was not derived from WCAG and has been removed: 11px and 13px are both normal text requiring 4.5:1, and the relaxed 3:1 threshold does not begin until 24px, or 18.7px bold. The constraint that matters is which background, and it is stated above.

The earlier revision also mis-stated the muted row of this table in all four columns. The figures above are recomputed.

The claim in earlier revisions that all text met 4.5:1 was not accurate. The accurate statement is: all text carrying information meets 4.5:1 on the backgrounds it is permitted to appear on, and the permitted combinations are enumerated above.

The green, amber and red palette was checked under protanopia, deuteranopia and tritanopia simulation, and every state carries a shape cue as well (§24.1).

#24.5 Motion

prefers-reduced-motion collapses transitions and animations to 50 ms and stops the LED pulse and fixture node transitions entirely. Static LED glow is kept — it is an indicator, not an animation.

#24.6 Hirer surface

Designed for someone who has used the system once, or never.

  • 72×72px minimum on every interactive element, against 44×44px elsewhere
  • No configuration surfaces — nothing can be broken from here
  • Plain language where admin naming allows: Turn up mic 1 rather than Fader Ch1 +1 dB
  • The whole surface fits one scrollable page per tab, with no hidden navigation
  • Failure messages say what to do — speak to venue staff — rather than what went wrong

#24.7 Testing checklist

□  Tab through every admin screen — logical order, no traps
□  Every modal and sheet traps focus and returns it on close
□  Escape closes every modal and sheet
   (open owner decision: whether Escape dismisses the re-authentication overlay
   and the other session prompts, which are hand-built, not sheets — §6.6; until
   decided, they do not close on Escape)
□  Every interactive element has a visible focus ring
□  Stage plan is ONE tab stop; arrow keys move within it; skip link present
□  Surface configurator uses the same roving-tabindex pattern
□  Three simultaneous error toasts do not block a fourth toast appearing
□  Faders operable by Arrow, Page, Home and End
□  Fader aria-valuetext announces dB, not raw NRPN
□  Detent announces itself — "0.0 decibels, unity" — not visual only
□  No text below 11px anywhere, including fader and meter scale legends
□  Every image and icon has a label or is aria-hidden
□  Colour-blind simulation — no information lost in any state
□  Reduced motion — no layout shift, animations stopped
□  No interactive element below 44px; none below 72px on the hirer surface
□  Measured contrast matches §24.4; no prohibited combination in use
□  No `text-muted` on `bg-elevated` or `bg-overlay` anywhere
□  Group palette values used only for identity, never for status
□  No group colour used as the sole distinction — a name is always present
□  Muted text legibility verified **in the room, with the lights down** —
   4.94:1 is a compliance floor, not a guarantee in a dark auditorium
□  After an in-app navigation, focus moves to the new screen's heading —
   not on first load or a redirect, never into an open dialog or a text field
□  The active navigation item announces "current page" (hidden text
   beside aria-current)
□  The skip link is visible on :focus, not only :focus-visible
□  One device change is announced once, by the connection announcer only
□  Screen reader walkthrough of the hirer surface — Narrator on the Windows 11 touch-screen PC and TalkBack on Android, the installation's hirer devices (the iOS VoiceOver pass joins when an iPad is available)
□  Print stylesheet — light background, text labels beside every indicator

#Appendix A — Known Limitations

Constraints that are real, permanent, and easy to rediscover painfully. Collected here for handover and for setting expectations.

Audio

  • Metering depends on a second, reverse-engineered connection (§7.3). A firmware update could break it without notice; the failure degrades a display and leaves control unaffected.
  • Metering is post-compressor on inputs and post-limiter on outputs. It answers "is this channel live?", not "is my gain structure right?".
  • One TCP MIDI connection. MixPad must use the CQ's WiFi or USB, never Ethernet, or it takes the connection and this application goes offline.
  • Stereo output link state is not readable. The configuration must be kept in step with MixPad by hand.
  • Soft key presses are not reported. They can be sent but not tracked.
  • Gain, phantom power, EQ, compression, routing, delay and limiters are not controllable. They are captured in CQ internal scenes and recalled.
  • Phantom power cannot be switched remotely, including by a critical scene. Mutes stop signal reaching the PA, which is sufficient.

Lighting

  • While a visiting desk is running, the KNX panel cannot control stage lighting. This is deliberate lockout, reported on the panel by a dedicated status indicator, and it clears automatically when the desk stops. It is a change from current practice.
  • The alarm cannot black out a rig a visiting desk is holding up. The node merges HTP downstream of the controller, so the controller's zeros lose. Inherent to any auditorium with a visiting desk; mitigated by powering the desk down, which is on the post-hire checklist.
  • A desk left powered overnight leaves stage lighting unresponsive at the wall panel. A desk sending zeros is still in control. The panel indicator shows why, and switching the desk off resolves it within five seconds.
  • A desk patched directly to the fixtures cannot be detected or observed. The manual flag exists for that case; levels shown are the controller's last values.
  • House lighting is not exposed to visiting desks. It is KNX-only, controlled from the booth touch panel, which offers a proper actuator-driven fade and level setting. A DMX-to-KNX path was considered and rejected — it would have added a merge problem the controller cannot solve, since KNX owns the endpoint and has multiple masters.
  • One universe is configured. 512 channels, ample for this rig. With the eDMX8 MAX in service this is now a software constraint rather than a hardware one — the node has eight ports, so expanding is a code and configuration change, not a purchase.
  • KNX software fades cost telegram budget. Two dimmers fading in software consume the entire 15/sec allowance; hardware fade mode is the default for this reason.

Control surface (optional)

  • The eight LED meters light only when the mixer's native connection is available (§7.3). Otherwise they stay dark, with the reason stated. Driving them from fader position remains prohibited in every case.
  • Inverted text blocks on the scribble strips are unreachable — no vendor sysex message exists for them.
  • The surface has no authentication. It is operator-tier by construction and is disabled during hirer access by default (§7.6).
  • RTP-MIDI is implemented without the recovery journal. A lost packet means a dropped update, corrected by the next state broadcast within about 70 ms. Acceptable on a wired point-to-point link; it would not be on a congested or wireless one.
  • The Extender does not speak Xctl, so Behringer's native X32 protocol is not an option on this hardware.

System

  • Anyone on VLAN 30 can control the downstream devices directly (§3.5). The permission model guards against accident, not intent.
  • Single SSD means no failure independence. OS, application, database and local backups share one device. Mitigated by commodity replaceability and two real backup copies.
  • Restoring to replacement hardware requires re-entering device passwords, because the device secret is deliberately excluded from backups.
  • No email from emergency mode if the SSD has failed entirely — the machine will not boot. The fallback SMTP configuration covers /data being unavailable on a machine that still boots.
  • Self-signed certificates block PWA installation on iOS and break WebSocket connections rather than warning.
  • The HDMI matrix accepts front-panel and IR remote input, which this application cannot prevent. Changes are detected within 30 seconds by the routing poll. The IR remote is stored out of the rack for this reason.
  • The matrix's audio breakout follows an HDMI output, selected by a front-panel button that RS-232 cannot reach. Harmless while both outputs carry the same source.
  • Independent per-output HDMI routing is supported by the hardware but not exposed in the interface, which always drives both outputs together.
  • system_state is throttled to 500 ms, so the last half-second of fader movement before an ungraceful power loss is not persisted.
  • Rate limit counters reset on restart — acceptable, since restarting requires SSH.

#Appendix B — Decisions in Version 3.0 and 3.1

The substantive changes from v2.1, with the reasoning, so a successor can see why things are the way they are. Items 68–76 are the owner's decisions taken on site during Phase 7 (September–October 2026); each says what it superseded, and the superseded text has been changed in place and points back here.

# Decision Reasoning
1 Single SSD, five partitions, A/B root slots — replaces the read-only eMMC plus separate NVMe design The carrier's SD slot does not work with an eMMC CM5, which took out the entire backup and recovery tier. Rather than patch it, the storage model was re-derived. Loses failure independence; gains a failed component that is a commodity M.2 stick obtainable locally rather than a Compute Module on unpredictable lead time.
2 Application on /data/app/<version> with an atomic symlink The previous design wrote updates to a read-only overlay, so they would have silently reverted at the next reboot while appearing to succeed. Rollback also becomes a symlink change rather than restoring a directory tree — which matters most on the unattended automatic path.
3 /srv/appliance partition The device encryption secret must not travel in the same archive as the values it encrypts. Also carries the SMTP fallback and boot health marker, both of which need to survive /data being unavailable.
4 Level store → compositor → universe buffer The fade engine previously wrote straight to the buffer while a separate section defined output as individual × group × master. Both could not be true. The compositor also makes external control handoff coherent and resolves the RGB master-level question. Amended by item 73 (30 September 2026): the group term is gone — groups set levels — so output is individual × master.
5 Lighting values are 0–100 with one decimal, colour is 0–255 Six sections previously disagreed, and one WebSocket example mixed both scales in a single message. Converting at the protocol boundary optimises for the common path — the value crosses the API far more often than it hits the DMX wire.
6 Custom Art-Net implementation pyartnet is send-only, with no receive path and no ArtPoll — both of which this design needs. It also provides an abstraction, fade engine and correction curves that go unused. Around 300 lines in the repository beats maintaining a fork of a dormant upstream to use 15% of it.
7 Hirer permissions on entity columns, JSON removed Permissions lived in two places, two admin screens edited different copies, and server enforcement read only one of them.
8 Eight scene action domains, down from ten dmx_fade was dmx with a duration; knx_dimmer was a KNX write with a level. Neither had the schema columns to be anything else.
9 scene_triggers gains match_type, match_value_max, debounce_ms The six-mode matching model was specified but unimplementable against the schema. Debounce is new — KNX panels send repeats, and a critical scene firing twice cancels its own first execution. Superseded by item 50: scene_triggers has since collapsed into rules, which carries all three fields.
10 Platform abstraction layer; hardware requirements separated from the reference platform Compute Module supply has been intermittent. The application was already portable; the appliance image was not. Drawing the line explicitly costs little and keeps x86 and Rockchip open.
11 Battery-backed RTC required The CM5 has an RTC and the carrier has a battery holder — the previous claim that it had neither was wrong. Fitting a coin cell removed a large amount of subtle degraded-time-mode code defending a case that no longer occurs.
12 Network-layer exposure documented as an accepted risk, with Venue Baseline Restore as the response A wired drop on the same VLAN makes a wireless ACL half a measure. Rather than imply protection that does not exist, §3.5 states the position plainly and §13.5 provides the procedural mitigation.
13 Protocol tables removed from this document The frontend specification already forbade duplicating protocol data. The system specification was doing exactly that, and had already accumulated errors — a fabricated unity value, a wrong pan centre, wrong mute bytes, a spurious bank-select message.
14 Frontend specification merged in as §21 Cross-reference drift between two documents had left the mixer view specified two different ways, an obsolete companion version number, and a channel that does not exist on the hardware.
15 Display typeface removed; text-muted lightened; semantic text colours added The latest mockup had already dropped the serif. Measured contrast showed text-muted at 2.75:1 while being specified for timestamps and placeholders, and semantic colours failing as text on the upper background layers. Muted was subsequently lightened again to #7589A0 after recomputation showed the first correction still failing on bg-surface by a hundredth.
16 nginx corrected Missing X-Forwarded-For would have silently broken per-IP rate limiting and the audit log; the 1 MB body limit would have rejected every firmware upload; the 60-second read timeout would have closed every idle WebSocket.
17 HDMI matrix is a Lenkeng LKV422 on RS-232, reached by USB-to-serial cable Hardware was selected in April 2026. It takes the matrix off the network — no address, no firewall rules, no TCP client — and replaces an $800–$1,800 line in the BOM with a budget device. USB was chosen over the carrier's GPIO UART: it travels with the controller to any platform, it deletes the UART overlay and getty configuration rather than moving it, it can be unplugged and tested from a laptop, and — since the vendor does not state the port's signalling level — it puts the cost of guessing wrong on a $200 switcher rather than on the Compute Module.
18 DMX runs on the eDMX8 MAX over Ethernet The node was acquired in 2026, making Art-Net the production path rather than the intended one. That removed a running service, a startup dependency and a udev rule from the normal configuration, and freed the USB port the HDMI serial cable now uses. See item 48 for the eventual removal of OLA entirely.
19 Backup archives are not encrypted; the Cloudflare token is excluded instead A system examined once a year fails at the "where is the passphrase" step, and an archive that cannot be decrypted loses the data outright. The token is the only item whose blast radius reaches beyond the auditorium and is also the easiest to reissue, so removing it achieves the security goal without introducing a key to lose.
20 OS upgrades are signed, sharing one package format and one verification path with application packages An OS image is the whole machine and a larger prize than an application package; signing one and not the other was the wrong way round. Trust anchors moved from a single file to a directory, which is what makes key rollover possible at all — replacing the anchor needs an OS upgrade, which needs a signature from the key being replaced.
21 Presentation audio takes the matrix's optical S/PDIF output through a converter into ST2, not the 3.5 mm analogue tap The existing installation runs ST2's gain at maximum to get a usable level from an equivalent switcher's analogue output. A converter's line stage is specified; the tap is not. Optical is chosen over coaxial for galvanic isolation, which removes a ground-loop hum path rather than treating it later.
22 Emergency lighting independence confirmed and recorded The master dimmer can take the stage to zero. It scales every DMX channel; KNX house dimmers were taken out of its reach in September 2026 (§9.5). That is only safe because egress lighting is a separate battery-backed system. The assumption was previously unstated, which meant nothing would have flagged it if the lighting were ever re-scoped.
23 Control surface uses Mackie Control over RTP-MIDI, not Xctl or custom hardware The Extender does not implement Xctl, so the question was moot for this device — but MCU would have won regardless: it is documented, it is spoken by several manufacturers so the hardware stays substitutable, and it avoids building a public repository on a reverse-engineered vendor protocol. Custom hardware was rejected on handover grounds rather than cost: it breaks the §20 promise that a successor needs only Python and React.
24 Arbitrary per-strip banking replaces MCU's fixed channel paging MCU pages through eight consecutive channels because a DAW owns the mapping. This application owns both ends, so a bank is eight slots each pointing at any target in any domain — which is what allows one surface to carry two microphones, HDMI, Main LR and four lighting rows simultaneously. It also makes fader scaling a per-strip property rather than a per-device one.
25 External lighting desk handover is automatic, detected from ArtDmx on the booth input Merging was rejected first: under HTP, staff pressing OFF against a desk holding a bank up leaves the stage lit with no recovery an ordinary staff member could perform — and that arbitration happens in the node regardless, since the input must share the output universe. Standing down entirely is the only coherent option. Detection then comes free: configuring the input port to broadcast makes frame presence the signal and carries the desk's levels, so the operator sees real stage state instead of a stale model. ArtPollReply was rejected as the signal because it refreshes only per poll period. The procedural rule and sACN priority takeover are retained as documented fallbacks.
26 Compositor split into independent DMX and KNX passes A single loop branching per channel type meant any condition suspending the compositor suspended KNX dimmers too — so external control took out house lighting, which it has no business touching. Two passes sharing a level store. Also gave colour and the clamp point somewhere coherent to live.
27 KNX group bindings are a first-class concept, not scenes The four stage banks are the daily control path. As scenes they would fill the execution log with routine traffic, duplicate status feedback into every scene, and bury the address-to-group mapping. As bindings they are four rows on one admin screen. The binding forces the group multiplier to 1.0 on recall, so leftover web-interface state cannot make a panel press come up dim while the indicator reports "on". Superseded by item 50: bindings are now one shape of rule, in §8. The forced 1.0 is itself superseded by item 73: with no multiplier, a recall is a group-level write and there is nothing to force.
28 Rendering is change-driven with an idle keepalive, not a fixed frame rate Art-Net is not DMX512 — the node regenerates the physical refresh and holds last value, so continuous sending is unnecessary. At rest this is one packet per second instead of forty, and the compositor does not run at all. Amended (v0.1.16, 30 September 2026): while output is moving frames go on a steady 40 fps grid, returning to the keepalive 250 ms after it stops, and operator fader writes glide the output over 75 ms (§7.2.3). Sending only on change had made the frame rate follow fader writes arriving over Wi-Fi — an irregular ~20 fps the room saw as flicker. The reasoning above still holds at rest, where the appliance spends nearly all its time.
29 House lighting stays KNX-only A DMX channel mapped to the house dimmer would let a visiting desk fade the house, but KNX owns that endpoint and has several masters, so the controller cannot merge — only write. The KNX telegram budget of 15/sec also forecloses continuous translation. The booth touch panel already provides a fade and a level, three metres from where the desk sits.
30 One write connection behind a lock; reads from a small pool aiosqlite serialises statements on a connection but not transactions, so two coroutines interleaving BEGIN/COMMIT would corrupt each other's scope — one rollback discarding the other's work. Long deletes are chunked because the nightly prune would otherwise hold the write lock for seconds during an event. synchronous = NORMAL because the 500 ms state throttle already concedes the last half-second on power loss, so an fsync per commit buys nothing.
31 Hirer tokens carry identity only; permissions resolve live Resolving permitted IDs into the JWT at issue time reintroduced the two-sources-of-truth defect §15.4 was written to remove: ceilings applied immediately, channel removal did not. Permissions now resolve from the entity columns via state.hirer, rebuilt on config change, so both come from one place. token_version is reduced to invalidating on a PIN change or access being disabled — never on a permission change, which is the whole point: a page reassignment or a lowered ceiling takes effect through state.hirer on the next broadcast, with no re-login. The bump on disable stays, so re-enabling access for the next hire never revives a phone still holding the previous hire's session.
32 Frontend live state uses a purpose-built store with a pending overlay; continuous writes move to the WebSocket The obvious optimistic-update pattern — write the value, save the old one, restore on failure — restores a stale value whenever an authoritative frame lands between the write and the rejection. Separating authoritative and pending maps makes reconciliation a single delete in both directions, and gives gesture arbitration the same shape as the X-Touch's motor arbitration (§7.6). Per-key subscription makes render work proportional to what changed rather than to how many components exist. Moving continuous writes off REST was forced by arithmetic: one operator dragging one fader at 30 Hz would consume a third of the §23.1 request budget.
33 Migrations apply at startup only; rollback repoints the symlink before restoring the snapshot Applying migrations in both the update process and the startup runner meant two code paths for one job, and the seam showed in rollback. Ordering is stated because the intuitive order — restore the data, then the code — leaves new code against an old schema, which migrates forward and silently undoes the rollback. Symlink first leaves old code against a new schema, which the existing schema-ahead guard refuses to start on.
34 Scene precedence is explicit, not a consequence of write frequency The claim that scenes won by last-write-wins was wrong about the implementation — the fade engine already cancels on a competing write, so the operator wins — and wrong as a design, since a fade writing at 50 Hz beats a human by timing rather than by rule. Normal scenes yield the channel; critical scenes lock it, because an alarm blackout must not be defeatable by leaning on a fader.
35 A closed error-code vocabulary shared by REST and the WebSocket Status codes cannot distinguish "not permitted" from "mixer offline" from "out of range", and those want different messages and different recovery. Grouping codes by required client behaviour means the frontend has one mapping rather than a per-endpoint reading of intent. value_out_of_range matters most: a hirer's fader clamped to its ceiling has succeeded, and presenting it as a failure would make a working limit look broken.
36 Optimistic concurrency on configuration writes Control state resolves last-write-wins; configuration cannot, because a silently lost scene edit is discovered months later in a scene that stopped working. The column already exists, the check is one comparison, and this installation's single admin makes conflicts rare rather than impossible.
37 A toast reports an event; a banner reports a state Error toasts previously had no auto-dismiss and were ineligible for eviction, so three offline devices produced three permanent toasts after which no toast of any kind could appear — including the success message for the fix. The underlying error was using a toast for a persistent condition when a banner already existed for exactly that.
38 ProtocolClient separates connect from probe Five clients across four transport classes, and for three of them a successful connect proves nothing — a serial port opens with nothing attached, UDP has no connection, RTP-MIDI is negotiated rather than connected. Each had solved this locally, in prose, in its own section. Status now derives from probe, and the two failure kinds surface differently because "check the config" and "check the cable" are different jobs.
39 State ownership is enforced by domain-scoped write handles The rule was a comment, and Python does not enforce comments. The violation it guards against — a second component writing a domain it does not own — appears as an untraceable last-write-wins bug weeks later. Raising in development and logging in production is deliberate: a stray write is a bug, but crashing a control system mid-performance over one is worse.
40 Every device category sits behind a driver interface with declared capabilities Two of six categories were abstracted; the mixer, projector and control surface were not, and core/mixer.py was the CQ-20B. More importantly the hardware was in the data model — six NRPN columns, a source_key naming scheme, a three-value fixture_type enum and pjlink_* scene domains. Capability declaration is what lets one interface serve different hardware: supports_metering alone replaces three separately hard-coded statements of the same fact.
41 Core value domains are hardware-independent; drivers publish their fader law as data Mixer levels were stored as 14-bit NRPN — the CQ-20B's wire format — in hirer ceilings, in every saved scene and over the API. A mixer swap would have left them silently wrong rather than obviously broken. The core stores dB; the driver publishes a position-to-dB table so the browser can interpolate at frame rate without the core owning a curve that belongs to the desk.
42 KNX is a subsystem, not a driver category A generic building-control interface over KNX would be a rename rather than a generalisation — group addresses are already an abstract addressing model and DPTs are already a datatype system. DALI is differently shaped, not merely a different transport. knxd is the transport abstraction, which is the level that actually needs one. A second system would be added alongside, not behind a shared interface.
43 The virtual surface is configuration; it does not derive from the mixer mixer_channels describes what the venue chose to expose, in the order that suits the operator, mapped to whatever inputs the desk happens to have. A typical installation shows operators four rows — two mics, HDMI, master — bearing no relation to the desk's numbering (since 28 September 2026 every desk channel is created with the mixer and the rest are hidden, not absent), and inserting an AUX mid-list is normal rather than a deviation. This is what makes a swap cheap: names, order, ceilings, scene actions and surface assignments never depended on the driver's numbering, so only the reference changes. The number column was removed for the same reason — it would have fought the first mid-list insertion.
44 A control surface driver publishes a layout manifest, not markup Driver-supplied SVG was rejected: it is markup injected into the admin interface, which §6.11 exists to prevent; it cannot use the design system's tokens or contrast rules; interaction would need an implicit schema embedded in attributes anyway; and §21.25's accessibility cannot be generated from arbitrary paths. A grid-coordinate manifest renders at any scale and carries functional, so a control that cannot be driven in the current configuration is declared rather than discovered — and re-evaluated when capabilities change, which is what let the X-Touch's meters come alive once the CQ's native protocol was found.
45 Transport is separate from protocol The X-Touch selects Mackie Control or HUI on one encoder and Network, USB or MIDI on another — the hardware already treats them as orthogonal, and MIDI over TCP, USB and DIN carry identical semantics. A driver declares which transports it works over; the transport owns addressing, so a driver never mentions a host or a device path. This is also what the connect/probe split was finding: connect is transport-level and liveness is protocol-level. Swapping a networked projector for an RS-232 one becomes a dropdown rather than a driver.
46 Serial ports enumerate from /dev/serial/by-id/, never ttyUSB* pyserial reports ttyUSB0, and storing that is exactly the defect §4.12 exists to prevent. By-id paths are udev-populated, stable across replug, and carry the serial number — which makes custom udev rules documented best practice rather than a commissioning requirement. A cable with no serial number is flagged as having no stable path, surfacing the chip-family problem while it can still be solved by buying a different cable.
47 Interface methods take the full set of targets for one intent A single call is one intent the driver can optimise; two calls are two intents, and the driver has already committed the first before the second arrives. The scene engine makes this load-bearing — §8.13 fires grouped actions as separate calls, so modelling video destinations rather than individual outputs is what guarantees the LKV422's single PA{n}R is reachable at all.
48 OLA removed; the spare USB adapter gets a serial driver instead OLA's only remaining job was driving a fallback adapter that will almost certainly never be used, and it was the sole reason the transport layer needed a custom escape hatch. The Open DMX USB has no microcontroller, so driving it is about forty lines on the serial transport — OLA was not buying timing precision, only breadth of device support this system does not need. Removing it drops a service, two boot steps, a config symlink, a backup entry and the transport exception. The opendmx driver is recorded as unverified, because a fallback the spec implies works is worse than one it admits is untested.
49 Video matrix inputs and outputs use device_id and driver_ref like every other addressable thing These two tables predated the driver model and kept integer port numbers, which left scene_actions.hdmi_input_id resolving to an integer against a driver interface taking a reference string, and left matrix_outputs with no incoming foreign key because destinations repeated the reference instead of pointing at the row. The default input moved to the destination, since a single global default cannot express two destinations defaulting differently.
50 §8 becomes Rules and Scenes; rules say when, scenes say what The daily control path — four KNX buttons switching four lighting banks — had nowhere to live and was specified inside the KNX chapter, which is why it lacked debounce, a module owner and a defined completion point while scenes had all three. Three rule shapes cover everything: event, binding, and derived status. Rules stay one-line forever because anything needing steps or delays runs a scene, so the rules engine never grows sequencing. scene_triggers and knx_group_bindings both collapse into one rules table — they were rules before the layer existed.
51 Status is derived from state, not written by whatever changed it A pair of on/off event rules leaves a panel indicator wrong until the next transition if a telegram is lost — possibly hours. A derived rule recomputes on every state change and writes when the value differs, so it self-corrects. It also explains the All button: one evaluation, is everything this button controls on?, applied to every binding after any change, with no mixed state to represent in a boolean. Bindings therefore hold no state of their own. Amended (v0.1.10, 29 September 2026): a KNX panel flips its own icon when pressed, so after a press on an enabled rule's trigger address every addressed status is re-sent at its current value once what the press started has finished — the one exception to writing only on change, since a refused press changes no value and would otherwise leave the icon wrong (§8.6). And see item 72: "on" is judged on what the room sees.
52 Projector commands during warm-up and cool-down are rejected, not queued Queuing produced delayed surprise: power-on pressed during cool-down fires ninety seconds later, possibly into an empty room. Rejection is not silent as long as the interface says why. Sequencing is what scenes are for — §8.13's worked example already covers "set the input once it has warmed". A second boolean status address carries "changing state", because DPT 1.001 cannot represent a six-state model and a button that looks dead for ninety seconds gets pressed again. Extended by item 74: rejection covers only what the projector admits to, so the controller also holds a minimum warm-up of its own.
53 A control surface button fires a rule, not a scene With rules existing, a scene target was the redundant one — routing a button through the rule layer gives it a guard, debounce and a log entry for free, and means one mechanism decides when things happen regardless of which surface asked. Also corrected a mismatch where §8.3 listed surface as a trigger source that §7.6 had no target for.
54 Python floor is 3.13, tracking the base OS; frontend moves to Vite 8, TypeScript 7 and Vitest The 3.11 floor reaches end of life in October 2027, inside this system's first maintenance cycle. Targeting newer than Debian 13 ships was also rejected: it would mean this project owning CVE tracking for the interpreter on an appliance examined once a year, for no feature it needs. The floor moves when the OS moves, which keeps interpreter patching inside the A/B upgrade mechanism rather than creating a second one. Vitest fills a real gap — the frontend carries fader-law, pending-overlay and error-mapping logic that browser tests are too slow to cover well — and it reuses Vite's transform pipeline, so test and build configuration cannot drift apart.
55 The mixer driver holds two connections: MIDI for control, the native protocol for metering An earlier revision recorded that the CQ exposes no metering by any means and that MixPad did not meter either. Both were wrong — MixPad meters, and the data is on the CQ's proprietary protocol. A capture confirmed every input the venue uses is metered, including the ST2 HDMI feed. MIDI stays primary because it is A&H's published contract, because writing to an undocumented protocol on a live console is a different risk class from reading, and because scene recall is not in the reverse-engineered work and is central to how the venue operates. Protocol detail lives in docs/protocols/cq20b-native.md rather than here, since it is reverse-engineered and will change.
56 Capabilities are resolved at connect, not declared statically A driver's capabilities can depend on what the connection achieved: if the CQ's native connection is refused, the mixer works completely and only metering is lost. Static capabilities would render meters that never move and light 64 LEDs on the control surface that stay dark, with nothing to explain why. Reporting what was actually achieved turns a mystery into a diagnosis, and it generalises — a projector's supported inputs are not knowable until it is asked either.
57 The fader law table also produces the printed scale and the detents The law was specified for positioning a thumb, and the tick marks were then drawn separately — which would have hard-coded one manufacturer's legend into the interface. Desks print different scales and put unity in different places, so a scale that survives a mixer swap has to come from the same table the thumb does. Snapping is to the dB value rather than the position, so a scene saves exactly 0.0 and not 0.03, and break-out is counted in pointer movements rather than pixels so it behaves the same on a 4K display and a phone.
58 Metering has a defined path from driver to both surfaces Meters are the only data with two independent consumers and a rate mismatch at every hop — a mixer emitting at its own rate, a browser batching at 10–15 fps, a control surface capped per strip. One rule carries the design: meters never enter the control path, which is the same rule as lighting's observed store and keeps a 30 fps feed out of persistence and out of anything that could act on it. Ganged channels carry a list rather than a scalar, so a mono channel is a one-element list and ganging one later does not change its type. A channel with no data is absent rather than at the floor, which is what makes "absent, not empty" implementable. Meter range and measurement point are driver capabilities, not constants — the same mistake as hard-coding a fader legend, avoided.
59 Channel names come from our configuration, with a short form only where hardware forces it The desk's labels belong to the audio engineer and describe a physical input; ours belong to the operator and describe what the venue calls it. Deriving one from the other would change the operator's interface because someone renamed a channel at soundcheck. The virtual strip fits about sixteen characters on one line and never wraps — a name that does not fit is being used as a description, and notes exists for that. Only the surface's seven-character scribble needs a short form, and it is plain truncation with a collision check rather than a heuristic: "Wireless Mic 1" and "Wireless Mic 2" both truncate to "Wirele", and truncation removes exactly the discriminating part. A cleverer algorithm would be right often enough to be trusted and wrong often enough to mislead.
60 Pages are the everyday surface; Mixer and Lighting are the advanced view A page groups by activity rather than domain and is the only place a button panel can live, because a button has no position in a linear channel scroll. Panels are one column of square buttons per unit of width, with rows worked out per viewport rather than fixed (§21.9, B63) — so there is no grid to configure and no way to produce unusably small targets. Buttons fire rules and take their lamps from derived status, both of which already existed (§8), which means the wall panel and the tablet read one source rather than two. A default page is generated so the surface still cannot require configuration (§5.5).
61 Hirer visibility becomes page assignment Pages control what, ceilings control how far. Six visible_hirer columns across five tables collapse into one join, and a rule a hirer can fire becomes a button on a page someone can look at rather than a flag somewhere. Ceilings survive because a ceiling is not a visibility question — the same channel may sit on a staff page and a hirer page, and the limit applies whenever the session is a hirer.
62 A twelve-colour identity palette, selected by measurement Hue separation is a constraint in its own right: two colours can measure far apart in ΔE and still read as one colour at two brightnesses, which is what happened to a pair of cyans 19° apart. Every adjacent pair now clears 24° of hue and ΔE2000 18, with 44° required through the blue region where discrimination is poorest. CIEDE2000 throughout, because CIE76 overstates blue differences by roughly a factor of two. Distance from the semantic colours is relaxed — identity and status appear in different places — but there is no true red, because on a near-black background every usable red either is the danger red or is a salmon.
63 Panel buttons are square; size and columns are chosen together, and panels never scroll Fixing rows at three wasted a booth screen and overflowed an 11″ tablet. Buttons are square so the count follows from height, and when they do not fit the button shrinks before the panel widens — searched jointly, because expanding columns first commits to a shape before finding that a slightly smaller button would have kept the configured one. Vertical scrolling was removed rather than indicated: two attempts at an affordance were unconvincing, and removing the case leaves the surface with one scroll direction and no "did I scroll or tap" ambiguity. A panel also occupies the same footprint as N channel strips including their gaps, so the surface reads as one grid, and the slack becomes the horizontal gap rather than widening the buttons.
64 Display scale is offered only at or above the design target Filling the height stops being right sooner than expected — 1080p already gives twice a console fader's travel, and 4K unscaled gives a third of a metre. A tall display therefore scales rather than stretching, and the layout sees a smaller logical viewport so there is no second code path. The control is hidden below 1920 × 1080 because the layout is already choosing button size and column count there and a manual factor would fight it. Viewport size decides, not device type: a booth touchscreen is a coarse pointer and a large display, so it reads as a tablet to every media query that would otherwise be consulted. Clarified (v0.1.12, 29 September 2026): the scale is app-wide, on the root element, and "viewport" means the screen's size — a browser window on a 4K monitor is shorter than 2160 px and would miss the threshold.
65 Foreground presence holds a session, not traffic "Refreshed on activity" was ambiguous in a way that broke both directions: counting WebSocket frames meant metering refreshed the session fifteen times a second and it never expired, while counting only REST calls logged out an operator working entirely over the socket. The Page Visibility API gives the honest signal — a booth screen showing the page all evening holds its session, a tablet in a bag with the screen off times out in thirty minutes. The absolute twelve-hour cap now applies to every tier rather than hirers alone, so nothing lives forever.
66 The application owns connection liveness, and backgrounded clients stop receiving continuous events A closed browser sends FIN and is detected immediately; a sleeping tablet sends nothing and is indistinguishable from an idle one, so a 20-second ping with a two-interval timeout is required — and §10.3 already removed the proxy's own timeout, which makes this the application's job by construction. A client that backgrounds says so, and the server drops it to discrete events only: metering is the most expensive thing on the wire and composing it for a suspended tablet is wasted work. Returning resyncs, which already existed.
67 The status bar moves to the bottom and gains a clock and a shared timer The top of the screen is for moving around; putting five device indicators between the operator and the navigation they were reaching for was wrong, and on a tablet held in two hands the bottom edge is where thumbs already are. The clock is deliberately the appliance's, not the browser's, because it is the clock scheduled rules fire against — a tablet showing its own idea of the time would have someone setting a 23:00 rule against a clock that is not deciding. The timer is server state rather than a per-client stopwatch, so two operators see one number and a reload does not lose it; only running and started_at are stored and elapsed is computed. Amended by item 70 at phone width.
68 Phones are portrait-only (29 September 2026) — supersedes §21.9's phone-landscape design (the floating rail, the dropped status bar, compact furniture) and §21.7's short-viewport rule Even with every adaptation, phone landscape was the one target where the shell itself changed, and it gave about 226 px of fader travel on a 390 px-tall screen. A phone is held upright; refusing landscape outright with a "turn your phone upright" overlay is simpler and honest about the device, where a cramped landscape layout looks supported and is not. The test is landscape, under 500 px tall and a coarse pointer, so no tablet and no short desktop window is ever blocked. The landscape code stays, covered.
69 The phone Mixer is one scrolling row, Main last (29 September 2026) — supersedes §21.13's narrow design of exactly three strips, swipe-paged with page chips, beside a pinned Main The pinned Main column left no room for even two strips at a phone's width. A row of at least two faders that scrolls sideways, with Main as its last item and no chips, is how the Pages surface and the Lighting rows already behaved, so one gesture works everywhere. Wider screens show as many inputs as fit and page only when they do not.
70 At phone width the status bar is one summary LED (29 September 2026) — amends §21.7 and item 67, which showed five indicators and the timer at every width Five dots, a timer, a clock and the account chip do not fit across a phone, and squeezing them (v0.1.12's condensed dots) still overlapped. One LED showing the worst state — red over amber over green, unconfigured not counting — answers the question a glance asks ("is anything wrong?"), and a tap opens the named list with each device's detail sheet, so nothing is lost. The shared timer is dropped on phones as the least needed there; the clock and account chip stay.
71 The controller is a lighting desk; KNX gets only the panel's indicators. "Stage all" is indicator-only (30 September 2026) — new lighting_groups.indicator_only; the panel's all-button becomes four row bindings Under the multiplier model a group containing every stage fixture held every row whenever it sat at or above them, and a binding forced it to 1.0, so after any wall-panel use the row faders stopped working. The whole-stage fader is the Lighting view's Master, as on a desk, and "Stage all" survives only as membership for the panel's all indicator: no fader, never bound, not on pages. The all-button drives the four rows directly, so the rows remain the controls.
72 Every panel indicator is on only when every member is at 100% of output (30 September 2026) — new derived_status.basis (level | output); amends item 51's predicate for the stage panel The indicator should say what the room shows: a row lamp lit while the Master is down, or while one fixture has been pulled down, is the panel claiming something untrue. So the stage statuses compare the composited output — level × master, as last sent — not the stored level. "Any fixture in the row on" was proposed and rejected: the button sets every fixture to full, and its lamp claims exactly that. level stays the default so existing statuses read as before.
73 Group faders set levels, not multipliers (30 September 2026) — supersedes §9.4's multiplier and the §7.2.3 "highest multiplier applies" rule, the group ghost mark, the dominated-group hint, the restoring of multipliers at boot (§12.3), the groups section of lighting_state, and the forced 1.0 on a binding recall (item 27, §8.8); amends item 4 A traditional lighting desk's model, which is what operators expect: moving a group fader sets its members, fixture faders trim them, and the next group move sets them again. The multiplier model needed a max rule to avoid double attenuation, which made one group silently override another; a ghost mark and a "held by" hint to explain it; and a forced 1.0 on every panel recall to keep the indicator honest — three mechanisms to make a surprising model survivable. With levels there is nothing to explain or force, and output is simply level × master.
74 The projector holds a minimum warm-up (30 September 2026) — min_warmup_s, default 60 s, 0–600; extends item 52 The installation's PT-EZ570 reports PJLink on 13 seconds after power-on while its lamp is still visibly warming, so item 52's refusal let a power-off through at +20 s, followed by ninety seconds of cooling. Rejection only covers what the projector admits to. The hold is measured from the controller's own command and shows warming until it ends; any fault is still shown at once, so it can delay but never hide. Adjustable per projector, because another model may report honestly.
75 Every backup verifies after writing (1 October 2026) — with the index reconciled against the destinations, and "missing" told apart from "corrupt" (§13.4) The first monthly check after a re-image reported a merely missing local copy as corrupt: the re-image had recreated /srv/local empty while the restored index still claimed local copies, and the check tried only local. The data was safe on USB. Checking a copy only once a month, at random, also meant nearly every archive was never read back at all. Now every run checks the built archive's integrity and reads each copy back, the index follows what each reachable destination actually holds, and only a copy read and found corrupt is called untrusted.
76 A "Shut down" control in Admin (1 October 2026) — POST /system/shutdown, through the privileged helper, beside restart and reboot, all three reachable from Admin → System Moving the controller into the rack needed a clean power-off, and the only way was SSH. Restart and reboot existed but only on the Updates screen. Powering off is privileged for the same reason as rebooting — the application never does it directly. The confirmation states the cost plainly: the power must be cycled to start the controller again, so nobody uses it to end a day.

Also corrected: /boot/firmware/config.txt path, FallbackNTP hostnames, PARTUUID mounting, udev matching on serial rather than duplicated vendor and product IDs, deadline-based fade timing, four USB ports rather than two, PoE stated at the port rather than the device, backup scheduling in local time rather than "NZST", hirer_config primary key constraint, users.tier uniqueness, explicit ON DELETE behaviour throughout, the scene log's reference to a PIN identifier that no longer exists, and the trust anchor moved off any path an update can write.

End of specification.

Version 3.1 · September 2026 · Simon / Symple Solutions Next review: after Phase 1 completion.
